### [1] [Clustered Codebook Quantization for 2D Gaussian-based Image Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05667)
*Runze Cheng,Yicheng Zhan,Josef Spjut,Kaan Akşit*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CGVQ is a Gaussian primitive-based image compression method that partitions Gaussian parameters into homogeneous groups before vector quantization, achieving 20% bpp reduction with maintained visual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian-based image representations suffer from degraded rate-distortion efficiency at higher fidelity targets due to storing a large number of floating-point parameters per primitive.

Method: Cluster-Guided Vector Quantization (CGVQ) partitions Gaussian parameters into homogeneous groups prior to quantization to improve compression efficiency and parameter reconstruction accuracy.

Result: CGVQ decreases bits per pixel (bpp) by 20% relative to the baseline while maintaining on-par visual quality.

Conclusion: Cluster-guided grouping of Gaussian parameters before vector quantization improves rate-distortion performance in Gaussian primitive-based image compression.

Abstract: Gaussian-based image representations effectively model image content using compact parametric primitives while preserving high visual fidelity, yet storing a large number of floating-point parameters per primitive degrades rate-distortion efficiency at higher fidelity targets. To improve the rate-distortion performance in Gaussian representation, we present our Cluster-Guided Vector Quantization (CGVQ), a Gaussian primitive based image compression method. Our key idea is to partition Gaussian parameters further into homogeneous groups prior to quantization, enabling higher compression efficiency and accurate parameter reconstruction. In practice, our extensive experiments show that CGVQ decreases the bpp by 20% with respect to our baseline, while maintaining on-par visual quality

</details>


### [2] [CanvasAgent: Enabling Complex Image Creation and Editing via Visual Tool Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05465)
*Hairui Zhu,Yiying Yang,Tengjin Weng,Ziyu Lu,Xiao Yao,Xiaoyang Ye,Lin Ma,Wenhao Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CanvasCraft is a large-scale multimodal dataset (140K executable trajectories, 10K RL task specs) for complex image creation/editing, and CanvasAgent is a tool-augmented multimodal agent trained via SFT + GRPO to orchestrate heterogeneous visual tools across multi-turn interactions, demonstrating effectiveness in multi-tool image workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal tool-use agents are optimized for perception, search, or domain-specific editing, lacking large-scale supervision for executable image-creation trajectories. Complex image creation requires orchestrating multiple heterogeneous visual tools (synthesis, segmentation, editing, compositing, text reading, enhancement) rather than mere inspection.

Method: CanvasAgent is a tool-augmented multimodal agent trained first with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn executable reasoning-action trajectories, then optimized with GRPO using a hybrid reward combining outcome- and process-level signals. It inspects intermediate results, tracks visual assets, and adapts tool decisions to evolving visual states during rollout.

Result: Experiments evaluate both final image quality and trajectory behavior, demonstrating effectiveness of CanvasAgent and the CanvasCraft dataset for complex multi-tool image creation workflows.

Conclusion: CanvasCraft and CanvasAgent enable complex multi-tool image creation by providing large-scale supervised trajectories and a learned agent that orchestrates heterogeneous visual tools through multi-turn interaction, validated on both final image quality and behavioral metrics.

Abstract: Complex image creation and editing often require more than a single generation or editing model. A user request may involve synthesizing images, localizing objects, segmenting regions, editing selected content, compositing intermediate assets, reading text, and enhancing the final result. Such tasks shift multimodal agents from perception-augmented reasoning to manipulation-centered visual creation, where tools must actively transform visual states rather than merely inspect them. However, existing multimodal tool-use agents are mostly optimized for perception, search, or domain-specific editing, and lack large-scale supervision for executable image-creation trajectories. In this paper, we introduce CanvasCraft, a large-scale multimodal tool-use dataset for complex image creation and editing, and \textbf{CanvasAgent}, a tool-augmented multimodal agent that learns to orchestrate heterogeneous visual tools through multi-turn interaction. CanvasCraft contains 140K fully annotated executable trajectories and 10K
  RL task specifications. CanvasAgent is first trained with SFT to learn executable reasoning-action trajectories, and is then optimized with GRPO using a hybrid reward that combines outcome- and process-level signals. During rollout, CanvasAgent inspects intermediate results, tracks visual assets, and adapts tool decisions to the evolving visual state. Experiments evaluate both final image quality and trajectory behavior, demonstrating the effectiveness of CanvasAgent and the proposed dataset for complex multi-tool image creation workflows.

</details>


### [3] [A Task-Driven Evaluation of UAV Detection and Tracking under Synthetic Fog](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05467)
*Amir Pouladi,Vesal Ahsani,Haijun Li,Homayoun Najjaran,Afzal Suleman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a task-driven evaluation framework linking fog generation, image restoration, object detection, and tracking for small UAVs in foggy, sky-dominant long-range imagery. It uses synthetic fog from real clear images via monocular depth estimation, compares restoration methods (classical, CNN, transformer), and evaluates detection and tracking under different training regimes. Key finding: fog degrades performance via missed detections; fog-inclusive training helps most, and restoration quality doesn't directly translate to perception gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fog severely degrades UAV visibility in sky-dominant long-range imagery, reducing detection and tracking reliability. Collecting and annotating real foggy UAV scenes is practically difficult, motivating the need for synthetic fog generation and a unified evaluation framework.

Method: A task-driven evaluation pipeline combining depth-aware synthetic fog generation (monocular depth estimation + atmospheric scattering model), restoration (classical, CNN-based, transformer-based), object detection (clean-only vs. fog-inclusive training, multiple detector variants), and tracking-by-detection on clean, foggy, and restored videos.

Result: Fog substantially degrades detection and tracking primarily through increased missed detections. Fog-inclusive training yields the most consistent robustness improvement. Test-time restoration is most beneficial when detectors are trained only on clean imagery. Restoration quality does not proportionally translate to downstream perception gains.

Conclusion: Restoration quality should be evaluated jointly with detection and tracking performance, as it does not necessarily translate into proportional gains in downstream perception tasks for foggy UAV imagery.

Abstract: Fog severely degrades the visibility of small unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) in skydominant, long-range imagery, reducing the reliability of downstream detection and tracking. This paper presents a task-driven evaluation framework that links depth-aware synthetic fog generation, image restoration, object detection, and tracking within a unified pipeline. Given the practical difficulty of collecting and annotating foggy UAV scenes, synthetic fog is generated from real clear-weather outdoor images containing UAV targets using monocular depth estimation and the atmospheric scattering model. Representative restoration methods from classical, convolutional neural network (CNN)-based, and transformer-based families are first compared, after which the selected restoration model is integrated into the downstream perception pipeline. Detection is evaluated under both clean-only and fog-inclusive training regimes using multiple detector variants, while tracking-by-detection is assessed on clean, foggy, and restored video sequences. Beyond image-level restoration metrics, the study evaluates how fog and restoration affect detection robustness and tracking performance. The results show that fog substantially degrades both detection and tracking, primarily through increased missed detections. Fog-inclusive training provides the most consistent improvement in robustness, whereas test-time restoration is most beneficial when the detector has been trained only on clean imagery. These findings show that restoration quality does not necessarily translate into proportional gains in downstream perception and therefore should be evaluated jointly with detection and tracking performance.

</details>


### [4] [Binocular Gaze Estimation with Single Camera and Single Light Source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05473)
*Tongbing Huang,Yang Fu,Yunfei Wang,Zhaocan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a gaze estimation method using only one camera and one light source by introducing a "virtual light source" and "virtual glint" to mimic a two-light-source system, enabling free head movement with reduced hardware requirements at the cost of some accuracy degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce hardware components (especially light sources) for gaze tracking on mobile devices, where using fewer components is preferable, while still enabling gaze estimation with free head movements.

Method: Introduces a "virtual light source" placed symmetrically to the real light source with respect to the camera, generating a "virtual glint" estimated from the relationship between pupil distances and glint distances in captured images. Gaze is estimated using polynomial regression assuming two light sources, with a new normalization factor designed for one-glint systems.

Result: The proposed one-camera-one-light-source method achieves acceptable performance, though with degradation compared to systems using two actual light sources.

Conclusion: Gaze estimation with minimal hardware (one camera, one light source) is feasible using the virtual light source concept, offering a practical trade-off between hardware simplicity and accuracy for mobile eye tracking.

Abstract: According to commonly consented theories, the minimum hardware requirement for gaze tracker is one camera and two light sources to realize gaze estimation with free head movements. However, in some scenarios such as eye tracking on mobile devices, it is preferable to use less components, especially light sources. We propose a gaze estimation method with one camera and one light source. A "virtual light source" is introduced, which is geometrically placed symmetrically to the real light source with respect to the camera, and generates a "virtual glint" in the acquired image. We estimate the "virtual glint" by exploiting the relationship between the distance between two pupils and two glints in the captured image, and estimate the gaze with polynomial regression assuming two light sources are available. A new normalization factor for regression method is verified, which turns out to be practical for one-glint system. The performance is proved to be acceptable, while degradation is noticed compared to system with two actual light sources.

</details>


### [5] [WebRetriever: A Large-Scale Comprehensive Benchmark for Efficient Web Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06118)
*Wei Dong,Tianyu Fu,Zhe Yu,Hanning Wang,Anyang Su,Zhizhou Fang,Yuyang Chen,Shuo Wang,Minghui Wu,Ping Jiang,Zhen Lei,Chenxu Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WebRetriever is a large-scale benchmark (800 websites, 1,550 tasks) with NavEval, an LLM-as-Judge framework using rich interaction context, establishing three complementary evaluation protocols that reveal navigation success alone is insufficient for predicting real-world web agent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing web agent benchmarks suffer from limited scale, domain diversity, and inadequate evaluation methodologies (LLM-as-Judge fails to capture fine-grained interaction semantics), while overemphasizing navigation success metrics at the expense of real-world deployment requirements.

Method: Constructed WebRetriever with 800 websites and 1,550 tasks across consumer, professional, and enterprise domains. Proposed NavEval, a novel LLM-as-Judge framework leveraging rich interaction context beyond visual screenshots. Established three evaluation protocols: navigation proficiency, knowledge-assisted interaction, and end-to-end task completion with information extraction.

Result: NavEval achieves state-of-the-art alignment with human judgment. Substantial performance disparities exist across evaluation protocols, showing navigation success alone is an insufficient predictor of real-world application effectiveness.

Conclusion: WebRetriever provides fine-grained diagnostic insights into web agent capabilities and establishes a rigorous evaluation foundation that better reflects real-world deployment needs beyond simple navigation metrics.

Abstract: As web agents increasingly demonstrate capabilities in automated task execution, the development of robust evaluation frameworks for assessing their navigation and task completion performance has emerged as a critical research priority. However, existing benchmarks exhibit fundamental limitations. First, they suffer from insufficient scale and limited domain diversity, constraining comprehensive evaluation of cross-domain generalization. Second, prevailing LLM-as-Judge evaluation methodologies inadequately capture fine-grained interaction semantics, particularly regarding precise query formulation and filtering operations. Third, current benchmarks predominantly emphasize navigation success metrics while neglecting critical requirements for real-world deployment scenarios. To address these limitations, we introduce WebRetriever, a large-scale benchmark encompassing 800 websites and 1,550 tasks across diverse domains, including consumer, professional, and enterprise sectors, with comprehensive coverage of user intent patterns. We propose NavEval (Navigation Evaluation), a novel LLM-as-Judge framework that leverages rich interaction context beyond visual screenshots, achieving state-of-the-art alignment with human judgment across multiple evaluation datasets. Furthermore, we establish three complementary evaluation protocols that collectively provide holistic assessment of web agent capabilities: navigation proficiency, knowledge-assisted interaction, and end-to-end task completion with information extraction. Extensive experimental analysis reveals substantial performance disparities across evaluation protocols, demonstrating that navigation success alone is an insufficient predictor of real-world application effectiveness. WebRetriever delivers fine-grained diagnostic insights into agent capabilities and establishes a rigorous foundation for advancing web agent research and development.

</details>


### [6] [Ground3D-LMM: Fine-Grained 3D Point Grounding and Spatial Reasoning with LMM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05493)
*Amol Harsh,Zongyan Han,Jean Lahoud,Ye Liu,Rao Muhammad Anwer,Hisham Cholakkal,Salman Khan,Fahad Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Ground3D-LMM is a unified 3D large multimodal model that takes point clouds and optional RGB images as input, supporting spatial conversations with point-grounded responses and metric numeric outputs (size, thickness, clearance, distance) at object/part granularity. It introduces the 3D Grounded Measurement task, a large-scale dataset (~2.5M QA pairs on ScanNet/ScanNet++), and achieves strong baselines for grounded, metric-aware 3D conversational understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D LMMs either respond without explicit 3D grounding (conversational systems) or are not designed for interactive, metric-aware dialogue (3D grounding models). There is no unified approach that combines verifiable 3D region grounding with metric physical measurements in real-world units for conversational queries.

Method: Ground3D-LMM takes a point cloud and optional RGB image as input, produces point-grounded responses and metric numeric outputs at object/part granularity, including multi-object queries. A large-scale dataset built on ScanNet/ScanNet++ with dense annotations and ~2.5M QA pairs across eight tasks, plus a manually verified test set, supports training and evaluation.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple datasets and tasks demonstrate that Ground3D-LMM provides a strong baseline for grounded, metric-aware 3D conversational understanding.

Conclusion: Ground3D-LMM unifies 3D grounding and metric measurement in a single conversational framework, addressing the gap between grounding-only models and non-grounded conversational systems. The dataset and model are publicly available.

Abstract: Natural-language queries about 3D environments become actionable when responses are verifiable and metric. Verifiability requires explicit grounding to the referred 3D region, while metric answers report physical measurements in real-world units (e.g., size, thickness, clearance, and distance). Existing 3D large multimodal models (LMMs) approaches remain limited: conversational systems typically respond without explicit 3D grounding, while 3D grounding models are not designed for interactive, metric-aware dialogue. In this paper, we present Ground3D-LMM, a unified model that takes a point cloud and an optional RGB image as input and supports 3D spatial conversation with (i) point-grounded responses and (ii) metric numeric outputs at both object and part granularity, including multi-object queries. To evaluate this intersection of grounding and measurement, we define the 3D Grounded Measurement task, which requires predicting the referred 3D region and the corresponding metric quantities in real-world units. We introduce a large-scale dataset built on ScanNet and ScanNet++ datasets with dense object and part annotations and roughly 2.5M question-answer pairs spanning eight tasks, along with a manually verified test set. Extensive experiments on multiple datasets and tasks show that our proposed Ground3D-LMM model provides a strong baseline for grounded, metric-aware 3D conversational understanding. Our dataset and model are publicly available.

</details>


### [7] [Tuning-Free Latent Diffusion Models for Ultrahigh-Resolution Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06136)
*Wanglong Lu,Lingming Su,Kaijie Shi,Minglun Gong,Xiaogang Jin,Hanli Zhao,Xianta Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UltraDiffEdit is a tuning-free image editing framework that extends latent diffusion models to ultra-high resolutions (up to 8K) using multi-scale progressive editing, multi-patch encoding, global-local consistency denoising, and patch-based hybrid sampling, running on a single RTX 3090 GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based methods are limited to resolutions below 1K due to memory constraints and high cost of high-resolution training data, while modern mobile devices capture images up to 8K. Simply upscaling low-resolution edits produces blurry results lacking fine details.

Method: UltraDiffEdit employs (1) multi-scale progressive editing strategy that iteratively blends high-resolution edited content with unedited areas coarse-to-fine; (2) multi-patch encoding to preserve visual details in latent space; (3) global-local consistency denoising to integrate edited and unedited latent features and ensure smooth transitions at boundaries; (4) patch-based hybrid sampling to capture local, intermediate, and global features for semantic coherence and fine detail enhancement.

Result: The framework handles image resolutions up to 8K using only a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 GPU, demonstrating superior editing quality and flexibility over existing methods.

Conclusion: UltraDiffEdit successfully bridges the gap between current diffusion-based editing capabilities (sub-1K) and real-world ultra-high-resolution demands (up to 8K), offering a tuning-free solution that produces detailed, artifact-free results.

Abstract: Recent diffusion-based generative models have shown impressive performance in image generation and editing. However, due to memory limitations and the high cost of collecting high-resolution training images, existing methods are typically restricted to inputs with linear resolutions below 1K. In contrast, photos captured by modern mobile devices often reach linear resolutions up to 8K, revealing a significant gap between current capabilities and real-world demands. Simply upscaling low-resolution edited results often results in visually enlarged but blurry images that lack fine details. This paper introduces UltraDiffEdit, a novel, tuning-free image editing framework that extends off-the-shelf latent diffusion models (LDMs) to ultrahigh resolutions. UltraDiffEdit employs a multi-scale progressive editing strategy, iteratively blending high-resolution edited content with unedited areas in a coarse-to-fine manner. We employ multi-patch encoding to preserve both edited and unedited visual details within the latent space. To mitigate editing artifacts, our global-local consistency denoising technique consistently integrates edited and unedited latent features, ensuring smooth transition at editing boundaries from the latent representation to the final image. We also introduce a patch-based hybrid sampling approach that captures local, intermediate, and global features, ensuring semantic coherence and enhancing fine detail during denoising. We conduct extensive experiments demonstrating UltraDiffEdit's superior editing quality and flexibility: it can handle image resolutions up to 8K using only a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 GPU. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/LonglongaaaGo/UltraDiffEdit.

</details>


### [8] [Light-Omni: Reflex over Reasoning in Agentic Video Understanding with Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05511)
*Chang Nie,Jiaju Wei,Junlan Feng,Chaoyou Fu,Caifeng Shan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Light-Omni is a multimodal agent framework for lightweight video understanding that uses dual contextual states (global state from episodic memory consolidation and parametric latent state) to achieve reflexive, single-forward-pass responses without iterative reasoning, outperforming prior methods like M3-Agent in accuracy (+2.4%), speed (12.1×), and GPU memory efficiency (2.6×).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video agents rely on costly "detective-style" iterative reasoning (e.g., search actions, evidence aggregation) for action control, which incurs prohibitive latency and computational costs. This heavy reasoning primarily compensates for lack of global context and semantic misalignment in retrieval, motivating the need for a more efficient, reflexive framework.

Method: Light-Omni introduces dual contextual states: (1) a global state — a finite-sized multimodal script continuously consolidated from episodic memory via hierarchical merging, preserving recent details while summarizing past events; (2) a parametric latent state generated conditioned on the global context, which directly drives autonomous actions and produces retrieval embeddings in a single forward pass, eliminating iterative reasoning loops.

Result: Outperforms M3-Agent with +2.4% average accuracy, 12.1× speedup, and 2.6× GPU memory efficiency improvement across multiple video benchmarks. Also serves as an effective memory system to enhance existing MLLMs.

Conclusion: The coupled design of global and parametric latent states enables semantically aligned retrieval and reflexive responses while avoiding iterative reasoning, making Light-Omni an effective and efficient framework for agentic video understanding.

Abstract: Agentic video understanding equips models with long-term memory to autonomously process and respond to continuous, long-horizon multimodal streams. However, advanced video agents often rely on ``detective-style'' iterative reasoning for action control (e.g., $\mathtt{search}$) and evidence aggregation, incurring prohibitive costs and latency. We argue that such heavy reasoning primarily compensates for the lack of global context and semantic misalignment in retrieval. This paper introduces Light-Omni, a multimodal agent framework for reflexive and lightweight video understanding. It achieves this through dual contextual states that instantly build the required context in a single forward pass. First, we maintain a global state, a finite-sized multimodal script continuously consolidated from episodic memory, serving as the global context for Light-Omni. Through hierarchical merging, it preserves recent details while summarizing past events. Second, conditioned on this global context, we generate a parametric latent state that directly drives autonomous actions and produces retrieval embeddings, with minimal latency. Benefiting from this coupled design, Light-Omni achieves semantically aligned retrieval and reflexive responses while avoiding iterative reasoning. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness of Light-Omni across multiple video benchmarks. Notably, it outperforms M3-Agent with an average 2.4% accuracy gain, a 12.1$\times$ speedup, and a 2.6$\times$ improvement in GPU memory efficiency. Furthermore, it serves as a memory system to enhance both the performance and efficiency of existing MLLMs. Project page: https://clare-nie.github.io/Light-Omni.

</details>


### [9] [Statistical Adversaries: Natural Backdoor-like Features in Vision Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05516)
*Paul K. Mandal,Pavan Reddy,Tristan Malatynski*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper identifies "statistical adversaries"—naturally occurring statistical correlations in vision datasets (e.g., ImageNet) that function like backdoor triggers without malicious insertion—and shows they can predictably alter model predictions across architectures, revealing that dataset structure itself creates exploitable adversarial surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While model-specific adversarial attacks are well-studied, the authors investigate a different failure mode: naturally occurring statistical signals in datasets that can act like backdoor triggers without any malicious insertion, suggesting vulnerabilities arise from dataset structure itself.

Method: The authors analyze ImageNet to find patterns strongly linked to certain labels, apply statistical controls to remove random correlations from candidate signals, and then demonstrate that these signals directly and predictably alter model predictions.

Result: These statistical adversaries are more targeted than generic corruptions and transfer across different model architectures, indicating that some vulnerabilities are driven by dataset structure and distribution rather than a single model's idiosyncrasies.

Conclusion: Ordinary datasets can contain exploitable adversarial surfaces even without poisoning; dataset audits should treat spurious structure not only as a source of bias or interpretability failure, but also as a latent attack surface for vision models.

Abstract: Model-specific adversarial attacks have been extensively studied. We study a different failure mode: naturally occurring statistical signals in vision data that can behave like backdoor-like triggers without being maliciously inserted. We call these signals statistical adversaries. We analyse Imagenet to find patterns that are strongly linked to certain labels. We then use statistical controls to remove random correlations from our candidate signals. Finally, we demonstrate that these signals directly and predictably alter model predictions. These statistical adversaries are more targeted than generic corruptions and transfer across different model architectures. This suggests that some vulnerabilities are driven by dataset structure and distribution rather than a single model's idiosyncrasies. We conclude that ordinary datasets can contain exploitable adversarial surfaces even in the absence of poisoning, and suggest that dataset audits should treat spurious structure not only as a source of bias or interpretability failure, but also as a latent attack surface for vision models.

</details>


### [10] [Rendering-Aware Bayesian 3D Gaussian Splatting with Native Uncertainty and Adaptive Complexity Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05522)
*Gaoxiang Jia,Vikram Appia,Junzhou Huang,Xinlei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: We propose a rendering-aware Bayesian 3DGS framework using Normal-Inverse-Wishart posteriors (with optional Dirichlet-process extension) to provide native uncertainty quantification, principled complexity control, and active view selection, achieving consistent PSNR/LPIPS gains over standard ensembles at lower training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard 3DGS relies on point estimates and hand-tuned heuristics, lacking native uncertainty or complexity control, which is particularly limiting under sparse views or fixed acquisition budgets where weakly supported geometry must be identified and informative views selected.

Method: Tracks Gaussian geometry with a Normal-Inverse-Wishart posterior over means and covariances using renderer-derived surrogate summaries, with an optional Dirichlet-process extension for probabilistic component-usage signal. Re-rendering posterior geometry samples yields predictive uncertainty for interval calibration and active view selection.

Result: In active-view (16→32) tasks, NIW acquisition outperforms 3-member ensemble (+0.453 dB PSNR, -0.0146 LPIPS), PPU-style (+0.355 dB), and NIW-proxy (+0.401 dB) baselines. NIW intervals reduce 95% coverage error by ~17× vs shared proxy (0.046 vs 0.796) and ~10× vs 3-member deep ensemble (0.047 vs 0.454) at ~1/3 training cost. Reconstruction check shows +0.030 dB PSNR with 1.6% extra training time.

Conclusion: Bayesian 3DGS is a practical probabilistic scene representation for decision-facing tasks like active view selection, providing native uncertainty quantification with minimal computational overhead.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) is a strong representation for real-time novel-view synthesis, but its standard training pipeline relies on point estimates and hand-tuned heuristics, providing no native uncertainty or principled complexity control. This is most limiting under sparse views or fixed acquisition budgets, where a model must identify weakly supported geometry and select informative views. We introduce a rendering-aware Bayesian 3DGS framework that tracks Gaussian geometry with a Normal-Inverse-Wishart posterior over means and covariances using renderer-derived surrogate summaries. An optional Dirichlet-process extension adds a probabilistic component-usage signal, and the training schedule makes the closed-form versus approximate inference boundary explicit. Re-rendering posterior geometry samples yields native predictive uncertainty for interval calibration and active view selection. In a fixed-budget 16-to-32 active-view task, native NIW acquisition improves PSNR by +0.453 dB and LPIPS by -0.0146 over a scoring-only 3-member standard-ensemble baseline, winning 29/39 scene-seed pairs and 10/13 scene means; it also improves over PPU-style (+0.355 dB) and NIW-proxy (+0.401 dB) acquisition. NIW native intervals reduce 95% coverage error by about 17x relative to a shared proxy (0.046 vs. 0.796) and are about 10x closer to nominal coverage than a 3-member deep ensemble (0.047 vs. 0.454) at roughly one-third the training cost. As a reconstruction compatibility check, paired NIW-vs-standard analysis over 39 scene-seed runs yields +0.030 dB PSNR with 1.6% additional training time. These results position Bayesian 3DGS as a practical probabilistic scene representation for decision-facing tasks such as active view selection.

</details>


### [11] [Multi-Teacher Contrastive Distillation for Edge-Efficient Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05533)
*Tim Lenz,Maurice Heide,Marco Gustav,Nic G. Reitsam,Jakob Nikolas Kather*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MuCoDi is a pretraining framework that distills knowledge from multiple large computational pathology foundation models (Virchow2, UNI2, H-Optimus-1) into compact edge-oriented encoders (MobileOne, RepViT) using contrastive distillation (adapted from MoCo v3). The resulting MuCoEdge models achieve near-teacher performance (e.g., 71.0% external AUROC vs. 71.8% for Virchow2) while reducing model size by orders of magnitude (e.g., MuCoEdge-R1.0: 6.4M params, 1.12 GFLOPs) and enabling up to 605-fold speedup on a Raspberry Pi 5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large computational pathology foundation models (PFMs) achieve strong performance on whole-slide image analysis but are too large and computationally expensive for local deployment in pathology departments. This work aims to produce compact edge-oriented encoders that retain PFM-level representation quality.

Method: MuCoDi distills frozen tile embeddings from multiple teacher PFMs (Virchow2, UNI2, H-Optimus-1) into lightweight student encoders (MobileOne, RepViT) using a contrastive distillation objective adapted from MoCo v3. Cached teacher embeddings replace momentum-encoder keys. Pretraining is performed on 14.3M TCGA tiles from 11.8K WSIs.

Result: RepViT-based MuCoEdge students achieve 71.0% external AUROC (within 0.8 pp of Virchow2's 71.8%). MuCoEdge-R1.0 reaches 70.9% AUROC with only 6.4M parameters and 1.12 GFLOPs. On a Raspberry Pi 5, MobileOne students achieve up to 605-fold speedup over Virchow2 while retaining 66.5-66.9% external AUROC.

Conclusion: PFM-quality pathology representations can be effectively distilled into compact models suitable for practical edge deployment, reducing model size by orders of magnitude while maintaining competitive performance across 23 clinically curated downstream tasks.

Abstract: Computational pathology foundation models (PFMs) have advanced whole-slide image analysis. However, their size and inference cost hinder local deployment in pathology departments. We propose MuCoDi, a pretraining framework that distills frozen tile embeddings from multiple PFMs into compact edge-oriented encoders. Instead of regressing individual teacher features, MuCoDi trains lightweight MobileOne and RepViT students with a contrastive distillation objective adapted from MoCo v3, where cached Virchow2, UNI2, and H-Optimus-1 embeddings replace momentum-encoder keys. We pretrain students on 14.3M TCGA tiles from only 11.8K WSIs and evaluate frozen encoders on 23 clinically curated downstream classification tasks. RepViT-based MuCoEdge students retain near-teacher performance while reducing model size by orders of magnitude: MuCoEdge-R2.3 and MuCoEdge-R1.5 reach 71.0% external AUROC, within 0.8 percentage points of the best teacher (Virchow2, 71.8%), while MuCoEdge-R2.3 obtains the best external F1 and the second-best AUPRC (51.8% and 53.3%). MuCoEdge-R1.0 reaches 70.9% AUROC with only 6.4M parameters and 1.12 GFLOPs. On a Raspberry Pi 5, sub-million-parameter MobileOne students achieve up to 605-fold single-tile speedup over Virchow2 while retaining 66.5% to 66.9% external AUROC, demonstrating that PFM-quality pathology representations can be moved toward practical edge deployment. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/mucodi-6243.

</details>


### [12] [Harnessing Generative Image Models for Training-Free Primitive Shape Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05568)
*Gregor Kobsik,Tim Elsner,Leif Kobbelt*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free pipeline that uses pre-trained generative image models and vision-language models to parse 3D objects into semantic parts and fit superquadric primitives, achieving category-agnostic and orientation-invariant 3D shape decomposition without any learned parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To harness the pretrained capabilities of generative image models directly for 3D shape decomposition into geometric primitives, without requiring fine-tuning or task-specific training, thereby achieving category-agnostic and orientation-invariant parsing that prior learning-based methods struggled with.

Method: The pipeline: (1) renders multi-view images of a 3D object, (2) uses a vision-language model to analyze semantic parts, (3) prompts a generative image model to produce color-coded part segmentation masks, (4) reprojects masks onto the 3D geometry, and (5) fits superquadric primitives to each part via parameter optimization. The entire pipeline contains no learned parameters.

Result: Achieves the lowest Chamfer distance on HumanPrim and Toys4K benchmarks among all evaluated methods, using 5-9 primitives per object on average. A ground-truth segmentation study confirms that part segmentation (not primitive fitting) is the current accuracy bottleneck, indicating potential for future improvement with better generative models.

Conclusion: Pre-trained generative image models can be directly harnessed for 3D primitive decomposition without any training, offering category-agnostic and orientation-invariant properties. The approach's accuracy is bottlenecked by part segmentation quality, meaning it will naturally improve as generative models advance.

Abstract: Representing 3D shapes as compact sets of geometric primitives is fundamental to robotics, simulation, and scene understanding. Generative image models trained at scale have recently emerged as generalist visual learners that can identify and segment object parts directly in the image domain, across arbitrary categories and without task-specific training. Adapting such models to downstream tasks typically requires fine-tuning; we ask whether their pretrained capability can instead be harnessed directly, without any training, and answer affirmatively with a training-free harness. Our pipeline renders multi-view images of a 3D object, uses a vision-language model to analyze its semantic parts, prompts a generative image model to paint a color-coded part segmentation mask, reprojects it onto the geometry, and fits a superquadric primitive to each part via parameter optimization. The approach contains no learned parameters: it is category-agnostic and orientation-invariant, properties that previous learning-based models struggled with. Its accuracy ceiling rises with future generative-model improvements, which we confirm with a ground-truth segmentation study showing that part segmentation, not primitive fitting, is the current accuracy bottleneck. On HumanPrim and Toys4K, our method achieves the lowest Chamfer distance among all evaluated methods, using 5--9 primitives per object on average.

</details>


### [13] [Hierarchical Classification via Cascading Feature Elimination: Application to Human Phenotype Ontology-Aligned Facial Phenotyping (FaceMesh2HPO)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05585)
*Fabio Hellmann,Alexander Hustinx,Benjamin D. Solomon,GestaltMatcher Database Consortium,Tzung-Chien Hsieh,Peter Krawitz,Elisabeth André*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FaceMesh2HPO is a hierarchical PointNet-based framework that uses 3D facial meshes, facial outlines, and demographic metadata to classify facial phenotypic descriptors linked to the Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO). Trained on annotations from 124 clinicians across 10 disorders (107 HPO terms), the model achieves AUROCs of 0.55–0.89, performing better on parent nodes than rare leaf terms, with variable external generalizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable interpretable, ontology-linked automated classification of facial phenotypes from 2D images to support clinical diagnosis by aligning with the Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO).

Method: Annotations from 124 clinicians across 10 disorders (107 HPO terms) were collected; 3D facial meshes (478 landmarks) were reconstructed from 2D images; a hierarchical PointNet-based pipeline with cascading classification and feature elimination was trained using 3D meshes, facial outline, and demographic metadata.

Result: Best models achieved AUROCs between ~0.55 and ~0.89; performance was higher at parent nodes than leaf terms; external validation showed variable generalizability across disorders.

Conclusion: Hierarchical modeling of 3D facial geometry enables interpretable, ontology-linked phenotype classification, but performance on rare leaf terms is limited; improved data diversity and feature selection strategies are needed for robustness and clinical utility.

Abstract: FaceMesh2HPO is a framework for classifying facial phenotypic descriptors aligned with the Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO) to support clinical diagnosis. Using annotations from 124 clinicians across 10 disorders (107 HPO terms) combined with non-syndromic controls, we generated 3D facial meshes (478 landmarks) from 2D images and trained a hierarchical PointNet-based pipeline with cascading classification and feature elimination. The best models, incorporating 3D meshes, facial outline, and demographic metadata, achieved AUROCs between ~0.55 and ~0.89, with higher performance at parent nodes than leaf terms. External validation showed variable generalizability across disorders. Results demonstrate that hierarchical modeling of 3D facial geometry enables interpretable, ontology-linked phenotype classification, though performance on rare leaf terms remains limited. Improved data diversity and feature selection strategies are needed to enhance robustness and clinical utility.

</details>


### [14] [Patch Knowledge Transfer for Efficient AI-Generated Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05605)
*Jiquan Yuan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Patch Knowledge Transfer (PKT), a knowledge distillation framework that transfers visual representation capability from a computationally expensive teacher model (local-global hybrid processing) to an efficient student model (global-only processing), achieving 67.7% reduction in computational cost while maintaining comparable quality assessment performance on AI-generated images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the critical trade-off between assessment accuracy and inference efficiency in perceptual quality assessment of AI-generated images, where complex methods are accurate but computationally prohibitive, and simple scaling-based methods are efficient but inaccurate.

Method: A knowledge distillation framework named PKT with a dual-model architecture: a teacher model employing local-global hybrid processing to provide high-quality supervision signals, and a student model using only global processing that efficiently inherits the teacher's representation capacity through multi-level knowledge transfer.

Result: On 4 AIGIQA databases, the student model maintains comparable performance to the teacher while reducing computational costs by 67.7%, achieving a superior balance between efficiency and assessment accuracy compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: PKT effectively resolves the accuracy-efficiency dilemma in AIGI quality assessment, enabling real-time inference without sacrificing perceptual quality prediction capability.

Abstract: With the rapid advancement of image generation technologies, perceptual quality assessment of AI-generated images has emerged as a crucial research direction in computer vision. The core challenge of this task lies in achieving efficient quality assessment for massive generated images. Current mainstream approaches exhibit two key limitations: 1) Methods employing complex feature extraction strategies, while improving performance, incur prohibitive computational costs that hinder real-time inference; 2) Simple image scaling-based solutions, despite their computational efficiency, demonstrate significantly inferior assessment accuracy. To address this critical issue, we propose Patch Knowledge Transfer (PKT), a knowledge distillation-based optimization framework that achieves synergistic optimization of visual representation capability and inference efficiency through an innovative multi-level knowledge transfer mechanism. Specifically, we design a dual-model architecture: a teacher model with local-global hybrid processing provides high-quality supervision signals, while a student model relying solely on global processing efficiently inherits the teacher's representation capacity through multi-level supervision. Extensive experiments conducted on 4 AIGIQA databases demonstrate that the PKT framework enables the student model to maintain performance comparable to the teacher while reducing computational costs by 67.7\%. Furthermore, compared to existing methods, our approach achieves a superior balance between model efficiency and assessment accuracy.

</details>


### [15] [Cross-Contextual Vision-Language Adaptation with LoRA for Personalized Severe Adverse Event Detection in Clinical Wound Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05625)
*Aditi Naiknaware,Jian Sun,Aminreza Khandan,Shengyang Huang,Sean Dow,Bijan Najafi,Salimeh Sekeh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a multimodal framework for automated wound monitoring and severe adverse event (SAE) detection, using a dual-stream LoRA approach on a frozen BiomedCLIP backbone with cross-contextual fusion, combined with a wound-specific OOD detection mechanism incorporating semantic matching, visual typicality, caption alignment, temporal drift penalties, and covariate consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wound monitoring is a critical but underserved clinical challenge; timely identification of severe adverse events (SAEs) such as infection, tissue deterioration, and delayed healing is difficult. Existing VLMs lack domain-specific grounding to integrate wound imagery with heterogeneous clinical information and offer limited mechanisms for detecting out-of-distribution cases.

Method: A dual-stream Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) framework built on a frozen BiomedCLIP backbone, with a cross-contextual LoRA fusion mechanism for information exchange between clinical semantics and visual wound descriptors. For SAE detection, a wound-specific OOD detection framework combines semantic matching, visual typicality, caption-text alignment, and caption-visual alignment into a unified score, augmented with covariate consistency and temporal drift penalties across visits.

Result: Experiments on a longitudinal wound dataset collected through clinical visits show promising performance on both wound healing assessment and SAE detection.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates the potential of semantically enriched, temporally aware vision-language systems for clinical wound monitoring and early risk identification.

Abstract: Wound monitoring is a critical yet underserved clinical challenge, where timely identification of severe adverse events (SAEs) such as infection, tissue deterioration, and delayed healing can significantly impact patient outcomes. While vision-language models (VLMs) show strong multimodal reasoning, they often lack domain-specific grounding to integrate wound imagery with heterogeneous clinical information, and provide limited mechanisms for detecting cases that diverge from the training distribution. We present a multimodal framework for automated wound monitoring and SAE detection. Our approach leverages paired clinical notes and wound descriptions capturing visual characteristics such as appearance, surrounding skin condition, color changes, and signs of inflammation or healing progression, encoded through a dual-stream Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) framework built on a frozen BiomedCLIP backbone. We introduce a cross-contextual LoRA fusion mechanism enabling information exchange between clinical semantics and visual wound descriptors, producing context-aware multimodal representations without full model fine-tuning. To identify personalized SAEs, we propose a wound-specific out-of-distribution (OOD) detection framework combining semantic matching, visual typicality, caption-text alignment, and caption-visual alignment into a unified SAE (OOD) score. To capture healing dynamics, we incorporate covariate consistency and temporal drift penalties that leverage changes in wound characteristics across visits. Experiments on a longitudinal wound dataset collected through clinical visits show promising performance on both wound healing assessment and SAE detection, highlighting the potential of semantically enriched, temporally aware vision-language systems for clinical wound monitoring and early risk identification.

</details>


### [16] [Taxlifier: Leveraging Disease Taxonomy for Enhanced Multi-Label Classification in Chest Radiography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05628)
*Mohammad S. Majdi,Jeffrey J. Rodriguez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes two hierarchical multi-label classification techniques (loss-based and logit-based) for thoracic disease classification in chest X-rays, leveraging disease taxonomy relationships. Evaluated on CheXpert, PADCHEST, and NIH datasets, both methods significantly outperform baselines, with logit-based achieving 12% accuracy, 13% AUC, and 24% F1 improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thoracic diseases in CXR images often present with overlapping visual characteristics, making automated multi-label classification challenging. Existing methods fail to leverage hierarchical relationships among pathologies, limiting accuracy and interpretability for clinical decision support.

Method: Two hierarchical multi-label classification techniques: (1) loss-based method integrating hierarchical information into the optimization process, and (2) logit-based method adjusting predicted probabilities based on parent-class relationships in the disease taxonomy. Evaluated on CheXpert (224,316 CXRs), PADCHEST (160,000 CXRs), and NIH (112,120 CXRs) datasets.

Result: Logit-based method: 12% accuracy, 13% AUC, 24% F1 improvement. Loss-based method: 11% accuracy, 10% AUC, 12% F1 improvement. Both significantly outperform baseline across all three datasets. Comprehensive statistical analysis confirms robustness.

Conclusion: Integrating domain-specific hierarchical knowledge enhances classification performance and provides more interpretable outputs for clinical decision support, highlighting the potential of hierarchical multi-label classification for advancing computer-aided diagnosis in chest radiography.

Abstract: Accurate and efficient classification of thoracic diseases in chest X-ray (CXR) images is crucial for timely diagnosis and treatment. However, the presence of multiple pathologies with overlapping visual characteristics poses significant challenges for automated classification systems. In this study, we propose two novel hierarchical multi-label classification techniques, namely the loss-based and logit-based methods, to address these challenges by leveraging the hierarchical relationships among different thoracic pathologies. The loss-based technique integrates hierarchical information directly into the optimization process, while the logit-based method adjusts the predicted probabilities of each class based on its parent class in the disease taxonomy. We evaluate the performance of both techniques using three large-scale CXR datasets: CheXpert (224,316 CXRs), PADCHEST (160,000 CXRs), and NIH (112,120 CXRs). The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in accuracy, AUC, and F1 scores compared to the baseline method across various pathologies. The logit-based and loss-based methods improve accuracy by 12\% and 11\%, AUC by 13\% and 10\%, and F1 scores by 24\% and 12\%, respectively compared to the baseline. These results represent a substantial improvement over the baseline method. Furthermore, we conduct a comprehensive statistical analysis to validate the robustness and reliability of the proposed techniques. The integration of domain-specific hierarchical knowledge not only enhances the classification performance but also provides a more interpretable output for clinical decision support. Our findings highlight the potential of hierarchical multi-label classification in advancing computer-aided diagnosis systems for chest radiography.

</details>


### [17] [Recovering Cloud Microstructures with Cascaded Diffusion Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05637)
*Hanan Gani,Guy Pulik,Daniel Rosenfeld,Duncan Watson-Parris,Salman Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A two-stage diffusion-based super-resolution framework enhances 4× resolution of multi-spectral cloud satellite imagery, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in capturing fine cloud microstructures critical for cloud seeding decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution satellite imagery is essential for observing fine-scale cloud structures needed for weather modification (e.g., cloud seeding), but current satellite spatial resolution is insufficient to capture small cloud features, and existing super-resolution methods designed for natural images fail to generalize to spectral satellite cloud imagery.

Method: A two-stage diffusion-based super-resolution framework: Stage 1 uses real-world paired data to learn robust degradation handling and inter-sensor alignment; Stage 2 employs self-supervised internal downgrading of high-resolution data to refine structural learning and texture synthesis. The framework enhances multi-spectral cloud microstructures by 4× using inverse diffusion.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art transformer and diffusion-based baselines in both reconstruction accuracy and visual quality, better capturing fine cloud microstructures (e.g., convective turrets, cloud gaps). Stage 1 excels in coarse structural fidelity, Stage 2 enhances detail and realism.

Conclusion: The two-stage method provides a practical path toward improving cloud microphysics analysis and represents a step toward utilizing AI for climate and sustainability applications.

Abstract: High-resolution satellite imagery is critical for observing fine-scale cloud structures that inform weather modification strategies like cloud seeding for rain-enhancement. However, the spatial resolution of current geostationary and polar-orbiting satellites is often insufficient for capturing small cloud features. Current super-resolution methodologies are suited for natural images and, therefore, struggle to generalize to satellite-captured spectral images of cloud cover. To address this, we propose a two-stage diffusion-based super-resolution framework to enhance the resolution of multi-spectral cloud microstructures by a factor of $4\times$. Specifically, we use inverse diffusion to recover the high resolution properties from low resolution. Stage 1 utilizes real-world paired data to learn robust degradation handling and inter-sensor alignment, while Stage 2 employs a self-supervised internal downgrading of high resolution data to refine structural learning and texture synthesis. Our approach outperforms the state-of-the-art transformer and diffusion-based baselines in both reconstruction accuracy and visual quality. We demonstrate that the two-stage method better captures fine cloud microstructures (e.g. convective turrets and cloud gaps) that are crucial for effective cloud seeding decisions. Ablation studies confirm the complementary benefits of the two stages: Stage 1 excels in coarse structural fidelity, while Stage 2 contributes enhanced detail and realism. These results highlight a practical path toward improving cloud microphysics analysis and as a step towards utilizing AI for climate and sustainability. Our code and models are publicly available at: https://github.com/hananshafi/superresolution-cloud-microphysics.

</details>


### [18] [VEIL: How Visual Encoding Hijacking Induces Bias In Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05641)
*Suranjana Sooraj,Xuyang Chen,Madhumitha Venkatesan,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VEIL is a systematic study showing that CNN-based time-series classification from chart images is significantly influenced by visualization design choices (encodings) rather than just underlying temporal patterns, and attention-guided training can help but only in specific cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It remains unclear whether CNN-based time-series classification models learn genuine temporal patterns or rely on encoding-specific visual cues introduced by chart design, motivating a systematic investigation of how chart encodings shape learned representations.

Method: VEIL uses complementary analyses of similarity, transferability, and attribution to systematically examine how chart encoding choices affect learned representations in CNN-based time-series classification.

Result: Attention-guided training mitigates encoding sensitivity when consistently identified across diagnostics, but provides limited or negative benefit when such signals are absent.

Conclusion: Chart-based time-series classification should be treated as a representation and measurement problem rather than a simple modeling decision, extending the study of graphical perception from human readers to vision models.

Abstract: Rendering time series as chart images for CNN-based classification has become increasingly common in time-series classification (TSC). However, it remains unclear whether models learn underlying temporal patterns or rely on encoding-specific visual cues introduced by chart design. We present VEIL: a systematic study examining how chart encodings influence learned representations through complementary analyses of similarity, transferability, and attribution. Attention-guided training appears to mitigate this effect when encoding sensitivity is consistently identified across diagnostics, but provides limited or negative benefit when such signals are absent. These findings position VEIL within the broader question of how machines perceive visualizations -- extending graphical perception from human readers to vision models -- and show that visualization design choices shape learned representations in ways that warrant treating chart-based TSC as a representation and measurement problem rather than a simple modeling decision.

</details>


### [19] [REVIVE: A Multi-Modal Framework for Vandalism Detection and Recovery in Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05649)
*Abdullah Tariq Choudhry,Tapadhir Das*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: REVIVE is a vandalism recovery framework for autonomous vehicle cameras that detects and restores occlusion-attacked images using a combination of binary/multi-class detection, EfficientNet-U-Net segmentation, and type-aware inpainting (BLIP-guided Stable Diffusion, direct pixel replacement, adaptive median filtering). It demonstrates that aligned direct pixel replacement nearly restores detection performance (recall 0.967), while Stable Diffusion offers incomplete recovery (SSIM 0.667-0.867). A quality gate ensures the forwarded stream is never worse than unrecovered frames.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles face growing threats from vandalism-induced occlusion attacks (VOAs) that compromise camera-based perception. While detection methods exist, restoring camera-stream utility after physical occlusion remains largely unexplored, creating a critical gap in AV safety and reliability.

Method: REVIVE pipeline integrates four stages: (1) binary VOA detection, (2) multi-class VOA pattern identification, (3) EfficientNet-based U-Net segmentation of occluded regions, and (4) type-aware recovery using BLIP-guided Stable Diffusion inpainting, direct pixel replacement (with aligned reference), or adaptive median filtering. A quality gate filters recovered candidates before downstream use.

Result: On 500 tracked clean/vandalized pairs: unrecovered VOAs reduce YOLOv8l recall to 0.588; direct pixel replacement restores recall to 0.967 and F1 to 0.970 under aligned-reference condition. Stable Diffusion achieves per-pattern SSIM 0.667-0.867, PSNR 15.4-26.7dB. Without quality gate, type-aware routing degrades per-image recall to 0.304; with it, recall returns to 0.608.

Conclusion: REVIVE provides a structured, multi-strategy recovery framework for VOAs in AVs. Direct pixel replacement with aligned reference is highly effective; Stable Diffusion inpainting offers variable quality and is best used as an asynchronous branch subject to a quality gate, ensuring downstream perception never degrades below the unrecovered baseline.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles (AVs) face increasing threats from vandalism-induced occlusion attacks (VOAs) that compromise camera-based perception. While detection frameworks can identify vandalized images, restoring camera-stream utility after physical occlusion remains underexplored. This paper presents present the Recovery and Enhancement of Vandalized Images for Vision Excellence (REVIVE) framework, a vandalism recovery pipeline integrating: (1) binary VOA detection, (2) multi-class VOA pattern identification, (3) EfficientNet-based U-Net segmentation, and (4) type-aware recovery using Bootstrapping Language-Image Pre-training (BLIP)-guided Stable Diffusion inpainting, direct pixel replacement, or adaptive median filtering. Stable Diffusion shows variable reconstruction performance (per-pattern SSIM 0.667-0.867, PSNR 15.4-26.7dB) across VOA patterns, while aligned direct pixel replacement achieves near-identical reconstruction under the aligned-reference condition. On 500 tracked clean/vandalized image pairs, unrecovered VOAs reduce YOLOv8l object-detection recall to 0.588, while direct pixel replacement restores recall to 0.967 and F1-score to 0.970 under that aligned-reference condition. LaMa, Telea, and Navier-Stokes baselines improve image similarity but provide more limited downstream detection recovery, and Stable Diffusion is treated as an asynchronous recovery branch subject to a quality gate rather than a blocking real-time perception step. We evaluate a reference-available quality gate that filters recovered candidates before downstream use: without it, type-aware routing degrades per-image recall to 0.304, whereas with it, recall returns to 0.608, at or above the unrecovered baseline, ensuring the forwarded stream is never worse than the unrecovered frame. REVIVE therefore, provides a structured recovery framework from VOAs in AVs.

</details>


### [20] [Robust Face Super-Resolution and Recognition Through Multi-Feature Aggregation in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05702)
*Marcelo dos Santos,Rayson Laroca,João Carlos Raposo Neves,David Menotti*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FASR++ is a diffusion-model-based super-resolution algorithm that leverages multiple auxiliary low-quality images from surveillance cameras to generate high-quality super-resolved outputs, improving face recognition performance by recovering facial features while minimizing identity distortions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Surveillance images suffer from low resolution, pose variations, irregular illumination, and occlusions, causing face recognition algorithms to struggle. Super-resolution techniques can help but often introduce distortions in both image quality and identity. The authors aim to leverage multiple low-quality images captured by surveillance cameras to enhance both super-resolution and facial recognition robustness.

Method: FASR++ uses a diffusion-model-based super-resolution algorithm that takes a reference low-resolution image and features extracted from multiple auxiliary low-quality images (e.g., consecutive video frames) to generate a super-resolved output. It recovers facial features without requiring explicit soft attributes or gradient-guided reconstruction.

Result: Validated on two standard face recognition datasets, FASR++ achieves state-of-the-art results for verification, face recognition, and image quality metrics including PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS.

Conclusion: FASR++ generates high-quality images that significantly improve face recognition performance when used as a pre-processing step, minimizing identity distortions without explicit attribute guidance.

Abstract: Images acquired in surveillance environments often suffer from conditions such as low resolution, variations in pose, irregular illumination, and occlusions. Due to the low quality of these images, face recognition algorithms often struggle. This major limitation can be addressed by employing super-resolution techniques that enhance the details of the image. However, due to the high degree of difficulty of the problem, most super-resolution algorithms tend to cause distortions in the image and in the individual's identity. Thus, additional information must be incorporated into the processing to improve recognition robustness. In this regard, surveillance cameras can capture multiple images, even at low quality, and the data extracted from these images, such as consecutive video frames, can significantly enhance both super-resolution and facial recognition. In this work, we introduce FASR++, a diffusion-model-based super-resolution algorithm. It leverages a reference low-resolution image and features extracted from multiple auxiliary low-quality images to generate a super-resolved output, minimizing distortions in the individual's identity. Our approach recovers facial features without explicitly providing soft attributes or computing a function gradient to guide the reconstruction process. FASR++ generates high-quality images that can considerably improve performance in face recognition tasks when used as a pre-processing step. We validate our approach on two standard face recognition datasets and attain state-of-the-art results for verification, face recognition, and image quality metrics such as PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS.

</details>


### [21] [Scene Graph Thinking: Reinforcing Structured Visual Reasoning for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05716)
*Zhiwei Yang,Yuanchen Wu,Nan Zhang,Yucong Meng,Ke Yan,Shouhong Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SaGe (Scene Graph Thinking) is a paradigm for MLLMs that converts flat image-text data into structured scene graphs and uses two-stage graph-aligned post-training (supervised fine-tuning + reinforcement fine-tuning with node-as-proxy graph rewards) to enable fine-grained, structured visual reasoning, achieving strong results across eight multimodal benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLMs focus on isolated objects and neglect structured relationships, limiting their performance on visually intensive tasks like efficient target navigation.

Method: (1) An automated data engine converts flat image-text corpora into structured scene graphs (hierarchical entities as nodes, diverse visual relations as edges). (2) 120K high-quality training data is constructed by sampling reasoning traces from scene graphs. (3) Two-stage graph-aligned post-training: supervised fine-tuning to internalize structured reasoning, followed by reinforcement fine-tuning with node-as-proxy graph rewards for efficient graph exploration.

Result: Significant improvements across eight multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating strong effectiveness on fine-grained perception and reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: SaGe effectively addresses the structured reasoning gap in MLLMs via explicit scene-graph representations and graph-aligned training, with open-sourced code for reproducibility.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong perception and reasoning capabilities. However, most existing models focus on isolated objects and neglect structured relationships for efficient target navigation, limiting their performance on visually intensive tasks. To address this challenge, we introduce Scene Graph Thinking (SaGe), a novel paradigm that enables fine-grained and structured visual reasoning through explicit scene-graph representations. Specifically, we first introduce an automated data engine that converts flat image-text corpora into structured scene graphs, where hierarchical entities constitute the nodes and diverse visual relations define the edges. Building upon this, we construct 120K high-quality training data by sampling reasoning traces from scene graphs. Then, two-stage graph-aligned post-training paradigms are introduced, where supervised fine-tuning internalizes MLLMs with structured reasoning, and subsequent reinforcement fine-tuning proposes node-as-proxy graph rewards to consolidate efficient graph exploration. With curated data and graph-aligned training, our approach achieves significant improvements across eight multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating strong effectiveness on fine-grained perception and reasoning tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/zwyang6/SaGe.

</details>


### [22] [Association Restoration Test: Revealing Restorable Shortcuts after Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05726)
*Amy Lu,Changxiu Ji*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Association Restoration Test (ART), a diagnostic that assesses whether shortcut associations are functionally restorable in classifiers after unlearning, revealing that existing output and probe metrics capture distinct aspects of shortcut mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for association unlearning only measure output-level robustness or feature-level readability, but fail to test whether a shortcut association remains functionally usable by the original classifier head.

Method: ART estimates class-conditional association directions, amplifies residual shortcut components in frozen features, and evaluates the modified features using the original classifier head to measure functional shortcut restorability.

Result: Across Waterbirds, CelebA, SpuCoDogs, and an ISIC timestamp-artifact extension, output metrics, representation probes, and ART characterize distinct aspects of shortcut mitigation.

Conclusion: The findings motivate restoration-aware evaluation for unlearning and shortcut-mitigation methods that target learned associations rather than individual classes or concepts.

Abstract: Association unlearning aims to disable learned label-attribute shortcuts while preserving task performance. Existing evaluations mainly measure output-level robustness or probe whether shortcut attributes remain readable in frozen features, but neither test determines whether a retained association remains functionally usable by the original classifier. We propose the Association Restoration Test (ART), a post-hoc diagnostic for functional shortcut restorability. ART estimates class-conditional association directions, amplifies residual components, and evaluates the modified features with the original classifier head. Across Waterbirds, CelebA, SpuCoDogs, and an ISIC timestamp-artifact extension, we show that output metrics, representation probes, and ART characterize distinct aspects of shortcut mitigation. These findings motivate restoration-aware evaluation for unlearning and shortcut-mitigation methods that target learned associations rather than individual classes or concepts.

</details>


### [23] [SAMPLe: SAM-based Optimizer for Prompt Learning in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05727)
*Hossein Rajoli,Fatemeh Lotfi,Niloufar Alipour Talemi,Hossein Kashiani,Xiaolong Ma,Fatemeh Afghah*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAMPLe is a sharpness-aware optimizer that improves prompt learning in VLMs by reducing overfitting on seen distributions and enhancing generalization to unseen data, outperforming existing optimizers across multiple prompt learning frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt learning in VLMs faces a performance-generalization dilemma: tuning prompts on seen distributions often undermines generalizability to unseen data due to overfitting of the limited learnable prompts.

Method: SAMPLe (Sharpness-Aware Minimization Prompt Learning) is a plug-in optimizer that accounts for loss landscape sharpness. It balances exploration and exploitation by satisfying objective function constraints dynamically based on local curvature and gradient properties, applied to prompt learning in VLMs.

Result: SAMPLe elevates multiple prompt learning frameworks (CoOp, CoCoOp, MaPLe, TCP, Co-Prompt) and consistently outperforms existing optimizers across diverse settings, establishing itself as a robust, model-agnostic solution for prompt learning.

Conclusion: SAMPLe effectively addresses the performance-generalization dilemma in VLM prompt learning by reducing overfitting and improving adaptability to unseen data, preserving the generalization potential of pre-trained VLMs.

Abstract: Pre-trained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) like CLIP have proven highly effective as foundation models for various downstream applications. However, prompt learning in VLMs encounters a performance-generalization dilemma: while prompts can be tuned to achieve high accuracy on seen distributions, this tuning process often undermines their generalizability to unseen data. The limited set of learnable prompts, which contextualize and condition the input to steer it toward the task within the pretrained VLM, tends to overfit the training data, leading to a trade-off between task-specific performance and preserving generalization. To address this dilemma, we introduce SAMPLe (Sharpness-Aware Minimization Prompt Learning), a plug-in sharpness-aware optimizer that enhances prompt generalizability by accounting for loss landscape sharpness. Unlike conventional methods, SAMPLe balances exploration and exploitation by satisfying objective function constraints at each step, dynamically adapting to the current optimization state based on the local curvature and gradient properties. This approach reduces overfitting on seen distributions and improves adaptability to unseen data, preserving the generalization potential of pre-trained VLM models. We integrate SAMPLe into multiple prompt learning frameworks, including CoOp, CoCoOp, MaPLe, TCP, and Co-Prompt, demonstrating its effectiveness across diverse methods. Experiments show that SAMPLe elevates prompt learning frameworks and consistently outperforms existing optimizers across diverse settings, establishing itself as a robust, model-agnostic solution for prompt learning.

</details>


### [24] [ARMS: Anchor-Relational Motion Streaming for Seamless Solo-Social Motion Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05733)
*Huakun Liu,Qing Yu,Kent Fujiwara,Hideaki Uchiyama,Kiyoshi Kiyokawa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ARMS is an Anchor-Relational Motion Streaming framework that unifies solo and interactive human motion generation within a single causal generative process, enabling seamless transitions between solo and social states over long horizons using dynamics-asymmetric representation and causal relational diffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods generate fixed-length motion clips under static agent configurations, making them brittle to solo-social transitions and unsuitable for incremental generation over long horizons.

Method: (1) Dynamics-asymmetric representation decoupling per-person temporal evolution from inter-person alignment via partner-referenced relative-translation term; (2) Causal relational diffusion model that progressively refines motion segment by segment using only past context; (3) Mode-aware relational gating that activates or masks cross-agent connections for solo/interaction switching.

Result: ARMS improves transition smoothness and social coherence compared to interaction-centric baselines, and achieves competitive results on human-human interaction benchmarks.

Conclusion: ARMS successfully unifies solo and interactive motion generation in a streaming paradigm, enabling seamless social coupling switching with long-horizon stability and spatial consistency.

Abstract: Generating temporally continuous and socially coherent human motion from text remains a fundamental challenge, particularly in realistic streams where people act alone, enter interactions, and later disengage. Most existing methods generate fixed-length motion clips under static agent configurations, which makes them brittle to solo-social transitions and unsuitable for incremental generation over long horizons. We propose ARMS, an Anchor-Relational Motion Streaming framework that unifies solo motion and human-human interaction within a single causal generative process. ARMS introduces a dynamics-asymmetric representation that decouples per-person temporal evolution from inter-person alignment via a partner-referenced relative-translation term, enabling seamless switching of social coupling without sacrificing long-horizon stability or spatial consistency between agents. On top of a causal latent space, a causal relational diffusion model progressively refines motion segment by segment using only past context, capturing both intra-person temporal dependencies and inter-person relations. Mode-aware relational gating activates or masks cross-agent connections, allowing the same model to support both solo and interaction generation. Experiments show that ARMS improves transition smoothness and social coherence compared to interaction-centric baselines, while also achieving competitive results on human-human interaction benchmarks.

</details>


### [25] [Optimized Adaptive Loop Filter in Versatile Video Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05737)
*Meng Xuewei,Zhang Jiaqi,Jia Chuanmin,Zhang Xinfeng,Wang Shanshe,Ma Siwei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an optimized ALF framework for VVC that enables parallel processing of GALF and CCALF, adaptive parameter decisions, and one-pass CCALF estimation, reducing picture buffer access from 152 to 1 and achieving ~25% time savings with negligible coding loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ALF in VVC has high coding complexity and requires many picture buffer accesses in the encoder, increasing external memory access and being unfriendly to software/hardware design.

Method: An optimized ALF framework with: (1) parallel design of GALF and CCALF, (2) adaptive parameter decision for GALF, and (3) one-pass CCALF scheme that estimates CCALF filtering distortion without performing the actual filter operation.

Result: Reduced picture buffer access from 152 to 1, roughly 25% time savings of the ALF module with negligible coding performance change under RA configuration.

Conclusion: The proposed methods significantly reduce complexity and memory access in VVC ALF while maintaining coding performance, with some methods adopted in VVC reference software.

Abstract: In the Versatile Video Coding~(VVC) standard, adaptive loop filter~(ALF), including Geometry transformation-based Adaptive Loop Filter~(GALF) and Cross Component Adaptive Loop Filter~(CCALF), plays an essential role in reducing compression artifacts.
