However, it also has high coding complexity and requires many picture buffer accesses in the encoder that will increase external memory access and is unfriendly to the software and hardware design. Therefore, we propose an optimized ALF framework, including the parallel design of GALF and CCALF, the adaptive parameter decision of GALF, and one-pass CCALF scheme by effectively estimating the CCALF filtering distortion without conducting filter operation. Compared to VTM-8.0, the proposed method can reduce the picture buffer access from 152 to 1 and achieve roughly 25\% time-savings of the ALF module with negligible coding performance change under RA configuration. Some of the proposed methods have been adopted in the VVC reference software.

</details>


### [26] [Image2Sim: Scaling Embodied Navigation via Generative Neural Simulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05765)
*Zihan Wang,Seungjun Lee,Yinghao Xu,Gim Hee Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Image2Sim is a real-time neural simulation framework that constructs interactive 3D environments from posed RGB-D image sequences using a feature Gaussian model for scene construction and a Geometry-Aware One-Step Pixel Flow model for rendering, generating 20K interactive scenes and 10M+ navigation training samples that enable strong real-world transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied navigation progress is constrained by lack of scalable, high-fidelity, physically grounded interactive environments — real-world scanned datasets lack scale, while synthetic simulators suffer from large sim-to-real gaps.

Method: Image2Sim decouples 3D spatial anchoring from photorealistic observation synthesis. Scene construction uses a feed-forward feature Gaussian model to lift posed RGB-D into 3D feature-Gaussian representation. Rendering uses a Geometry-Aware One-Step Pixel Flow model to transform sparse Gaussian projections into high-quality panoramic RGB-D. It also serves as an embodied data engine generating observations, actions, and navigation instructions.

Result: Converts videos/images into ~20K interactive scenes and synthesizes 10M+ navigation training samples. Navigation models trained solely in these neural environments achieve strong improvements on major benchmarks and transfer effectively to real-world zero-shot settings.

Conclusion: Scalable neural simulation can serve as a practical training substrate for embodied navigation at scale, bridging the sim-to-real gap.

Abstract: Embodied navigation aims to build agents that interpret multimodal goals, reason in 3D space, and reach target destinations reliably in the real world. However, progress remains constrained by the lack of scalable, high-fidelity, and physically grounded interactive environments. Although real-world scanned datasets offer visual realism, they are limited by scale. In contrast, synthetic simulators scale more easily but often exhibit large sim-to-real gaps. We introduce Image2Sim, a real-time neural simulation framework that constructs high-quality interactive environments from posed RGB-D image sequences. The central idea is to decouple 3D spatial anchoring from photorealistic observation synthesis. For scene construction, Image2Sim uses a feed-forward feature Gaussian model that lifts posed RGB-D observations into a 3D feature-Gaussian representation in a single pass. For rendering, we propose a Geometry-Aware One-Step Pixel Flow model that transforms sparse and noisy Gaussian projections into high-quality panoramic RGB-D observations. Image2Sim also serves as a fully automated embodied data engine that generates high-fidelity observations, executable actions, and diverse navigation instructions at scale. It converts large collections of videos and images into nearly 20K interactive scenes and synthesizes more than 10 million navigation training samples. Navigation models trained entirely in these neural environments achieve strong improvements on major benchmarks and transfer effectively to real-world zero-shot settings. These results suggest that scalable neural simulation can serve as a practical training substrate for embodied navigation at scale.

</details>


### [27] [LEGATO 2: Toward Multimodal Sheet Music Recognition and Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05769)
*Guang Yang,Brian Siyuan Zheng,Victoria Ebert,Noah A. Smith*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Legato 2 is a novel pipeline for optical music recognition (OMR) that operates system-by-system (following horizontal notation lines), handles arbitrarily long inputs, embeds textual content (titles, annotations), and achieves state-of-the-art results in OMR and downstream sheet music understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OMR models treat the page as an undifferentiated image, which limits scaling to long inputs and fails to handle embedded textual content (e.g., titles, annotations). Legato 2 aims to address these limitations by following the natural reading flow of musical notation.

Method: Legato 2 combines system-level segmentation with an autoregressive vision-language model (vision-LM) to process sheet music system-by-system along horizontal notation lines, enabling sequential reading and better scalability.

Result: Legato 2 consistently outperforms prior state-of-the-art OMR models across multiple datasets. Symbolic transcriptions from Legato 2 also improve frontier language models' interpretation of dense musical documents.

Conclusion: Legato 2 establishes new state-of-the-art performance in both OMR and downstream sheet music understanding, demonstrating the value of system-by-system processing and the integration of symbolic transcriptions with vision-LM approaches.

Abstract: We propose a novel pipeline, Legato 2, for extracting symbolic notation and semantic knowledge from images of sheet music. Legato 2 features the first large-scale neural model for optical music recognition (OMR) to operate sequentially on a system-by-system basis, following the horizontal lines of notation as they are read on the page, rather than treating the page as an undifferentiated image, enabling better scaling to arbitrarily long inputs. It is also the first OMR model capable of generating symbolic transcriptions that include embedded textual content, such as titles and annotations. The pipeline combines system-level segmentation with an autoregressive vision-LM to capture both local notation details and score structure. Across multiple datasets, Legato 2 consistently outperforms prior state of the art. We also show that symbolic transcriptions complement visual inputs for frontier language models, improving their interpretation of dense musical documents. Legato 2 establishes new state-of-the-art performance in both OMR and downstream sheet music understanding.

</details>


### [28] [Benchmarking the Robustness of Autonomous Driving to Environmental Illusions: A Lane Perception Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05783)
*Tianyuan Zhang,Xianglong Liu,Aishan Liu,Lu Wang,Yitong Zhang,Peng Yue,Mingchuan Zhang,Siyuan Liang,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LanEvil++ is the first benchmark for evaluating autonomous driving robustness against 14 types of environmental illusions (shadows, reflections, tire marks, etc.), showing that current lane perception models (LD and ADVLMs) are significantly vulnerable (up to 7.20% accuracy drop from shadows), and proposing MIDA defense that improves robustness by ~4%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Environmental illusions (shadows, reflections, tire marks) are naturally occurring in real-world driving but systematically overlooked in AD research. They mislead visual perception and cause safety risks, yet no benchmark existed to evaluate AD robustness against these phenomena.

Method: Created LanEvil++ benchmark with 14 illusion types across 94 CARLA-generated 3D scenes; evaluated two lane perception model types (conventional LD and vision-language ADVLMs); proposed MIDA (Multimodal Illusion Defense Approach) for robustness enhancement.

Result: LD models: 5.27% accuracy drop, 10.49% F1 decline; ADVLMs: 2.03% GPT-score drop, 0.75% Language-score drop; shadows most disruptive (7.20% accuracy reduction). MIDA improves LD robustness by 4.23% and ADVLM by 3.82%.

Conclusion: Environmental illusions pose a significant and previously unaddressed threat to AD lane perception; the proposed benchmark and defense method provide a foundation for future research toward robust perception under naturally occurring visual illusions.

Abstract: Environmental illusions (eg., shadows, reflections, and tire marks) are naturally existing yet overlooked phenomena in real-world driving environments. They can disturb visual perception, leading to misinterpretation of the scene and posing serious safety risks to autonomous driving (AD) systems. However, existing researches largely overlook these phenomena, leaving a critical gap. To address this issue, we study AD robustness through the lane perception perspective, a fundamental task supporting core functions like cruise control and lane centering. We focus on two representative models: conventional lane detection (LD) and vision-language model-based systems (ADVLMs). In this work, we introduce the first benchmark, LanEvil++, for evaluating the robustness of lane perception under environmental illusions. LanEvil++ encompasses 14 types of illusions and leverages the CARLA simulator to generate 94 high-fidelity, fully controllable 3D scenes, yielding a dataset of 90,292 annotated images, 1,596 video clips, and 41,855 visual question answering pairs. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that environmental illusions substantially degrade the performance of state-of-the-art LD methods. On average, LD models experience a 5.27% drop in Accuracy and a 10.49% decline in F1-score, while ADVLMs show a 2.03% reduction in GPT-score and a 0.75% drop in Language-score. Among all illusions, shadows emerge as the most disruptive factor, reducing accuracy by up to 7.20%. Furthermore, closed-loop simulations reveal that these illusions can lead to incorrect driving decisions. Complementary real-world case studies highlight safety-critical failures in actual traffic scenes. To enhance robustness, we propose the Multimodal Illusion Defense Approach (MIDA). MIDA achieves substantial gains under challenging conditions, boosting robustness by 4.23% on LD models and 3.82% on ADVLMs.

</details>


### [29] [DeSeG: Decoupling Semantic Intent and Geometric Constraints for Physically Plausible Human-Scene Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05787)
*Jiakun Li,Zhe Li,Wenqiang Wu,Zheng Chang,Mingqi Gao,Jinyu Yang,Feng Zheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DeSeG is a hierarchical framework that decouples semantic intent from geometric constraints in human-scene interaction synthesis, using a Residual Semantic Planner and physics-regularized diffusion to reduce penetration by 47% and improve semantic alignment by 29%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative models for human-scene interaction suffer from semantic-geometric entanglement, where spatial constraints override semantic intent, causing shortcut biases and physical hallucinations like body-scene penetrations.

Method: DeSeG introduces a hierarchical framework with two key components: (1) a Residual Semantic Planner that decouples semantic intent from spatial trajectories by encoding text and canonicalized goal voxels into a compact latent space; (2) a physics-regularized diffusion executor that integrates differentiable repulsive potential fields into the diffusion objective for collision-aware motion generation.

Result: On the Lingo dataset, DeSeG reduces mean scene penetration by 47% and improves semantic alignment by 29% compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Explicitly decoupling semantic intent from geometric constraints via a hierarchical framework effectively resolves the entanglement issue in human-scene interaction synthesis, yielding physically plausible and semantically aligned motions.

Abstract: Synthesizing physically plausible human-scene interactions (HSI) remains a critical challenge in computer vision and the development of human avatars. Although recent generative models enable diverse motion synthesis, they suffer from an inductive bias referred to as semantic-geometric entanglement. Because spatial constraints often strongly correlate with specific actions in training data, monolithic models will learn the shortcut bias, aggressively overriding the semantic intent when faced with strict geometric cues. Furthermore, this entanglement exacerbates physical hallucinations, such as body-scene penetrations. To address these limitations, we propose DeSeG, a hierarchical framework that explicitly decouples semantic intent from geometric constraints. First, we introduce a Residual Semantic Planner that encodes textual instructions and canonicalized goal voxels into a compact latent space, enabling fine-grained semantic control independent of spatial trajectories. Second, we propose a physics regularized diffusion executor that incorporates differentiable repulsive potential fields directly into the diffusion objective, enforcing collision-aware motion generation. Extensive experiments on the Lingo dataset demonstrate that DeSeG achieves state-of-the-art performance, reducing mean scene penetration by 47% and improving semantic alignment by 29% over the SOTA baselines.

</details>


### [30] [Segmentation before Answering: Pixel Grounding for MLLM Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05798)
*Yake Wei,Yuan Wang,Fengyun Rao,Jing Lyu,Di Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SegAnswer replaces bounding-box zoom-in with pixel-level segmentation masks for MLLMs, enabling finer visual detail extraction and improved reasoning, perception, and hallucination benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs use bounding boxes for region zoom-in during visual reasoning, which introduces redundant background and interfering objects. The authors propose using segmentation masks instead to achieve more precise region isolation.

Method: SegAnswer shifts zoom-in units from bounding boxes to pixel-level segmentation masks, isolating target regions from clutter. The segmented visual patches align better with MLLMs' positional embedding structures for visual tokens.

Result: Consistent improvements across high-resolution perception, general perception, and hallucination benchmarks. Also achieves competitive performance on segmentation tasks, demonstrating reliable pixel grounding.

Conclusion: Segmentation-based zoom-in is more effective than bounding-box zoom-in for MLLM visual reasoning, providing cleaner region isolation and better alignment with MLLM token structures.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have evolved from static perception to interleaved visual-language reasoning, often referred to as ``thinking with images''. A basic operation in this reasoning process is to zoom in on regions of interest (often represented with bounding boxes) to acquire finer visual details. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Seg}mentation before \textbf{Answer}ing (SegAnswer), which shifts the unit of zoom-in from the popular bounding box to pixel-level segmentation mask. By employing fine-grained masks to isolate the target area from cluttered environments, segmented visual input yields a more precise region of interest, effectively filtering out redundant background and interfering objects. Furthermore, the discrete patches of segmented visual input align more seamlessly with how MLLMs structure visual tokens via positional embeddings. In experiments, we evaluate SegAnswer across diverse benchmarks, including high-resolution perception, general perception, and hallucination. It achieves consistent improvements and also exhibits considerable performance on segmentation tasks, validating its capability for reliable pixel grounding.

</details>


### [31] [TRIG: Trajectory-Rig Decoupled Metric Geometry Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05801)
*Lizhou Liao,Wentao Xu,Handong Wang,Lirong Yang,Shuai Yang,Weiwei Liu,Chang Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TRIG is a geometry perception framework that decouples camera poses into ego-trajectory and camera-rig components for metric-consistent learning in vision-centric autonomous driving, achieving SOTA on pose estimation, depth prediction, and 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual geometry models encode camera poses as entangled representations that jointly model time-varying ego-motion and static camera-rig geometry, which limits the utilization of vehicle-side geometric priors in multi-camera driving systems.

Method: TRIG factorizes camera poses into ego-trajectory and camera-rig components with decoupled pose encoding and supervision, and introduces sparse Temporal-Spatial attention to separate cross-camera interaction from temporal aggregation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance across five autonomous driving benchmarks on pose estimation, metric depth prediction, and 3D reconstruction tasks.

Conclusion: Decoupling ego-trajectory from camera-rig geometry enables better geometric priors utilization and metric-consistent learning, while sparse temporal-spatial attention reduces computational cost without sacrificing geometric reasoning quality.

Abstract: Vision-centric autonomous driving requires accurate metric geometry and ego-motion estimation from synchronized multi-camera observations. Recent visual geometry models show strong performance in pose estimation, depth prediction, and 3D reconstruction, but are not tailored to rigid multi-camera driving systems. They often encode camera poses as entangled representations, in which time-varying ego-motion and static camera-rig geometry are jointly modeled, limiting the utilization of vehicle-side geometric priors. We propose Trajectory-Rig Decoupled Metric Geometry Learning (TRIG), a geometry perception framework for autonomous driving. TRIG factorizes camera poses into ego-trajectory and camera-rig components, enabling separate modeling of ego-motion and static multi-camera topology. We introduce decoupled pose encoding and supervision, which separately constrain trajectory evolution and rig geometry for metric-consistent learning. Moreover, sparse Temporal--Spatial attention separates cross-camera interaction from temporal aggregation, reducing global attention cost while preserving geometric reasoning. Experiments on five autonomous driving benchmarks show that TRIG achieves state-of-the-art performance in pose estimation, metric depth prediction, and 3D reconstruction.

</details>


### [32] [Complementary Roles of Image Classification and Vessel Segmentation in AI-Based Screening for Retinopathy of Prematurity Plus Disease in a Kenyan Preterm Cohort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05825)
*Fred Mutisya,Oscar Onyango,Sarah Sitati,Syokau Ilovi,Aeesha NJ Malik,Brenda W'mosi,Brian Makini,Jalemba Aluuvala,Josiah Onyango,Rachael Kanguha Mmene,Steven Wanyee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study evaluates automated approaches for detecting Plus disease in retinopathy of prematurity using 121 Kenyan preterm infants (237 eyes, 1,635 images). Combining image classification with vessel segmentation improved performance: an ensemble achieved sensitivity 0.692, specificity 0.914, and balanced accuracy 0.803, outperforming classifiers alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retinopathy of prematurity (ROP) is a preventable cause of childhood blindness with rising burden in low- and middle-income countries where ROP-trained ophthalmologists are scarce. Plus disease assessment is subjective and variable, and automated screening could extend specialist reach, but African evidence remains limited.

Method: Analysed 121 Kenyan preterm infants (237 eyes, 1,635 fundus images) graded as No Plus, Pre-Plus or Plus. Vessel annotations from two graders supported segmentation training. Eleven configurations were evaluated for eye-level Plus detection using patient-grouped nested cross-validation, including image classifiers, multiple-instance learning, multi-task segmentation-classification, and segment-then-classify pipelines.

Result: Vessel segmentation achieved pooled Dice 0.533, IoU 0.368, sensitivity 0.623, specificity 0.979. RGB classifiers were highly sensitive but over-referred; segmentation-coupled models were more specific. An OR-based screen achieved highest sensitivity, AND-based confirmation highest specificity, and a probability ensemble gave best balanced performance: sensitivity 0.692, specificity 0.914, balanced accuracy 0.803.

Conclusion: Classification and vessel segmentation are complementary for ROP Plus detection in Kenyan data. Classifiers support sensitive case-finding, while segmentation improves specificity and reduces over-referral. African ROP AI systems should use combined workflows and undergo prospective multi-site validation.

Abstract: Background. Retinopathy of prematurity (ROP) is a preventable cause of childhood blindness, with rising burden in low- and middle-income countries where ROP-trained ophthalmologists are scarce. Plus disease, marked by retinal vessel dilation and tortuosity, triggers treatment but is subjective and variable. Automated screening could extend specialist reach, but African evidence remains limited.
  Methods. We analysed 121 Kenyan preterm infants, covering 237 eyes and 1,635 fundus images graded as No Plus, Pre-Plus or Plus. Vessel annotations from two graders supported segmentation training. Eleven configurations were evaluated for eye-level Plus detection using patient-grouped nested cross-validation, including image classifiers, multiple-instance learning, multi-task segmentation-classification, and segment-then-classify pipelines.
  Results. Vessel segmentation was feasible, achieving pooled Dice 0.533, IoU 0.368, sensitivity 0.623 and specificity 0.979 on held-out images. RGB classifiers were highly sensitive but over-referred, while segmentation-coupled models were more specific. Combining approaches improved performance: an OR-based screen achieved the highest sensitivity, an AND-based confirmation achieved the highest specificity, and a probability ensemble gave the best balanced performance, with sensitivity 0.692, specificity 0.914 and balanced accuracy 0.803, outperforming the vision classifier alone.
  Conclusions. Classification and vessel segmentation are complementary for ROP Plus detection in Kenyan data. Classifiers support sensitive case-finding, while segmentation improves specificity and reduces over-referral. African ROP AI systems should use combined workflows and undergo prospective multi-site validation.

</details>


### [33] [Realistic Compound-Lens Defocus Blur Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05837)
*Yunkyu Lee,Woohyeok Kim,Sunghyun Cho*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A pipeline for synthesizing realistic defocus deblurring datasets with diverse compound lenses, producing CLDefocus dataset that improves cross-device generalization over existing real and synthetic datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Defocus blur degrades fine image structures and limits visual perception, adversely affecting downstream vision tasks. Existing deep learning deblurring methods suffer from degraded performance across cameras/lenses due to limited optical diversity and realism in current datasets.

Method: A pipeline integrating (1) efficient wave-optics PSF computation via Debye CZT propagation, (2) depth-aware defocus rendering with occlusion handling, and (3) blur synthesis in radiometrically linear space with camera ISP simulation.

Result: Generated CLDefocus, a large-scale synthetic dataset with lens-diverse defocus image pairs. Models trained on CLDefocus achieve improved cross-device generalization compared to models trained on existing real and synthetic datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline enables scalable generation of photorealistic defocus datasets with diverse lens characteristics, addressing cross-device generalization limitations in defocus deblurring.

Abstract: Defocus blur degrades fine image structures and limits visual perception, which can adversely affect downstream vision tasks. Although recent deep learning deblurring methods have achieved strong performance, their effectiveness depends on training data and often degrades across cameras and lenses due to limited optical diversity and realism in existing datasets. In this paper, we propose a pipeline for synthesizing realistic defocus deblurring datasets for diverse compound lenses. It integrates efficient wave-optics PSF computation via Debye CZT propagation, depth-aware defocus rendering with occlusion handling, and blur synthesis in the radiometrically linear space with camera ISP simulation. This unified pipeline enables the scalable generation of photorealistic defocus datasets with diverse lens characteristics. Using our pipeline, we generate CLDefocus, a large-scale synthetic dataset containing lens-diverse defocus image pairs. We further analyze the limitations of real-captured defocus datasets and show that such imperfections can bias full-reference evaluation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that models trained on CLDefocus achieve improved cross-device generalization compared to models trained on existing real and synthetic datasets.

</details>


### [34] [Breaking Spurious Correlations via Generative Randomization and Cross-Variant Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05850)
*Suraj Yadav,Anjaneya Sharma,Siddharth Yadav*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a two-stage framework using generative intervention and cross-variant self-supervised learning to learn background-invariant visual representations, achieving strong worst-group performance on distribution-shift benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks trained with ERM exploit spurious correlations between object labels and background context, leading to failures under distribution shifts.

Method: (1) Isolate foreground via zero-shot segmentation, generate context-shifted variants with structure-preserving diffusion model. (2) Cross-Variant Self-Supervised Learning: contrastive objective where background-shifted variants of same object form positive pairs. (3) Fine-tune with ERM warm-up + GroupDRO with layer-wise learning rates.

Result: Best worst-group accuracy: 92.5% on Waterbirds, 81.7% on MetaShift, 87.4% on NICO++.

Conclusion: Generative intervention combined with self-supervised contrastive learning effectively learns background-invariant representations, outperforming prior methods on distribution-shift benchmarks.

Abstract: Deep neural networks trained with Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM) often fail under distribution shifts because they exploit spurious correlations between object labels and background context. Recent generative approaches address this issue by creating counterfactual images with altered contexts, but typically use these samples as standard data augmentation, leaving the model free to retain background-sensitive representations. We propose a two-stage framework that uses generative intervention to explicitly learn background-invariant visual representations. First, we isolate the foreground object using zero-shot segmentation and generate context-shifted variants with a structure-preserving diffusion model, preserving object identity while varying the surrounding environment. We then introduce Cross-Variant Self-Supervised Learning, where variants of the same object under different backgrounds form positive pairs in a contrastive objective. This encourages the encoder to align object-centric representations while suppressing background-specific cues. Then, we fine-tune the pretrained encoder using an ERM warm-up followed by GroupDRO with layer-wise learning rates. Experiments on distribution-shift benchmarks demonstrate best worst-group performance, achieving 92.5% on Waterbirds, 81.7% on MetaShift, and 87.4% on NICO++. Code: https://github.com/surajyadav-research/GRSSL

</details>


### [35] [AVA-VLM: Adaptive Visual Attention-Vision Language Model for In-the-Wild Construction Site Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05859)
*Younggun Kim,Taeheon Kim,Youngseo Kim,Seunghee Park*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AVA-VLM introduces a coarse-to-fine, human-inspired reasoning strategy for VLMs in construction-site monitoring, enabling selective high-resolution local crops only when needed, improving reliability under reduced-resolution inputs while reducing visual-token usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing construction-tailored VLMs rely on direct QA-style fine-tuning from a single global image, which limits operational range, reliability under reduced-resolution inputs, and inference efficiency for real-world deployment.

Method: AVA-VLM employs a coarse-to-fine reasoning strategy: first reasoning over low-resolution global images, then selectively requesting high-resolution local crops for detailed inspection. It also introduces a region-aware Chain-of-Thought dataset to teach the model when to inspect, where to crop, and how to use local evidence.

Result: AVA-VLM improves reliability under long-distance and reduced-resolution conditions while substantially reducing visual-token usage compared to direct QA-style fine-tuning approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed coarse-to-fine adaptive attention mechanism effectively addresses the limitations of global-image-only approaches for construction-site monitoring VLMs, offering better efficiency and robustness for in-the-wild deployment.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are promising for construction-site monitoring, and recent construction-tailored VLMs have primarily adapted pretrained VLMs through direct QA-style fine-tuning from a single global image. We argue that this direct paradigm remains limited for in-the-wild deployment in terms of operational range, reliability under reduced-resolution inputs, and inference efficiency. To address these challenges, we propose AVA-VLM, an Adaptive Visual Attention-Vision Language Model that follows a human-inspired coarse-to-fine reasoning strategy. AVA-VLM first reasons over a low-resolution global image and selectively requests a high-resolution local crop only when detailed inspection is needed, similar to how a human inspector zooms in on hard-to-see yet important areas. We further introduce a region-aware Chain-of-Thought dataset that teaches the model when to inspect, where to crop, and how to use local evidence. Experiments show that AVA-VLM improves reliability under long-distance and reduced-resolution conditions while substantially reducing visual-token usage.

</details>


### [36] [Harrison.Rad 1.5 Technical Report: A radiology foundation model that can draft reports from images, priors and clinical context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05880)
*Suneeta Mall,Vladimir Nekrasov,Ashnil Kumar,Sajith Karunasena,Aiden Nibali,Alix Bird,Mateo Diaz Shine,Jarrel Seah*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Harrison.Rad 1.5 (HR1.5) is a radiology-specific multimodal LLM trained via a three-stage pipeline (domain-adapted language model, contrastive vision encoder with curriculum hard negatives, and VQA fine-tuning) that generates structured/unstructured reports across plain-film modalities. It is the only system to pass a simulated FRCR 2B exam and achieves top accuracy on clinical questions, multi-body-part reporting, and public chest reporting, with explainability tools (Grad-CAM, attention, confidence) for responsible clinical evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imaging demand is growing faster than the radiology workforce, and backlogs cannot be resolved solely through recruitment and training. The most direct opportunity is reducing radiologists' time and effort in report generation, which requires interpreting images, integrating clinical history and prior studies, and drafting structured findings.

Method: Three-stage training pipeline: (1) domain adaptation of a base language model on radiology reports; (2) contrastive vision-encoder training with curriculum-based hard negatives on ~6 million image-report instances; (3) visual-question-answering fine-tuning on multi-turn conversations. Evaluation uses a Findings-Diagnosis scoring framework extending RadGraph-XL with ontology-based synonym matching and polarity-contradiction detection, benchmarked on RadBench (simulated FRCR 2B Short Case with Angoff thresholds), ReXGradient, and internal multi-modality datasets.

Result: HR1.5 is the only evaluated system to meet the simulated FRCR passing standard. It achieves the highest accuracy on closed-format clinical questions, across anatomical regions, on internal multi-body-part and mammography reporting, and on the primary clinically-aligned score for public chest reporting.

Conclusion: HR1.5 demonstrates state-of-the-art radiology report generation performance, including passing a simulated FRCR 2B exam, with explainability tools (Grad-CAM, attention analysis, confidence estimation) supporting responsible evaluation toward clinical use and a framework for clinically grounded assessment of report quality.

Abstract: Imaging demand is growing faster than the radiology workforce can expand, and reporting backlogs cannot be resolved through training and recruitment alone. The most direct opportunity is reducing the time and effort radiologists spend producing reports, a task that requires interpreting images, integrating clinical history and prior studies, and drafting structured findings. We present Harrison.Rad 1.5 (HR1.5), a radiology-specific multimodal large language model that accepts interleaved text and visual inputs and generates structured and unstructured text across plain-film radiology, spanning computed radiography, chest, musculoskeletal, abdominal, spine, and pelvic x-rays, and mammography. HR1.5 is trained through a three-stage pipeline: domain adaptation of a base language model on radiology reports, contrastive vision-encoder training with curriculum-based hard negatives on ~6 million image-report instances, and visual-question-answering fine-tuning on multi-turn conversations. We evaluate it with a Findings-Diagnosis scoring framework that extends RadGraph-XL entity extraction with ontology-based synonym matching and polarity-contradiction detection, benchmarked on RadBench, a simulated FRCR 2B Short Case examination scored against Angoff-method thresholds, ReXGradient, and internal multi-modality datasets. HR1.5 is the only system evaluated to meet the simulated FRCR passing standard and achieves the highest accuracy on closed-format clinical questions, across anatomical regions, on internal multi-body-part and mammography reporting, and on the primary clinically-aligned score for public chest reporting. We further examine explainability and model behaviour, including question-sensitive Grad-CAM heatmaps, attention analysis, and confidence estimation, to support responsible future evaluation toward clinical use, and a framework for clinically grounded assessment of report quality.

</details>


### [37] [Few-Medoids: An Embarrassingly Simple Coreset Selection Method for Few-Shot Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05891)
*Cemil-Andrei Dilmac,Florinel-Alin Croitoru,Radu Tudor Ionescu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Few-Medoids is a simple coreset selection strategy that picks samples closest to the class centroid, consistently outperforming random selection and other baselines in few-shot knowledge distillation across multiple datasets and model architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coreset selection remains challenging in few-shot knowledge distillation, where typical sample selection strategies often fail to surpass random selection baselines, motivating the need for a simple yet effective approach.

Method: Few-Medoids selects coresets by choosing the samples closest to the centroid (average image) of each class, serving as a drop-in replacement for common baselines like herding or k-center greedy.

Result: Extensive KD experiments on four datasets and three teacher-student model pairs (convolutional and transformer networks) show few-medoids consistently surpasses random selection and other coreset selection strategies.

Conclusion: Few-Medoids can serve as a simple, effective drop-in replacement for commonly-used baselines in future coreset selection research, with publicly released code for reproducibility.

Abstract: Coreset selection aims to identify a small and highly representative subset of a massive dataset for efficient model training. The problem remains challenging even in the few-shot knowledge distillation (KD) setup, where a full-scale pre-trained teacher informs the student network. Typical sample selection strategies often struggle to surpass the random selection baseline. In this paper, we showcase few-medoids, an embarrassingly simple coreset selection strategy that chooses the samples closest to the centroid (average image) of each class. We present extensive KD experiments on four datasets, covering a wide range of image classification problems, and three teacher-student model pairs, comprising both convolutional and transformer networks. Although the proposed method is embarrassingly simple, our empirical results indicate that few-medoids is able to consistently surpass the random selection baseline, as well as the other coreset selection strategies. We therefore consider that few-medoids can be used as a drop-in replacement for commonly-used baselines (e.g. herding or k-center Greedy), in future research on coreset selection. To reproduce the reported results, we publicly release our code at https://github.com/CemilAndreiDilmac/Few-Shot-KD-Coreset.

</details>


### [38] [GaussFusion: Towards Multimodal 3D Gaussian Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05906)
*Zhixuan You,Jihua Zhu,Yiding Sun,Zihao Guo,Haozhe Cheng,Dongxu Zhang,Lin Chen,Hainan Luo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GaussFusion is a multimodal pre-training framework for 3D Gaussian representations that integrates image and text supervision into masked Gaussian modeling via cross-modal semantic alignment, and proposes Gaussian Salience-guided Multi-scale Hole Masking (GSHM) to handle non-uniform Gaussian primitives. It outperforms Gaussian-MAE on ModelNet40 and ScanObjectNN by 0.61% and 3.85% respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pre-training methods for Gaussian representations (e.g., masked Gaussian reconstruction) primarily capture local structures but offer limited semantic supervision. The paper aims to inject both visual and language-level semantic information into Gaussian encoders during pre-training.

Method: GaussFusion integrates image and text supervision into masked Gaussian modeling through cross-modal semantic alignment. It also proposes Gaussian Salience-guided Multi-scale Hole Masking (GSHM), which constructs spatially continuous masked regions based on Gaussian salience at multiple scales to capture both fine-grained local patterns and broader structural dependencies.

Result: GaussFusion outperforms Gaussian-MAE on ModelNet40 by 0.61% and on ScanObjectNN (PB-T50-RS) by 3.85%, demonstrating improved transferability of Gaussian representations.

Conclusion: Multimodal pre-training with cross-modal semantic alignment and salience-guided multi-scale masking significantly enhances the representation quality and transferability of 3D Gaussian representations for downstream tasks.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting provides an explicit representation that jointly models geometry and appearance, serving as a scalable foundation for 3D representation learning. Existing pre-training methods for Gaussian representations, such as masked Gaussian reconstruction, primarily capture local structures but offer limited semantic supervision. In this paper, we propose GaussFusion, a multimodal pre-training framework for 3D Gaussian representations. GaussFusion integrates image and text supervision into masked Gaussian modeling through cross-modal semantic alignment, enabling the Gaussian encoder to learn both visual and language-level semantic information during pre-training. To better adapt masked modeling to the non-uniform distribution of Gaussian primitives, we further propose Gaussian Salience-guided Multi-scale Hole Masking (GSHM). GSHM constructs spatially continuous masked regions based on Gaussian salience. By applying hole masks at multiple scales, GSHM encourages the encoder to capture both fine-grained local patterns and broader structural dependencies. Extensive experiments on downstream tasks demonstrate that GaussFusion improves the transferability of Gaussian representations. Notably, GaussFusion outperforms Gaussian-MAE on ModelNet40 and ScanObjectNN (PB-T50-RS) by 0.61\% and 3.85\%, respectively.

</details>


### [39] [PolicyShiftGuard: Benchmarking and Improving Policy-Adaptive Image Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05910)
*Mingyang Song,Luxin Xu,Haoyu Sun,Minzhou Pan,Yu Cheng,Bo Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper studies policy-adaptive image guardrailing, introduces PolicyShiftBench (2,000 instances over 265 images with 7.55 policy-conditioned prompts per image) and PolicyShiftGuard (a compact guardrail trained with RP-SFT and BP-Adapt), showing that existing VLMs fail under policy shifts while the proposed 7B model achieves 76.9 Avg. F1 and 72.1 Avg. PSS, with matched boundary pairs being critical for stable adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing image guardrails treat safety as an intrinsic image property and are trained/evaluated under fixed safety policies, but real deployments require models to adapt to varying and changing policies across different products and over time.

Method: Proposes PolicyShiftGuard trained with a two-stage recipe: (1) Randomized Policy SFT (RP-SFT) for initial policy-conditioned fine-tuning, and (2) Boundary-Pair Policy Adaptation (BP-Adapt) using matched pass/block prompt pairs for the same image and risk category with pairwise comparison loss to separate blocking from passing policies.

Result: PolicyShiftGuard (7B) achieves 76.9 Avg. F1 and 72.1 Avg. PSS on PolicyShiftBench, outperforming existing VLMs and specialized guardrails. Transfers well to UnSafeBench and SafeEditBench with improved latency-performance trade-off via concise output format.

Conclusion: Policy-adaptive guardrailing is necessary for real deployments; matched pass/block boundary pairs are essential for stable policy adaptation; the proposed approach sets SOTA on policy-shift scenarios while maintaining efficiency.

Abstract: Image guardrails are typically trained and evaluated under a fixed safety policy, implicitly treating safety as an intrinsic property of an image. Real deployments are different: the same image may be allowed in one product, restricted in another, and newly disallowed when a policy boundary changes. We study policy-adaptive image guardrailing, where a model must decide whether an image violates the currently supplied policy and generalize to held-out policy definitions. We introduce PolicyShiftBench, a comprehensive benchmark with 2,000 policy-discriminative instances over 265 images, where each image is paired with 7.55 policy-conditioned prompts on average to test whether models adapt to the active policy rather than relying on image-level safety priors. We then propose PolicyShiftGuard, a compact policy-conditioned guardrail trained with a two-stage training recipe that combines Randomized Policy SFT (RP-SFT) with Boundary-Pair Policy Adaptation (BP-Adapt). BP-Adapt trains matched prompts for the same image and risk category using standard label supervision and a pairwise comparison loss that separates blocking policies from passing policies. Experiments show that existing VLMs and specialized guardrails remain brittle under policy shifts, while PolicyShiftGuard substantially improves policy-sensitive performance. The 7B model achieves SOTA performance of 76.9 Avg. F1 and 72.1 Avg. PSS on PolicyShiftBench, transfers well to UnSafeBench and SafeEditBench, and improves the latency-performance trade-off with a concise output format. Ablations confirm that matched pass/block boundary pairs are essential for stable policy adaptation.

</details>


### [40] [Progressive Reasoning with Primitive Correction for Compositional Zero-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05911)
*Ziyi Chen,Haoyan Shi,Sunhan Xu,Congyan Lang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PRPC is a progressive reasoning framework for Compositional Zero-Shot Learning that uses bidirectional dependency modeling and mutual primitive correction via step-wise inference, enhanced by reinforcement learning (GRPO) post-training, achieving SOTA on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CZSL methods either predict attributes and objects independently (missing contextual dependency) or use unidirectional conditional modeling (prone to error propagation). The authors aim to explicitly model bidirectional dependency between attributes and objects.

Method: PRPC formulates CZSL as structured Chain-of-Thought reasoning with Q&A steps, constraining MLLM to follow predefined semantic steps for intermediate decisions. It performs mutual correction of primitives (attributes and objects) to suppress error propagation. A GRPO-based reinforcement learning post-training with step-level rewards is used to enhance reliability and logical consistency.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on three CZSL benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of progressive reasoning and bidirectional correction for robust compositional generalization.

Conclusion: Progressive reasoning with bidirectional primitive correction effectively addresses error propagation in CZSL, and reinforcement learning with step-level rewards further improves intermediate reasoning consistency.

Abstract: Compositional Zero-Shot Learning (CZSL) aims to combine known attributes and objects as primitives for recognizing previously unseen attribute-object pairs. Prior works either predict attributes and objects independently, missing their strong contextual dependency, or use unidirectional conditional modeling (e.g., object-guided attribute prediction), which is prone to error propagation. We propose PRPC, a Progressive Reasoning framework with Primitive Correction, which explicitly models the bidirectional dependency between attributes and objects via step-wise inference. PRPC performs mutual correction of primitives to suppress prediction errors in earlier steps. Specifically, we formulate CZSL as structured, Q&A-style Chain-of-Thought reasoning process and constrain the MLLM to follow predefined semantic steps to generate intermediate decisions. To further enhance the reliability and logical consistency of intermediate reasoning, we introduce reinforcement learning post-training with a GRPO-based objective, providing step-level rewards aligned with the progressive inference procedure. Extensive experiments on three CZSL benchmarks demonstrate that PRPC achieves state-of-the-art performance, validating the effectiveness of progressive reasoning and bidirectional correction for robust compositional generalization.

</details>


### [41] [NegROI: Click-Centric Uncertainty-Guided Refinement with Scene-Conditioned Negative Prompts for Robust Interactive 3D Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05955)
*Shuheng Zhang,Feng Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NegROI is a transformer-based interactive 3D segmentation framework that combines click-centric multi-resolution ROI refinement with scene-conditioned negative prompts, using uncertainty-driven selective refinement, diversity-regularized negative prompts, and boundary-aware hard negative mining to improve fine boundary accuracy and reduce false positives across datasets with varying density/scale (ScanNet, S3DIS, KITTI).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interactive 3D segmentation methods suffer from (i) coarse voxel resolution blurring fine boundaries under limited clicks, and (ii) hard false positives from confusing background structures. These issues worsen across datasets with density/scale shifts (e.g., dense RGB-D vs. sparse LiDAR), where fixed heuristics and click-only decoding generalize poorly.

Method: The framework has four key components: (1) click-centric multi-resolution ROI refinement — refines only the local ROI around the current click on a finer grid and fuses back; (2) uncertainty-driven selective refinement — prioritizes ambiguous regions for efficiency; (3) scene-conditioned negative prompts — models hard background patterns via cross-attention over scene tokens with a diversity regularizer for stability; (4) boundary-aware hard negative mining — supervises negative-prompt attention to target boundary-proximal, high-confidence false positives.

Result: Experiments on ScanNet, S3DIS, and KITTI show improved click efficiency, reduced false positives, and stronger cross-dataset robustness than state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: NegROI effectively addresses fine boundary blurring and hard false positives in interactive 3D segmentation by coupling multi-resolution ROI refinement with scene-conditioned negative prompts, achieving robust cross-dataset performance.

Abstract: Interactive 3D segmentation aims to extract object masks in point clouds with minimal user clicks. Despite recent progress, most existing approaches still struggle with (i) coarse voxel resolution that blurs fine boundaries under limited clicks and (ii) hard false positives caused by confusing background structures. These issues are exacerbated by density and scale shifts across datasets (e.g., dense RGB-D reconstructions vs. sparse LiDAR scans), where fixed refinement heuristics and purely click-driven decoding generalize poorly. To address them, we propose NegROI -- a novel transformer-based interactive framework that couples click-centric multi-resolution refinement with scene-conditioned negative prompts. Given a coarse voxel prediction, it refines only a local Region Of Interest (ROI) around the current click on a finer grid and fuses refined logits back to the coarse mask. To improve robustness and efficiency, we introduce uncertainty-driven selective refinement that prioritizes ambiguous regions. Meanwhile, we model hard background patterns via a set of scene-conditioned negative prompts obtained by cross-attention over scene tokens. We further stabilize these prompts with a diversity regularizer. Finally, we propose boundary-aware hard negative mining to supervise negative-prompt attention toward boundary-proximal, high-confidence false positives. Our experiments on common benchmark datasets (i.e., ScanNet, S3DIS, and KITTI) demonstrate improved click efficiency and reduced false positives, with stronger cross-dataset robustness than the state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [42] [Decoupled Single-Mask Annotation Noise Detection via Cross-Sectional Patch Self-Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05965)
*Yinheng Zhu,Xiaowei Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A decoupled framework detecting single-mask annotation noise in vascular CT by leveraging cross-sectional patch self-consistency, producing interpretable evidence of label errors without requiring multirater fusion or network coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vascular CT datasets often have only single-mask annotations per scan, resulting in pervasive annotation noise. Existing solutions need costly multirater fusion or are tied to network training, preventing explicit auditing of where and why labels fail.

Method: Cross-sectional patches are sampled orthogonally along vessel centrelines; intensity-equivalent neighbours are retrieved via scalable vector search; a patch-level noise score is computed from statistical mask disagreement, producing explicit image-mask evidence for flagged regions. Scores are aggregated into scan-level quality maps.

Result: Coronary CT validation shows detected noise improves training robustness; reveals systematic biases: transverse/oblique vessels have 5.1× higher error rates than axis-aligned structures, with correlations to cross-sectional area and intensity.

Conclusion: The decoupled framework provides interpretable, auditable noise detection for single-mask annotations, enabling dataset quality assessment and quality-weighted training without requiring multirater fusion.

Abstract: Vascular computed tomography datasets are commonly annotated only once per scan, yielding the pervasive yet under addressed problem of single mask annotation noise. Existing solutions either require costly multirater fusion or are coupled with network training, preventing explicit auditing of where and why labels fail. We introduce a decoupled framework for single-mask annotation noise detection that leverages cross-sectional patch self-consistency to produce interpretable and auditable noise evidence. Tubular anatomy exhibits strong cross-sectional recurrence: patches extracted orthogonally along vessel centrelines recur in appearance across locations and subjects. Thus, anatomically similar patches should have consistent masks, and disagreement signals unreliable annotation. Our method samples cross-sectional patches, retrieves intensity-equivalent neighbours via scalable vector search, and computes a patch-level noise score from statistical mask disagreement, yielding explicit image-mask evidence for every flagged region. Aggregating scores produces scan-level quality maps for dataset quality assessment or quality-weighted training. Experiments on the coronary CT dataset validate the detected noise for improving training robustness and reveal systematic annotation biases. Specifically, transverse and oblique vessels exhibit 5.1 times higher error rates than axis-aligned structures, with additional correlations to cross-sectional area and intensity. Code is available here.

</details>


### [43] [Propose and Attend: Training-free MLLM Grounding Confidence via Multi-Token Localized Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05978)
*Daniel Shalam,Emanuel Ben Baruch,Avi Ben Cohen,Tal Remez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MTLA is a training-free, post-hoc score for measuring grounding quality of multimodal LLM predictions across images, video, and audio, improving hallucination detection AUROC by +7 to +38 over prior baselines and nearly doubling zero-shot COCO detection AP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs produce localized predictions (bounding boxes, temporal windows) but suffer from prolific hallucinations. Existing token log-probabilities conflate grounding quality with input ambiguity and are nearly uninformative, motivating the need for a better training-free grounding score.

Method: MTLA computes how strongly a prediction's tokens attend to the claimed spatial/temporal region by summing attention scores within that region and aggregating across all prediction tokens, rather than summing over the entire input modality and reading a single response token as prior attention-based detectors do.

Result: MTLA improves hallucination AUROC by +7 to +38 over the best prior training-free baseline across multiple MLLM families and three modalities. As a confidence score for re-ranking, it nearly doubles zero-shot COCO detection AP of an open-source 8B generalist from 20.4 to 37.0.

Conclusion: The same simple recipe applies across modalities (image object detection, video and audio temporal localization), narrowing the gap to supervised detectors without any task-specific training.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models can emit localized predictions, bounding boxes for objects and temporal windows for video and audio events, but they hallucinate these regions prolifically. The model's own token log-probabilities are nearly uninformative: they conflate grounding quality with input ambiguity, and coordinate tokens become near-deterministic once the model commits. We propose Multi-Token Localized Attention (MTLA): a training-free, post-hoc score that measures how strongly a prediction's tokens attend to the region they claim. Prior attention-based detectors, which sum attention over the entire input modality and read a single response token, are weaker special cases; we show that summing only within the claimed region and aggregating across all prediction tokens recovers a stronger grounding signal. The same recipe applies almost trivially to other modalities and tasks: object detection in images and temporal localization in video and audio. Across multiple MLLM families and three modalities, MTLA improves hallucination AUROC by +7 to +38 over the best prior training-free baseline. Used as a confidence score for re-ranking, it nearly doubles the zero-shot COCO detection AP of an open-source 8B generalist (from 20.4 to 37.0), narrowing the gap to supervised detectors without any task-specific training.

</details>


### [44] [SpecTrack: Spectral Prompt Guided Adaptive Experts for Multispectral Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05988)
*Xingyu Tan,Yunrong Qin,Mengjie Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpecTrack is a spectral-spatial complexity-aware tracker that uses adaptive capacity allocation via a Spectral Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts (SAMoE) module for multispectral/hyperspectral object tracking, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-offs on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MSI/HSI trackers use fixed-capacity spectral-spatial paths for all search regions, ignoring that tracking difficulty varies across frames and target states; clear regions need only lightweight discrimination while ambiguous boundaries and distractors require stronger reasoning.

Method: SpecTrack introduces a Spectral Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts (SAMoE) module with a capacity-ordered expert pool of increasing rank, receptive field, and depth. Expert selection is guided by a Spectral Prompt Router fusing semantic context, spatial boundary cues, and latent channel-variation cues. A Shared Global Expert provides common context to reduce fragmented routing decisions.

Result: SpecTrack-L384 achieves state-of-the-art AUCs of 65.2%, 51.9%, and 72.6% on MUST, MSITrack, and HOTC20 benchmarks; SpecTrack-B224 achieves 62.4% AUC at 43.7 FPS on MUST; RGB-domain generalization on GOT-10k yields 79.3% AO.

Conclusion: Adaptive capacity allocation based on per-region complexity enables superior accuracy-efficiency trade-offs in spectral-spatial tracking, and the architecture generalizes well to RGB tracking.

Abstract: Multispectral image(MSI) and hyperspectral image(HSI) object tracking object tracking exploits recorded band-wise observations to improve target--background discrimination under similar RGB appearance, mixed pixels, illumination variation, occlusion, and clutter. However, existing trackers commonly process all search regions through a fixed capacity spectral--spatial path, ignoring that tracking difficulty varies substantially across frames and target states. Clear regions may require only lightweight local discrimination, whereas ambiguous boundaries and spectrally similar distractors often demand stronger contextual reasoning. To address this limitation, we propose SpecTrack, a spectral--spatial complexity-aware tracker that formulates MSI tracking as search-region-level adaptive capacity allocation. Its core component, the Spectral Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts (SAMoE) module, provides a capacity-ordered expert pool with progressively increasing latent rank, receptive field, and depth. Expert selection is guided by a Spectral Prompt Router, which fuses semantic context, spatial boundary cues, and a latent channel-variation cue computed after multispectral patch embedding to activate a sparse subset of SAMoE experts for each search region. In parallel, a Shared Global Expert supplies common latent spectral--spatial context to reduce fragmented sparse-routing decisions. Experiments on MUST, MSITrack, and HOTC20 demonstrate a favorable accuracy--efficiency trade-off. The accuracy-oriented SpecTrack-L384 achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive AUCs of 65.2\%, 51.9\%, and 72.6\% on the three benchmarks, while the balanced SpecTrack-B224 reaches 62.4\% AUC at 43.7 FPS on MUST. An additional GOT-10k evaluation indicates RGB-domain architectural generalization, with SpecTrack-L384 achieving 79.3\% AO.

</details>


### [45] [SparseCtrl-HOI: Sparse Temporal Control for Human-Object Interaction Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05994)
*Shenbo Xie,Mingrui Cai,Xu Yang,Yifei Liu,Changxing Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SparseCtrl-HOI introduces a sparse temporal control framework for HOI video generation, using only a few keyframes with TiRoPE mechanism and Motion Prior Injection Module to reduce annotation costs while generating high-quality interaction videos, validated on a new SparseHOI-5K dataset for live-streaming e-commerce applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing HOI video generation methods require dense temporal guidance (e.g., frame-wise hand-object pose sequences), which incurs high annotation costs and limits motion synthesis diversity. The authors aim to overcome these limitations by enabling sparse control with only a few keyframes.

Method: The method employs a Time-Controlled Rotary Positional Embedding (TiRoPE) mechanism to temporally anchor sparse keyframes while preserving spatial integrity, and a Motion Prior Injection Module that leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to extract high-level motion priors for generating physically plausible intermediate transitions.

Result: Comprehensive evaluations confirm substantial reduction in annotation overhead while synthesizing superior live-streaming e-commerce videos compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: SparseCtrl-HOI effectively addresses the trade-off between control precision and annotation cost in HOI video generation, demonstrating practical viability for AI-driven live-streaming e-commerce applications.

Abstract: Human-Object Interaction (HOI) video generation aims to synthesize realistic videos of humans manipulating diverse objects, serving as a promising avenue for AI-driven live streaming e-commerce. A primary obstacle in this domain lies in the complexity of modeling fine-grained physical dynamics and the intricate spatial-temporal coordination between human hands and objects. Existing approaches to this problem typically rely on dense temporal guidance, e.g., frame-wise hand-object pose sequences, to strictly control the interaction process. However, such dense guidance incurs high annotation costs and affects motion synthesis diversity. To overcome these limitations, we introduce SparseCtrl-HOI, a novel sparse temporal control framework for HOI video generation. It requires only a few keyframes that capture interaction states at designated timestamps. Specifically, we employ a Time-Controlled Rotary Positional Embedding (TiRoPE) mechanism to temporally anchor these keyframes while preserving their spatial integrity. Subsequently, to govern the dynamics across intermediate frames, we propose a Motion Prior Injection Module that leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to extract high-level motion priors. This empowers the model to hallucinate logically and physically plausible transitions. Furthermore, we build SparseHOI-5K, a high-quality and richly annotated dataset for HOI video generation with sparse temporal control. Comprehensive evaluations confirm that our method substantially reduces annotation overhead while synthesizing superior live-streaming e-commerce videos. Both our code and dataset are publicly available at https://mpi-lab.github.io/SparseCtrl-HOI.

</details>


### [46] [Unlearnable Faces: Privacy Protection Surviving Extraction Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05996)
*Byunghoon Oh,Sunghwan Park,Jaewoo Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LPID introduces a robust unlearnable example method that builds face extraction into the protection objective, making perturbations survive crop+resize preprocessing and achieve under 10% attacker accuracy on unseen identities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unlearnable example methods fail when attackers extract faces (crop+resize) from shared photos before training, because the perturbation crafted on the full image collapses under this preprocessing.

Method: LPID confines perturbations to the extracted face region and optimizes them through a differentiable model of the crop+resize extraction, concentrating perturbation energy in the frequency band preserved by the extraction transform.

Result: LPID achieves the lowest attacker accuracy across all settings, holding attackers below 10% under crop+resize extraction on unseen identities, while remaining imperceptible at 32.7 dB PSNR and 0.161 LPIPS.

Conclusion: LPID is the first method to provide robust protection under face extraction preprocessing, and because robustness derives from the extraction transform itself (not identity), it generalizes to protect unseen identities when re-optimized per album.

Abstract: Unlearnable examples keep publicly shared photos from being learned by unauthorized face-recognition models. An imperceptible perturbation, added before sharing, makes any model trained on the protected photos fail on clean faces. The perturbation is crafted on the shared image, however the attacker trains on the face it extracts, cropped and resized to the recognizer input, and under this extraction the protection collapses. We propose LPID, which builds the extraction into the unlearnable-example objective. LPID confines the perturbation to the extracted face region and optimizes it through a differentiable model of the extraction, concentrating its energy in the frequency band the extraction preserves. Because this robustness is a property of the transform rather than of any identity, LPID is re-optimized per album and protects even users it has never seen. LPID attains the lowest attacker accuracy of all methods in every setting we evaluate, holding the attacker below $10\%$ under crop+resize extraction on identities unseen at protection time, while remaining imperceptible at $32.7$\,dB PSNR and $0.161$ LPIPS.

</details>


### [47] [OBBSeg: Irregular Lesion Segmentation under Oriented Bounding Box Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06007)
*Jun Wei,Xinchang Liu,Yu Liu,Chuhua Yang,Shuhui Wang,Hui Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OBBSeg introduces Oriented Bounding Box (OBB) supervision for medical image segmentation, achieving performance comparable to fully supervised methods while requiring only weak annotation. It uses a Mask-to-OBB loss and prompt-driven modules (PAFE, DBFE) to bridge the gap between full and weak supervision, validated across 13 datasets and 5 imaging modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pixel-level annotation is a major bottleneck in medical image segmentation; existing weak supervision methods are under-constrained and suffer from ambiguity, especially for elongated or anisotropic lesions.

Method: OBBSeg uses Oriented Bounding Boxes (OBBs) as intermediate supervision, a Mask-to-OBB differentiable loss for geometric consistency, and two prompt-driven modules (PAFE and DBFE) for foreground enhancement and background suppression.

Result: Outperforms existing weakly supervised methods and achieves performance comparable to fully supervised approaches across 13 datasets covering 5 imaging modalities.

Conclusion: OBBSeg demonstrates potential for efficient and scalable medical image segmentation by reducing annotation burden without sacrificing segmentation quality.

Abstract: Pixel-level annotation remains a major bottleneck in medical image segmentation, making weak supervision an attractive yet under-constrained alternative. We propose OBBSeg, an intermediate supervision paradigm guided by Oriented Bounding Boxes (OBBs) that bridges the gap between full and weak supervision. By jointly encoding spatial extent and orientation, OBBs provide compact geometric supervision that better aligns with elongated or anisotropic lesions, reducing the ambiguity of coarse box annotations. To mitigate the inherent rectangular bias of OBBs, we introduce a Mask-to-OBB loss, a differentiable formulation that enforces geometric consistency between predicted masks and OBB regions. Furthermore, we incorporate prompt-driven semantic guidance through two complementary modules-PAFE and DBFE-which enhance foreground representation and suppress background interference. Extensive experiments on 13 datasets across 5 imaging modalities show that OBBSeg not only outperforms existing weakly supervised methods but also achieves performance comparable to fully supervised approaches, demonstrating its potential for efficient and scalable medical image segmentation. The code is available at https://github.com/StarLxc3/OBBSeg.

</details>


### [48] [Structured Data Extraction from Real Estate Documents using Clustering, Classification, and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06012)
*Muhammad Assad Shehbaz,Carlos Francisco Moreno-García*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents an end-to-end pipeline that acquires, classifies, and extracts structured data from heterogeneous property questionnaire documents using reverse-engineered APIs and DeepSeek R1 LLM, successfully processing 2781 documents into 2766 unique property records with a Jaccard consistency score of 0.82.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real estate property listings expose structured metadata via APIs, but the richest property-level information (legal status, structural condition, utility supplies, heating systems) resides in heterogeneous questionnaire documents—some digitally generated, some scanned, some with complex checkbox annotations—that no automated system currently processes at scale.

Method: An end-to-end pipeline was developed: (1) 3965 questionnaire documents were collected from a live property platform via reverse-engineered REST APIs; (2) documents were classified into three structural categories (text_only, scanned, special_char); (3) 35 predefined property attributes were extracted from eligible documents using DeepSeek R1 as the LLM, prompted to return structured JSON objects.

Result: All 2781 submitted documents were processed successfully, producing a final dataset of 2766 unique property records. Cosine similarity matching achieved a Jaccard consistency score of 0.82, and K-Means clustering produced interpretable market segments with a silhouette score of 0.2088.

Conclusion: The proposed extraction from each property document is both feasible and reliable at scale, demonstrating that automated structured data extraction from heterogeneous real estate questionnaire documents is achievable.

Abstract: Real estate property listings expose structured metadata through the API. Still, the richest property-level information (i.e., legal status, structural condition, utility supplies, heating systems) sits in attached questionnaire documents that no automated system currently processes at scale. These documents are heterogeneous. Some are digitally generated with selectable text, others are scanned physical forms. There are even more complex layouts that contain checkbox annotations that defeat conventional text extraction. In this paper, we present an end-to-end pipeline for acquiring, classifying, and extracting structured data from selectable text documents. The pipeline was applied to 3965 questionnaire documents collected from a live property platform via reverse-engineered REST APIs. First, we classified each document into one of three structural categories (text_only, scanned, and special_char), then extracted 35 predefined property attributes from eligible documents using DeepSeek R1 as the Large Language Model, prompted to return a structured JSON object. All 2781 submitted documents were processed successfully, producing a final dataset of 2766 unique property records. Downstream validation confirmed the data quality. Cosine similarity matching achieves a Jaccard consistency score of 0.82, and K-Means clustering produces interpretable market segments with a silhouette score of 0.2088. Results show that the proposed extraction from each property document is both feasible and reliable at this scale.

</details>


### [49] [KOAL: Knowledge-Driven Prostate Cancer Grading with Ordinal-Aware Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06019)
*Zheng Guo,Jiaqi Cui,Haocheng Xiong,Jize Han,Bo Liu,Qianwen Zhang,Rui Chen,Yan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: KOAL is a framework for non-invasive Gleason Grade Group prediction from mpMRI that integrates clinical variables, LLM-derived expert knowledge, and hierarchical ordinal-aware learning to outperform existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GGG prediction methods overlook non-image information (age, PSA, radiology reports) and oversimplify GGG as flat labels without considering the intrinsic hierarchy of primary/secondary Gleason patterns.

Method: KOAL has three modules: (1) Clinical-Context Modulation (CCM) using clinical variables to modulate image representations; (2) Knowledge-Guided Prototype Alignment (KGPA) using LLM-extracted expert knowledge from radiology reports for prototype contrastive alignment; (3) Hierarchical Ordinal-aware Constraints (HOC) decoupling primary/secondary Gleason predictions via a Differentiable Bio-logic Mapping Layer (DBML).

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods on public PI-CAI and in-house datasets.

Conclusion: Incorporating clinical context, LLM-derived knowledge priors, and hierarchical ordinal constraints significantly improves non-invasive GGG prediction from mpMRI.

Abstract: Non-invasive prediction of Gleason Grade Group (GGG) in prostate cancer using multiparametric MRI (mpMRI) is clinically vital for reducing unnecessary biopsies. Existing GGG prediction methods face two major limitations. First, they often overlook non-image information critical for GGG prediction, including age, prostate-specific antigen (PSA), and expert priors embedded in radiology reports. Second, they tend to oversimplify GGG as flat categorical labels, failing to account for its intrinsic hierarchy of primary and secondary Gleason patterns. To this end, we propose a novel Knowledge-Driven Ordinal-Aware Learning (KOAL) framework with three synergistic modules. Specifically, the Clinical-Context Modulation (CCM) module uses clinical variables (e.g., age and PSA) to dynamically modulate discriminative image representations. The Knowledge-Guided Prototype Alignment (KGPA) module leverages an LLM to extract group-specific expert knowledge from training radiology reports and clinical guidelines, producing offline semantic anchors describing grade-specific radiological findings without requiring patient-specific reports at inference. Through prototype contrastive alignment, patient-specific mpMRI representations are matched with these anchors to promote pathology-aligned representation learning. The Hierarchical Ordinal-aware Constraints (HOC) module decouples primary and secondary Gleason pattern prediction and maps their probabilistic outputs to GGG via a Differentiable Bio-logic Mapping Layer (DBML), ensuring pathological grading consistency. Experiments on public PI-CAI and in-house datasets demonstrate that KOAL outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Code is available at: https://github.com/Gother-GZ/KOAL.

</details>


### [50] [Why does Deep Learning Improve Visual SLAM?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06023)
*Giovanni Cioffi,Davide Scaramuzza*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether the superior performance of deep learning-based Visual SLAM systems is driven by learned 2D data association, the combination of learned 2D data association and uncertainty, or the recurrent architecture, finding that success hinges on learned 2D data association and uncertainty rather than the recurrent architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While DL-based V-SLAM systems outperform classical geometry-based methods, it remains unclear which specific components (learned 2D data association, uncertainty, or recurrent architecture) are fundamentally responsible for this success.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled empirical study to isolate and evaluate the contribution of learned 2D data association, learned uncertainty, and recurrent architecture to overall V-SLAM performance.

Result: The success of DL-based V-SLAM systems is driven by learned 2D data association and uncertainty, not by the recurrent architecture.

Conclusion: Learning-based paradigms are necessary for designing 2D data association and uncertainty components in V-SLAM systems, underscoring the importance of these components over architectural choices like recurrence.

Abstract: Visual SLAM is a well-established technology utilized in a wide range of real-world applications. However, its performance still degrades under challenging visual conditions, such as low texture, severe motion blur, and poor illumination. Systems based on deep learning outperform classical geometry-based ones and achieve state-of-the-art results by combining learned 2D data association and uncertainty with differentiable geometric optimization in recurrent architectures. Still, it remains unclear exactly which components are fundamentally responsible for this success. In this paper, we ask: Is the superior performance of deep learning-based systems driven primarily by learned 2D data association, the combination of learned 2D data association and uncertainty, or the recurrent architecture itself? We investigate this question empirically by conducting a controlled study. Our findings reveal that the success of DL-based V-SLAM systems hinges on learned 2D data association and uncertainty rather than their recurrent architecture, underscoring the necessity of learning-based paradigms for the design of these components. Upon acceptance, the code will be released as open source.

</details>


### [51] [MSA-DCNN: A Data-Efficient Multi-Scale Deformable CNN for Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06083)
*Hamza Hussaini,Shahana Bano,Eyad Elyan,Carlos Francisco Moreno-García*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MSA-DCNN is a scale-consistent deformable attention learning framework that jointly handles multi-scale morphology and limited annotations via adaptive multi-scale sampling, within-scale saliency refinement, learned cross-scale fusion, and self-distillation, outperforming ViT/CNN/MICCAI baselines on medical image classification with fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning methods for medical image classification struggle with multi-scale morphology and limited annotations because DCN-based models lack multi-scale attention fusion and label-efficient regularisation, multi-scale architectures rely on static fusion, and semi-supervised methods ignore adaptive cross-scale representations. These challenges are only addressed in isolation.
