Motivation: Synthesizing hand motion that matches full body motion and semantic labels is difficult due to high degrees of freedom and lack of semantic labels in motion data.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) learns a generic body-hand kinematic prior from large-scale unstructured/unlabeled data via a streaming, autoregressive model; (2) introduces semantically-layered adapters on top of the frozen prior for lightweight, controllable semantic conditioning (self-supervised attribute and weakly-supervised text-driven control).

Result: Outperforms end-to-end conditioned baselines in kinematic plausibility, robustness, and controllability, especially in low-resource and cross-dataset settings; supports real-time inference and interactive authoring.

Conclusion: The prior-first, condition-second paradigm effectively decouples kinematic learning from semantic control, enabling practical, real-time hand motion synthesis for production animation pipelines with minimal labeled data.

Abstract: Synthesizing hand motion that matches the full body motion and the semantic labels is a difficult task due to their high degrees of freedom and the lack of semantic labels. To cope with this issue, we propose a prior-first, condition-second framework for body-conditioned hand motion completion. Our framework first learns a generic body-hand kinematic prior from large-scale unstructured and unlabeled motion data, capturing the intrinsic coordination between global body dynamics and hand articulation. Semantic control is then introduced through lightweight adaptation on top of the frozen prior, avoiding the need to relearn kinematic structure for each control interface. Our framework centers on a streaming, autoregressive body-hand prior that generates coherent, kinematically consistent hand motion from body dynamics in real time, using structured kinematic modeling to maintain mechanical body-hand coupling. To enable practical controllability under limited supervision, we introduce semantically-layered adapters that inject conditioning signals at appropriate kinematic levels, supporting both self-supervised attribute control and weakly supervised text-driven control with only a few hours of labeled data. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our framework improves kinematic plausibility, robustness, and controllability compared to end-to-end conditioned baselines, particularly in low-resource and cross-dataset settings. We further showcase real-time inference and an interactive authoring workflow, highlighting the applicability to production animation pipelines. Homepage: https://AIGAnimation.github.io/HandPrior/

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [101] [Statistically Meaningful Geometry and Gauge Symmetry Breaking: A Geometric Foundation for Scientific Discovery and Intelligence Emergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05436)
*Bing Cheng,Yi-Shuai Niu,Howell Tong,Shing-Tung Yau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents Statistically Meaningful Geometry (SMG), a framework modeling over-parameterized learning as infinite-dimensional Orlicz fiber bundles. It shows that under persistent OOD stimuli, unmodeled variance accumulates as Active Acausal Tension, leading to a conjugate focal boundary singularity that triggers a Gauge Symmetry Break (GSB). This phase transition yields a discrete +1.0 integer step-jump in Structural G-Entropy, enabling the differentiation between genuine causal discovery and malignant hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the crisis of whether large-scale over-parameterized models (e.g., LLMs) exhibit genuine intelligence or mere statistical pattern matching, by introducing a geometric framework that can distinguish continuous interpolation from autonomous discovery of novel causal laws.

Method: Non-parametric Orlicz fiber bundles, statistical manifold curvature analysis, conjugate focal boundary theory, Gauge Symmetry Break mechanics, Structural G-Entropy, Minimal Energy Path Criterion, and Causal Invariance Filter.

Result: Continuous optimization fails under OOD conditions; unmodeled variance leaks into the fiber space, generating tension that triggers a singularity at T_crit = π²/K_max. This induces a GSB, crystallizing a new horizontal coordinate axis with a discrete +1.0 entropy step-jump.

Conclusion: SMG provides a parameter-free, falsifiable dashboard for mathematically certifying true intelligence, transforming AI for Science into an engine of autonomous paradigm shifts by decoupling genuine discovery from malignant hallucinations.

Abstract: The rapid scaling of over-parameterized machine learning architectures, particularly LLMs, raises a profound crisis: do these systems exhibit genuine intelligence, or are they merely sophisticated statistical pattern matchers? Classical flat Euclidean statistics cannot differentiate continuous interpolation from the autonomous discovery of novel causal laws. To resolve this, we introduce Statistically Meaningful Geometry (SMG), a framework modeling over-parameterized learning systems as infinite-dimensional non-parametric Orlicz fiber bundles. We prove that under persistent out-of-distribution (OOD) stimuli governed by unmodeled causal mechanisms, continuous optimization fails. Unmodeled variance is rejected by the visible horizontal base manifold, leaking into the unobservable vertical fiber space and generating an accumulation of Active Acausal Tension. Driven by the statistical manifold's non-linear curvature, this tension inevitably strikes a conjugate focal boundary ($T_{\text{crit}} = π^2 / K_{\text{max}}$), triggering localized volumetric collapse and a catastrophic matrix singularity ($[G_f]^{-1} \to \infty$). We demonstrate this geometric breakdown acts as the strict non-equilibrium trigger for a Gauge Symmetry Break (GSB). The system purges hidden tension from unobservable gauge redundancies, spontaneously crystallizing a new, mathematically independent horizontal coordinate axis. This non-parametric phase transition registers as a discrete $+1.0$ integer step-jump in observable Structural G-Entropy. By decoupling parameter charts and subjecting emergent axes to a Minimal Energy Path Criterion and a Causal Invariance Filter, we distinguish genuine discovery from malignant hallucinations. Ultimately, SMG provides a parameter-free, falsifiable dashboard to mathematically certify true intelligence, transforming AI for Science into an engine of autonomous paradigm shifts.

</details>


### [102] [Design-CP: Context Parallelism for Design of Protein Nanoparticles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05439)
*Lorenzo Tarricone,Helen E. Eisenach,Aiko Muraishi,Charlotte M. Deane*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Design-CP introduces context-parallel inference strategies (1D row-sharding and 2D grid sharding with ring attention) for RFdiffusion 3 that distribute quadratic activations across multi-GPU meshes, enabling end-to-end all-atom design of large protein assemblies like icosahedral and octahedral nanoparticles on small GPU clusters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing all-atom generative protein models face GPU memory bottlenecks when modelling large multimeric complexes due to quadratic token- and atom-pair representations that exceed single-GPU memory as chain and residue counts grow.

Method: Two context-parallel (CP) inference strategies for RFdiffusion 3: (1) 1D row-sharding and (2) 2D grid sharding with ring attention, which distribute quadratic activations across a multi-GPU mesh while preserving pretrained weights.

Result: Maximum feasible asymmetric subunit (ASU) size scales with square-root trend in GPU count; 2D sharding achieves better wall-clock scaling; strong point-group symmetry constraints enable out-of-the-box all-atom design of icosahedral nanoparticles with favourable in silico metrics; octahedral nanoparticle design demonstrated on small 16GB GPU clusters.

Conclusion: Design-CP provides a practical path towards democratising large-assembly protein design by enabling end-to-end, all-atom design of large symmetric protein nanoparticles on modest GPU hardware.

Abstract: Many all-atom generative protein models can in principle design large multimeric complexes by jointly modelling all chains, but their quadratic token- and atom-pair representations quickly exceed single-GPU memory as the number of chains and residues modelled grows. We introduce Design-CP, two context-parallel (CP) inference strategies for RFdiffusion 3 (1D row-sharding and 2D grid sharding with ring attention) that distribute the quadratic activations across a multi-GPU mesh while preserving pretrained weights. We characterise their scaling when sampling icosahedral assemblies, showing that the maximum feasible asymmetric subunit (ASU) size grows with the expected square-root trend in GPU count and that 2D sharding achieves better wall-clock scaling. Moreover, we show how strong point-group symmetry constraints make CP usable out of the box for end-to-end, all-atom design of icosahedral nanoparticles, yielding favourable in silico structural and interface metrics. Finally, we demonstrate octahedral nanoparticle design on a small cluster of workstation-grade 16GB GPUs, illustrating how Design-CP can be a practical path towards democratising large-assembly protein design.

</details>


### [103] [Geometry-Aware Infrastructure-Anchored Denoiser for UWB Sensing and Work-Zone Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05449)
*Weizhe Tang,Jiaxi Liu,Junwei you,Steven T. Parker,Pei Li,Sikai Chen,Meng Ran,Bin Ran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAIA is a geometry-aware, infrastructure-anchored learning framework for denoising outdoor UWB ranging data, achieving the lowest range MSE and highest polygon IoU for work-zone geometry reconstruction, outperforming baselines like PoseMLP by 18.4% in MSE reduction and 15.5% in polygon IoU improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate work-zone geometry perception is critical for intelligent transportation systems, but outdoor UWB ranging suffers from non-line-of-sight propagation, burst noise, and long-tail errors that distort downstream spatial reconstruction. Existing methods lack geometry-aware denoising tailored for infrastructure-aided reconstruction.

Method: GAIA couples temporal range modeling with latent anchor-layout estimation and deterministic distance projection, preserving range denoising as the supervised task while orienting learned distances toward boundary-consistent reconstruction.

Result: GAIA achieves the lowest overall range MSE and highest polygon IoU among evaluated baselines, reducing MSE by 18.4% and improving polygon IoU by 15.5% over PoseMLP on a real-world outdoor UWB dataset with synchronized UWB, GNSS, and IMU measurements, further validated on a real-data-calibrated stress-test simulator.

Conclusion: Geometry-aware range denoising provides an effective path toward spatially coherent work-zone reconstruction, demonstrating that coupling denoising with latent geometric constraints significantly improves reconstruction quality over purely filtering-based or learning-based approaches.

Abstract: Accurate work-zone geometry perception is critical for intelligent transportation systems, and ultra-wideband sensing offers a low-cost approach for infrastructure-aided reconstruction. However, outdoor UWB ranging is often degraded by non-line-of-sight propagation, burst noise, and long-tail errors, which can distort downstream spatial reconstruction. We present GAIA, a geometry-aware, infrastructure-anchored learning framework that couples temporal range modeling with latent anchor-layout estimation and deterministic distance projection. GAIA preserves range denoising as the supervised task while orienting the learned distances toward boundary-consistent reconstruction. We evaluate GAIA on a real-world outdoor UWB dataset with synchronized UWB, GNSS, and IMU measurements, and further test robustness using a real-data-calibrated stress-test simulator. GAIA achieves the lowest overall range MSE and highest polygon IoU among evaluated filtering-based and learning-based baselines, reducing MSE by 18.4% and improving polygon IoU by 15.5% over PoseMLP. These results show that geometry-aware range denoising provides an effective path toward spatially coherent work-zone reconstruction.

</details>


### [104] [The Granularity Paradox: How Temporal Disaggregation Inflates In-Sample Fit and Compounds Out-of-Sample Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05450)
*Hugo Moreira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the "Granularity Paradox" in time-series forecasting, where finer temporal disaggregation improves in-sample diagnostics but degrades out-of-sample accuracy due to recursive error compounding. Benchmarking 10 models across 6 granularities on procurement data reveals non-monotonic threshold structures, with LSTM overcoming the paradox at daily granularity, while Linear Regression remains stable. Standard pointwise metrics mask cumulative error propagation, motivating a consensus-dissensus diagnostic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize and empirically demonstrate the "Granularity Paradox"—the trade-off where finer temporal disaggregation increases dataset size but introduces recursive error compounding over longer forecast horizons, while coarser aggregation reduces error propagation but limits available data.

Method: Benchmarking 10 models (naïve, statistical, ML, and deep learning) across six temporal granularities using a 13-year public procurement dataset, with analysis of pointwise metrics (RMSE, MAE) versus cumulative metrics (TPFE), and introduction of a consensus-dissensus diagnostic.

Result: Recursive autoregressive and seasonal models degrade severely at high-frequency (Holt-Winters: R²=-151, TPFE=425.85% at Daily). LSTM shows a U-shaped error curve, improving at Daily (TPFE=4.35%, R²=0.66). Linear Regression remains stable across all granularities (16.3-17.0% TPFE). Standard metrics mask cumulative error propagation.

Conclusion: The Granularity Paradox is driven by recursive feedback topology, not model complexity. Pointwise metrics systematically underreport cumulative errors, and evaluating forecasts without goal-dependent cumulative metrics yields misleading assessments of model adequacy.

Abstract: This paper explores the "Granularity Paradox" in time-series forecasting, wherein finer temporal disaggregation (e.g., Monthly to Weekly/Daily) improves in-sample diagnostics and dataset size (N), but degrades out-of-sample accuracy due to recursive error compounding over longer horizons (H). Conversely, coarse aggregation (Annual) eliminates recursive error propagation but reduces data available to estimators. We formalize this trade-off and benchmark 10 models - spanning naïve, statistical, machine learning, and deep learning architectures - across six granularities using a 13-year public procurement dataset. The empirical results reveal a non-monotonic threshold structure: recursive autoregressive and seasonal models degrade substantially under high-frequency forecasting (e.g., Holt-Winters reaches a Test R-squared of -151 and TPFE of 425.85% at the Daily grain), while the LSTM traces a U-shaped error curve, worsening from Monthly (19.66%) through Bi-Weekly (35.94%) before overcoming the error propagation penalty at Daily (TPFE of 4.35%, R-squared of 0.66). Linear Regression remains stable across all granularities (16.3-17.0% TPFE), confirming that the paradox is driven by recursive feedback topology, not model complexity. The results demonstrate that standard pointwise metrics (RMSE, MAE) systematically mask cumulative error propagation, and that evaluating forecasts without goal-dependent cumulative metrics produces misleading assessments of model adequacy. We introduce a consensus-dissensus diagnostic comparing the directional behaviour of pointwise metrics against cumulative TPFE across granularities, enabling the identification of models whose standard diagnostics mask systematic error propagation.

</details>


### [105] [Exogenous Dropout: A Simple, Strong Baseline for Corruption-Robust Time Series Forecasting with Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05452)
*Hao Hu,Xue-shan Ai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exogenous dropout, a simple training intervention that randomly zeros exogenous channels, robustly improves time series forecasting under covariate corruption (noise, misalignment, missing data) without sacrificing clean accuracy, outperforming even deliberately bounded architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series forecasters using exogenous covariates are brittle in deployment when covariates are noised, misaligned, or missing; the authors investigate whether robustness requires specialized architectures or can be achieved via a simple training intervention.

Method: Proposes exogenous dropout, a model-agnostic method that randomly zeros entire exogenous channels during training. Evaluated on electricity-price forecasting, reservoir hydrology, and meteorology tasks under Gaussian noise, temporal misalignment, and missing channels. Compared against BoundEx (a bounded architectural foil with learnable gate, fallback residual, and FiLM modulation).

Result: Exogenous dropout substantially improves robustness under all corruption types while preserving clean accuracy. Applied to a dual-correlation network, it yields the most robust model, outperforming the bounded BoundEx architecture. Explicit architectural boundedness is unnecessary; an unbounded model with exogenous dropout is more robust than the bounded model in every domain.

Conclusion: Architectural boundedness is not required for robustness against covariate corruption; exogenous dropout is a simple, strong baseline recommended for future time series forecasting with covariates. The authors release a corruption-robustness benchmark.

Abstract: Time series forecasters that use exogenous covariates are fragile in deployment: when those covariates are noised, temporally misaligned, or missing, strong exogenous-fusion and exogenous-adapted models can degrade far above the endogenous-only floor. We study whether such robustness requires specialized architectures, or whether it can be obtained through a simple training intervention. We propose exogenous dropout, a model-agnostic method that randomly zeros whole exogenous channels during training. Across electricity-price forecasting, reservoir hydrology, and meteorology, exogenous dropout substantially improves robustness under Gaussian noise, temporal misalignment, and fully missing channels, while preserving clean accuracy. Applied to a dual-correlation network, it yields the most robust model in our experiments, outperforming a deliberately strong bounded architectural foil, BoundEx, which combines a learnable gate, a fallback residual to the endogenous backbone, and per-channel exogenous FiLM modulation. Architecture-by-dropout ablations, gate-behavior diagnostics, and a representation-level bound show that explicit architectural boundedness is not necessary for this robustness: an unbounded model trained with exogenous dropout is more robust than the bounded model in every domain. We release a corruption-robustness benchmark and recommend exogenous dropout as a simple, strong baseline for future work on time series forecasting with covariates.

</details>


### [106] [Empirical Minimal-Realisation Compression of Deep Neural Networks via Controllability-Observability Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05457)
*Anis Hamadouche,Amir Hussain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a controllability-observability framework for reducing hidden-state redundancy in deep neural networks by treating them as depth-indexed nonlinear dynamical systems, constructing Gramians from hidden states and Jacobians, and using the resulting balanced ranks to determine compressed layer widths. Experiments show ~73% parameter compression on MNIST and ~83% on CIFAR-10 with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks contain substantial hidden-state redundancy, but existing compression methods (weight pruning, quantization, SVD) do not explicitly characterize the dynamical role of internal states. A principled framework for identifying and removing redundant hidden states is lacking.

Method: The authors view a trained DNN as a depth-indexed nonlinear dynamical system, construct data-driven reachability, observability, and balanced Gramians from hidden-state snapshots and output Jacobians, compute layer-wise reachable/observable/joint ranks, and use these ranks as compressed layer widths for reduced networks via balanced realization.

Result: On MNIST: 4-layer SiLU DNN reduced from state order 1024→277 (72.95% state compression, 73.48% parameter compression), maintaining 95.45% vs 96.60% accuracy. On CIFAR-10: state order 4608→1339 (70.94% state, 83.09% parameter compression), preserving 54.44% vs 54.45% accuracy with ~3X CUDA latency reduction.

Conclusion: Balanced reachable-observable ranks provide a principled empirical minimal-realisation criterion for designing compact neural architectures with little or no accuracy loss, outperforming projection-based reduction, pruning, SVD, and quantization baselines.

Abstract: Deep neural networks often contain substantial hidden-state redundancy, but most compression methods operate directly on weights, neurons, or quantised representations without explicitly characterising the dynamical role of internal states. This paper proposes a controllability-observability framework for empirical state-order reduction of deep neural networks. By viewing a trained network as a depth-indexed nonlinear dynamical system, we construct data-driven reachability, observability, and balanced Gramians from hidden-state snapshots and output Jacobians. The resulting A/B/C tests estimate layer-wise reachable, observable, and jointly reachable--observable ranks. These ranks are then used not only as diagnostic measures of hidden-state redundancy, but also as actual compressed layer widths for realised reduced networks. Experiments on MNIST and CIFAR-10 compare the proposed balanced realisation against projection-based reduction, unstructured pruning, structured pruning, low-rank SVD, dynamic INT8 quantisation, and linear baselines. On MNIST, a four-layer SiLU DNN is reduced from state order 1024 to 277, giving 72.95% state compression and 73.48% parameter compression, while maintaining 95.45% accuracy compared with 96.60% for the full model. On CIFAR-10, a larger SiLU DNN is reduced from state order 4608 to 1339, giving 70.94% state compression and 83.09% parameter compression, while preserving accuracy from 54.45% to 54.44% and reducing CUDA inference latency by approximately 3X. The results show that balanced reachable-observable ranks provide a principled empirical minimal-realisation criterion for designing compact neural architectures with little or no loss in accuracy.

</details>


### [107] [Learning to Control LLM Agent Harnesses with Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05458)
*Haiwen Yi,Xinyuan Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The execution harness around a frozen LLM agent can be treated as a learnable control layer. A lightweight controller trained via advantage-weighted regression on offline rollouts selects structural execution actions, improving verification behavior and final task quality in controlled domains and benchmarks, with gains limited by offline data support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent pipelines treat the execution harness (e.g., control flow, error handling, verification loops) as fixed infrastructure. This paper argues that the harness is itself a learnable layer that can be optimized separately from the LLM, enabling better agent behavior without modifying the model or prompts.

Method: Formalizes harness operation as a finite-horizon Harness MDP, where a lightweight controller selects structural execution actions (e.g., verify, check, retry) while the LLM executor remains frozen. The controller is trained via advantage-weighted regression from offline rollouts using only terminal task-rubric rewards. Introduces Harness Maturity Score (HMS) to decouple process quality from final answer correctness.

Result: Learned controller consistently improves verification behavior and selectively improves final task quality across six controlled domains and two public-benchmark adapters (tau-bench retail, AgentBench DB-Bench). Coding tasks with a calibrated structural verifier show largest gains. Ablations confirm improvements are not from imitation learning or simply adding checks.

Conclusion: Harness control is a learnable layer for frozen LLM agents. Offline data support limits when better process control translates into better final answers, revealing a finite-buffer perspective on harness learning.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are usually improved by changing prompts, models, or hand-written workflows, while the execution harness around the model is treated as fixed infrastructure. We argue that this harness is itself a learnable control layer. We formalize harness operation as a finite-horizon Harness MDP, where a lightweight controller selects structural execution actions while the LLM executor remains frozen. The controller is trained from offline rollouts using advantage-weighted regression with only terminal task-rubric rewards. We also separate final task quality from a post-hoc Harness Maturity Score, which measures whether the harness follows reliable execution patterns rather than only whether the final answer is correct. This separation gives a finite-buffer view of harness learning: final-quality gains require high-return support in the offline buffer, while process behavior can shift whenever it aligns with advantage-weighted actions. Across six controlled domains and two public-benchmark adapters, the learned controller consistently improves verification behavior and selectively improves final task quality, with the largest gains on adapted tau-bench retail, adapted AgentBench DB-Bench, and coding with a calibrated structural verifier. Ablations against behavior cloning and Forced CHECK show that the gains are not explained by imitation or by simply adding checks. These results identify harness control as a learnable layer for frozen LLM agents, while showing that offline support limits when better process control becomes better final answers.

</details>


### [108] [AdaStop: Cost-Aware Early Stopping for DNN Test Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05461)
*Bonan Shen,Wei-Jung Huang,Xin Liu,Jiazhou Gao,Tao Ning*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaStop is a framework that solves the stopping problem in DNN testing by treating it as a cost-benefit decision process. It stops labeling when the estimated marginal fault discovery rate falls below the cost/value threshold, achieving 65-84% fault discovery using only 9-31% of the labeling budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DNN testing methods prioritize fault-revealing inputs under a fixed labeling budget, but choosing the right budget is difficult—too little testing misses failures, too much incurs unnecessary costs. There is no principled way to determine when to stop testing.

Method: The authors formulate DNN testing as a cost-benefit decision process where labeling costs $c$ and fault discovery yields value $v$. They introduce AdaStop, which estimates the marginal fault discovery rate during testing and stops labeling when this rate falls below threshold $\tau = c/v$.

Result: Across multiple datasets, architectures, and selection strategies, AdaStop discovers 65-84% of faults using only 9-31% of the labeling budget.

Conclusion: AdaStop provides a principled, adaptive stopping criterion for DNN testing that balances fault discovery against labeling cost, significantly reducing unnecessary labeling while still capturing the majority of faults.

Abstract: Existing methods for testing deep neural networks (DNNs) primarily prioritize test inputs likely to reveal model faults under a fixed labeling budget. In practice, choosing that budget is difficult: too little testing misses failures, while too much incurs unnecessary labeling costs. This work studies the stopping problem in DNN testing. We formulate testing as a cost--benefit decision process in which labeling an input incurs cost $c$ and discovering a fault yields value $v$. Based on this formulation, we introduce \textit{AdaStop}, a framework that estimates the marginal fault discovery rate during testing and stops labeling when the estimated rate falls below the threshold $τ= c/v$. Experiments across multiple datasets, architectures, and selection strategies show that $65$--$84\%$ of faults can be discovered using only $9$--$31\%$ of the labeling budget.

</details>


### [109] [Learnable Weighting of Intra-Attribute Distances for Categorical Data Clustering with Nominal and Ordinal Attributes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05464)
*Yiqun Zhang,Yiu-ming Cheung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified graph-based distance metric for categorical data clustering that distinguishes nominal from ordinal attributes and jointly optimizes distance weights and data partitioning to avoid suboptimal solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing categorical clustering methods treat nominal and ordinal attributes identically, ignoring the ordinal order information and the interdependence between attribute types, which leads to suboptimal dissimilarity measurement.

Method: A graph-based distance metric that unifies intra-attribute distance computation for both nominal and ordinal values while preserving ordinal relationships, integrated into a clustering algorithm that jointly learns distance weights and partitions data in a single paradigm.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed algorithm compared to existing clustering methods.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling nominal-ordinal distinctions and distance weight learning within a single optimization framework improves categorical clustering performance.

Abstract: The success of categorical data clustering generally much relies on the distance metric that measures the dissimilarity degree between two objects. However, most of the existing clustering methods treat the two categorical subtypes, i.e. nominal and ordinal attributes, in the same way when calculating the dissimilarity without considering the relative order information of the ordinal values. Moreover, there would exist interdependence among the nominal and ordinal attributes, which is worth exploring for indicating the dissimilarity. This paper will therefore study the intrinsic difference and connection of nominal and ordinal attribute values from a perspective akin to the graph. Accordingly, we propose a novel distance metric to measure the intra-attribute distances of nominal and ordinal attributes in a unified way, meanwhile preserving the order relationship among ordinal values. Subsequently, we propose a new clustering algorithm to make the learning of intra-attribute distance weights and partitions of data objects into a single learning paradigm rather than two separate steps, whereby circumventing a suboptimal solution. Experiments show the efficacy of the proposed algorithm in comparison with the existing counterparts.

</details>


### [110] [Breaking Structural Isolation: Scalable Graph Clustering via Community-Aware Sampling and Structural Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05469)
*Jingyun Zhang,Hao Peng,Jianxin Li,Angsheng Li,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCISE is a scalable unsupervised graph clustering framework that addresses the "structural isolation" issue in mini-batch Graph Contrastive Learning by combining community-aware sampling, constrained Structural Entropy optimization, and structural contrastive learning, achieving state-of-the-art results on six benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised graph clustering methods using Graph Contrastive Learning suffer from "structural isolation" during mini-batch training, which makes it difficult to capture cohesive community structures and global topological distributions in large-scale networks.

Method: SCISE introduces three key components: (1) Structural Entropy Community Constraint operator (SECC) to mitigate community fragmentation via constrained structural entropy optimization; (2) Community-Aware Sampling Expansion (CSampE) mechanism to incorporate community context into sampling batches; (3) Structural Contrastive Learning (StructCL) module to refine edge weights using intra-batch structural similarity for higher-order representation learning.

Result: Extensive experiments on six mainstream benchmark datasets show SCISE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art algorithms. Ablation studies and robustness analyses further validate its effectiveness and reliability for real-world large-scale graphs.

Conclusion: SCISE effectively preserves structural integrity in unsupervised graph clustering by synergizing community-aware sampling with constrained Structural Entropy, overcoming the limitations of prior contrastive learning approaches for large-scale graphs.

Abstract: Unsupervised graph clustering is a fundamental technique for uncovering underlying semantic patterns in large-scale networks. Although Graph Contrastive Learning has demonstrated promising performance, existing methods often suffer from the "structural isolation" issue during mini-batch training, making it challenging to capture cohesive community structures that characterize the global topological distribution. To address these challenges, we propose SCISE, a Scalable unsupervised graph Clustering framework that preserves structural Integrity by synergizing community-aware sampling with constrained Structural Entropy. Specifically, we first introduce the Structural Entropy Community Constraint operator (SECC), which optimizes structural information within a constrained solution space to mitigate community fragmentation and enhance partition cohesion. Second, to prevent global information loss during batch training, we design a Community-Aware Sampling Expansion (CSampE) mechanism that incorporates the community context of target nodes into sampling batches, effectively breaking structural barriers and preserving topological integrity. Finally, we devise a Structural Contrastive Learning (StructCL) module that refines edge weights based on intra-batch structural similarity, guiding the encoder to learn representations in a higher-order structural space. Extensive experiments on six mainstream benchmark datasets demonstrate that SCISE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art algorithms, with ablation studies and robustness analyses further validating its effectiveness and reliability for real-world large-scale graphs.

</details>


### [111] [Parameter-Free Encoders Remain Viable for RDB Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05476)
*Linjie Xu,David Wipf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether parameter-free or parameterized encoders are more effective for predicting missing values in relational databases (RDBs) without retraining new models for each task, finding that simpler parameter-free encoders achieve competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To avoid learning a new model from scratch for each new prediction task in enterprise RDB settings, as the space of potential targets is vast. The ambiguity between parameter-free vs. parameterized RDB encoders needs resolution.

Method: The authors analyze RDB encoder properties when labels are present as inputs, proving theoretical limitations on the efficacy of trainable encoder parameters, and empirically validate that simpler parameter-free encoders achieve strong performance across benchmarking tasks.

Result: Parameter-free encoders are capable of strong performance across many relevant benchmarking tasks, comparable to or exceeding parameterized alternatives.

Conclusion: Parameter-free subgraph encoders with single-table foundation models can achieve near SOTA performance without RDB-specific pre-training, challenging the need for complex parameterized encoders.

Abstract: Given a relational database (RDB) storing heterogeneous tabular information, how can we predict missing (or future) values in some target column of interest? As the space of potential targets is vast across enterprise settings, it is preferable to avoid learning a new model from scratch each time there is a new prediction task. Frozen foundation models based on RDB-specific encoders provide a viable solution, but ideal design remains an open question. On the one hand, it has recently been argued that certain parameter-free subgraph encoders combined with single-table foundation models can achieve near SOTA performance, with no RDB-specific pre-training required. Meanwhile, other contemporary studies advocate for parameterized encoders pre-trained to exploit observable labels for learning task-specific representations. To address this ambiguity, we analyze RDB encoder properties specifically when labels are present as inputs, proving limitations on the potential efficacy of trainable encoder parameters. As empirical validation, we demonstrate that considerably simpler parameter-free encoders are still capable of strong performance across many relevant benchmarking tasks.

</details>


### [112] [InvWeaver: Deductive Feedback for Invariant Synthesis in Interacting-Loop Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05478)
*Guangyuan Wu,Weining Cao,Zehui Tan,Yuan Yao,Hengfeng Wei,Taolue Chen,Xiaoxing Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: InvWeaver is a neuro-symbolic framework for loop invariant inference that handles multi-loop programs by modeling inter-loop dependencies, combining loop abstraction, obligation-guided inference, and weakest-precondition refinement. It solves 72/82 multi-loop benchmarks and maintains strong performance on single-loop tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-aided invariant inference techniques perform well on single-loop programs but struggle with programs containing multiple interacting loops due to complex inter-loop dependencies.

Method: InvWeaver uses a neuro-symbolic framework combining: (1) loop-level abstraction to expose inter-loop dependencies, (2) obligation-guided inference driven by proof obligations, and (3) weakest-precondition-based refinement to propagate obligations across loops.

Result: Evaluated on a comprehensive benchmark suite (including a new multi-loop dataset from classic algorithms), InvWeaver solves 72 out of 82 multi-loop problems and retains strong performance on single-loop tasks, outperforming existing invariant inference methods.

Conclusion: The neuro-symbolic approach of modeling and propagating inter-loop dependencies significantly advances invariant synthesis for multi-loop programs, setting a new state-of-the-art.

Abstract: Loop invariant inference is a fundamental yet challenging problem in program verification. Recent LLM-aided guess-and-check techniques have shown strong performance on single-loop programs, but they often struggle with programs containing multiple interacting loops. This paper presents InvWeaver, a neuro-symbolic framework for synthesizing invariants for such programs. The key idea is to expose inter-loop dependencies and propagate proof obligations through a combination of loop-level abstraction, obligation-guided inference, and weakest-precondition-based refinement. We evaluate InvWeaver on a comprehensive benchmark suite, including a newly curated dataset derived from classic algorithms. Experimental results show that InvWeaver substantially outperforms existing invariant inference methods, solving 72 out of 82 multi-loop benchmark problems and maintaining strong performance on single-loop tasks.

</details>


### [113] [PatchOptic for Shared-State LLM Workflows with Projected Views and Verified Structured Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05483)
*Zhaoyu Bai,Jiaqi Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PatchOptic introduces an optic-inspired interface for shared-state LLM workflows that enforces contracts between local updates and global validity through projected reads and verified structured patches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing progressive disclosure methods (keyword search, RAG, AST queries, agent skills) manage the read side of shared state but lack a mechanism to verify when locally proposed rewrites are valid when applied back to the full state—there is no contract between local updates and global validity.

Method: PatchOptic borrows the view/update intuition from optics (compositional bidirectional accessors) and realizes it through projected reads and verified structured patches. Each workflow step declares a projected read view, an authorized write region, and a patch-source region. This declaration yields a path-level footprint supporting delegation, sub-workflow composition, and static certificates for reordering independent steps.

Result: Evaluated with PatchBench (46 cases across domains). Projected reads reduce reported leakage and token cost while preserving accepted-output quality under the strong actor. Runtime verification blocks declared workflow-contract violations before commit. Patch-read enforcement rejects compromised patch artifacts using hidden sources.

Conclusion: PatchOptic provides a formal contract between local updates and global validity in LLM agentic workflows, improving safety and efficiency through projected reads and verified patches.

Abstract: Agentic workflows often operate over shared, structured state. Because LLM context windows are limited, each model invocation is typically shown only the state fragment needed for the current workflow step, a pattern commonly known as progressive disclosure. Modern systems construct such model-facing views using grep-like keyword search, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), abstract-syntax-tree (AST) queries, and task-specific agent skills. These methods make the read side manageable, but they do not define when a locally proposed rewrite is valid after it is applied back to the full state. The missing piece is a contract between local updates and global validity. We introduce PatchOptic, an optic-inspired interface for shared-state LLM workflows. Optics are compositional bidirectional accessors that describe how views of structured data are read and updated. PatchOptic borrows this view/update intuition and realizes it through projected reads and verified structured patches. Each workflow step declares a projected read view, an authorized write region, and a patch-source region. Beyond runtime enforcement, the same declaration yields a path-level footprint that supports delegation, sub-workflow composition, and static certificates for reordering independent steps within the same phase. We evaluate this design with PatchBench, a benchmark with 46 cases across domains. The results show that projected reads reduce reported leakage and token cost while preserving accepted-output quality under the strong actor. Runtime verification blocks declared workflow-contract violations before commit, and patch-read enforcement rejects compromised patch artifacts that use hidden sources.

</details>


### [114] [$\mathbfλ$-VAE: Variance Equalization for Posterior Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05531)
*Girum Demisse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VAEs suffer from posterior collapse due to two causes—gradient imbalance and information gap—which the authors unify theoretically. They propose λ-VAE, which scales sampling noise per dimension while keeping the KL penalty unchanged, achieving variance equalization and reducing collapsed dimensions across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Posterior collapse in VAEs has remained an open problem without a unified theoretical account. The authors aim to identify and formalize the underlying causes and propose a principled solution.

Method: The authors formalize two causes: (1) gradient imbalance (reconstruction signal vanishes faster than KL pressure), and (2) information gap (stochastic sampling discards encoder information, attenuating decoder sensitivity). They show both are algebraically equivalent to aggregate posterior-prior mismatch. They propose λ-VAE, which scales sampling noise by a per-dimension exponent while keeping the KL penalty based on original variance, creating asymmetry that drives variance equalization toward a non-collapsed equilibrium.

Result: On Binary MNIST, Binary Omniglot, CIFAR-10, and CelebA-64, λ-VAE shows consistent reductions in collapsed dimensions, information capacity gains up to 2.8× nats, and reconstruction quality improvements up to +0.33 BPD.

Conclusion: Posterior collapse stems from two coupled causes that can be unified theoretically. λ-VAE resolves both via a single modification to the reparameterization step, with a closed-form optimal exponent derived from a net information gain objective.

Abstract: Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) frequently suffer from posterior collapse, a failure mode in which the approximate posterior converges to the prior, rendering the latent code uninformative. Despite extensive research, a unified account of why collapse occurs has remained an open question. We identify and formalize two logically independent but coupled causes. \emph{Gradient imbalance} occurs when the decoder's reconstruction signal vanishes faster than the $\mathbb{KL}$ regularization pressure as the posterior widens. \emph{Information gap} occurs when the stochastic sampling step discards a substantial fraction of the encoder's computed representation, attenuating decoder sensitivity and making collapse inexpensive. Both causes share the same collapse trajectory, and we show that the information gap is algebraically equivalent to mismatch between the aggregate posterior and the prior, unifying two pathologies. Subsequently, we introduce $λ$-VAE, which resolves both causes through a single modification to the reparameterization step: the sampling noise is scaled by per-dimension exponent, while the $\mathbb{KL}$ penalty retains the original posterior variance. This asymmetry shifts the stable training attractor away from the degenerate collapsed state, driving all latent dimensions toward the same equilibrium -- a mechanism we term \emph{variance equalization}. A closed-form optimal exponent per dimension follows from a net information gain objective, with a single hyperparameter controlling the reconstruction-generation tradeoff. We validate on standard benchmarks (Binary MNIST, Binary Omniglot, CIFAR-10, CelebA-64), showing consistent reductions in collapsed dimensions, information capacity gains of up to $2.8\times$ nats, and reconstruction quality improvements of up to $+0.33$ BPD.

</details>


### [115] [Self-Review Reinforcement Learning (SRRL) with Cross-Episode Memory and Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05541)
*Muhammad Zain Amin,Kibele Sebnem Yildirim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SRRL embeds explicit self-review steps into RL episodes, using policy gradients and selective distillation to transform failures into improved responses, outperforming standard RLVR on GSM8K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL for LLMs suffers from sparse/delayed feedback, making it hard for models to identify which reasoning steps caused success or failure, limiting effective learning from environmental signals.

Method: SRRL adds a self-review step after failed responses to analyze errors and condition improved second attempts. It uses policy gradients for optimization and selective distillation to internalize improvements into the base policy. A cross-episode memory stores successful self-reviews for reuse on similar tasks during training.

Result: On GSM8K benchmark with Qwen 3-4B and OLMo-3-7B using GRPO optimizer, SRRL consistently achieves higher final reward and greater learning efficiency than the standard RLVR baseline.

Conclusion: SRRL effectively transforms sparse feedback into behavioral improvements, outperforming standard RLVR and demonstrating that explicit self-review with policy gradient optimization improves both performance and learning efficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning is commonly used to train large language models using environmental feedback. In applied settings, the environment usually provides sparse or delayed feedback. This makes it difficult for the model to pinpoint which actions in its reasoning led to success or failure. So, learning effectively from these signals is hard because the model must determine how each failure should inform meaningful behavioral corrections in subsequent iterations. We introduce a training framework, Self-Review Reinforcement Learning, that embeds an explicit self-review step into each RL episode. When a first-pass response fails, the model generates a self-review to identify what went wrong, which conditions an improved second attempt. Unlike inference-time reflection approaches, such as Reflexion, the framework optimizes self-review with policy gradients and internalizes improvements into the base policy via selective distillation, ensuring they persist across future episodes. A cross-episode memory keeps successful self-reviews for reuse when encountering similar tasks in future episodes during training. We evaluate SRRL against a standard RLVR baseline using the GRPO optimizer across two language models, Qwen 3-4B and OLMo-3- 7B, on GSM8K benchmark. SRRL consistently outperforms the RLVR in final reward performance and achieves greater learning efficiency by successfully transforming feedback into behavioral improvement.

</details>


### [116] [Federated Physics-Grounded Reinforcement Learning for Distributed Stability Control in Smart Grids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05553)
*Omar Al-Refai,Ibrahim Shahbaz,Adam Ali Husseinat,Eman Hammad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedPPO-PG is a federated multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for transient stability control in smart grids. It uses physics-grounded neighborhoods, guided policy initialization from classical controllers, and CTDE paradigm. On IEEE 39-bus system, it achieves 100% stabilization, reduces mean stability time by 72.4%, and cuts control power by 7-14x with real-time inference compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transient stability control in smart grids requires rapid post-fault damping of generator frequency and rotor angle deviations to prevent cascading failures, motivating a learning-based approach that directly optimizes against closed-loop stability objectives.

Method: Proposes FedPPO-PG: a Federated Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization framework with Physics-Grounded neighborhoods. Each generator has a local actor augmented with frequency deviations of its two most strongly coupled electrical neighbors (from Kron-reduced susceptance matrix). Uses guided policy initialization from classical decentralized controller, centralized critic for advantage estimation under CTDE paradigm.

Result: On IEEE 39-bus benchmark across five training and three unseen fault contingencies, achieves 100% stabilization in 24 trials, reduces mean stability time by 72.4%, and cuts control power by 7-14x compared to centralized baseline. Per-actor inference latency satisfies IEEE/IEC 60255-118-1-2018 real-time reporting requirements.

Conclusion: FedPPO-PG effectively provides decentralized, real-time transient stability control with no central coordinator at deployment, outperforming centralized baselines in stabilization rate, speed, and power efficiency.

Abstract: Transient stability control in smart grids requires rapid post-fault damping of generator frequency and rotor angle deviations to prevent cascading failures. This paper proposes FedPPO-PG, a Federated Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization framework with Physics-Grounded neighborhoods, which reformulates transient stability control as a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning problem optimized directly against closed-loop stability objectives. Each generator hosts an independent local actor augmented with the frequency deviations of its two most strongly coupled electrical neighbors, identified from the post-fault Kron-reduced susceptance matrix. A guided policy initialization phase warm-starts all actors from the classical decentralized controller, while a centralized critic guides advantage estimation under the centralized training--decentralized execution (CTDE) paradigm. Evaluated on a simulation of the IEEE 39-bus benchmark system across five training and three unseen fault contingencies, FedPPO-PG achieves 100% stabilization in all 24 trials, reduces mean stability time by 72.4%, and cuts the control power by 7-14 times compared to the centralized baseline. Each actor executes independently with no central coordinator at deployment, and the per-actor inference latency satisfies the IEEE/IEC 60255-118-1-2018 real-time reporting requirements.

</details>


### [117] [EquiFiLM: Charge-Conditioned Equivariant Force Fields via Feature-wise Linear Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05559)
*Samuel Sahel-Schackis,Ken-ichi Nomura,Aiichiro Nakano,Matthias F. Kling,Thomas Linker*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EquiFiLM is a lightweight, backbone-agnostic extension that adds continuous external conditioning (charging, fields, excitation) to equivariant foundation MLFFs via per-layer FiLM blocks, achieving significant accuracy gains on charged liquid water with minimal training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing foundation MLFFs like MACE-MP-0 and UMA assume equilibrium ground-state physics and cannot handle externally induced changes to the electronic state (e.g., charging, applied fields, electronic excitation), limiting their use for driven processes like photoexcitation and charge injection.

Method: EquiFiLM adds a per-layer Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) block that modulates only scalar channels of equivariant MLFFs, preserving E(3)-equivariance. Applied to MACE-MatPES backbone for charged liquid water (yielding E-MACE), trained on only thousands of DFT frames rather than millions.

Result: E-MACE achieves 3.1x force RMSE reduction (21.3→6.96 meV/Å) and 61x per-atom energy RMSE reduction (6.1→0.1 meV/atom) over baseline. Across 7 held-out charges: force RMSE 18-61 meV/Å, energy RMSE 0.7-5.4 meV/atom. Stable MD across full range; predicts charge-dependent first-shell response in pair distribution function.

Conclusion: EquiFiLM provides an efficient, backbone-agnostic conditioning approach for equivariant MLFFs that requires minimal data (thousands vs. ~10^8 frames), is computationally cheap (no added inference cost), and generalizes to interpolation/extrapolation regimes.

Abstract: Foundation machine learning force fields (MLFFs) such as MACE-MP-0 and UMA cover broad chemical space at near density functional theory (DFT) accuracy. However, they assume equilibrium ground-state physics and do not natively handle externally induced changes to the electronic state, such as charging, applied fields, or electronic excitation, which limits their use for driven processes such as photoexcitation and charge injection. We propose EquiFiLM, a lightweight extension that adds continuous external conditioning to any equivariant foundation MLFF via a per-layer Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) block, learning externally driven changes to the potential energy surface from minimal training data. The block modulates only scalar channels and preserves E(3)-equivariance exactly. We demonstrate the recipe on charged liquid water with the foundation model MACE-MatPES as the backbone, yielding E-MACE. On the four training charges, E-MACE delivers a $3.1\times$ reduction in force RMSE ($21.3$ to $6.96$ meV/$\mathring{A}$) and a $61\times$ reduction in per-atom energy RMSE ($6.1$ to $0.1$ meV/atom) over a baseline without EquiFiLM trained on the same data, at indistinguishable inference cost. Across seven held-out interpolation and extrapolation charges, force RMSE stays within $18-61$ meV/$\mathring{A}$ and energy RMSE within $0.7-5.4$ meV/atom. The model runs stable molecular dynamics across the full range tested and predicts the charge-dependent first-shell response of the reduced pair distribution function probed by ultrafast electron diffraction. Adding this conditioning axis to the foundation requires only a few thousand DFT-labeled frames, against the $\approx 10^8$ structures of a charge-aware foundation trained from scratch. The recipe is backbone- and conditioning-agnostic: it applies without architectural change to any equivariant MLFF with scalar interaction-layer channels.

</details>


### [118] [SafeImpute: Reliable Clinical Data Imputation via Conformal Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05613)
*Xinrui He,Mengting Ai,Junting Wang,Curtiss B. Cook,Jingrui He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SafeImpute is a reliable clinical imputation framework that uses graph neural networks and conformal prediction with FDR control to impute missing lab values in sparse, irregular longitudinal records, while selectively releasing only trustworthy imputations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world clinical data suffers from pervasive missingness due to sparse patient visits and irregular test ordering. Existing imputation methods improve average accuracy but provide no reliability guarantees for individual imputed values, which is critical for high-stakes clinical downstream use.

Method: SafeImpute constructs an event graph capturing intra-patient temporal trajectories and inter-patient clinical similarity, then learns imputations via a two-relation GNN with adaptive fusion and masked reconstruction regularization. For reliability, it converts a proxy risk score into conformal p-values and applies the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure to control the FDR of unacceptable errors among released imputations.

Result: Experiments on Mayo Clinic data, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV show SafeImpute achieves strong imputation accuracy while providing reliable error control, outperforming diverse baselines in both standard imputation evaluation and FDR-controlled selective-release evaluation.

Conclusion: SafeImpute effectively combines accurate imputation with statistical guarantees on reliability, making it suitable for high-stakes clinical applications where uncontrolled imputation errors are unacceptable.

Abstract: Clinical care often relies on key laboratory indicators, yet real-world patient visits are sparse and tests are ordered irregularly, leading to pervasive missingness. While many imputation methods improve average accuracy, they provide limited guidance on which imputed values are reliable enough for high-stakes downstream use. In this work, we study reliable clinical imputation, aiming to produce accurate imputations while selectively releasing the reliable results, with statistical control over clinically unacceptable errors. To achieve this goal, we propose SafeImpute, a reliable imputation framework for irregular and sparse clinical longitudinal records. SafeImpute constructs an event graph that captures both intra-patient temporal trajectories and inter-patient clinical similarity, and learns imputations with a two-relation GNN and adaptive fusion, regularized by an auxiliary masked reconstruction objective. For reliability guarantees, SafeImpute converts a proxy risk score into conformal p-values and applies the Benjamini--Hochberg procedure to control the false discovery rate (FDR) of unacceptable errors among released imputations at a user-specified tolerance. Experiments on our Mayo Clinic data, the public MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV datasets show that SafeImpute achieves strong imputation accuracy while providing reliable error control, outperforming diverse baselines in both standard imputation evaluation and FDR-controlled selective-release evaluation.

</details>


### [119] [A Coin Flip Per Token: Bernoulli Sparse Steering of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05615)
*Nima Eshraghi,Lovedeep Gondara,Yuqing Huang,Sagarika Suresh,Leizer Teran,Jithin Pradeep,Xiaotong Xu,Fanny Chevalier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Stochastic Token Steering (STS) and Stochastic Block Steering (SBS) show that sparse, gated activation steering (e.g., 50% of tokens) achieves similar behavioral control to dense token-level steering while preserving fluency, revealing that SAE-mediated control is rate-limited by cumulative signal dosage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard activation steering via SAEs applies intervention at every token, degrading fluency. The authors ask whether dense per-token intervention is necessary for effective behavioral control.

Method: Proposes STS (gates each token independently with probability p) and SBS (gates a leading window once per sequence), both requiring no reward model or learned gating. Evaluated across two model families and two behavioral tasks.

Result: Steering 50% of tokens recovers most dense-steering effects while preserving fluency; 30% surpasses prompt-based control. Optimal steering magnitude scales inversely with intervention ratio.

Conclusion: SAE-mediated behavioral control is rate-limited by cumulative signal dosage across a sequence, not merely per-token perturbation, making stochastic gating a simple and effective alternative to dense intervention.

Abstract: Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning, but standard methods apply the steering signal at every generated token, incurring constant per-token perturbation that risks degrading fluency. We ask: is dense intervention necessary? We introduce Stochastic Token Steering (STS), which gates each token independently with probability $p$, and Stochastic Block Steering (SBS), which gates a leading window once per sequence; neither requires a reward model or learned gating policy. Across two model families and two behavioral tasks, steering only 50% of the tokens recovers most of the dense-steering effect while preserving fluency, and steering as few as 30% surpasses prompt-based control. The optimal steering magnitude scales inversely with the intervention ratio, revealing that SAE-mediated control is rate-limited: the behavioral outcome depends on cumulative signal dosage across a sequence.

</details>


### [120] [Safe Bayesian Optimization with Counterfactual Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05620)
*Katherine Avery,Bruno Castro da Silva,David Jensen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper integrates conformal prediction into safe Bayesian optimization to ensure new interventions do not worsen outcomes below a threshold defined by a counterfactual baseline policy, with provable safety guarantees and adaptation to covariate shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many decision-making settings (e.g., clinical medicine), new interventions are only acceptable if they do not reduce outcomes below an established threshold. However, the baseline policy's outcomes are counterfactual and unobserved, requiring estimation under uncertainty to safely optimize.

Method: The authors use conformal prediction to construct valid uncertainty intervals for counterfactual baseline outcomes, then integrate these intervals into safe Bayesian optimization. They also adapt conformal estimates to different types of covariate shift.

Result: The method ensures constraint violations occur at or below a user-specified rate, supported by a safety proof, experimental evidence, and a sensitivity analysis.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a principled way to safely optimize interventions when the safety baseline is counterfactual and unobserved, with formal guarantees on violation rates.

Abstract: In many decision-making settings, new interventions are acceptable only if they do not reduce outcomes below some established threshold. For example, in clinical medicine, new treatments are often acceptable only if they do not worsen outcomes relative to an established standard of care. Safe Bayesian optimization maximizes an objective subject to safety constraints. In the setting that we consider here, safety is defined relative to a known baseline policy whose outcomes are counterfactual and therefore unobserved. Thus, the counterfactual outcomes of the baseline policy must be estimated and those (uncertain) estimates must be used to safely optimize the objective. We address this estimation problem by using conformal prediction to construct valid uncertainty intervals for counterfactual baseline outcomes, and we show how these intervals can be integrated into safe Bayesian optimization to ensure that constraint violations occur at or below a user-specified rate. We also show how to adapt these conformal estimates to different kinds of covariate shift. We provide a safety proof, experimental evidence, and a sensitivity analysis.

</details>


### [121] [Intuitionistic Fuzzy Graph Embedded Random Vector Functional Link with Multiview Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05635)
*Vrushank Ahire,Yogesh Kumar,M. A. Ganaie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes IFGRVFL-MV, an enhanced RVFL network integrating intuitionistic fuzzy sets, graph embedding, and multiview learning to handle uncertainty, preserve geometric structures, and leverage multiple feature views, outperforming existing models on benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RVFL networks suffer from limitations in preserving geometric relationships and effectively utilizing multiple feature views, especially under uncertainty and outlier-prone conditions.

Method: The model combines three components: (1) intuitionistic fuzzy sets (membership & non-membership) for robustness to outliers and uncertainty, (2) graph embedding to capture and preserve intrinsic topological structures, and (3) multiview learning to exploit complementary information from multiple feature spaces.

Result: Experiments on UCI and KEEL benchmark datasets show that IFGRVFL-MV achieves superior classification accuracy compared to existing models.

Conclusion: IFGRVFL-MV is a promising advancement for classification tasks involving uncertainty and multiview data environments.

Abstract: Random Vector Functional Link (RVFL) networks are popular due to their fast training and universal approximation capabilities. However, RVFL models face challenges in preserving geometric relationships and utilizing multiple feature views effectively. To address these limitations we propose the Intuitionistic Fuzzy Graph Embedded Random Vector Functional Link with Multiview Learning (IFGRVFL-MV) model. The proposed approach comprises three key components: intuitionistic fuzzy sets for uncertainty handling, graph embedding to capture intrinsic geometric structures, and multiview learning to use complementary information from multiple feature spaces. The model assigns intuitionistic fuzzy membership and non-membership values to data points making it robust to outliers. Also, the graph embedding framework preserves topological structures, increasing the generalization performance. We performed experiments on benchmark datasets from UCI and KEEL repositories which concludes that IFGRVFL-MV outperforms existing models in classification accuracy. Our results establish that IFGRVFL-MV is a promising advancement in the domain of uncertainty and multiview environments.

</details>


### [122] [Domain-Adaptive Climate Downscaling Under Temporal Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05645)
*Shuochen Wang,Nishant Yadav,Auroop R. Ganguly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a temporal domain-adaptive downscaling framework that aligns historical and future climate distributions to address the out-of-distribution challenge in deep-learning-based climate downscaling, demonstrating consistent improvements over non-adaptive methods, especially under strong distribution shifts, in high-elevation regions, and for extreme temperature events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep-learning-based climate downscaling faces a temporal out-of-distribution (OOD) challenge: models trained on historical data are applied to future projections with potentially different distributions, leading to degraded performance under non-stationary climate conditions.

Method: A temporal domain-adaptive downscaling framework combining supervised high-resolution reconstruction on historical data with domain alignment between historical and future climate distributions.

Result: The domain-adaptive model consistently outperforms statistical and deep-learning-based bias-correction methods, with largest gains under strongest temporal shifts; stronger improvements over high-elevation and topographically complex regions; higher spatiotemporal correlation with HR target; reduced upper-tail temperature bias compared to non-adaptive model.

Conclusion: Temporal domain adaptation can improve the robustness of high-resolution climate projections under non-stationary climate conditions.

Abstract: Deep-learning-based climate downscaling aims to learn relationships from historical low-resolution (LR) and high-resolution (HR) climate data to generate HR climate projections. However, this setting faces a temporal out-of-distribution (OOD) challenge: models trained on historical data are commonly applied to future projections whose distributions may differ substantially from the training period. This study investigates temporal OOD shift for daily temperature downscaling over the Continental United States using paired LR-HR model simulations. We propose a temporal domain-adaptive downscaling framework that combines supervised HR reconstruction on historical data with domain alignment between historical and future climate distributions. Experiments across future validation periods show that the proposed domain-adaptive model consistently outperforms statistical and deep-learning-based bias-correction methods, with the largest gains occurring when the temporal distribution shift is strongest. Spatial analyses indicate stronger improvements over high-elevation and topographically complex regions, along with higher spatiotemporal correlation with the HR target. The extreme analysis shows that domain adaptation also reduces upper-tail temperature bias relative to the non-adaptive model. These results demonstrate that temporal domain adaptation can improve the robustness of HR climate projections under non-stationary climate conditions.

</details>


### [123] [Orthogonal Dendritic Intrinsic Networks: An Architecture for Significance-Ordered, Orthogonal Latent Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05653)
*Jeanie Schreiber,Tyrus Berry,Zeeshan Ahmed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ODIN is a deep autoencoder that enforces orthogonality and variance ranking in latent dimensions, combining PCA-like interpretability with non-linear representation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PCA-like properties (orthogonality, variance ranking) are rarely achieved in deep autoencoders; the authors aim to bridge interpretable linear decomposition (PCA) with expressive non-linear deep networks.

Method: ODIN incorporates geometric constraints (orthogonality and ordered variance) directly into the training objective of a deep autoencoder, providing theoretical grounding for these constraints within standard encoder-decoder frameworks.

Result: Empirical results on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that ODIN recovers PCA-like latent structure while maintaining deep network expressivity.

Conclusion: ODIN establishes a principled path toward interpretable, structured feature learning and dimensionality reduction in non-linear autoencoder architectures.

Abstract: Principal Component Analysis or PCA-like properties (orthogonality, variance ranking) are seldom realized in deep autoencoder architectures. In this work, we present ODIN (Orthogonal Dendritic Intrinsic Network), a novel autoencoder architecture that recovers PCA-like latent structure in a fully non-linear regime. By incorporating a set of geometric constraints directly into the training objective, ODIN encourages latent dimensions to be mutually orthogonal and ordered by explained variance, mirroring the interpretable decomposition of PCA while retaining the expressive power of deep networks. We provide theoretical grounding for these constraints and demonstrate their compatibility with standard encoder-decoder frameworks. We also establish empirical results for both synthetic and real world datasets, establishing a principled path toward interpretable, structured feature learning and dimensionality reduction.

</details>


### [124] [Deep Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Battery Management of Autonomous Order Pickers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05683)
*Taniya Shaji,Abhay Sobhanan,Christof Defryn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A PPO-based Deep Reinforcement Learning framework is proposed to solve the dynamic AMR charging problem in multi-block warehouses, learning both charging station selection and optimal charging duration under stochastic order arrivals, achieving up to 6% higher order-completion rates than the strongest baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Battery charging of AMRs in warehouses critically impacts order processing times and throughput, and traditional fixed-rule heuristics are suboptimal in dynamic environments, failing to account for multi-AMR coordination and causing resource inefficiencies.

Method: A PPO-based DRL framework for multi-block warehouses with fixed charging stations, dynamically learning: (1) charging station selection and (2) optimal charging duration, explicitly considering anticipated queuing times at stations.

Result: The PPO framework increases order-completion rates by up to 6% over the strongest baseline and significantly reduces total time spent on recharging operations; robustness is validated across diverse warehouse configurations and stochastic arrival rates.

Conclusion: The learned DRL policy is superior to standard benchmarks, and the authors interpret the policy to offer operational insights into its advantage.

Abstract: Battery charging of Autonomous Mobile Robots (AMRs) in warehouses is a critical operational challenge that heavily impacts both order processing times and throughput. In this study, we address the dynamic AMR charging problem under stochastic order arrivals, where robots must learn optimal charging decisions. Traditional fixed-rule heuristics often prove suboptimal in dynamic environments and fail to account for multi-AMR coordination, leading to severe resource inefficiencies. To overcome these limitations, we propose a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-based Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) framework designed for multi-block warehouses with fixed charging stations. Our model dynamically learns two key decisions: charging station selection and optimal charging duration, explicitly accounting for anticipated queuing times at the stations. Extensive numerical experiments benchmark the proposed model against state-of-the-art DRL and traditional heuristic approaches. Results demonstrate that our PPO framework increases order-completion rates by up to 6\% compared to the strongest baseline, while significantly reducing the total time dedicated to recharging operations. Furthermore, we validate the model's robustness across diverse warehouse configurations and stochastic arrival rates. Finally, we interpret the learned DRL policy, offering valuable operational insights into its superiority over standard benchmarks.

</details>


### [125] [LLM-Driven Neural Network Generation with Same-Family Architecture Guidance: Disentangling Transfer and Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05704)
*Kabir Dev Paul Baghel,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can improve weak target models by using source-guided candidate generation from a stronger same-family source model, significantly outperforming non-source controls and direct copying across CIFAR-10 and SVHN benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-generated neural-network modifications are often invalid or harmful; this paper narrows the setting to improving a weak target model using a stronger same-family source model from a neural-network database.

Method: Proposes a source-guided candidate-generation protocol with non-source controls, source-conditioned candidates, and a no-LLM hp_copy ablation under equal evaluation budgets. Reports validity separately from accuracy and selects the best valid candidate only when it improves the target.

Result: On CIFAR-10: source-guided candidate reaches 0.5049 vs 0.2398 non-source (+0.2651), improving weak target from 0.1254. On SVHN AlexNet: 0.7880 vs 0.2254 (+0.5626); fresh repeat 0.8069 vs 0.2509 (+0.5560). Direct source-recipe copy produces 0.1959, while hp_transfer reaches 0.7880, showing adaptation not copying. AlexNet has 6/8 wins across SVHN, Imagenette, CelebA-Gender; alt_nn1 has 8/10 wins on CIFAR-10.

Conclusion: Source-guided candidate generation with LLMs effectively improves weak target models, with the LLM adapting rather than copying source recipes, showing consistent positive signals across multiple architectures and datasets.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can generate neural-network modifications, but unrestricted generation is often invalid or harmful. This paper studies a narrower setting: improving a weak target model using a stronger same-family source model from a neural-network database. We propose a source-guided candidate-generation protocol with non-source controls, source-conditioned candidates, and a no-LLM hp_copy ablation under equal evaluation budgets. The protocol reports validity separately from accuracy and selects the best valid candidate only when it improves the target. On CIFAR-10, the strongest source-guided candidate reaches 0.5049 accuracy versus 0.2398 for the best non-source candidate, a +0.2651 advantage, while improving a weak target originally at 0.1254; a five-epoch check preserves the gain at 0.7686 versus 0.4839. On SVHN AlexNet with DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B, source-guided transfer reaches 0.7880 versus 0.2254, a +0.5626 advantage; a fresh repeat reaches 0.8069 versus 0.2509, a +0.5560 advantage. Direct source-recipe copy produces 0.1959 on SVHN AlexNet, matching the original target, while hp_transfer reaches 0.7880, showing that the LLM adapts rather than copies the source recipe. Family-level analysis shows the clearest positive signals for AlexNet, with 6/8 wins across SVHN, Imagenette, and CelebA-Gender, and alt_nn1, with 8/10 wins on CIFAR-10.

</details>


### [126] [FourTune: Towards Fully 4-Bit Efficient Post-Training for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05711)
*Bowen Xue,Zihan Min,Xingyang Li,Zhekai Zhang,Haocheng Xi,Lvmin Zhang,Maneesh Agrawala,Jun-Yan Zhu,Song Han,Yujun Lin,Muyang Li*

Main category: cs.LG
