Method: EISAM employs a two-step extragradient-inspired update: (1) a prediction step that probes the loss landscape geometry, and (2) a perturbation step that refines updates using a base optimizer, steering parameters toward flatter minima.

Result: EISAM consistently outperforms SGD, Adam, and SAM in test accuracy and training efficiency across various architectures and benchmark datasets. It reduces sensitivity to the perturbation radius, simplifying tuning.

Conclusion: EISAM tightens the generalization bound by converging to flatter minima with reduced curvature, offering a robust, scalable, and broadly applicable optimization solution with practical tuning guidance.

Abstract: Generalization remains a pivotal challenge in deep learning, where traditional optimizers like Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) often converge to sharp minima, leading to overfitting and reduced performance on unseen data. Building on Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM), for seeking flat minima associated with improved generalization, we propose the Extragradient-Inspired Sharpness-Aware Minimization (EISAM), a novel optimizer that enhances generalization via the extragradient technique. EISAM uses a two-step update process: a prediction step investigating the geometry of the loss landscape and a perturbation step that refines updates with a base optimizer. This approach achieves better generalization performance than SAM. Crucially, EISAM reduces sensitivity to the perturbation radius, enhancing robustness, and simplifying the tuning across diverse settings. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that EISAM consistently outperforms SGD, Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam), and SAM in test accuracy and training efficiency across various architectures. Theoretical analysis further confirms that EISAM tightens the generalization bound by steering parameters toward flatter minima with reduced curvature. Accompanied by a thorough hyperparameter analysis, EISAM offers practical tuning guidance, establishing it as a robust, scalable, and broadly applicable optimization solution that advances both the theory and practice in deep learning.

</details>


### [152] [X-FEMR: A Token-level Explainable Approach for Electronic Health Records Foundation Models using Transformer-based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06163)
*Jie Huang,Pengfei Yin,Zihan Xu,Daniel Capurro,Mike Conway,Ting Dang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes the first token-level explainability approach for Foundation Models for Electronic Health Records (FEMRs) using a Transformer-based surrogate model, demonstrating that token-level explanations align well with clinical knowledge through a novel clinical alignment metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FEMRs are black-box models with concerns about bias, interpretability, and clinical trust; there is a lack of token-level explainability methods for these models.

Method: Training a Transformer-based surrogate model on input-output pairs from the FEMR across two prediction tasks to approximate its behavior while preserving temporal dynamics; identifying the most influential tokens; introducing a novel clinical alignment metric to quantify correspondence between surrogate model's key tokens and clinically validated features.

Result: The surrogate closely approximates FEMR predictions, and token-level explanations align well with clinical knowledge.

Conclusion: The proposed approach offers a practical framework for interpretable and trustworthy clinical AI by providing token-level explainability for FEMRs.

Abstract: Foundation Models for Electronic Health Records (FEMRs) are pretrained on large-scale structured patient data, enabling them to convert longitudinal patient trajectories into generalizable representations for diverse clinical prediction tasks. Despite their effectiveness, FEMRs remain black-box models, raising concerns about bias, interpretability, and clinical trust. To address this, we propose the first token-level explainability approach for FEMRs. We train a Transformer-based surrogate model on input-output pairs from the FEMR across two prediction tasks, approximating its behavior while preserving temporal dynamics. We identify the most influential tokens, providing insights into how FEMRs leverage different aspects of patient history for predictions. To evaluate clinical relevance, we introduce a novel clinical alignment metric that quantifies the correspondence between the surrogate model's key tokens and clinically validated features. Our results demonstrate that the surrogate closely approximates FEMR predictions and that token-level explanations align well with clinical knowledge, offering a practical framework for interpretable and trustworthy clinical AI.

</details>


### [153] [Canopy: A Heterograph Foundation Model for Metabolic Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06224)
*Jake Bowden,Laurence Legon,Satnam Surae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Canopy is a heterogeneous graph foundation model that integrates multi-modal biological data into a unified knowledge graph and uses a Heterogeneous Graph Transformer with self-supervised pretraining to predict fermentation titers, outperforming tabular baselines (R²=0.41 vs 0.24).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing metabolic engineering approaches either use stoichiometric models that cannot learn from experimental data, or tabular ML that discards the relational structure of biological knowledge. A unified framework that leverages the relational and multi-modal nature of biological data is needed.

Method: Canopy constructs a heterogeneous KG (6.9M nodes, 13 types, 34 edge types) from ten data sources; encodes nodes via domain-specific foundation models (ESM-2, MoLFormer, PubMedBERT); pretrains a Heterogeneous Graph Transformer (HGT) with SignNet positional encodings, Jumping Knowledge, virtual nodes, and four self-supervised objectives (link prediction, masked node modeling, distance prediction, contrastive experiment clustering) balanced by homoscedastic uncertainty weighting.

Result: On fermentation titer prediction, frozen Canopy embeddings achieve R²=0.41 with a lightweight probe, outperforming tabular baselines (best R²=0.24) and homogeneous GNN variants.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous graph foundation models pretrained on multi-modal biological knowledge graphs can significantly improve prediction of strain performance, offering a promising paradigm for AI-driven metabolic engineering.

Abstract: Designing microbial strains that produce high-value chemicals at commercially viable titers remains a central challenge in metabolic engineering. Existing computational approaches either rely on stoichiometric constraint-based models that cannot learn from experimental data, or apply tabular machine learning to hand-crafted features that discard the relational structure of biological knowledge. We present Canopy, a heterogeneous graph foundation model that integrates ten public and proprietary data sources into a unified knowledge graph (KG) of 6.9M nodes across 13 types and 34 edge types, covering genes, proteins, metabolites, reactions, pathways, strains, and fermentation experiments. Node features are encoded through domain-specific foundation models (ESM-2 for protein sequences, MoLFormer for chemical SMILES, and PubMedBERT for biomedical text), yielding a multi-modal representation within a single graph. We pretrain a Heterogeneous Graph Transformer (HGT) augmented with SignNet positional encodings, Jumping Knowledge aggregation, and virtual nodes using four self-supervised objectives (link prediction, masked node modelling, distance prediction, and contrastive experiment clustering), balanced via learned homoscedastic uncertainty weighting. On the downstream task of fermentation titer prediction, frozen Canopy embeddings achieve $R^{2} = 0.41$ with a lightweight probe, outperforming tabular baselines (best $R^{2} = 0.24$) and homogeneous GNN variants.

</details>


### [154] [Quantitative Gaussian-Process limits of Tensor Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06290)
*Andrea Agazzi,Eloy Mosig García,Dario Trevisan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a quantitative convergence theory for the infinite-width Gaussian-process limit of random neural networks using tensor programs, with explicit finite-width error bounds of order inverse square-root of the widths in Wasserstein distance, applicable to feed-forward, recurrent, and transformer architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a quantitative convergence theory that bridges finite-width random neural networks and their infinite-width Gaussian-process limits, moving beyond qualitative or asymptotic results.

Method: The framework uses tensor programs to analyze the infinite-width Gaussian-process limit of random neural networks, deriving explicit finite-width error bounds in Wasserstein distance that scale as the inverse square-root of the widths.

Result: Explicit finite-width error bounds of order inverse square-root of the widths in Wasserstein distance between finite-network executions and their Gaussian-process limits.

Conclusion: The proposed framework is architecture-agnostic, covering feed-forward models as well as weight-sharing schemes used in recurrent and transformer-type architectures, providing a unified quantitative convergence theory.

Abstract: We study the infinite-width Gaussian-process limit of random neural networks
  through the lens of tensor programs, and we provide a quantitative convergence
  theory in Wasserstein distance.
  Our main result gives explicit finite-width error bounds, of order inverse square-root of the widths
  between finite-network executions and their
  Gaussian-process limits. The framework is architecture-agnostic and covers feed-forward models together
  with weight-sharing schemes relevant for recurrent and transformer-type
  architectures.

</details>


### [155] [Physics-Informed Neural Embeddings of PDE Solution Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06348)
*Raul Jimenez,Svitlana Mayboroda,Pavlos Protopapas,Leonid Sarieddine,David N. Spergel,Pedro Tarancón-Álvarez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-informed multihead PINN learns finite-dimensional embeddings of PDE solution families, enabling dimensional reduction and robust spectral analysis via head-orthogonalization and Fourier-shell decomposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To discover low-dimensional structure in PDE solution spaces and derive robust, interpretable observables of solution-manifold geometry using neural networks.

Method: Multihead Physics-Informed Neural Network with a shared latent body, linear heads for different initial conditions, head-orthogonalization penalty, and Fourier-shell frequency profiling.

Result: For Burgers equation with n_b=20, 2-4 PCs capture ~95% variance and 4-7 capture ~99%; similar compression for heat/wave equations. Frequency profiles are invariant across training runs.

Conclusion: Learned spectral profiles and principal components are robust, reproducible observables of solution-manifold geometry, and the method reveals intrinsic dimensional reduction in PDE dynamics.

Abstract: We introduce a physics-informed framework for learning finite-dimensional embeddings of solution families of partial differential equations. The method uses a multihead Physics-Informed Neural Network in which a shared body learns a latent manifold representing the solution space, while linear heads reconstruct individual solutions associated with different initial conditions. A head-orthogonalization penalty removes degeneracies in the latent representation and stabilizes the principal-component spectrum across training realizations. Because the initial condition is built into the network output by construction, these principal components measure the additional variability the network learns on top of the initial profile, not the full solution itself. We apply the method to the one-dimensional viscous Burgers equation, with the heat and wave equations as robustness checks. For a latent dimension $n_b=20$, the learned manifolds exhibit pronounced effective dimensional reduction: for Burgers dynamics, only $2$-$4$ principal components capture about $95\%$ of the latent-space variance, while $4$-$7$ capture about $99\%$, depending on the initial-condition family; the same qualitative compression holds for the heat and wave equations. We also split the wavenumber axis into bands (``Fourier shells'') and measure how much each band contributes to every principal component. The resulting frequency profile is invariant under the change-of-basis freedom that the orthogonalization penalty leaves in the latent space, and is therefore reproducible across independent training runs. More broadly, this establishes the learned spectral profiles and principal components as robust observables of solution-manifold geometry.

</details>


### [156] [TILDE: TILt-based Distributional Erasure for Concept Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06432)
*Naveen George,Naoki Murata,Yuhta Takida,Konda Reddy Mopuri,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TILDE formulates concept unlearning in text-to-image diffusion models as a distributional alignment problem using energy-tilted, anchor-free targets, achieving strong forgetting with improved retention and fidelity via residual ∇-GFlowNet training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concept unlearning methods effectively remove target concepts but fail to explicitly preserve quality, diversity, and semantic coverage on benign generation. The gold standard (retain-only model trained from scratch) is not approximated by common erasure objectives, leaving retention as an implicit consequence rather than a specified goal.

Method: TILDE (TILt-based Distributional Erasure) formulates unlearning as distributional alignment to a minimum-deviation conditional distribution under a forgetting constraint using an energy-tilted, anchor-free target. This is instantiated with residual ∇-GFlowNet training to learn the score correction induced by the forget energy relative to the pretrained diffusion model.

Result: Across objects, artistic styles, and characters, TILDE achieves strong forgetting while improving retention and distributional fidelity over prior baselines.

Conclusion: Explicitly specifying the target distribution (rather than relying on implicit retention) yields superior trade-offs between forgetting and retention in diffusion model unlearning.

Abstract: Concept unlearning in text-to-image diffusion models is critical for safe and practical deployment: with rising privacy concerns, copyright disputes, trademark constraints, and safety regulations, deployed systems must be able to suppress unwanted concepts after training. Existing methods often remove the target concept effectively, but practical unlearning also requires an equally fundamental property: the unlearned model should retain quality, diversity, and semantic coverage on benign generation. The gold standard is a retain-only model trained from scratch without the unwanted data. However, common erasure objectives do not specify which post-unlearning distribution should approximate this reference, leaving retention as an implicit consequence of the update rule. We propose TILDE, TILt-based Distributional Erasure, which formulates concept unlearning as a distributional alignment problem: the desired target is the minimum-deviation conditional distribution from the pretrained model under a forgetting constraint. This energy-tilted, anchor-free target suppresses concept-expressing images while preserving benign relative mass for each prompt. We instantiate this principle with residual $\nabla$-GFlowNet training, which learns the score correction induced by the forget energy relative to the pretrained diffusion model. Across objects, artistic styles, and characters, TILDE achieves strong forgetting while improving retention and distributional fidelity over prior baselines.

</details>


### [157] [EntroPath: Maximum Entropy Path Ensemble Embedding for Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06497)
*Przemysław Rola*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EntroPath is a manifold learning method that uses ensembles of maximum entropy random walk paths to recover geodesic distances, outperforming existing methods on non-uniformly sampled manifolds and branching trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based embeddings either rely on locally normalised random walks (which concentrate diffusion in dense regions) or shortest-path distances (which are sensitive to spurious shortcut edges). EntroPath aims to overcome both limitations by leveraging path-ensemble diffusion.

Method: EntroPath builds dissimilarities using the maximum entropy random walk (MERW), aggregating the full ensemble of k-step paths between points. It uses free-energy dissimilarity that converges to squared geodesic distance via Varadhan's heat-kernel formula. The method includes scalable extensions via landmark projection and diffusion-potential pseudotime, and admits an exact Gram factorisation connecting it to kernel methods.

Result: On synthetic manifolds and single-cell benchmarks, EntroPath matches or outperforms diffusion- and shortest-path-based methods, and remains competitive with UMAP and t-SNE on local-structure metrics. Gains are most pronounced on manifolds with non-uniform sampling density and well-separated branching trajectories.

Conclusion: EntroPath more faithfully preserves underlying geodesic geometry through path-ensemble diffusion, particularly where density varies and trajectories branch, offering a principled alternative to existing graph-based embedding techniques.

Abstract: We introduce EntroPath, a manifold learning method that recovers geodesic geometry from data graphs through ensembles of diffusion paths. Many existing graph-based embeddings rely either on locally normalised random walks or on shortest-path distances. The former can concentrate diffusion in densely sampled regions, while the latter are sensitive to spurious shortcut edges in the graph. EntroPath instead builds its dissimilarities from the maximum entropy random walk (MERW), which aggregates the full ensemble of k-step paths between points rather than relying on any single trajectory. We show that the resulting free-energy dissimilarity converges to squared geodesic distance in the short-time limit, via Varadhan's heat-kernel formula. The diffusion depth k interpolates smoothly between local neighbourhood structure and global manifold geometry, and the symmetrised kernel admits an exact Gram factorisation connecting EntroPath to kernel methods. We further provide scalable extensions via landmark projection and diffusion-potential pseudotime. Across synthetic manifolds and single-cell benchmarks, EntroPath consistently matches or outperforms diffusion- and shortest-path-based methods, while remaining competitive with neighbourhood-preserving embeddings (UMAP, t-SNE) on local-structure metrics. Its gains are most pronounced on manifolds with non-uniform sampling density and well-separated branching trajectories, where path-ensemble diffusion more faithfully preserves the underlying geodesic geometry.

</details>


### [158] [GraphBU: MILP Instance Generation with Graph-Native Block Units](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06532)
*Xiaolei Guo,Chenyu Zhou,Jianghao Lin,Dongdong Ge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GraphBU is a graph-native MILP generator that uses local subproblems plus their interfaces (coupling nodes) as basic units for compatibility-checked replacement, achieving ~93.4% graph-statistical similarity, ~96.7% feasibility, and ~8.0% improvement in downstream Predict-and-Search training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MILP instances for solver development are scarce due to privacy/application-specific pipelines; existing generators fail to explicitly record how local parts couple to the rest of the instance.

Method: GraphBU uses a graph-native approach where the basic unit is a local subproblem plus its interface; it promotes coupling nodes into master constraints or boundary variables and performs compatibility-checked block replacement.

Result: Average graph-statistical similarity ~93.4%, average feasibility ~96.7%, average increase in downstream Predict-and-Search main index ~8.0%.

Conclusion: The graph construction is invariant to row-column permutations; the promotion separates interfaces; replacement preserves feasibility under interface-slack condition; the method improves downstream PS training.

Abstract: Mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) instances used for solver development are hard to obtain when models come from private or application-specific pipelines. A generator must keep the structure that solvers and learned policies rely on. Existing general generators usually choose their generation unit from a formulation template, summary statistics, local graph edits, or blocks found after recombination. These units do not explicitly record how a local part of the MILP is coupled to the rest of the instance. We propose GraphBU, a graph-native generator whose basic unit is a local subproblem plus its interface. The method promotes coupling nodes into master constraints or boundary variables and uses the resulting block units for compatibility-checked replacement. The analysis focuses on the properties needed by this construction: promotion separates interfaces, replacement can preserve feasibility under an interface-slack condition, and the graph construction is invariant to row-column permutations. On MILP instances generation, this unit keeps graph statistics close to the source family, preserves feasibility on most datasets, and improves downstream Predict-and-Search training. Genrated by GraphBU, The average graph-statistical similarity was approximately 0.934, the average feasibility was approximately 96.7%, and the average increase in the main index of downstream PS was approximately 8.0%.

</details>


### [159] [Graph Convolutional Attention: A Spectral Perspective on Graph Denoising and Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06546)
*Shervin Khalafi,Igor Krawczuk,Sergio Rozada,Charilaos Kanatsoulis,Antonio G Marques,Alejandro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies a fundamental limitation of linear attention in graph denoising—it can only learn an average spectral filter, which is suboptimal when graphs vary spectrally across the distribution. To address this, the authors propose Spectral Attention and its practical permutation-equivariant realization Graph Convolutional Attention (GCA), which uses graph-filtered queries and keys to perform spectral denoising. They prove GCA outperforms linear attention by a margin tied to spectral diversity, show softmax provides additional denoising through eigenvector projection, and empirically demonstrate consistent improvements on synthetic and real datasets, including faster inference in DiGress when combined with PEARL positional encodings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The principled understanding of attention-based graph denoising is limited, and it is unclear whether standard (linear) attention is the right mechanism. The authors show linear attention is fundamentally suboptimal for denoising because it can only learn an average spectral filter across the training distribution, failing when graphs have diverse spectral properties.

Method: The authors introduce Spectral Attention, a mechanism that directly utilizes the input graph spectrum, and prove it provably outperforms linear attention by a margin governed by spectral diversity. They derive Graph Convolutional Attention (GCA) as a practical, permutation-equivariant realization that implements spectral denoising via graph-filtered queries and keys. They also analyze how the softmax operation provides additional denoising by approximately projecting noisy eigenvectors onto the clean eigenspace.

Result: For stochastic block models, GCA provably matches the idealized Spectral Attention. Empirically, replacing linear attention with GCA consistently improves graph denoising and diffusion on synthetic and real datasets, with gains strongly correlated with spectral diversity. In DiGress, GCA matches standard graph-transformer performance without expensive structural features, and combined with PEARL positional encodings avoids explicit eigendecomposition, yielding faster inference without quality degradation.

Conclusion: Linear attention is suboptimal for graph denoising due to its inability to handle spectral diversity across graphs. The proposed Spectral Attention and its practical GCA realization overcome this limitation, providing provably better denoising. The findings offer both theoretical insights and practical improvements for graph diffusion models.

Abstract: Denoising graphs is a fundamental problem in graph learning and the core operation of graph diffusion models. Attention-based architectures like graph transformers have recently shown promise in denoising graphs. However, our principled understanding of attention-based graph denoising remains limited, making it unclear whether standard attention is the right mechanism for this task. Here we show that, under a denoising objective, linear attention is suboptimal and can only learn an average spectral denoising filter over the training distribution. This creates a fundamental limitation as graphs often vary spectrally across the distribution. To overcome this limitation, we introduce Spectral Attention, which directly utilizes the input graph spectrum and provably outperforms linear attention by a margin governed by the spectral diversity of the distribution. We then derive Graph Convolutional Attention (GCA), a practical and permutation-equivariant realization of this idea that implements spectral denoising through graph-filtered queries and keys. For stochastic block models, GCA provably matches the idealized Spectral Attention mechanism. We further show that the softmax operation, that follows the attention, provides additional denoising by approximately projecting noisy eigenvectors onto the clean eigenspace. Empirically, replacing linear attention with GCA consistently improves graph denoising and diffusion on synthetic and real datasets, with gains strongly correlated with spectral diversity. In DiGress, GCA matches standard graph-transformer performance without computing expensive structural features, and when combined with the recently proposed PEARL positional encodings, avoids explicit eigendecomposition computations resulting in faster inference without degrading quality. The code can be found here: github.com/shervinkhalafi/graph_conv_att

</details>

### [160] [TMF-RSE: Tri-Modal Fusion with Regional Semantics and Evidential Uncertainty for Lung Severity Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06356)
*Fadi Abdeladhim Zidi,Salah Eddine Bekhouche,Abdellah Zakaria Sellam,Gaby Maroun,Fadi Dornaika,Cosimo Distante*

Main category: eess.IV

TL;DR: TMF-RSE is a tri-modal deep learning framework that fuses appearance, structural, and semantic features with evidential uncertainty estimation for lung disease severity quantification from chest imaging, outperforming transformer-based baselines on COVID-19 and RALO datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate quantification of lung disease severity from chest imaging is critical for clinical decision-making and resource allocation, motivating the need for a more effective multi-modal fusion approach.

Method: TMF-RSE integrates three modalities: appearance features from 2D chest inputs, structural features from lung segmentation masks, and semantic features from VLMs, using complementary fusion mechanisms (semantic guidance, structural priors, hierarchical interactions) and evidential regression for uncertainty-aware severity prediction.

Result: Outperforms transformer-based baselines: Per-COVID-19 (MAE 4.02, PC 0.9629) and RALO geographic extent (MAE 0.339, PC 0.973).

Conclusion: Tri-modal fusion with regional semantics and evidential uncertainty effectively improves lung disease severity quantification and provides reliable uncertainty estimates.

Abstract: Accurate quantification of lung disease severity from chest imaging is critical for clinical decision-making and resource allocation. We propose a tri-modal deep learning framework, TMF-RSE (Tri-Modal Fusion with Regional Semantics and Evidential Uncertainty), that combines appearance features from two-dimensional chest inputs, structural features from lung segmentation masks, and semantic features from vision-language models (VLMs) for severity quantification. Our approach employs complementary fusion mechanisms that integrate semantic guidance, structural priors, and hierarchical interactions across modalities. The model employs evidential regression to provide both severity predictions and uncertainty estimates. Experiments on the Per-COVID-19 CT and RALO datasets show that TMF-RSE outperforms recent transformer-based baselines, achieving MAE of 4.02 and Pearson correlation of 0.9629 on Per-COVID-19 validation, and 0.339 MAE / 0.973 PC on RALO geographic extent.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [161] [From Textural Counterpoint to Feature Encoding: A Multi-Dimensional Machine Representation Study of Haydn's "The Lark" Integrating Electroacoustic Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05902)
*Yakun Liu,Zhiyu Jin,Hai Luan,Dong Liu,Xiaonan Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel human-computer collaborative composition paradigm by analyzing Haydn's String Quartet in D Major (The Lark) through a three-stage process: Classical Morphology Qualitative Analysis, Electroacoustic Quantitative Measurement, and Machine Representation Reconstruction. It introduces Event-based Timestamps and Role-Aware Encoding to address the lack of role perception in deep music generation models, aiming to imbue AI systems with "social attributes" and "otherness awareness" for polyphonic music interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep music generation models lack role perception capabilities during polyphonic interactions. Chamber music's inherent logic of "role assignment and dynamic interaction" offers a valuable blueprint for developing human-computer collaborative composition paradigms that address this gap.

Method: The study follows a three-stage research path: (1) Classical Morphology Qualitative Analysis (auditory analysis of counterpoint and groove in the first movement of Haydn's The Lark); (2) Electroacoustic Quantitative Measurement (using DAW-based spectrum and dynamic feature analysis to convert subjective auditory perception into measurable physical parameters); (3) Machine Representation Reconstruction (replacing traditional quantization grids with Event-based Timestamps for micro-timing, and transforming acoustic features into Role-Aware Encoding as a phenomenological anchor).

Result: The paper completes a logical loop spanning classical analysis, electronic music mapping, and AI symbolic generation. It establishes a theoretical foundation for constructing human-computer collaborative music systems that incorporate "social attributes" and "otherness awareness" through interactive aesthetics and media philosophy perspectives.

Conclusion: The proposed Role-Aware Encoding and Event-based Timestamp approach provides a fundamentally new framework for imbuing AI music generation models with role perception and interactive awareness, moving beyond mechanical quantization toward more socially intelligent polyphonic composition systems.

Abstract: Chamber music, as a highly precise multi-part interactive system, contains a logic of "role assignment and dynamic interaction" that provides an extremely valuable blueprint for exploring human-computer collaborative composition paradigms. Addressing the lack of role perception capabilities in existing deep music generation models during polyphonic interactions, this paper conducts an interdisciplinary analysis of Haydn's String Quartet in D Major, The Lark (Op. 64, No. 5). We propose a novel research path: "Classical Morphology Qualitative Analysis-Electroacoustic Quantitative Measurement-Machine Representation Reconstruction." The study first utilizes auditory analysis to dissect the counterpoint morphology of the leading voice and the underlying groove in the first movement. Subsequently, it introduces spectrum and dynamic feature analysis tools from a Digital Audio Workstation (DAW) to translate subjective auditory perception into objective, measurable physical parameters. Building on this, the paper introduces a fundamentally new approach to low-level computer feature extraction: completely abandoning the traditional mechanical quantization grid, introducing Event-based Timestamps to record the duration of micro-timing, and transforming acoustic features into an independent "Role-Aware Encoding" as an aesthetic heuristic mechanism (a phenomenological anchor). This study not only completes the logical loop spanning classical analysis, electronic music mapping, and AI symbolic generation but also establishes a profound theoretical foundation-from the perspectives of interactive aesthetics and media philosophy-for constructing human-computer collaborative music systems imbued with "social attributes" and "otherness awareness."

</details>


### [162] [Escaping the Procrustean Bed: Groupwise Orthogonal Connectors for Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06014)
*Ho-Lam Chung,Ke-Han Lu,Yi-Cheng Lin,Guan-Ting Lin,Yiming Chen,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Audio-language models suffer from query collapse in Q-Former connectors, where outputs collapse to a single direction and lose speaker-specific information. ORCA solves this by grouping queries and constraining them to different directions, achieving 75.2% on SAKURA (vs. 49.0% for 8B Audio Flamingo-3), reducing query redundancy by 12x, and increasing cross-speaker variance by 75x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Q-Former connector in audio-language models produces collapsed output vectors (single direction) and fails to preserve paralinguistic cues (speaker identity, gender, prosody), making different speakers nearly indistinguishable after compression.

Method: ORCA splits queries into groups and constrains each group's outputs to point in different directions, preventing the collapse observed in standard Q-Former connectors.

Result: ORCA achieves 75.2% on SAKURA multi-hop reasoning (vs. 49.0% for 8B Audio Flamingo-3), cuts query redundancy by 12x, and raises cross-speaker variance by 75x compared to an identically trained 4B baseline.

Conclusion: The query collapse problem in audio-language model connectors can be effectively mitigated by grouping and directional constraints, significantly improving both task performance and preservation of speaker-specific information.

Abstract: Audio-language models compress a speech encoder's output through a Querying Transformer (Q-Former) connector before feeding it to a large language model. We identify two failures in this compression. The connector's output vectors collapse to a single direction, and different speakers produce nearly indistinguishable outputs, with paralinguistic cues such as speaker identity, gender, and prosody lost along the way. Our method, ORCA, reverses this collapse by splitting the queries into groups whose outputs are constrained to point in different directions. On SAKURA multi-hop reasoning, ORCA gains 26.4 points over an identically trained 4B baseline, reaching 75.2% (vs. 49.0% for the 8B Audio Flamingo-3). At the connector level, the same change cuts query redundancy by 12x and raises cross-speaker variance by 75x.

</details>


### [163] [Music I Care About: Automated Multimodal Benchmarking of LLM Music Perception Skills on (Almost) Any Music](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06015)
*Tomáš Sourada,Katia Vendrame,Jan Hajič*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MusICA-MetaBench is a framework for automatically generating on-demand, multi-modal music perception benchmarks from user-provided data, enabling efficient and reliable evaluation of MLLMs across audio, notation images, and symbolic files.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current music benchmarks for MLLMs suffer from three limitations: (1) they are resource-intensive and their results may not generalize to diverse music types, (2) they often fail to truly measure music perception, and (3) they lack support for systematic cross-modal comparisons.

Method: The framework automatically derives benchmarks from user-provided data by leveraging structured symbolic representations (e.g., MusicXML) and pre-defined question templates aligned with music pedagogy, generating multiple-choice QA pairs across audio, music notation images, and symbolic files.

Result: Demonstrated with the ChoraleBricks dataset; experimentally determined benchmark sizes for statistically reliable comparisons; text-only and white-noise baselines confirm the questions genuinely measure music perception.

Conclusion: MusICA-MetaBench advances cross-modal assessment of music perception for MLLMs by introducing a dataset-specific, on-demand benchmarking paradigm that is efficient, reliable, and pedagogically grounded.

Abstract: Music represents a cornerstone of human culture, existing digitally across diverse modalities, including audio, symbolic encodings (e.g., MIDI, MusicXML), and sheet music. Despite the advancement of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), current music benchmarks face three major limitations. First, large static benchmarks are resource-intensive to evaluate, and it remains unclear how their results transfer to diverse kinds of music beyond those included in the benchmark. Second, benchmarks claiming to measure "music understanding" often fail to require music perception. Third, they do not support systematic performance comparisons across musical modalities. To overcome these issues, we introduce the Music I Care About Meta-Benchmark (MusICA-MetaBench), a framework that automatically derives on-demand benchmarks directly from user-provided data. By leveraging structured symbolic representations (e.g., MusicXML) and our pre-defined question templates, we build multiple-choice question-answer pairs that probe music perception competencies, aligned with music pedagogy, across audio, music notation images, and symbolic files. We demonstrate our framework with the ChoraleBricks dataset, and experimentally determine benchmark sizes that ensure statistically reliable model comparisons for this setup. By comparing against text-only and white-noise baselines, we show our questions do measure music perception. Ultimately, MusICA-MetaBench represents a significant advancement in the cross-modal assessment of music perception for MLLMs. By proposing a dataset-specific benchmarking paradigm, it enables efficient on-demand evaluation of music perception capabilities.

</details>


### [164] [Fréchet Distance Loss on Speech Representations for Text-to-Speech Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06027)
*Ho-Lam Chung,Kuan-Po Huang,Bo-Ru Lu,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SR-FD is a training-time distributional regularizer for few-step flow-matching TTS that matches mean and covariance of Whisper/CTC features to reference statistics, reducing WER from 2.2279% to 1.4147% (36.5% relative) on Seed-TTS English without a discriminator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing few-step TTS models trained with local objectives (flow matching, reconstruction, stop prediction) never verify whether sampled speech follows the distribution of high-quality speech, leaving a gap in distribution-level supervision.

Method: SR-FD fine-tunes a tokenizer-free flow-matching autoregressive TTS model by computing Fréchet distance between the mean/covariance of Whisper and CTC features from synthesized speech (using the same few-step sampler as deployment) and offline reference statistics from three complementary content targets.

Result: On Seed-TTS English, four-step SR-FD fine-tuning reduces WER from 2.2279% (original four-step baseline) to 1.4147% (36.5% relative reduction), surpassing the original ten-step baseline at 1.7366%. Speaker similarity and objective quality are preserved, with gains from content substitutions across all prompt lengths.

Conclusion: SR-FD is an effective, discriminator-free distributional regularizer that improves intelligibility in few-step TTS while preserving speaker similarity and quality.

Abstract: Few-step diffusion and flow-matching text-to-speech (TTS) models are usually trained with local objectives, such as conditional flow matching, reconstruction, and stop prediction. These losses provide stable optimization, but they never ask whether sampled speech follows the distribution of high-quality speech. We propose Speech Representation Fr'echet Distance loss (SR-FD), a training-time distributional regularizer for tokenizer-free flow-matching autoregressive TTS. During fine-tuning, the model synthesizes speech with the same few-step sampler used at deployment, and SR-FD matches the mean and covariance of frozen Whisper and CTC features of this speech to reference statistics computed offline from three complementary content targets. The loss requires no discriminator and no inference-time computation. On Seed-TTS English, four-step SR-FD fine-tuning reduces WER from the original four-step VoxCPM2 baseline's 2.2279% to 1.4147%, a 36.5% relative reduction, and also surpasses the original ten-step baseline at 1.7366%; both gains are significant under an utterance-level paired bootstrap. Speaker similarity and objective quality proxies are preserved at the ten-step level, and an error analysis shows the gain comes from content substitutions across all prompt lengths. SR-FD is thus an intelligibility-improving distributional regularizer for few-step TTS.

</details>


### [165] [BlueMagpie-TTS: A Token-Efficient Tokenizer, Language Model, and TTS for Taiwanese-Accent Code-Switching Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06054)
*Ho Lam Chung,Bo-Xuan Zheng,Cheng-Chieh Huang,Cheng-Han Chang,Jung-Ching Chen,Lok-Lam Ieong,Ting-Lin Hsiao,Yu-Cheng Lee,Yi-Hsin Chung,Yu-Kai Guo,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: BlueMagpie-TTS adapts Taiwanese Mandarin TTS by introducing PangolinTokenizer (byte-level BPE tokenizer), Barbet (Traditional-Chinese LM), and a bridge to VoxCPM2's acoustic stack, achieving significant CER/WER reductions and preference in listening tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-the-shelf TTS systems fail on Taiwanese Mandarin due to accent mismatch, over-segmentation of common text, and degraded pronunciation at code-switching boundaries, all stemming from lack of text-side adaptation to the Taiwanese context.

Method: (1) PangolinTokenizer: byte-level BPE trained on Taiwan-context data, achieving lowest token rate (0.485 tokens/char) with smallest vocabulary. (2) Barbet: billion-parameter Traditional-Chinese LM as text-semantic frontend. (3) BlueMagpie-TTS: attaches Barbet to VoxCPM2's pretrained acoustic stack via a learned bridge, keeping acoustic stack fixed.

Result: On a 1000-sentence Taiwan-localized test set: CER from 11.45%→4.81% (58.0% reduction), WER from 14.83%→5.36% (63.9% reduction). Blind listening study (500 sentences, 10 listeners): 65.6% majority preference for BlueMagpie-TTS.

Conclusion: Text-side adaptation (tokenizer + language model) effectively addresses Taiwanese Mandarin TTS deficiencies, and the approach is non-invasive to the acoustic stack.

Abstract: Off-the-shelf TTS systems are poorly adapted to Taiwanese Mandarin. Their accent defaults to other Mandarin variants, their tokenizers over-segment common Taiwanese text, and their pronunciation degrades at code-switching boundaries where Chinese and English alternate within one utterance. These problems share one root: the text side lacks adaptation to the Taiwanese context. We address the text side from the bottom up. PangolinTokenizer, a byte-level BPE tokenizer trained on Taiwan-context data, reaches the lowest token rate (0.485 tokens/character) with the smallest vocabulary among nine tokenizers. Barbet, a billion-parameter Traditional-Chinese language model trained on PangolinTokenizer, serves as the text-semantic frontend and ranks first among comparable public models on a 14-task evaluation. BlueMagpie-TTS attaches Barbet to the pretrained acoustic stack of VoxCPM2 through a learned bridge, keeping the acoustic stack fixed. On a 1000-sentence Taiwan-localized test set, it lowers CER from 11.45% to 4.81% and WER from 14.83% to 5.36%, relative reductions of 58.0% and 63.9%. In a blind listening study on 500 of these sentences with ten listeners, 65.6% of majority votes prefer BlueMagpie-TTS.

</details>


### [166] [Determinantal point process sampling for bioacoustic active learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06063)
*Hugo Magaldi,Gabriel Dubus*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: CARE-DPP is a batch active learning method for bioacoustics that combines class-balanced uncertainty with embedding novelty, using DPP for diverse batch selection and annealing the uncertainty-novelty balance over the annotation budget, achieving 0.50 mean AULC vs 0.46 CoreSet baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eco-acoustic monitoring generates massive audio data, making active learning essential to reduce annotation effort while training reliable biodiversity classifiers efficiently.

Method: CARE-DPP combines class-balanced predictive uncertainty with embedding-space novelty, uses DPP for non-redundant batch selection, anneals uncertainty-novelty balance over budget (early: geometric coverage, later: classifier uncertainty), mixes top-quality candidates with decreasing random exploration, and employs an adaptive batch-size schedule (small early, large later).

Result: Mean development AULC of 0.50 for macro mAP, compared with 0.46 for the official CoreSet baseline, evaluated on BirdSet HSN, POW, UHH and ATBFL. Ablations show DPP batch diversification and adaptive schedule are largest contributors.

Conclusion: CARE-DPP effectively reduces annotation effort for bioacoustic classification, with DPP diversification and adaptive scheduling as key design choices driving performance gains over the CoreSet baseline.

Abstract: Eco-acoustic monitoring generates vast volumes of audio data, making active learning a promising approach for reducing annotation effort while efficiently training reliable biodiversity classifiers. This report presents CARE-DPP, a batch active-learning acquisition method submitted to BioDCASE Active Learning for Bioacoustics 2026 challenge. The method combines class-balanced predictive uncertainty with embedding-space novelty, while a determinantal point process (DPP) objective selects a high-quality and non-redundant acquisition batch. The uncertainty-novelty balance is annealed over the annotation budget: early cycles emphasize geometric coverage, whereas later cycles increasingly exploit classifier uncertainty. To mitigate unreliable early scores, the DPP candidate pool mixes top-quality candidates with a decreasing proportion of random exploration. An adaptive acquisition schedule uses smaller batches early and larger batches later. Evaluated over five repeats on the BirdSet HSN, POW and UHH subsets and on ATBFL, CARE-DPP obtains a mean development AULC of 0.50 for macro mAP, compared with 0.46 for the official CoreSet baseline. Ablations identify DPP batch diversification and the adaptive acquisition schedule as the largest contributors.

</details>


### [167] [Flow Matching-Based Speech Source Separation with Best-of-N Biometric Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06088)
*Anastasia Zorkina,Alexandr Anikin,Nikita Khmelev,Anastasiya Korenevskaya,Sergey Novoselov,Vladimir Volokhov,Maxim Korenevsky,Yuriy Matveev*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A conditional flow-matching-based speech separation method using a Transformer U-Net with frozen speaker encoder for ordered output, biometric candidate selection, and chunk-level alignment, achieving competitive separation quality and state-of-the-art downstream ASR and speaker verification performance on Libri2Mix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-channel speech separation faces three key challenges for real-world deployment: source permutation ambiguity, sampling variability of generative models, and difficulty in processing long recordings with chunk-wise inference.

Method: A conditional flow-matching approach producing ordered two-source output conditioned on the mixture. A frozen speaker encoder defines source order during training and is reused at inference for biometric best-of-N candidate selection and chunk-level channel alignment. The architecture is a Transformer U-Net variant.

Result: Competitive with strong baselines on SI-SDR, PESQ, and ESTOI; achieves the lowest cpWER for ASR and EER for speaker verification across all evaluated settings on Libri2Mix.

Conclusion: The proposed method addresses permutation ambiguity, generative sampling variability, and long-recording chunk-wise processing issues, delivering both strong separation metrics and superior downstream task performance.

Abstract: Single-channel speech separation remains challenging for real-world deployment due to source permutation ambiguity, sampling variability of generative models, and the difficulty of processing long recordings with chunk-wise inference. We address these issues with a conditional flow-matching-based method that produces an ordered two-source output conditioned on the mixture. A frozen speaker encoder defines the source order during training and is reused at inference for biometric best-of-$N$ candidate selection and chunk-level channel alignment. We evaluate separation quality on Libri2Mix benchmark using SI-SDR, PESQ, and ESTOI, and measure downstream impact using cpWER for automatic speech recognition and EER for speaker verification. The results show that the proposed Transformer U-Net variant is competitive with strong baselines in objective separation metrics and achieves the lowest downstream automatic speech recognition and speaker verification error rates in all evaluated settings.

</details>


### [168] [Learning-based Physics-Constrained Neural Kernel for Sound Field Estimation With Source-Position-Dependent Directional Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06274)
*Mattia Marella,Shoichi Koyama*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Proposes a learning-based physics-constrained neural kernel for sound field estimation using source-position-dependent implicit neural representation (INR) to improve generalization over snapshot-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing kernel-regression-based sound field estimation methods optimize the kernel function for single snapshot measurements, leading to strong overfitting and poor generalization to unseen source positions.

Method: Introduces a source-position-dependent implicit neural representation (INR) for the directional weighting function in the kernel, enabling the kernel to capture common directional patterns and generalize to unseen source positions in the target acoustic environment.

Result: The proposed method outperforms the snapshot-based method by estimating a directional weighting function that better matches the directivity of the target sound field.

Conclusion: Source-position-dependent INR improves generalization of physics-constrained neural kernels for sound field estimation compared to snapshot-based optimization.

Abstract: A learning-based physics-constrained neural kernel for sound field estimation is proposed. Sound field estimation aims to estimate the spatial distribution of an acoustic field from a discrete set of microphone measurements, which have a wide range of applications. Among existing sound field estimation methods, kernel-regression-based methods offer a flexible and principled framework for incorporating physical constraints and allow inference through linear operation. It is also possible to adapt the kernel function to the target acoustic environment by representing the directional weighting function as an implicit neural representation (INR) and optimizing hyperparameters using measurements. However, the kernel function is generally optimized for single snapshot measurements of the microphones, which can lead to strong overfitting and poor generalization. We propose a source-position-dependent INR for the directional weighting function, enabling the kernel function to capture common directional patterns and to generalize to unseen source positions in the target acoustic environment. Experimental results indicate that our proposed method outperforms the snapshot-based method by estimating a directional weighting function that matches the directivity of the target sound field.

</details>


### [169] [Designing Maintainable Hybrid Generative Systems: A Quantum-Inspired Approach to Automated Music Harmony Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06296)
*Josef Pavlicek*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A maintainable hybrid generative architecture for melody-to-harmony generation combines quantum-inspired candidate exploration with rule-based optimization, evaluated on structural coherence, functional agreement, harmonic similarity, and robustness. It preserves tonal structure and cadential behavior, improves coherence and stability without training data, and demonstrates that transparent hybrid generative systems can be systematically designed within Information Systems Development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To design a transparent, controllable, and maintainable hybrid generative system for automated music harmony generation from melody that balances generative flexibility with structural control, avoiding the black-box nature of purely neural approaches and the rigidity of purely rule-based systems.

Method: A hybrid architecture combining (1) quantum-inspired candidate exploration over overlapping melodic contexts for generative flexibility, and (2) explicit rule-based optimization for structural control. Evaluation uses reproducible metrics: structural coherence, functional agreement, harmonic similarity, and robustness.

Result: The approach produces harmonizations that preserve tonal structure and cadential behavior while allowing multiple valid harmonic realizations. The optimization layer improves structural coherence, stability, and predictability without requiring a training corpus.

Conclusion: Transparent and controllable hybrid generative systems can be systematically designed and evaluated within the context of Information Systems Development, demonstrating that rule-based optimization effectively compensates for the lack of training data in generative music tasks.

Abstract: This paper presents the design and evaluation of a maintainable hybrid generative architecture for automated music harmony generation from melody. The proposed system combines quantum-inspired candidate exploration over overlapping melodic contexts with explicit rule-based optimization to balance generative flexibility and structural control. The architecture is evaluated using explicit and reproducible metrics covering structural coherence, functional agreement, harmonic similarity, and robustness. The results show that the proposed approach produces harmonizations that preserve tonal structure and cadential behavior while allowing multiple valid harmonic realizations. Furthermore, the optimization layer improves structural coherence, stability, and predictability without requiring a training corpus. The study demonstrates that transparent and controllable hybrid generative systems can be systematically designed and evaluated within the context of Information Systems Development.

</details>


### [170] [InsideSSL: Understanding Self-Supervised Speech Representations using a Model-Centric Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06392)
*Samir Sadok,Xavier Alameda-Pineda*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: InsideSSL is a framework for analyzing SSL speech models (Wav2Vec2, HuBERT, WavLM) through layer-wise compression, geometry, robustness, and cross-layer functional transferability using Generative Compatibility Matrices, linking internal dynamics to downstream task encoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSL models like Wav2Vec2, HuBERT, and WavLM are widely used but their internal layer-wise dynamics remain poorly understood.

Method: Two-part model-centric framework: (1) task-agnostic analysis per layer (entropy/compression, curvature/geometry, robustness); (2) cross-layer Generative Compatibility Matrix (GCM) for functional transferability, plus linear probing for downstream task mapping.

Result: Training objectives produce distinct regimes of acoustic compression and manifold unfolding; GCM reveals stable phonetic cores, identity volatility, and deep-layer semantic pruning; layer topology dictates phoneme, pitch, and speaker encoding.

Conclusion: InsideSSL effectively bridges model-centric analysis with downstream task performance, uncovering how SSL objectives shape internal layer representations.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) models, such as Wav2Vec2, HuBERT, and WavLM, have become foundational across a wide range of speech and audio tasks. Despite their success, understanding their internal layer-wise dynamics remains an ongoing challenge. To address this, we propose a two-part model-centric framework called InsideSSL. First, we establish a task-agnostic analysis from three intrinsic per-layer perspectives: compression (entropy), geometry (curvature), and robustness to perturbations. We show that varying training objectives induce distinct regimes of acoustic compression and manifold unfolding. Second, we introduce the cross-layer Generative Compatibility Matrix (GCM) to evaluate functional transferability, exposing stable phonetic cores, identity volatility, and deep-layer semantic pruning. In addition to these evaluations, linear probing connects the model-centric perspective to downstream tasks, demonstrating how layer topology dictates phoneme, pitch, and speaker encoding.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [Multimodal Video-to-Music Recommendation via Semantic Retrieval and Temporal Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05971)
*Seungheon Doh,Minhee Lee,Sangmoon Lee,Ben Sangbae Chon,Juhan Nam*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: VTMR is a two-stage video-to-music recommendation framework that first retrieves candidates via multimodal alignment in an audio-visual-text space, then reranks them using temporal sequence modeling, achieving state-of-the-art results (R@10: 18.3, Median Rank: 46) and matching commercial baselines in human preference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve video-to-music recommendation by jointly leveraging comprehensive video and music signals and capturing both global semantic compatibility and fine-grained temporal correspondence between video and music.

Method: Two-stage framework: Stage 1 aligns video and music in a joint audio-visual-text embedding space and retrieves candidates via coarse global embeddings; Stage 2 reranks candidates by attending to temporal sequences of both modalities for fine-grained alignment.

Result: Stage 1 improves R@10 from 14.2 to 15.9 and Median Rank from 75 to 58 over the strongest baseline; Stage 2 further boosts R@10 to 18.3 and Median Rank to 46. Human study shows VTMR matches commercial baseline in overall preference and beats generative baseline in music quality.

Conclusion: The two-stage approach with coarse retrieval followed by temporal reranking yields complementary gains, demonstrating the value of richer query encoding and temporal alignment for video-to-music recommendation.

Abstract: We present VTMR, a two-stage framework for Video-To-Music Recommendation. In Stage~1, VTMR aligns comprehensive video and music signals in a joint audio-visual-text representation space and efficiently retrieves semantically compatible candidates using coarse global embeddings. In Stage~2, it reranks the retrieved candidates by attending to the temporal sequences of both video and music, thereby capturing fine-grained temporal correspondence. Evaluated on the video-to-music recommendation task, the multimodal retrieval stage improves R@10 from 14.2 to 15.9 and Median Rank from 75 to 58 over the strongest baseline; the temporal reranker further boosts R@10 to 18.3 and Median Rank to 46, demonstrating complementary gains from richer query encoding and temporal alignment. A human preference study confirms that VTMR is on par with a commercial baseline in overall preference, while outperforming a generative baseline in music quality.

</details>


### [172] [Precise Video-to-Audio Generation with Cross-Modal Alignment in Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06405)
*Thanh V. T. Tran,Ngoc-Son Nguyen,Luong Tran,Long-Khanh Pham,Paarth Neekhara,Shezheen Hussain,Van Nguyen*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: Flowley is an end-to-end, single-stage training architecture for video-to-audio generation that uses Progressive Soft-masked Cross-Attention for audio-visual synchronization and SoundCap for improved sound-aware captioning, achieving state-of-the-art performance on VGGSound without needing multi-stage training or pretrained alignment modules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing V2A methods suffer from high computational costs due to multi-stage training, or lose fine-grained temporal cues by converting visual input to text for leveraging pretrained text-to-audio models.

Method: Flowley is an end-to-end single-stage training architecture combining visual features with textual prompts. It introduces Progressive Soft-masked Cross-Attention for embedding audio-visual synchronization into attention with zero extra cost, and SoundCap, a plug-and-play pipeline for generating sound-aware captions.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on VGGSound across multiple metrics without any pretrained audio-visual alignment modules. With SoundCap, it exceeds the strongest closed-source methods in zero-shot audio quality.

Conclusion: Single-stage end-to-end training with carefully designed attention mechanisms and improved captioning can outperform complex multi-stage approaches and even closed-source methods in video-to-audio generation.

Abstract: Video-to-audio (V2A) generation aims to synthesize realistic audio that is both semantically consistent with and temporally synchronized to a silent video. Despite recent progress, many methods still rely on multi-stage training, resulting in high computational costs and long runtimes, or transform visual input into text to leverage pretrained text-to-audio models, sacrificing fine-grained temporal cues. To overcome these limitations, we propose Flowley, an end-to-end, single-stage training architecture that produces soundtracks by combining visual features with textual prompts. Crucially, we introduce Progressive Soft-masked Cross-Attention, which embeds audio-visual synchronization directly within its attention mechanism, adding zero additional computational cost compared to standard attention layers. We further observe that existing V2A benchmarks lack sound-oriented descriptive captions, which can potentially degrade the quality of the synthesized audio. To remedy this, we propose SoundCap, a plug-and-play pipeline for creating detailed, sound-aware captions that guide the model. Remarkably, without integrating any pretrained audio-visual alignment modules, Flowley achieves state-of-the-art performance on VGGSound across multiple metrics. Moreover, by incorporating SoundCap, we further exceed the performance of the strongest existing close-sourced methods in terms of audio quality in the zero-shot setting.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [173] [Prompt-to-Paper: Agentic AI System for Bioinformatics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05456)
*Ramsha Kamran,Maheera Amjad,Zartasha Mustansar,Arsalan Shaukat,Salma Sherbaz,Muhammad U. S. Khan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prompt-to-Paper is a multi-agent framework that addresses the evaluation gap in AI-generated manuscripts by combining deterministic retrieval-augmented generation, an autonomous coding agent for real experiments, and an eight-dimensional automated quality scorer, achieving submission-formatted PDFs at ~$0.31/paper with an average quality improvement of +17.96 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based manuscript generation systems suffer from three deficiencies: claims are not grounded in verifiable literature, experimental results are fabricated, and there is no standardized framework to assess if AI-generated manuscripts meet real-world publication quality and rigor.

Method: The multi-agent framework includes: (1) a deterministic RAG pipeline with section-aware relevance scoring and snowball citation expansion grounding claims in 60–100 papers; (2) an autonomous coding agent executing real computational biology experiments; (3) an eight-dimensional automated quality scorer with hallucination penalties and approximate reference statistics; and (4) a quality-driven improvement loop with context-rich reviser routing iterations to researcher actions and deep research cycles every ten iterations.

Result: Validated on five bioinformatics case studies, all produced submission-formatted PDFs with zero out-of-range citations. The improvement loop raised manuscript quality by an average of +17.96 points on a 0–100 scale (max +26.04). Human reviewers scored manuscripts at an average of 7.0/10. Cost is approximately $0.31 per paper.

Conclusion: Prompt-to-Paper successfully addresses the evaluation gap in AI-generated manuscripts by combining verifiable grounding, real experiment execution, and standardized quality assessment, producing publication-ready manuscripts at extremely low cost with measurable quality improvements.

Abstract: While recent advances in large language models have enabled end-to-end automated manuscript generation, existing systems suffer from three critical deficiencies: (i) generated claims are not deterministically grounded in verifiable literature, (ii) experimental results are frequently fabricated rather than executed, and (iii) there exists no standardized, multi-dimensional framework to assess whether AI-generated manuscripts meet the quality and rigor required for real-world publication. We present Prompt-to-Paper, a multi-agent framework that directly addresses this evaluation gap through three integrated innovations. First, a deterministic retrieval-augmented generation pipeline with section-aware relevance scoring and snowball citation expansion grounds every claim in a verifiable corpus of 60--100 papers. Second, an autonomous coding agent executes real computational biology experiments replacing synthetic outputs with genuine numerical results. Third, an eight-dimensional automated quality scorer, benchmarked with approximate reference statistics from published papers and augmented with explicit hallucination penalties, provides standardized, reproducible quality assessments. The quality-driven improvement loop uses a context-rich reviser that routes each iteration to one of three researcher actions and fires a deep research cycle every ten iterations to re-run experiments and re-manuscript from stronger outputs. We validate the system on five bioinformatics case studies; all five cases compiled submission-formatted PDFs with zero out-of-range citations. The improvement loop raises manuscript quality by an average of +17.96 points on a 0--100 scale (maximum +26.04. As partial external checks, a human reviewer scored the five manuscripts at an average of 7.0 out of 10. Complete manuscripts are produced at approximately 0.31 USD per paper.

</details>


### [174] [ArtisanCAD: An Industrial-Level CAD Agent with Expert-Grounded Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05750)
*Yunhan Xu,Qifeng Wu,Xunjin Li,Yuanwei Bin,Qingsong Yao,Jianghang Gu,Guan Wang,Weihao Lv,Huiyu Yang,Wenfa Luo,Jiao Xiang,Yuntian Chen,Shiyi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: We present a skill-guided industrial CAD agent (algname) that uses CAD intermediate representation (CAD-IR) to distill expert procedural knowledge into reusable parameterized skills. CAD-IR bridges ambiguous or high-level text prompts to executable CAD operations, enabling iterative refinement with multi-view visual feedback and production-grade B-Rep generation. It reduces Chamfer Distance from 14.83 to 9.88 on Text2CAD benchmark and generates editable CATIA-native models for complex automotive components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-CAD methods struggle with ambiguous, underspecified, or high-level prompts and fail to exploit expert procedural knowledge naturally available in industrial workflows (e.g., CATIA recordings, macro logs, engineering notes).

Method: CAD-IR (CAD Intermediate Representation) as an executable procedural encoding; expert knowledge distillation into reusable parameterized skills; retrieval, instantiation, and revision of CAD-IR; CATIA-MCP backend execution; multi-view visual feedback for iterative refinement.

Result: On Text2CAD, mean Chamfer Distance improved from 14.83 to 9.88 for intermediate prompts. On four complex automotive components, CAD-IR enables distillation of expert CATIA recordings into skills, producing editable CATIA-native B-Rep models for new variants.

Conclusion: CAD-IR effectively bridges ambiguous textual intent and executable CAD construction, enabling production-grade CAD generation from natural language by leveraging expert procedural knowledge.

Abstract: Computer-aided design (CAD) for industrial components requires long-horizon procedural modeling, robust feature dependencies, editable parametric geometry, and production-grade B-Rep execution. Existing text-to-CAD methods have made promising progress in generating CAD programs from natural-language descriptions, but they still struggle when user prompts are ambiguous, underspecified, or only describe high-level design intent. They also rarely exploit expert procedural knowledge naturally available in industrial workflows, such as CATIA operation recordings, macro logs, drawing notes, and engineering descriptions. We present \algname, a skill-guided industrial CAD agent with expert-grounded knowledge distillation. The core of \algname is CAD intermediate representation (CAD-IR), an executable procedural representation that encodes parameters, ordered operations, MCP tool bindings, dependencies, generated entities, and verification rules. CAD-IR plays two key roles: it first serves as the carrier for distilling expert CAD procedures into reusable parameterized skills; then it provides a procedural scaffold that turns vague or intermediate-level prompts into complete executable CAD operations. \algname retrieves expert-derived skills, instantiates and revises CAD-IR, executes the resulting procedure through a dedicated CATIA-MCP backend, and uses multi-view visual feedback for iterative refinement, and finally generates production-ready B-Rep models. On the Text2CAD benchmark, CAD-IR improves generation from intermediate prompts by reducing mean Chamfer Distance from $14.83$ to $9.88$, showing its ability to bridge ambiguous textual intent and executable CAD construction. On four complex automotive components, CAD-IR enables expert CATIA recordings to be distilled into reusable skills, allowing \algname to generate editable CATIA-native B-Rep models for new variant requests.

</details>


### [175] [From Graphs to Gradients: Physics-Inspired Structural Attribution for Cyber-Physical IoT Systems and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05563)
*Spyridon Evangelatos,Christos Diou,Georgios Th. Papadopoulos,Evangelos Markakis,Panagiotis Sarigiannidis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an energy-based, undirected framework inspired by statistical mechanics for interpretable causal explanation in cyber-physical IoT systems. It models variable dependencies without recovering a directed causal graph, enabling dependency-aware attribution and perturbation analysis. Experiments on an industrial IoT testbed show higher accuracy, robustness, and scalability over graph-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional explainability methods rely on correlations and require explicit directed causal structures, which are impractical for large-scale, hybrid cyber-physical systems with feedback loops and partial observability. The paper aims to provide robust, causal explanations without recovering a full causal graph.

Method: The framework models variable dependencies via an undirected, energy-based representation inspired by statistical mechanics. It analyzes variations in the energy landscape to attribute influence of individual components and reason about perturbation effects across hybrid interactions, without reconstructing a directed causal graph.

Result: Empirical evaluation on an industrial IoT testbed with hybrid continuous and discrete variables demonstrates higher attribution accuracy, improved robustness, and better scalability compared to state-of-the-art graph-based approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides valuable, dependency-aware explanations supporting human interpretation and downstream tasks. While not intended to fully recover generative dynamics, it is applicable beyond IoT security to other high-dimensional cyber-physical and socio-technical systems requiring principled, structural explanations.

Abstract: Interpretable explanation methods in Artificial Intelligence aim to uncover the underlying causes and their effects, enabling a deeper understanding of why a system behaves in a certain way under different inputs. Unlike traditional explainability methods, which mainly highlight correlations between input and output variables, causal explanation focuses on interventional questions. By doing so, it provides more robust insights, helping users understand automated decisions, especially in high-risk domains. Recovering an explicit directed causal structure, however, is often impractical in large-scale, hybrid cyber-physical systems with feedback loops and partial observability. This paper introduces a novel framework inspired by statistical mechanics that instead models variable dependencies through an undirected, energy-based representation of cyber-physical IoT systems. Our approach enables rigorous dependency-aware attribution by analysing how variations in the energy landscape reflect the influence of individual components, without recovering a directed causal graph. It also supports reasoning about perturbation effects across hybrid interactions, providing reliable explanations of abnormal behaviours. We empirically examined our framework through simulations on an industrial IoT testbed with hybrid continuous and discrete variables, demonstrating higher attribution accuracy, improved robustness and better scalability than state-of-the-art graph-based approaches. While the attributions are not intended to fully recover the system's generative dynamics, they provide valuable, dependency-aware explanations supporting both human interpretation and downstream predictive and diagnostic tasks. Although demonstrated in industrial IoT security, our framework also applies to other high-dimensional cyber-physical and socio-technical systems requiring principled, structural explanations.

</details>


### [176] [CSTutorBench: Benchmarking Small Language Models as Tutors for Block-Based Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05571)
*H. Chad Lane,Bryson Kageler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CSTutorBench is a benchmark for evaluating small language models as CS tutors in block-based programming (VEX VR), finding that models struggle with deeper pedagogical behaviors and that model family and instruction-tuning matter more than parameter count for tutoring quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying LLMs as AI tutors in K-12 settings raises privacy, cost, and proprietary model concerns. SLMs offer an alternative but selecting the right model for domain-specific educational contexts (e.g., block-based programming, which is underrepresented in training data) remains difficult without appropriate benchmarks.

Method: CSTutorBench includes 17 scenario-based questions scored against a pedagogical rubric grounded in tutoring and feedback research, using a human-in-the-loop LLM-as-judge pipeline. Evaluated 11 models (4B-120B parameters) and tested a targeted prompt revision grounded in educational prompt engineering research.

Result: Models performed well on surface-level criteria (vocabulary, tone) but struggled with deeper pedagogical behaviors (avoiding answer leakage, engaging with student debugging history). Model family and instruction-tuning approach predicted tutoring quality better than parameter count. Prompt revision improved scores for 10 of 11 models.

Conclusion: Context-specific, pedagogically grounded benchmarks like CSTutorBench are valuable for selecting SLMs in educational deployment; larger models do not necessarily equate to better tutoring performance.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly explored as AI tutors, yet deploying them in K-12 settings raises concerns around privacy, cost, and reliance on proprietary models. Small language models (SLMs) offer a promising alternative, but selecting the right model for a specific educational context remains difficult, particularly when the target domain, such as block-based programming, is largely absent from model training data. We introduce CSTutorBench, a benchmark for evaluating language models as CS tutors in VEX VR, a block-based robotics environment. The benchmark comprises 17 scenario-based questions scored against a pedagogical rubric grounded in established tutoring and feedback research, with a human-in-the-loop LLM-as-judge pipeline for evaluation. Preliminary findings across 11 models (4B-120B parameters) reveal that models perform well on surface-level criteria such as vocabulary and tone but struggle with deeper pedagogical behaviors, particularly avoiding answer leakage and engaging with student debugging histories. In our sample, model family and instruction-tuning approach appear to be better predictors of tutoring quality than parameter count alone, though the small number of models limits the strength of this conclusion. A targeted prompt revision grounded in recent educational prompt engineering research improved scores for 10 of 11 models. These results underscore the value of context-specific, pedagogically grounded benchmarks for SLM selection in educational deployment.

</details>


### [177] [Foundation Models for Automatic CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05573)
*J de Curtò,Victoria Guillén,I. de Zarzà*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces LLMForge, a text-to-CAD framework using LLMs/VLMs to generate parametric 3D mechanical designs.
