It evaluates 7 foundation models on 97 engineering problems under two critique regimes (IterTracer with analytic visual metrics and IterVision with VLM-based semantic reasoning). Top models achieve ~0.89 mean score and ~99% mesh success; VLM critique yields 100% watertight meshes but struggles with rotationally symmetric geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically evaluate foundation models for automatic CAD generation of mechanical parts and develop a benchmark-driven pipeline that bridges natural-language specifications to parametric 3D designs, addressing the lack of unified evaluation in this emerging area.

Method: LLMForge integrates JSON-schema validation, analytic feature scoring, mesh synthesis, and multi-round iterative refinement. Two critique regimes: IterTracer (Phong-shaded ray-trace renderer with analytic metrics like silhouette IoU, hole visibility, edge clearance, aspect-ratio conformance) and IterVision (Qwen2.5-VL-72B as VLM semantic critic using chain-of-thought visual reasoning). Evaluated on 97 problems across 4 geometry families using 7 LLMs.

Result: Top 4 models under IterTracer achieve overall mean in [0.885, 0.890] with 98.97% mesh success rate, showing compact instruction-tuned models can match larger systems. IterVision achieves 100% watertight mesh generation on the leading model but reveals systematic difficulty with rotationally symmetric geometries where visual and semantic scoring diverge.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the viability of LLM-driven CAD generation, identifies failure modes in rotational symmetry, and discusses implications for benchmark design, CAD-oriented prompting, and scalable industrial workflows.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable the automatic generation of parametric 3D designs from natural-language specifications. This chapter presents an empirical study of foundation models for automatic Computer-Aided Design (CAD) generation of mechanical parts, using a unified evaluation pipeline and a curated benchmark of 97 engineering design problems. We introduce LLMForge, a multi-model text-to-CAD framework integrating JSON-schema validation, analytic feature scoring, mesh synthesis, and multi-round iterative refinement, studied under two critique regimes. IterTracer uses a Phong-shaded ray-trace renderer with analytic visual metrics (silhouette IoU, hole visibility, edge clearance, aspect-ratio conformance) for lightweight geometry-aware feedback across rounds. IterVision replaces the analytic scorer with a VLM semantic critic (Qwen2.5-VL-72B) that evaluates rendered views via chain-of-thought visual reasoning, assessing spatial coherence and design intent. On a benchmark spanning four canonical geometry families (plates with holes and bolt circles, multi-feature boxes, flanged cylinders, and L-brackets), we evaluate seven foundation models: DeepSeek-V3.2, Qwen3-235B-A22B, Llama-3.3-70B, Gemma-3-27B, GLM-4.5, MiniMax-M2.1, and INTELLECT. Under IterTracer, the four highest-ranked models form a tight cluster (overall mean in [0.885, 0.890]) with 98.97% mesh success, showing that compact instruction-tuned models can match substantially larger systems. VLM-based critique in IterVision yields 100% watertight mesh generation on the leading model while surfacing systematic difficulty on rotationally symmetric geometries such as cylinders, where visual and semantic scoring diverge most. We discuss benchmark design, failure modes, CAD-oriented prompting, and implications for industrial workflows and scalable automated mechanical design.

</details>


### [178] [Narrative World Model: Narratology-Grounded Writer Memory for Long-Form Fiction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05577)
*Mohammad Saifullah,Thomas Kornmaier,Taaha Kazi,Vasu Sharma,Aditya Sanjiv Kanade,Aanand Kumar Yadav*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NWM is a writer-memory system using narratology-grounded typed temporal-state graphs and query-conditioned hybrid retrieval to answer multi-hop narratological questions about evolving story state, significantly outperforming existing temporal-knowledge-graph and retrieval baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing general-purpose retrieval and agent-memory systems represent entities and facts but lack narratological structure, making them unable to answer multi-hop questions about evolving story state (e.g., who knows a secret and when, whether a setup paid off, how relationships shifted).

Method: NWM pairs a narratology-grounded typed temporal-state graph with query-conditioned hybrid retrieval. To isolate memory performance, every system is read through a single held-constant Opus 4.8 reader using only chapter-safe evidence, on a reproducible public corpus and validated multi-hop benchmark.

Result: NWM substantially and significantly outperforms Graphiti/Zep, GraphRAG, and flat retrieval on multi-hop narratological QA. The advantage is representational: it persists when using NWM's own extractor in the baseline, and is traced to narratology-grounded structure and query-conditioned retrieval, not graph size or extractor quality.

Conclusion: NWM's narratological representation design is the key driver of improved multi-hop narrative QA performance, not extraction quality or graph size.

Abstract: Long-form fiction writers need memory that answers multi-hop questions about evolving story state: who knows a secret and when they learned it, whether an event preceded the narration that revealed it, whether a setup paid off, and how a relationship shifted. General-purpose retrieval and agent-memory systems represent entities and facts but not the narratological structure these questions turn on, so they surface the wrong evidence or none at all. We introduce the Narrative World Model (NWM), a writer-memory system that pairs a narratology-grounded typed temporal-state graph with query-conditioned hybrid retrieval. To measure memory rather than the answerer, we read every system through a single held-constant Opus 4.8 reader over only that system's chapter-safe evidence, on a reproducible public corpus and a validated multi-hop benchmark, and we compare against the strongest existing temporal-knowledge-graph agent-memory framework, Graphiti/Zep (Rasmussen et al., 2025). NWM substantially and significantly outperforms this baseline on multi-hop narratological QA across both corpora, and far exceeds GraphRAG and flat retrieval. The advantage is representational rather than an artifact of extraction: it survives rebuilding the baseline with NWM's own extractor, and traces to its narratology-grounded structure and query-conditioned retrieval, not to graph size or extractor quality.

</details>


### [179] [FirstResearch: Auditable Question Formation for LLM Scientific Discovery Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05682)
*Yufeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FirstResearch introduces a structured Research Question Certificate framework that makes LLM-generated scientific research questions auditable by requiring primitive definitions, assumptions, mechanism models, tensions, falsifiable hypotheses, decisive tests, and failure update rules, outperforming baselines on 40 research topics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based scientific discovery agents often propose research questions that sound plausible but lack transparency in their underlying mechanisms, falsifiers, or assumptions, making them difficult for scientists to inspect and validate before downstream execution.

Method: FirstResearch is a first-principles research-question formation framework that produces a structured Research Question Certificate containing: primitive definitions, assumptions, a mechanism model, a tension/contradiction, a falsifiable hypothesis, a minimal decisive test, and a failure update rule. Evaluated on 10 LLM-agent research topics against three baselines (AI co-scientist, Agent Laboratory, AI Scientist-v2) using a DeepSeek-blind-judge protocol and a Gemini-2.5-Flash independent rescore.

Result: FirstResearch scored 4.86/5 vs. 4.38/5 for the strongest baseline under DeepSeek-blind-judge; the ranking was preserved under Gemini rescoring. Pearson agreement between judges was 0.865. Ablation shows certificate-only scoring reached 4.90/5 (DeepSeek) and 4.88/5 (Gemini), while removing certificates dropped below 1/5 under both judges.

Conclusion: Explicit derivation constraints (certificates) are a promising mechanism for making LLM-generated scientific questions more auditable. Results are preliminary and rely on LLM judges rather than human experts.

Abstract: LLM systems for scientific discovery increasingly assist with ideation, literature synthesis, experiment planning, and report generation, but the first research question they propose can remain difficult to audit: it may sound plausible without exposing the mechanism, falsifier, or assumption that a scientist should inspect. We introduce FirstResearch, a first-principles research-question formation framework for scientific LLM agents whose core artifact is a structured Research Question Certificate. The certificate records primitive definitions, assumptions, a mechanism model, a tension or contradiction, a falsifiable hypothesis, a minimal decisive test, and a failure update rule, making the proposed question inspectable before downstream execution. On ten LLM-agent research topics, FirstResearch outperforms controlled prompt-level baselines inspired by AI co-scientist, Agent Laboratory, and AI Scientist-v2 under a primary DeepSeek-blind-judge protocol. A Gemini-2.5-Flash independent-judge rescore of the same 40 baseline packages preserves the system-level ranking, with FirstResearch scoring 4.86/5 versus 4.38/5 for the strongest baseline and Pearson agreement of 0.865 on average score. A one-repeat ablation checkpoint further suggests that the certificate-centered core is the strongest component: certificate-only scoring reaches 4.90/5 under DeepSeek and 4.88/5 under Gemini, while removing certificates drops below 1/5 under both judges. These results are preliminary and use LLM judges rather than human domain experts, but they support a narrow scientific-discovery claim: explicit derivation constraints are a promising mechanism for making LLM-generated scientific questions more auditable. Code, prompts, saved outputs, and reproduction scripts are available at https://github.com/louiswang524/FirstResearch.

</details>


### [180] [Memory in the Loop: In-Process Retrieval as ExtendedWorking Memory for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05690)
*Yusuf Khan,Carlo Lipizzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moving memory inside the agent loop (in-process, ~100µs latency) eliminates the 83x latency tax of networked stores, reducing redundant actions from 7.2/12 to 0.0/12 and improving recall from 0/5 to 3.6-4.8/5, though the bottleneck shifts to embedding (~200-400ms).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language agents store memory outside the observe-reason-act loop, queried at most once per turn due to high latency (10s-100s ms). This limits the frequency and immediacy of memory access, potentially causing redundant actions and poor recall.

Method: The paper proposes in-process memory (embedded in the agent process, ~100µs latency) vs. networked memory (cloud store, ~110ms). It tests across GPT-5-class models with a bounded window, measuring recall, redundancy, and latency. Also pairs in-process store with a local embedder to address the embedding bottleneck.

Result: In-loop memory eliminates redundant actions (0.0/12 vs. 7.2/12 with cloud store, p=0.0079). Recall improves from 0/5 to 3.6-4.8/5. Store ops at p50 80-165µs; zero writes lost (244/244). Embedding is the dominant cost (~200-400ms network), reducible to ~40µs with local embedder.

Conclusion: Latency is a property of where the store lives, not the in-loop pattern. Fast enough in-process memory effectively becomes extended working memory by the parity principle. The bottleneck shifts from retrieval to embedding, suggesting local embedding as the next target.

Abstract: Language agents run a loop - observe, reason, act - but the memory they reason over sits outside it: a store queried at most once per turn. We study the regime where memory moves inside the loop, read and written on every step. The obstacle has always been latency: networked stores answer in tens to hundreds of milliseconds, and in-loop retrieval can inflate end-to-end latency by up to 83x when retrieval is expensive. Prior work manages that cost rather than questioning it: serving-layer scheduling hides it, "memory-first" designs ration retrieval to once per turn. We argue latency is a property of where the store lives, not the in-loop pattern: an in-process store answers in ~100us, three orders of magnitude below the network regime, and at that speed the per-step tax collapses. By the extended-mind thesis's parity principle, a store fast enough to be constantly and directly available becomes extended working memory, not a tool the agent merely consults. The premise is causal: holding a fixed per-turn memory-latency budget and varying only the store's answer speed, redundant actions rise monotonically with latency - 0.0 of 12 at in-process speed, 7.2 of 12 at a 110ms cloud round trip (gpt-5-nano, gpt-5-mini; exact permutation p=0.0079). We demonstrate the regime end-to-end: across four GPT-5-class models under a bounded window, recall improves from 0/5 to 3.6-4.8/5 with in-loop memory, store ops at p50 80-165us - though an instructed restate-every-reply baseline also solves it perfectly, at a token cost that grows with the working set. The store never lost a fact in any run (244 of 244 writes kept); every miss traces to the agent's read policy, not the store. Our measurements also relocate the bottleneck: the dominant per-step cost is embedding (~200-400ms over the network); pairing the in-process store with a small local embedder returns the complete operation to a measured ~40us.

</details>


### [181] [Akashic: A Low-Overhead LLM Inference Service with MemAttention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05708)
*Yang Liu,Zhaokai Luo,Huayi Jin,Ruozhou He,Chenchen Hong,Zhiyong Wang,Yifei Liu,Yunfei Gu,Chentao Wu,Junhao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Akashic is a low-overhead memory system with MemAttention that organizes context into bounded chunks, models semantic relationships, and uses hardware-software co-designed memory placement to improve accuracy, throughput, and sustainable request rate in LLM-based agent systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Replaying full interaction histories across multi-turn, multi-tool, cross-session workflows is impractical due to high prefill cost, context limits, and task-relevant evidence being buried in irrelevant content, degrading serving efficiency and output quality.

Method: Akashic uses MemAttention to organize context into bounded chunks and model semantic relationships across chunks, preserving cross-chunk evidence without full history rewriting. It applies hardware-software co-designed memory placement to co-locate likely co-retrieved chunks, reducing fragmentation and I/O overhead.

Result: Across four workloads and three model sizes, Akashic improves task accuracy by up to 10.2 points, throughput by up to 1.21x, and sustainable request rate by up to 1.88x over strong prior memory baselines.

Conclusion: Akashic effectively addresses long-context inefficiencies in LLM-based agent systems through chunk-based semantic memory organization and hardware-aware placement, significantly outperforming prior memory systems.

Abstract: Recent LLM-based agent systems continuously accumulate context across multi-turn interactions, tool invocations, and cross-session workflows. Replaying the full history for every request quickly becomes impractical: long contexts increase prefill cost, may exceed context limits, and often bury task-relevant evidence in irrelevant content, degrading both serving efficiency and output quality. We propose Akashic, a low-overhead memory system built around MemAttention, which organizes context into bounded chunks and models semantic relationships across chunks, preserving cross-chunk evidence without repeatedly rewriting the full history. Akashic further applies hardware-software co-designed memory placement to co-locate likely co-retrieved chunks, reducing retrieval fragmentation and I/O overhead. Across four representative workloads and three model sizes, Akashic improves task accuracy by up to 10.2 points, throughput by up to 1.21x, and sustainable request rate by up to 1.88x over strong prior memory baselines.

</details>


### [182] [Synthetic Consumer Insight Generation with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05761)
*Stephen L. France,Pia. A. Albinsson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs can generate synthetic consumer data for projective marketing techniques, comparing LLM-generated responses to human responses on city tourism perceptions. Results show broad topic overlap but significant differences in linguistic style and diversity patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting real consumer data is costly, time-consuming, and difficult to scale. The research explores whether LLMs can serve as a scalable alternative for generating synthetic consumer data for projective techniques used in marketing.

Method: LLM-generated responses were tested across multiple projective tasks, different LLMs, prompting strategies, and temperature settings. Responses were compared to human responses from a study on city tourism perceptions using linguistic measures, diversity/concentration metrics, topic models, and top-term analyses.

Result: Substantial overlap was found between human and LLM responses in broad topics and associations, but important differences emerged in style, linguistic structure, and how diversity is generated.

Conclusion: The paper provides recommendations on utilizing LLMs for synthetic consumer data generation, how model/prompt choices affect quality, and acknowledges the limitations of this approach.

Abstract: Modern data-driven marketing relies on large amounts of consumer data, yet collecting such data can be costly, time-consuming, and difficult to scale. This research examines whether large language models (LLMs) can be used to generate synthetic consumer data for projective techniques, a set of methods designed to elicit consumer associations, emotions, wants, and needs. We test LLM-generated responses across multiple projective tasks, LLMs, prompting strategies, and temperature settings, and compare them with human responses from a primary research study on perceptions of city tourism destinations. Human and LLM responses were analyzed using linguistic measures, diversity and concentration metrics, topic models, and top-term analyses. The results show substantial overlap between human and LLM responses in broad topics and associations, but also important differences in style, linguistic structure, and the way diversity is generated. Recommendations are given on how to best utilize LLMs for generating synthetic consumer data, how model and prompt choices shape response quality, and on recognizing the limitations of LLM synthetic consumer data generation.

</details>


### [183] [Beyond Static Evaluation: Building Simulation Environments for Scalable Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05773)
*Akshay Arora,Ishan Nigam,Ashutosh Aggarwal,Shefali Bansal,Krishna Singh,Sweta Kumari,Nikhil Mittal,Shariq Farhan,Siddarth Malreddy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgenticAI-Supervisor is an API/UI-driven RL Gym environment for training and evaluating LLM-based autonomous agents, using verifiable execution outcomes and multi-dimensional reward shaping with anti-reward-hacking mechanisms, demonstrated in a Customer Support Agent case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional static evaluation fails to capture multi-step decision-making capabilities of LLMs evolving into autonomous agents.

Method: An API and UI-driven RL Gym environment that decouples environment creation from scalable execution, uses verifiable execution outcomes for high-fidelity trace generation, applies multi-dimensional reward shaping, and implements internal state validation/testing to mitigate reward hacking.

Result: Demonstrates consistent closed-loop feedback for model optimization through a Customer Support Agent case study.

Conclusion: Future work will focus on Computer Use, Tool Use, automated "stumping," and edge-case generation capabilities.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous agents, traditional static evaluation fails to capture multi-step decision-making. We introduce AgenticAI-Supervisor, an API and UI-driven RL Gym environment that decouples environment creation from scalable execution. By moving to verifiable execution outcomes, the platform generates high-fidelity traces and applies multi-dimensional reward shaping. Critically, our framework mitigates reward hacking through rigorous internal state validation and testing. This work provides a first look at our platform's core capabilities through a Customer Support Agent case study demonstrating a consistent closed-loop feedback for model optimization. Future work will focus on advanced features such as Computer Use, Tool Use, automated "stumping", and edge-case generation.

</details>


### [184] [Beyond the Leaderboard: A Synthesis of Tool-Use, Planning, and Reasoning Failures in Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05775)
*Wael Albayaydh,Rui Zhao,Ivan Flechais*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper synthesizes 27 LLM agent evaluation papers (2023-2026) across 19 benchmarks into a unified taxonomy of six failure clusters: tool invocation errors, planning failures, long-horizon degradation, multi-agent coordination failures, safety/security failures, and measurement validity problems. Key findings show failures compound nonlinearly with task length, sub-task performance doesn't guarantee end-to-end success, and additional scaffolding doesn't consistently improve reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reported benchmark gains in LLM agents often obscure recurring failure modes that appear across unrelated evaluation efforts. There was no existing synthesis integrating evidence across tool use, planning, long-horizon reasoning, multi-agent coordination, safety, and measurement validity into a single unified taxonomy.

Method: The authors synthesized 27 benchmark, taxonomy, and audit papers (2023-2026) spanning 19 distinct benchmarks. They iteratively derived a taxonomy by grouping independently reported error categories into themes corresponding to distinct stages of the agent reasoning-to-action pipeline, identifying six failure clusters.

Result: Identified six failure clusters: (1) tool invocation and parameter-level errors, (2) planning and constraint-satisfaction failures, (3) long-horizon degradation from context accumulation, (4) multi-agent coordination failures, (5) safety and security failures, (6) measurement validity problems. Found that failures compound nonlinearly with task length, sub-task performance doesn't translate to end-to-end success, and additional scaffolding doesn't consistently improve reliability.

Conclusion: Substantial progress exists in single-turn tool use, short-horizon web navigation, and narrowly scoped coding tasks. However, the identified failure patterns suggest fundamental limitations in current LLM agent architectures, particularly under extended task horizons and complex multi-step settings.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly evaluated on their ability to use tools, plan multi-step tasks, coordinate with other agents, and operate over extended horizons. Reported benchmark gains often obscure recurring failure modes documented across otherwise unrelated evaluation efforts. This paper synthesizes 27 benchmark, taxonomy, and audit papers (2023-2026), spanning 19 distinct benchmarks, into a cross-cutting taxonomy of agent limitations. To our knowledge, this is the first synthesis that integrates evidence across tool use, planning, long-horizon reasoning, multi-agent coordination, safety, and measurement validity into a single, unified taxonomy of LLM agent limitations. We identify six failure clusters: (1) tool invocation and parameter-level errors, (2) planning and constraint-satisfaction failures, (3) long-horizon degradation from context accumulation, (4) multi-agent coordination failures, (5) safety and security failures under adversarial or underspecified conditions, and (6) measurement validity problems. The taxonomy was derived iteratively by grouping independently reported error categories into themes corresponding to distinct stages of the agent reasoning-to-action pipeline. Across the literature, we find that failures compound nonlinearly with task length, that strong performance on individual sub-tasks does not reliably translate into end-to-end success, and that additional scaffolding does not consistently improve reliability. At the same time, substantial progress has been demonstrated in single-turn tool use, short-horizon web navigation, and narrowly scoped coding tasks.

</details>


### [185] [Controlling Tool Use with Heading-Specific Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05790)
*Yuqi Chen,Vincent Siu,Yang Liu,Dawn Song,Chenguang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether tool-use decisions in LLMs have stable internal representations that can be extracted and manipulated via steering vectors. While steering vectors from heading-anchor positions causally control tool invocation, geometric analysis shows the representations are diffuse, bimodal, and lack clean linear structure, distinguishing them from parametrically grounded concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether tool-use decisions in LLMs have stable internal representations that can be extracted and manipulated, given that tools exist only in context at inference time and have no direct encoding in model weights.

Method: Extracted steering vectors from heading-anchor positions across five open-source models and three domains; performed geometric analysis of tool-invocation representations.

Result: Steering vectors exert bidirectional causal control over tool invocation and suppress unnecessary tool use most effectively where parametric reasoning suffices. However, tool-invocation steps show diffuse, bimodal alignment with suppression vectors rather than clean negative alignment, and different tool types have largely distinct internal signatures with low cross-tool feature overlap.

Conclusion: Tool-use steering vectors exhibit geometric properties distinct from parametrically grounded concepts, likely due to the non-parametric nature of tools. The relationship between geometric irregularity and causal effectiveness remains an open question.

Abstract: Tool-augmented large language models extend their capabilities beyond parametric knowledge through external tools, but tend to invoke them unnecessarily. We investigate whether tool-use decisions have any stable internal representation that can be extracted and manipulated, a question that is non-trivial given that tools exist entirely in context at inference time and have no direct encoding in model weights. We show that steering vectors extracted from heading-anchors positions exert bidirectional causal control over tool-invocation behavior across five open-source models and three domains, suppressing unnecessary tool use most effectively in domains where parametric reasoning suffices. However, geometric analysis reveals that this causal effectiveness does not correspond to clean linear structure: tool-invocation steps exhibit diffuse, bimodal alignment with the suppression vector rather than the consistent negative alignment a linear encoding account would predict, and different tool types recruit largely distinct internal signatures with low cross-tool feature overlap. We hypothesize these geometric properties are indicative of the non-parametric nature of tools, and distinguish tool-use steering vectors from those extracted for parametrically grounded concepts. The relationship between this geometric irregularity and the observed causal effectiveness remains an open question.

</details>


### [186] [From Passive Retrieval to Active Memory Navigation: Learning to Use Memory as a Structured Action Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05794)
*Yue Xu,Yutao Sun,Yihao Liu,Mengyu Zhou,Jiayi Qiao,Lu Ma,Kai Tang,Wenjie Wang,Xiaoxi Jiang,Guanjun Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NapMem is a framework that treats long-term user memory as a structured action space (via a multi-granularity memory pyramid with tools) rather than passive context, using reinforcement learning to train agents to actively select and inspect memory at appropriate granularities, achieving competitive results on memory-intensive benchmarks while preserving general reasoning abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory systems expose user memory through passive retrieval interfaces, making the model a mere consumer of pre-selected evidence rather than an active participant in memory selection, which limits personalization in conversational agents.

Method: NapMem organizes user history into a linked multi-granularity memory pyramid (raw conversations, typed memory records, topic tracks, user profiles) with provenance relations, exposes these levels via memory tools, and trains the agent via memory-tool reinforcement learning to select and inspect memory at appropriate granularities based on queries and intermediate evidence.

Result: Competitive performance on PersonaMem-v2, LongMemEval, and LoCoMo across diverse memory-intensive tasks; general reasoning and tool-use abilities largely preserved on non-memory tasks; analyses on storage, inference cost, tool-use behavior, and ablations over navigation, memory granularity, and RL training provided.

Conclusion: Long-term user memory benefits from coupling structured storage with a learned policy for using memory at the appropriate granularity.

Abstract: Long-term user memory is essential for personalized conversational agents, yet many memory systems still expose memory through passive retrieval interfaces, making the model a consumer of pre-selected evidence. We introduce NapMem, a framework for learning to use long-term user memory as a structured action space rather than passively retrieved context. NapMem organizes user history into a linked multi-granularity memory pyramid, where raw conversations, typed memory records, topic tracks, and user profiles are connected through provenance relations, and exposes these levels through memory tools. The agent is trained to select memory according to the query and intermediate evidence, allowing it to inspect different memory granularities before answering. Experiments on PersonaMem-v2, LongMemEval, and LoCoMo show that a NapMem agent trained with memory-tool reinforcement learning is competitive across diverse memory-intensive tasks, while evaluations on non-memory tasks suggest that the learned policy largely preserves general reasoning and tool-use abilities. Additional analyses examine storage, inference cost, tool-use behavior, and ablations over navigation, memory granularity, and RL training. Our results suggest that long-term user memory benefits from coupling structured storage with a learned policy for using memory at the appropriate granularity.

</details>


### [187] [TurnOPD: Making On-Policy Distillation Turn-Aware for Efficient Long-Horizon Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05804)
*Yuhang Zhou,Kai Zheng,Haoling Li,Dengyun Peng,Can Xu,Jingjing Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TurnOPD introduces a turn-level budgeting strategy for on-policy distillation that improves training efficiency and accuracy in long-horizon agentic tasks by adaptively controlling rollout depth and rebalancing KL supervision across turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla on-policy distillation (OPD) for long-horizon agent tasks suffers from two inefficiencies: (1) full-horizon rollouts waste resources on tail turns with weak/noisy KL supervision, and (2) trajectory-level KL objectives over-emphasize shallow tokens, leaving deeper decision turns under-trained after initial alignment.

Method: TurnOPD proposes two budget controllers: (1) adaptive rollout-depth budgeting — uses probe-based turn statistics to dynamically determine rollout length; (2) progressive turn-normalized loss budgeting — gradually shifts KL weighting from token-level to turn-balanced supervision.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Multi-Hop Search show TurnOPD achieves superior validation accuracy under equal wall-clock training budgets and advances the accuracy–time frontier beyond vanilla OPD.

Conclusion: Turn-level budgeting strategies effectively address the inefficiencies of vanilla OPD in long-horizon agentic tasks, enabling more efficient on-policy distillation without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student policy by matching a stronger teacher on the student's own trajectories, offering a promising framework for language agent training. However, its application to long-horizon agentic tasks remains insufficiently explored. We identify two key inefficiencies in vanilla agent OPD: (1) full-horizon rollouts often waste wall-clock resources on tail turns that provide weak and noisy KL supervision, and (2) trajectory-level KL objectives concentrate most of the loss on shallow tokens, leaving deeper decision turns under-trained once initial behaviors are aligned. To address these challenges, we propose TurnOPD, a turn-level budgeting strategy for efficient on-policy distillation of long-horizon agents. TurnOPD consists of two budget controllers: adaptive rollout-depth budgeting, which uses probe-based turn statistics to determine rollout length, and progressive turn-normalized loss budgeting, which gradually shifts KL weighting from token-level to turn-balanced supervision. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Multi-Hop Search with task-specialized teacher models show that TurnOPD achieves superior validation accuracy under equal wall-clock training budgets and advances the accuracy--time frontier beyond vanilla OPD.

</details>


### [188] [Onnes: A Physics-Grounded Multi-Agent LLM Simulator for Cryogenic Fault Diagnosis in Quantum Computing Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05805)
*Praneeth Narisetty,Uday Kumar Reddy Kattamanchi,Shiva Nagendra Babu Kore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Onnes is a digital-twin simulator of a dilution refrigerator that combines a forward physics model with a learned noise fingerprint to drive a multi-agent LLM operations layer for cryogenic fault diagnosis. Zero-shot LLM agents match supervised ML on detection but lag on classification; curated few-shot demonstrations and self-consistency voting raise accuracy from 0.685 to 0.990, matching the supervised classifier (0.985) with no parameter updates. A sim-to-real check achieves 6.4% false-alarm rate and 100% recall on injected physics faults.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dilution refrigerator fault diagnosis is dominated by threshold alarms that only indicate something is wrong, not what the fault is, motivating a more intelligent diagnostic approach for superconducting quantum computer infrastructure.

Method: Onnes uses a physics-grounded digital-twin simulator (forward physics model + learned real-fridge noise fingerprint) driving a live multi-agent LLM operations layer, evaluated against a supervised ML classifier across 1000-turn evaluation with six physics-grounded fault classes. Contrastive few-shot demonstrations and self-consistency voting are employed to improve performance.

Result: Zero-shot LLM panel matches supervised classifier on detection but trails on classification (concentrating errors on confusable faults). Few-shot demonstrations + self-consistency voting raise classification accuracy from 0.685 to 0.990, matching the supervised classifier (0.985) with no parameter updates and six labeled demonstrations. Continuous monitor catches every developing fault within one poll interval. Sim-to-real check yields 6.4% false-alarm rate and 100% recall on injected faults.

Conclusion: LLM-agent-based fault diagnosis with few-shot demonstrations can match supervised ML performance without parameter updates, demonstrating a viable path for intelligent cryogenic fault diagnosis in quantum computing infrastructure.

Abstract: Dilution refrigerators are the enabling infrastructure of superconducting quantum computers, yet their fault diagnosis is still dominated by threshold alarms that report that something is wrong, not what. We present Onnes, a physics-grounded digital-twin simulator of a dilution refrigerator (a forward physics model with a learned real-fridge noise fingerprint) that drives a live multi-agent LLM operations layer, and use it for a controlled head-to-head between a zero-shot LLM agent panel and a supervised ML classifier on cryogenic fault diagnosis. The twin couples a real dilution-cooling floor, a noise-and-correlation fingerprint learned from real BlueFors logs, and six physics-grounded fault classes, three engineered to overlap on temperature but separate on flow and pressure. Across a 1000-turn evaluation the zero-shot panel shows no significant difference from the classifier on detection but trails on classification, its errors concentrating on the confusable faults. Curated contrastive few-shot demonstrations and self-consistency voting then raise classification accuracy from 0.685 to 0.990, matching the supervised classifier (0.985) with no parameter updates and six labeled demonstrations; an ablation attributes the gain almost entirely to the demonstrations. Run as a continuous monitor across a nine-run fault-by-seed sweep, the agent catches every developing fault within one poll interval, and a confidence gate suppresses pre-onset false alarms whose rate is backend-dependent. As a first sim-to-real check, a detector trained purely on real BlueFors telemetry posts a real-hardware false-alarm rate of 6.4% and 100% recall on physics faults injected onto real held-out windows. All numbers are drawn verbatim from released run logs.

</details>


### [189] [StateFuse: Deterministic Conflict-Preserving Memory for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05844)
*Sergey Volkov,Yang Li,Ye Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateFuse is a conflict-aware replicated memory contract built on standard CRDT merge that preserves conflicting observations (rather than collapsing them) using immutable history, explicit conflict objects, and deterministic predicate contracts. Evaluated on 282 conflict-bearing questions from MemoryAgentBench, it matches accuracy with baselines but keeps contradictions visible, enabling safer abstention and auditable correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent systems accumulate conflicting observations across branches, retries, and replicas, yet practical memory layers collapse disagreement behind opaque overwrite rules that are difficult to inspect or correct.

Method: StateFuse is built on standard OpSet/CRDT merge without introducing new join algebra. It defines immutable history, explicit conflict objects, exact (claim_id) and semantic (claim_ref) correction handles, deterministic predicate contracts, and projection-time resolution that cannot rewrite replicated state.

Result: On a 282-question official conflict-bearing MemoryAgentBench slice, all methods tied on answer accuracy, but conflict-preserving surfaces kept contradictions visible while collapsed surfaces did not. In controlled agent loops with uniform verification, preserving ambiguity enabled safer abstention and correction. Correction-handle ablation showed semantic handles matter when exact prior identifiers are unavailable.

Conclusion: StateFuse is best supported as a safer public memory contract for contradiction surfacing, abstention, and auditable correction—not as a universal accuracy gain.

Abstract: Agent systems accumulate conflicting observations across branches, retries, and replicas, yet many practical memory layers still collapse disagreement behind overwrite rules that are difficult to inspect or correct. We present StateFuse, a conflict-aware replicated memory contract built on standard OpSet/CRDT merge. StateFuse does not introduce a new join algebra; it defines an agent-facing semantics layer with immutable history, explicit conflict objects, exact and semantic correction handles (claim_id / claim_ref), deterministic predicate contracts, and projection-time resolution that cannot rewrite replicated state.
  We evaluate StateFuse against flat multi-value, raw-log, provenance-style, and collapsed baselines under matched resolver and verification policies. On a 282-question official conflict-bearing MemoryAgentBench slice, the compared methods tie on answer accuracy, but conflict-preserving surfaces keep contradictions visible while collapsed surfaces do not. In a controlled agent loop with uniform verification, preserving ambiguity enables safer abstention and correction than early collapse. A correction-handle ablation further shows that semantic handles matter when exact prior identifiers are unavailable.
  The resulting claim is narrow: StateFuse is best supported as a safer public memory contract for contradiction surfacing, abstention, and auditable correction, not as a universal accuracy gain.

</details>


### [190] [Uncovering Latent Depression Severity for Binary Depression Detection via Advantage-weighting Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05901)
*Manning Gao,Tingyi Liu,Leheng Zhang,Haifeng Hu,Yuncheng Jiang,Sijie Mai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a fine-grained multimodal framework with Binary Advantage-weighting Ranking Loss for automatic depression detection, achieving SOTA on D-vlog and LMVD datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic depression detection using audio-visual data struggles with overlapping feature distributions and unclear decision boundaries.

Method: A multimodal framework with temporal encoder and mutual transformer for cross-modal fusion, plus Binary Advantage-weighting Ranking Loss with two components: Advantage-weighted Separation (mining hard pairs via pairwise prediction difference) and Advantage-weighted Compactness (minimizing intra-class variance).

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on D-vlog and LMVD datasets by reconstructing latent ordinal structure through hard pair prioritization.

Conclusion: The proposed loss function effectively optimizes latent space distribution for better depression detection.

Abstract: Automatic depression detection using audio-visual data faces significant challenges, particularly in disentangling overlapping feature distributions and establishing robust decision boundaries. To address this, we propose a fine-grained multimodal framework featuring a temporal encoder and a mutual transformer to facilitate deep cross-modal fusion. Our core contribution is the Binary Advantage-weighting Ranking Loss, which optimizes the latent space distribution through two complementary mechanisms: Advantage-weighted Separation, which mines hard pairs by computing a pairwise prediction difference matrix and dynamically weighting them based on their difficulty; and Advantage-weighted Compactness, which minimizes intra-class variance to force features to cluster around their respective class centers. Extensive experiments on D-vlog and LMVD demonstrate that our model reconstructs the latent ordinal structure by prioritizing hard pairs, thereby achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [191] [PCBWorld: A Benchmark Environment for Engine-Grounded PCB Design Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05915)
*Hyungseok Song,Junseok Park,Won-Seok Choi,Seohui Bae,Han-Seul Jeong,Youngjoon Park,Soonyoung Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PCBWorld is an open-source PCB routing environment built on KiCad EDA, supporting interactive routing via RL policies and LLM agents with DRC feedback, including a benchmark of synthetic and real boards with 8 evaluation metrics. Agents using PCBWorld outperformed baselines, with RL policies transferring zero-shot from synthetic to real boards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-based PCB routing methods still lag behind traditional rule-based routers, largely due to the lack of realistic, interactive training environments that provide native design rule feedback comparable to what human engineers use.

Method: PCBWorld is built on the KiCad EDA engine, enabling agents to interactively route boards via native operations with DRC feedback. It supports both RL policies and tool-using LLM agents. PCBWorld-Bench provides three dataset families (synthetic and 679 real open-source boards) and scores completed boards with eight engine-checked evaluation metrics.

Result: Interactive agents in PCBWorld outperformed grid-action RL policies and open-loop LLM baselines. An RL policy trained solely on synthetic boards achieved zero-shot transfer to real boards, approaching the performance of rule-based routers.

Conclusion: The engine-grounded, interactive approach of PCBWorld is a promising foundation for advancing PCB routing ability for both RL and LLM agents, bridging the gap between learning-based methods and traditional rule-based routers.

Abstract: PCB routing is the task of connecting the nets of a board with copper traces under strict design rules, yet learning-based methods still lag behind rule-based routers. We introduce PCBWorld, an open-source engine-grounded PCB routing environment built on the KiCad EDA engine. As a human engineer does, agents in PCBWorld interactively route a board through the engine's native operations, using its Design Rule Check (DRC) feedback to keep the routing within the design rules. The environment supports both RL policies and tool-using LLM agents. Alongside the environment, PCBWorld-Bench provides three dataset families in KiCad's native board format (.kicad_pcb), covering two types of controllable synthetic instances and 679 real open-source boards. It scores any completed board with eight engine-checked evaluation metrics, regardless of the routing method. In our experiments, agents in PCBWorld consistently outperformed grid-action RL policies and open-loop LLM baselines, and an RL policy trained only on synthetic boards transferred zero-shot to real boards, approaching rule-based routers. These results position the engine-grounded, interactive approach of PCBWorld as a promising foundation for advancing the routing ability of both RL and LLM agents.

</details>


### [192] [SearchEyes: Towards Frontier Multimodal Deep Search Intelligence via Search World Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05943)
*Zhengbo Jiao,Yiming Cheng,Yilei Jiang,Kaituo Feng,Rui Huang,Tianyi Jiang,Juanxi Tian,Jiapeng li,Qunzhong Wang,Tailai Chen,Qianshan Wei,Chuan Xiao,Shanyu Rong,Yangfu Li,Yanhan Zhou,Yunpu Ma,Yifan Zhang,Xiangyu Yue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SearchEyes introduces a unified framework for training multimodal search agents using a typed knowledge graph as a simulated search world, Perception-Knowledge Chains (PKC) for constrained multi-hop path sampling, and Hop-Anchored Policy Optimization (HaPO) for step-level credit assignment, achieving SOTA performance on six multimodal benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pipelines have a structural disconnect where training data, search environments, and reward signals are constructed independently, leading to discarded structural metadata, reliance on irreproducible external engines, and sparse RL rewards at the trajectory level.

Method: Uses a typed knowledge graph as a simulated search world backbone. Proposes Perception-Knowledge Chains (PKC) to sample constrained multi-hop paths over Wikidata5M's visual-knowledge intersection, retaining hop-level entity metadata. Proposes Hop-Anchored Policy Optimization (HaPO) for step-level credit assignment using these anchors without a separate process reward model.

Result: SearchEyes achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-source multimodal search agents on six benchmarks; SearchEyes-27B improves over the strongest open-source baseline by 6.2 points on average.

Conclusion: The unified framework effectively addresses the structural disconnect in training multimodal search agents by tying together data construction, environment simulation, and reward signaling through the knowledge graph backbone.

Abstract: Training multimodal search agents to perform multi-hop reasoning remains challenging due to a fundamental structural disconnect: existing pipelines construct training data, search environments, and reward signals independently, causing synthesized structural metadata to be discarded, environments to rely on irreproducible external engines, and RL rewards to remain sparse at the trajectory level. We present \textbf{SearchEyes}, which uses a typed knowledge graph as the backbone of a \emph{simulated search world} that unifies all three components. We propose \textbf{Perception-Knowledge Chains (PKC)} to sample constrained multi-hop paths over the visual-knowledge intersection of Wikidata5M, retaining hop-level entity metadata that simultaneously defines a self-contained search world and step-level reward anchors. We further propose \textbf{Hop-Anchored Policy Optimization (HaPO)}, which reuses these anchors for step-level credit assignment without a separately trained process reward model. Experiments on six multimodal knowledge-intensive benchmarks show that SearchEyes achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-source multimodal search agents, with SearchEyes-27B improving over the strongest open-source baseline by 6.2 points on average.%

</details>


### [193] [Integrating knowledge graphs and multilingual scholarly corpora for domain-adaptive LLMs in SSH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05956)
*Adam Faci,Alessio Miaschi,Anne Combe,Pascal Cuxac,Francesca Frontini,Nicolas Larrousse,Stéphane Pouyllau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an ongoing use case within the LLMs4EU project and ALT-EDIC infrastructure that adapts foundation models for SSH research, focusing on bibliographic discovery, multilingual access, and literature synthesis, with evaluation combining quantitative benchmarks and qualitative expert assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating LLMs into SSH research (bibliographic discovery, literature synthesis) raises challenges around methodological rigor, epistemic responsibility, disciplinary diversity, and multilingual access that need to be addressed through domain-sensitive adaptation.

Method: The use case involves adapting foundation models within the LLMs4EU project and ALT-EDIC infrastructure, following the LLMs4EU evaluation protocol with quantitative benchmarking (retrieval, summarization, traceability, hallucination detection) and qualitative assessment by Digital Humanities experts, embedded within a legal and ethical compliance framework.

Result: Ongoing work; the evaluation framework combines independent quantitative benchmarking and qualitative expert assessment to explore how regulation-aware generative AI can support SSH scholarship.

Conclusion: Domain-sensitive and regulation-aware generative AI, embedded within research infrastructures and compliance frameworks, can support SSH scholarship while preserving reliability, epistemic responsibility, and addressing disciplinary and multilingual challenges.

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into scientific research workflows, particularly for bibliographic discovery and literature synthesis, raises significant methodological, epistemic and regulatory challenges for the Social Sciences and Humanities (SSH), especially with regard to disciplinary diversity, multilingual access to sources and the evaluation of results. This paper presents an on-going use case developed within the European project LLMs4EU and the ALT-EDIC infrastructure, aimed at adapting foundation models to SSH research practices and supporting tasks such as question answering, comparative document analysis and literature review. The evaluation framework follows the LLMs4EU protocol and encompasses both independent quantitative benchmarking (retrieval, summarisation, traceability and hallucination detection) and a qualitative assessment involving a panel of Digital Humanities experts. By embedding model adaptation within research infrastructures and a structured legal and ethical compliance framework, the use case explores how domain-sensitive and regulation-aware generative AI can support SSH scholarship while preserving reliability and epistemic responsibility.

</details>


### [194] [Auto-DSM Under the Lens: A Black-Box Evaluation Framework for LLM-Based DSM Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05985)
*Niels Potters,Theo Hofman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a black-box evaluation framework to assess LLMs' ability to generate Design Structure Matrices (DSMs) from technical documentation, using structural metrics, classification metrics, and stability measures with a Composite Quality Score (Q). Experiments on two datasets reveal LLMs produce plausible DSMs with high reproducibility under well-structured inputs but remain sensitive to ambiguity and prompt formulation, highlighting both potential and limitations for Auto-DSM pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The closed-source nature of current Auto-DSM pipelines lacks reproducibility and transparency. The paper aims to provide a systematic, reproducible benchmarking methodology to audit LLM-based DSM generation and establish foundations for integrating LLMs into MBSE workflows.

Method: A black-box evaluation framework compares generated DSMs (GEN-DSMs) against manually validated ground-truth DSMs (GT-DSMs) using both single-run and multi-run perspectives. Metrics include structural (Completeness, Correctness, Coupling Density), classification (Selective Accuracy, Abstention Coverage), and stability measures (Entropy, Fleiss' κ), synthesized into a Composite Quality Score (Q). Controlled experiments on two datasets (a fictive abstract system and a real-world refrigerator decomposition) vary phrasing, parameter-dataset alignment, and system complexity.

Result: LLMs can generate structurally plausible DSMs with high reproducibility given well-structured inputs. However, performance degrades with ambiguous phrasing, inconsistent dependency definitions, and prompt formulation variations. Systematic hallucination and abstention failure sources are identified.

Conclusion: The framework provides a transparent benchmark for auditing Auto-DSM pipelines. LLM-driven DSM automation shows promise but currently has limitations; the work lays foundations for integrating LLM-based decomposition methods into model-based systems engineering (MBSE) workflows.

Abstract: This paper presents a black-box evaluation framework to systematically assess the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate Design Structure Matrices (DSMs) from structured technical documentation. Motivated by the closed-source nature of current Auto-DSM pipelines, the framework introduces a reproducible methodology that benchmarks generated DSMs (GEN-DSMs) against manually validated ground-truth matrices (GT-DSMs). The evaluation integrates both single-run and multi-run perspectives, combining structural metrics (Completeness, Correctness, Coupling Density), classification metrics (Selective Accuracy, Abstention Coverage), and stability measures (Entropy, Fleiss' $κ$). To synthesize these aspects, a Composite Quality Score (Q) is proposed. Controlled experiments are conducted on two datasets: a fictive abstract system and a real-world refrigerator decomposition, covering variations in phrasing, parameter-dataset alignment, and system complexity. Results show that LLMs can produce structurally plausible DSMs and achieve high reproducibility under well-structured inputs, but remain sensitive to ambiguity, inconsistent dependency definitions, and prompt formulation. The findings highlight systematic sources of hallucination and abstention failure, demonstrating both the potential and current limitations of LLM-driven DSM automation. The proposed framework provides a transparent benchmark for auditing Auto-DSM pipelines and establishes foundations for integrating LLM-based decomposition methods into model-based systems engineering (MBSE) workflows.

</details>


### [195] [AgoraSim: A Hybrid Agent-Based Modeling Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05999)
*Chung-Chi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgoraSim is a hybrid agent-based modeling framework that converts textual/multimodal scenarios into editable ABM configurations, runs mixed populations of LLM, vision-language, custom, random, and classical agents, and enables comparative analysis against matched classical reference dynamics through shared structured decision objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-agent simulations produce natural-language social scenarios that are easy to instantiate but hard to compare with explicit social dynamics and are often overread as predictions.

Method: AgoraSim resolves textual/multimodal artifacts into editable ABM configurations; runs ratio-controlled populations mixing LLM, vision-language, custom-endpoint, random, and classical agents; and uses shared structured decision objects for common action spaces, interaction protocols, metrics, and audit records.

Result: The framework enables scenario trajectory inspection, comparison of modeling assumptions, and identification of cases warranting empirical validation, exposed through local UI, Python SDK/CLI, and REST API.

Conclusion: AgoraSim bridges LLM-agent simulations and classical ABM by providing a hybrid framework for scenario-oriented social reaction analysis with structured comparability and auditability.

Abstract: LLM-agent simulations make natural-language social scenarios easy to instantiate, but their outputs can be overread as predictions and are often difficult to compare with explicit social dynamics. We present AgoraSim, a hybrid agent-based modeling framework for scenario-oriented social reaction analysis. AgoraSim resolves textual or multimodal artifacts into editable ABM configurations, runs ratio-controlled populations that mix LLM, vision-language, custom-endpoint, random, and classical agents, and compares the same scenario against matched classical reference dynamics. All agents emit a shared structured decision object, enabling common action spaces, interaction protocols, metrics, and audit records. Exposed through a local UI, Python SDK/CLI, and REST API, AgoraSim helps users inspect scenario trajectories, compare modeling assumptions, and identify cases that warrant empirical validation.

</details>


### [196] [Information Limits and Attractor Dynamics in Economies of Frontier LLM Agents: A Pre-Registered Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06001)
*Cheng Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A pre-registered experiment on Claude Opus 4.8 multi-agent economies tests two predictions: an information-theoretic capacity region for wealth growth under coupling (confirmed within 46 millinats) and a mean-field residual-scaling law for population misalignment (structural negative — domain not found; goal dispersion collapsed across all 72 runs). Full protocol and cache released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether frontier language-model agents (Claude Opus 4.8) in coupled multi-agent economic systems obey two quantitative predictions: (1) an information-theoretic capacity region linking wealth growth to claimed information, and (2) a mean-field residual-scaling law for population goal dispersion under incentive and control levers.

Method: A pre-registered two-part experiment using parimutuel-coupled economies of Claude Opus 4.8 agents. Predictions, acceptance bands, and decision rules were frozen in a public git chain before any run. All results derive mechanically from cached model outputs. Total API cost: $138.76.

Result: Result 1 (confirmed): Gap law G_a - G_b = I_a - I_b holds to ≤46 millinats (band: 50); coalition value submodular under conditional independence, flipped supermodular by XOR synergy control (0.62 ≥ ln2/2 nats); joint growth ceiling G_S ≤ H(X) binds exactly; best-informed agent absorbs nearly all wealth in 4/5 seeds. Result 2 (structural negative): Residual-scaling test returned "domain not found"; goal dispersion collapsed (V ≤ 4.85 vs. floor 5.31) across all 72 runs; lever response was a step function, not smooth; boundary cells bistable with seed-selected outcomes.

Conclusion: The information-theoretic capacity region for coupled multi-agent wealth growth is empirically confirmed at tight tolerance. However, the smooth mean-field residual-scaling regime for population misalignment is not realized by any tested LLM population; goal dispersion collapses universally. The negative result suggests fundamental limitations of current LLM agents in maintaining the noise-maintained-dispersion regime assumed by mean-field models.

Abstract: We report a pre-registered, two-part experiment on small economies of frontier language-model agents (Claude Opus 4.8), testing two quantitative predictions about coupled multi-agent systems: an information-theoretic capacity region for wealth growth under market coupling, and a mean-field residual-scaling law for population misalignment under incentive and control levers. All predictions, acceptance bands, and decision rules were frozen in a public git chain before any run; every reported number re-derives mechanically from cached model outputs; the entire experiment cost $138.76 in metered API spend and is re-runnable at zero cost from the cache.
  Result 1 (confirmation): in parimutuel-coupled economies, relative growth equals relative claimed information -- the gap law G_a - G_b = I_a - I_b holds to a worst-case 46 millinats (pre-registered band: 50) across four perception structures; coalition value is submodular exactly where channels are conditionally independent, and a designed XOR synergy control flips it supermodular by 0.62 >= ln2/2 nats, with agents reasoning out the joint bit; the joint growth ceiling G_S <= H(X) binds exactly; and the best-informed agent absorbs essentially the whole wealth pool in 4/5 market seeds.
  Result 2 (structural negative): the residual-scaling test returned "domain not found." In all 72 population runs, goal dispersion collapsed (V -> 0; maximum 4.85 against a frozen floor of 5.31), the population's response to the two levers was a step function across the dominance boundary rather than a smooth response, and cells near the boundary were bistable with seed-selected outcomes. No tested LLM population at any capability level realizes the noise-maintained-dispersion regime the smooth mean-field model assumes. We release the full protocol, pre-registration chain, call cache, and analysis code.

</details>


### [197] [PolyWorkBench: Benchmarking Multilingual Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06008)
*Hongliang Li,Yijin Liu,Zhiwei Zhang,Zihe Liu,Xinyue Lou,Jinan Xu,Fandong Meng,Kaiyu Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyWorkBench is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on multilingual long-horizon workplace workflows across five domains, revealing that state-of-the-art agents suffer significant performance degradation when handling multilingual inputs and outputs compared to monolingual settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks assume monolingual agentic execution, but real-world workflows often involve multilingual inputs and outputs; the interaction between multilinguality and agentic execution remains underexplored.

Method: PolyWorkBench includes 67 tasks across commerce, knowledge work, legal analysis, localization, and manufacturing, requiring agents to process multilingual inputs, perform iterative reasoning, invoke tools, and produce structured outputs. A hybrid evaluation framework combining structural grading, executable verification, and LLM-based semantic assessment is proposed.

Result: State-of-the-art LLM agents show significant performance degradation in multilingual workflow settings compared to monolingual counterparts; multilinguality introduces compounding effects across reasoning and execution steps.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the importance of jointly modeling language variation and procedural decision-making in agent evaluation, suggesting that multilinguality compounds errors across reasoning and execution.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have shown strong performance in long-horizon tasks that require planning, tool use, and interaction with external environments. However, most existing benchmarks implicitly assume a monolingual setting, where the entire execution process, including reasoning, tool invocation, and output generation, is conducted within a single language. In contrast, real-world applications often involve multilingual inputs and outputs within a unified workflow, yet the interaction between multilinguality and agentic execution remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce PolyWorkBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on multilingual long-horizon workplace workflows. PolyWorkBench consists of 67 tasks across five domains, including commerce, knowledge work, legal analysis, localization, and manufacturing, where agents must process heterogeneous multilingual inputs, perform iterative reasoning, invoke external tools, and produce structured outputs. To enable comprehensive evaluation, we propose a hybrid framework that combines structural grading, executable verification, and LLM-based semantic assessment. This design allows us to capture both functional correctness and linguistic consistency across complex workflows. Empirical results show that state-of-the-art LLM agents suffer significant performance degradation in multilingual workflow settings compared to monolingual counterparts. Our analysis suggests that multilinguality introduces compounding effects across reasoning and execution steps, highlighting the importance of jointly modeling language variation and procedural decision-making in agent evaluation.

</details>


### [198] [Reward-Density Heuristic for Dynamic Multi-Vehicle Routing: Performance and Computational Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06066)
*Manish Kolachalam,Rani Malhotra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an Efficiency heuristic for dynamic multi-vehicle routing that combines VRP and Orienteering Problem elements. Tested on drone task allocation and taxi dispatch, it matches metaheuristic solution quality while requiring 2-3 orders of magnitude less computation time, establishing Pareto dominance on the reward-versus-compute frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic, online VRPs with time constraints are practically important for logistics and urban mobility, yet existing metaheuristic solutions are computationally expensive for real-time deployment. The authors seek a lightweight yet effective alternative.

Method: A reward-density heuristic (Efficiency heuristic) is proposed for dynamic multi-vehicle assignment. Evaluated on autonomous drone task allocation and urban taxi dispatch across multiple fleet sizes and task scales, compared against four classical construction heuristics and three metaheuristics (ALNS, GA, SA).

Result: The Efficiency heuristic matches the solution quality of the best metaheuristics while requiring two to three orders of magnitude less planning time, achieving Pareto dominance over all competing methods on the reward-versus-compute frontier.

Conclusion: In dynamic, time-constrained routing environments, carefully designed greedy heuristics can match sophisticated search procedures at a fraction of the computational cost, making them preferable for online deployment.

Abstract: The Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) and its variants represent some of the most practically consequential optimization challenges in modern logistics and urban mobility. In this study, we address a dynamic, online variant combining elements of the VRP and the Orienteering Problem (OP), in which a fleet of vehicles must maximise cumulative reward collected within a fixed time horizon while continuously replanning as new tasks arrive. We propose and evaluate a reward-density heuristic for dynamic multi-vehicle assignment, referred to as the Efficiency heuristic. We evaluate this formulation across two application domains: autonomous drone task allocation and urban taxi dispatch, across multiple fleet sizes and task scales. The proposed method is compared with four classical construction heuristics and three metaheuristic algorithms (Adaptive Large Neighbourhood Search, Genetic Algorithm, and Simulated Annealing), all evaluated under identical conditions. Across all tested configurations, the Efficiency heuristic matches the solution quality of the best metaheuristic algorithms while requiring two to three orders of magnitude less planning time, establishing Pareto dominance over all competing methods on the reward-versus-compute frontier. These findings suggest a practical design principle for real-time allocation and dispatch systems: in dynamic, time-constrained routing environments, carefully designed greedy heuristics can match the output of sophisticated search procedures at a fraction of the computational cost, making them preferable for online deployment.

</details>


### [199] [When do prophets profit in prediction markets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06166)
*Anri Gu,Nicole Kagan,Alec Sun,Jibang Wu,Haifeng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper establishes a formal equivalence between predictive accuracy and trading profitability in prediction markets with central limit order books, extending beyond the classical automated market maker design. The authors propose a "proper" betting strategy based on scoring rules that guarantees positive expected profit when a forecaster's prediction outperforms the market price, and demonstrate its effectiveness through empirical validation and a live deployment on Kalshi with +80.33% ROI and a Sharpe ratio of 3.35.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the discrepancy between classical theory (which predicts that forecasting accuracy and trading profit are equivalent) and the reality of modern prediction markets (central limit order books), where informed forecasters often lose money while uninformed strategies can profit.

Method: The authors (1) establish a formal theorem proving equivalence between predictive accuracy and profitability for any strictly proper scoring rule S via a "proper" betting strategy; (2) prove that proper betting is essentially the only strategy with robust profitability guarantees; (3) derive a decomposition of expected profit that generalizes the classical AMM guarantee; (4) empirically test across thousands of AI model forecasts; (5) conduct a month-long live deployment on Kalshi.

Result: Proper betting is the only strategy that reliably converts forecasting accuracy into profit across empirical tests. The live deployment on Kalshi achieved +80.33% ROI with a Sharpe ratio of 3.35. The paper also identifies systematic forecasting personas and shows how optimal proper strategy varies across them.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the theory-practice gap by formally establishing that predictive accuracy and profitability are equivalent in modern prediction markets, provided one uses the correct (proper) betting strategy. The work provides both theoretical foundations and practical guidance for profitable forecasting in limit order book markets.

Abstract: Prediction markets aggregate dispersed beliefs into prices that act as probabilistic forecasts of uncertain events. Classical theory establishes a clean equivalence between forecasting accuracy and trading profit, but only for the specific automated market maker (AMM) design. However, the largest exchanges today are based on central limit order books in which informed forecasters routinely lose money while uninformed strategies can profit on simple heuristics. We resolve this discrepancy by establishing a formal equivalence between predictive accuracy and profitability. For any strictly proper scoring rule $S$, we exhibit a "proper" betting strategy that depends only on the forecaster's prediction $\mathbf{p}$ and the market price $\mathbf{q}$, and earns positive expected profit whenever $\mathbf{p}$ outperforms $\mathbf{q}$ under $S$ and the market has sufficient liquidity. Moreover, this proper betting is essentially the only strategy with such robust profitability guarantee. The proof rests on a decomposition of expected profit that strictly generalizes the classical AMM guarantee and also explains how strategies can profit without an accuracy edge. Empirically, across thousands of forecasts by AI models, proper betting is the only strategy that reliably converts accuracy into profit, and we further identify systematic forecasting personas and show how the optimal proper strategy varies across them. A month-long live deployment on Kalshi achieves $+80.33\%$ return on investment with a Sharpe ratio of $3.35$.

</details>


### [200] [A toy framework for single and multi-agent human-AI curiosity ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06214)
*Ilya E. Monosov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a conceptual toy framework for studying curiosity as an ecosystem, examining how single-agent inquiry policies depend on uncertainty reduction, costs, delayed returns, and question-keeping value, then extending to multi-agent systems with shared knowledge landscapes tracking inquiry volume, diversity, frontier-direction, redundancy, and reusable knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a systematic framework for understanding curiosity as an ecological phenomenon rather than an isolated individual trait, enabling analysis of how inquiry behaviors evolve at both individual and collective levels.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual toy framework with two layers: (1) a single-agent model where inquiry policy weights on uncertainty reduction, costs, delayed returns, and question-keeping value can shift with experience; (2) a multi-agent extension that tracks inquiry volume, topic diversity, frontier-directed inquiry, redundancy, and reusable knowledge across a shared knowledge landscape.

Result: A conceptual toy framework for studying curiosity ecology, capturing how experience-dependent shifts in inquiry costs and values affect question-asking behavior at both individual and collective scales.

Conclusion: The framework provides a foundation for future efforts in designing multi-agent AI systems for discovery and serves as a companion piece for a paper under review in Trends in Neurosciences.

Abstract: This paper offers a toy framework for considering curiosity as an ecosystem. First, it suggests that a single agent's inquiry policy (how, when, and why an agent asks a question) depends on how the agent values immediate uncertainty reduction, costs, delayed return, and the value of keeping the question open. A key concept in the framework is that the weights on these decision-related terms can change with experience. For example, a period of cheap, quickly answered questions may change the cost of inquiry on a short timescale and change which kinds of questions the agent is drawn to answer over a longer timescale. Second, these ideas are extended to many agents exploring a shared knowledge landscape, and there the framework tracks inquiry volume, topic diversity, frontier-directed inquiry, redundancy, and reusable knowledge. The result is a conceptual toy framework for studying curiosity ecology and for future efforts towards designing multi-agent AI systems for discovery. It serves as a companion piece for a paper currently under review in Trends in Neurosciences.

</details>


### [201] [Information Gain-based Rollout Policy Optimization: An Adaptive Tree-Structured Rollout Approach for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06223)
*Yijun Zhang,Fan Xu,Jiaxin Ding,Yule Xie,Shiqing Gao,Xin Ding,Haoxiang Zhang,Luoyi Fu,Xinbing Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IGRPO is a policy optimization framework that allocates rollout budgets based on intermediate-state informativeness, using information gain to guide tree-structured exploration and derive a teacher distribution for policy learning, outperforming baselines on search-augmented QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reinforcement learning methods for LLM agents on long-horizon search tasks allocate rollout budgets without assessing the utility of intermediate states, wasting computation on low-value branches despite varying informativeness across branches.

Method: IGRPO performs budget-aware tree-structured rollouts where expansion budget is allocated by node-level informativeness (information gain), progressively suppressing unpromising branches. The information gain-based rollout induces an explicit limiting teacher distribution over trajectories, which serves as the policy optimization target, unifying adaptive exploration with principled policy learning.

Result: Consistently outperforms strong baselines under the same rollout budget constraints on seven challenging search-augmented QA benchmarks.

Conclusion: Leveraging the induced teacher distribution from information gain-based rollouts effectively guides policy optimization for long-horizon search agents, validating the approach's effectiveness.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a promising paradigm for improving large language model (LLM) agents on long-horizon search tasks, where the agent must make a sequence of intermediate decisions before receiving a final outcome. However, existing methods still face a key limitation: the rollout budget is often allocated without explicitly assessing the utility of intermediate states. As a result, substantial computation may be spent on low-value states, even though different branches can vary drastically in their informativeness. In this paper, we propose Information Gain-based Rollout Policy Optimization (IGRPO), a policy optimization framework that treats intermediate-state informativeness as the organizing principle of rollout collection. Specifically, IGRPO performs budget-aware tree-structured rollouts by allocating expansion budget according to node-level informativeness, so that more informative branches are expanded more frequently while unpromising branches are progressively suppressed.
