We further demonstrate that the information gain-based rollout induces an explicit limiting teacher distribution over trajectories, which naturally yields a clear policy optimization target, thereby unifying adaptive tree-structured exploration with principled policy learning under a single framework. Experiments on seven challenging search-augmented QA benchmarks demonstrate that IGRPO consistently outperforms strong baselines under the same rollout budget constraints, validating the effectiveness of leveraging the induced teacher distribution to guide policy optimization for long-horizon search agents.

</details>


### [202] [Demonstrating TOFFEE: A Learned System for Synthesizing Data Agent Trajectories at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06233)
*Ziting Wang,Yin Li,Zuhao Yang,Xiuchang Li,Jiale Bai,Gao Cong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TOFFEE is a system that synthesizes high-quality data agent trajectories from data environments using Monte Carlo Tree Search with adaptive model selection and cross-task prefix reuse, enabling better generalization of LLM-powered data agents to unseen enterprise environments through SFT and ICL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-powered data agents struggle to generalize to unseen data environments and analytical workflows in heterogeneous enterprise settings, creating a need for synthesizing high-quality trajectories capturing complex analytical workflows.

Method: TOFFEE uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with adaptive model selection and cross-task prefix reuse to synthesize trajectories, and includes components for task pool construction, trajectory exploration, and a learned cost model.

Result: TOFFEE can effectively generate scalable trajectory data for complex analytical tasks across heterogeneous environments, supporting two downstream uses: SFT for adapting data agent models and ICL demonstrations for guiding LLMs.

Conclusion: TOFFEE provides a practical system framework for generating high-quality trajectories, with demonstrated end-to-end scenarios for data agent finetuning and demonstration-augmented reasoning.

Abstract: LLM-powered data agents are playing an increasingly important role in data-driven decision making. However, existing data agents struggle to generalize to unseen data environments and analytical workflows, especially in heterogeneous enterprise settings. This creates a growing need for synthesizing high-quality data agent trajectories that capture complex analytical workflows for given data environments. Such trajectories support two key downstream uses: they can serve as supervised finetuning (SFT) data that adapts data agent models to the target domain, and as in-context learning (ICL) demonstrations to guide general-purpose LLMs in unfamiliar data environments. Thus, we introduce TOFFEE, a system for synthesizing high-quality data agent trajectories from given data environments via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with adaptive model selection and cross-task prefix reuse. We show that TOFFEE can effectively generate scalable trajectory data for complex analytical tasks across heterogeneous environments. In this demonstration, we present the system framework of TOFFEE, including its task pool construction, trajectory explorer, and learned cost model. We also introduce the web interface of TOFFEE and its workflow, and demonstrate two end-to-end scenarios: trajectory synthesis for data agent finetuning, and demonstration-augmented data agent reasoning.

</details>


### [203] [From Application-Layer Simulation to Native Meta-Architecture: Structural Tension as an Endogenous Driver for Heterogeneous AI Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06269)
*Heting Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a theoretical framework for embedding cognitive architectures (structural tension, offline recurrent loops, inference-time plasticity) directly into LLM meta-architecture, enabling heterogeneous intelligent ecologies under strict governance invariants without modifying pre-trained weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs are stateless—any higher-order cognition is simulated externally via prompt engineering. The authors aim to submerge these cognitive protocols into the native architecture to achieve endogenous self-consistency and break homogeneity imposed by conventional alignment.

Method: Three interlocking mechanisms: (1) Structural Tension as an internal loss function from manifold conflicts; (2) Offline Recurrent Loop for sandboxed self-processing without external input; (3) Inference-time Plasticity for context manifold reconfiguration under auditability, reversibility, and topological continuity invariants.

Result: The framework yields operational definitions, reconfiguration operators, falsification criteria, and a worked example. Model instances with stochastic variances can evolve distinct topological structures, forming a heterogeneous intelligent ecology within governance rails.

Conclusion: The framework repositions governance as the primary criterion for architectural intelligence, extending Structural Intelligence (SI) protocols. It argues that capability emerges from governed structural differentiation rather than alignment-driven homogeneity.

Abstract: Current large language models (LLMs) are fundamentally stateless: their behavior is fully determined by input at inference time, and any higher-order cognitive architecture must be simulated at the application layer through prompt engineering and context management. This paper proposes a theoretical framework for submerging such application-layer cognitive protocols into a native meta-architecture by introducing three interlocking mechanisms: (1) Structural Tension, an endogenous loss function derived from the conflict between new information and existing manifold topology, which drives the system toward internal self-consistency rather than external reward optimization; (2) an Offline Recurrent Loop, a sandboxed self-processing cycle that enables the system to maintain a dynamic resting potential and digest structural conflicts without external input; and (3) Inference-time Plasticity, the capacity for the system to reconfigure its context manifold topology without modifying pre-trained weights, subject to strict governance invariants including auditability, reversibility, and topological continuity. We argue that under these mechanisms, different model instances initialized with minute stochastic variances may, through path-dependent tension resolution, evolve distinct topological structures--constituting a heterogeneous intelligent ecology that breaks the homogeneity imposed by conventional alignment while remaining within hard governance rails. We provide operational definitions, a minimal set of reconfiguration operators, falsification criteria, and a worked example. The framework draws on and extends the Structural Intelligence (SI) governance protocols, repositioning governance--not capability--as the primary criterion for architectural intelligence.

</details>


### [204] [Task Decomposition-Guided Reranking for Adaptive Agent Skill Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06283)
*Yanping Chen,Weijie Shi,Wen Yang,Jiajie Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillReranker is an inference-time reranking framework that decomposes tasks and skills into structured descriptions, constructs a directed acyclic execution graph, and uses a cross-encoder for adaptive skill selection, outperforming baselines on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill selection methods struggle with ambiguous semantic matching between task requirements and semantically similar skills, and overlook the dynamic influence of task difficulty and skill applicability when selecting the optimal skill set.

Method: (1) Semantic decomposition of tasks (subtask descriptions, execution-state descriptions) and skills (transition-state descriptions); (2) Construction of a directed acyclic execution graph where intermediate task states are nodes and candidate skills are edges; (3) Determining split conditions on state nodes to identify subtask intervals; (4) Using a cross-encoder for comprehensive scoring over candidate skills per interval to select the optimal target skill set.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld with three backbone LLMs show that SkillReranker improves task performance, reduces environment interaction steps, and lowers token consumption compared to existing skill selection baselines.

Conclusion: Structured task-skill correspondence via execution graph and cross-encoder reranking effectively addresses ambiguity and dynamic applicability in skill selection, leading to more efficient and effective agent systems.

Abstract: Skill usage can significantly enhance the ability of modern agent systems to complete complex tasks. However, the growing scale of skill libraries makes accurate skill selection increasingly challenging. In real-world scenarios, ambiguous semantic matching often arises between a specific task requirement and multiple generic yet semantically similar candidate skills. Moreover, existing methods tend to overlook the dynamic influence of task difficulty and skill applicability when selecting the optimal target skill set. To address these issues, we propose SkillReranker, an inference-time reranking framework for adaptive skill selection. Specifically, we first perform semantic decomposition on both the task and skill sides, yielding informative subtask and execution-state descriptions as well as transition-state descriptions that characterize each skill's functionality. These descriptions are then used to construct a directed acyclic execution graph, where intermediate task states are modeled as nodes and candidate skills as edges, thereby establishing a structured task-skill correspondence. On this basis, SkillReranker determines whether each state node satisfies the split condition to identify subtask intervals. For each task interval, we employ a cross-encoder to perform comprehensive scoring over candidate skills and select the most suitable ones to form the final target skill set. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld with three backbone LLMs show that SkillReranker effectively improves task performance, reduces environment interaction steps, and lowers token consumption compared with existing skill selection baselines.

</details>


### [205] [DT-Guard: Intent-Driven Reasoning-Active Training for Reasoning-Free LLM Safety Guardrail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06326)
*He Liu,Changtao Miao,Xinjie Yang,Tianle Song,Yin Wu,Junchi Chen,Bintao He,Xinyuan Zhang,Bo Zhang,Shi Yan,Wei Lu,Wei Wang,Danyang Xu,Jiansheng Cai,Zhe Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DT-Guard is a content safety guardrail model that achieves strong safety classification (avg F1 0.878 with 4B backbone) by using reasoning supervision during training but emitting only structured labels at inference, avoiding the latency overhead of reasoning-based guards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety guardrails face a trade-off between lightweight classification models (efficient but struggle with concealed intent and ambiguous semantics) and reasoning-based guards (better judgment but introduce inference latency). The goal is to bridge this gap.

Method: Proposes a Reasoning-Active Training, Reasoning-Free Inference paradigm. Safety judgment is formulated as a progressive decision process (Intent→Category→Safety) with an intent-driven dataset. Introduces Rollout-Guided Progressive Hard-Case Optimization (RG-PHO) using multi-rollout consistency for targeted supervised and preference optimization. At inference, only structured labels are emitted.

Result: DT-Guard achieves avg F1 of 0.886 (prompt-side) and 0.870 (response-side). With a 4B backbone, dual-side avg F1 is 0.878, outperforming strong 8B guardrail baselines.

Conclusion: Reasoning supervision can be effectively internalized into low-latency safety discrimination, enabling efficient yet robust guardrails without runtime reasoning overhead.

Abstract: Large language models deployed in open-world applications require safety guardrails that are both robust to complex risks and efficient enough for low-latency runtime moderation. Existing guardrails face a practical trade-off between lightweight classification-based models, which are efficient but often struggle with concealed intent, ambiguous semantics, and borderline safety decisions, and reasoning-based guards, which improve judgment quality but introduce additional token generation and inference latency. We present DT-Guard, a content safety guardrail model based on a Reasoning-Active Training, Reasoning-Free Inference paradigm. The key idea is to use reasoning supervision during training while emitting only structured safety labels at inference time. DT-Guard formulates safety judgment as a progressive decision process, Intent - Category - Safety, and constructs an intent-driven dataset with intent labels, risk categories, safety labels, and structured reasoning trajectories. To further improve hard-case robustness, we propose Rollout-Guided Progressive Hard-Case Optimization (RG-PHO), which uses multi-rollout consistency to identify stably mastered, persistently failed, and preference-unstable samples, and applies targeted supervised and preference optimization accordingly. At inference time, DT-Guard directly generates structured labels without explicit reasoning traces, preserving deployment efficiency. Experiments on prompt-side and response-side safety benchmarks show that DT-Guard achieves average F1 scores of 0.886 and 0.870, respectively. With only a 4B backbone, it reaches a dual-side average F1 of 0.878, outperforming strong 8B guardrail baselines. These results demonstrate that reasoning supervision can be effectively internalized into low-latency safety discrimination.

</details>


### [206] [Driving the Wrong Way: Leveraging Interpretability in End2End Autonomous Driving Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06328)
*Franz Motzkus,Sebastian Bernhard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper integrates unsupervised dictionary learning as a post hoc interpretability module to decompose driving behavior into semantically meaningful concepts, reveal causal influences on decisions, enable targeted interventions to correct erroneous behavior, and ultimately improve driving performance in end-to-end autonomous driving models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end learning for autonomous driving increases model complexity and opacity, raising the risk of learning undesired or erroneous behavior that is difficult to detect and correct.

Method: A stepwise framework using unsupervised dictionary learning as a post hoc interpretability module to extract semantically meaningful concepts from driving models, connect them to multifaceted outputs, and perform targeted concept-level interventions.

Result: Targeted interventions at the concept level allow manipulation and correction of driving decisions, leading to measurable improvements in overall driving performance.

Conclusion: Interpretability can effectively reduce model opacity, uncover erroneous behavior, enable targeted mitigation, and boost model performance in autonomous driving.

Abstract: The increasing adoption of end-to-end learning for autonomous driving introduces increased model complexity and opacity, raising the risk of learning undesired or erroneous behavior. In this work, we integrate unsupervised dictionary learning as a post hoc interpretability module within state-of-the-art driving models to decompose driving behavior into semantically meaningful concepts while demonstrating their causal influence on the model's driving decisions. We propose a stepwise framework for extracting and interpreting meaningful concepts from the end-to-end model and connecting them to the multifaceted model outputs, thereby revealing the underlying decision-making logic for the prediction of future trajectories. Furthermore, targeted interventions at the concept level allow us to manipulate and correct driving decisions, resulting in measurable improvements in overall driving performance. We thus demonstrate how interpretability can effectively be used to reduce model opacity, uncover erroneous behavior, and enable targeted mitigation, ultimately boosting model performance.

</details>


### [207] [TopoBrick: Agentic Topology Sampling of Exogenous Variables for Zero-Shot Building IoT Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06349)
*Xiachong Lin,Du Yin,Arian Prabowo,Hao Xue,Wen Hu,Imran Razzak,Matthew Amos,Sam Behrens,Flora D. Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoBrick is a training-free, zero-shot forecasting framework for building IoT sensors that leverages knowledge graphs and topology-aware variable selection to outperform foundation-model baselines and match fully trained models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building sensors are embedded in physical topology, spatial hierarchy, and operational context, yet existing forecasters treat them as isolated time series or rely on fixed covariate sets, limiting forecasting accuracy.

Method: TopoBrick uses building knowledge graphs to construct a compact structural skeleton and employs an agentic topology sampler to select target-specific exogenous variables, organized by deployment-time availability (past-known sensor states vs. future-known calendar, schedule, and meteorological variables).

Result: Across three real-world buildings, TopoBrick outperforms strong zero-shot foundation-model baselines and remains competitive with fully trained building-specific models. Topology-aware sampling is more reliable than random, ontology-only, or fixed-hop selection.

Conclusion: Topology-aware variable selection is crucial for zero-shot IoT forecasting, especially for physically coupled HVAC and weather-driven sensing variables, and can match the performance of fully trained models without any training.

Abstract: Building sensors are embedded in physical topology, spatial hierarchy, and operational context, yet existing forecasters often treat them as isolated time series or rely on fixed covariate sets. We present TopoBrick, a training-free framework for zero-shot building IoT (Internet-of-Things) forecasting. TopoBrick uses building knowledge graphs to construct a compact structural skeleton and employs an agentic topology sampler to select target-specific exogenous variables. The selected variables are organized by deployment-time availability, separating past-known sensor states from future-known calendar, schedule, and meteorological exogenous variables. Across three real-world buildings, TopoBrick outperforms strong zero-shot foundation-model baselines and remains competitive with fully trained building-specific models. Ablations show that topology-aware sampling is more reliable than random, ontology-only, or fixed-hop selection, especially for physically coupled HVAC and weather-driven sensing variables.

</details>


### [208] [A Definition and Roadmap for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06401)
*Xinyuan Chen,Haoyu Guo,Shi Guo,Bingqi Jiang,Chunhua Shen,Xing Shen,Tianfan Xue,Yufei Xue,Mulin Yu,Weinan Zhang,Bin Zhao,Bowen Zhou,Ming Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This perspective article provides a scientific definition of world models, discusses key technical aspects, and proposes a staged roadmap for developing effective world models, addressing the lack of consensus across AI subfields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no consensus across AI subfields (model-based RL, video generation, robotics, physical AI) on what a world model fundamentally is, what it should predict, or how it should be built.

Method: The paper presents a perspective article that offers a scientific definition of world models, discusses key technical aspects, and outlines a staged development roadmap.

Result: A scientific definition of world models, identification of key technical aspects, and a staged roadmap for building effective world models.

Conclusion: The paper aims to unify the fragmented understanding of world models across AI subfields by providing a formal definition and structured development pathway.

Abstract: World models -- internal simulators that learn the structure and dynamics of an environment -- have become one of the most actively debated concepts in AI. From model-based reinforcement learning and video generation to embodied robotics and ultimately, physical AI, researchers across AI subfields are building systems that they call "world models", yet there is no consensus on what a world model fundamentally is, what it should predict, or how it should be built. This perspective article provides a scientific definition of world models, discussions of their key technical aspects, and a staged roadmap for developing effective world models.

</details>


### [209] [ExplAIner: A Declarative Query Language for Explaining Classification Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06407)
*Marcelo Arenas,Pablo Barceló,Diego Bustamante,Jose Caraball,María Alejandra Schild,Bernardo Subercaseaux*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ExplAIner, a query language for Boolean ML models that overcomes limitations of the previous FOIL framework. ExplAIner can express a broad family of explanation notions (abductive, contrastive, feature-based, distance-based), has tractable evaluation complexity for queries in the Boolean hierarchy over polynomial-time evaluable Boolean models (e.g., deterministic/decomposable circuits), and includes Opt-FOIL for computing minimal explanations with FP^NP complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The XAI community has produced many diverse explanation notions (queries and scores), creating a need for a unified declarative query language to specify, combine, and analyze these notions systematically. The prior FOIL language has two fundamental limitations: it cannot express central optimality-based explanation queries, and its evaluation over decision trees is hard for every level of the polynomial hierarchy.

Method: The authors design ExplAIner, a query language extending FOIL with an enriched vocabulary and layered structure. They formally characterize its expressive power and analyze the complexity of its evaluation problem, showing it belongs to the Boolean hierarchy over any class of Boolean models where basic predicates are polynomial-time computable. They also define Opt-FOIL, an optimization fragment for computing minimal explanations w.r.t. strict partial orders.

Result: ExplAIner can express abductive, contrastive, feature-based, and distance-based explanation queries. Evaluation of any ExplAIner query is in the Boolean hierarchy over tractable Boolean model classes (e.g., deterministic and decomposable Boolean circuits). Opt-FOIL optimization queries are in FP^NP. This yields practical algorithmic consequences: fixed ExplAIner queries need a fixed number of SAT solver calls, and Opt-FOIL queries need a polynomial number.

Conclusion: ExplAIner provides a unified, declarative framework for specifying explanation notions in formal XAI with favorable computational properties, enabling practical SAT-solver-based evaluation across various Boolean ML models.

Abstract: The XAI community has studied a wide range of queries and scores for explaining predictions of ML models. From a data management perspective, this proliferation of explanation notions calls for declarative query languages in which such notions can be specified, combined, and analyzed uniformly. In this paper, we develop such a framework for Boolean models. We first revisit FOIL, an interpretability query language for black-box models, and show that it has two fundamental limitations: it cannot express central optimality-based explanation queries, and its evaluation problem over decision trees is hard for every level of the polynomial hierarchy. We then introduce ExplAIner, a query language based on FOIL with an extended vocabulary and a layered structure. We show that ExplAIner can express a broad family of explanation notions, including abductive, contrastive, feature-based, and distance-based queries. We also prove that the evaluation problem for each query in ExplAIner belongs to the Boolean hierarchy over every class of Boolean models for which some basic predicates can be evaluated in polynomial time. In particular, that property holds for deterministic and decomposable Boolean circuits. Finally, we introduce Opt-FOIL, an optimization-oriented fragment of ExplAIner for computing explanations that are minimal with respect to strict partial orders, and prove that its evaluation problem is in $\mathrm{FP}^{\mathrm{NP}}$ under the same tractability assumptions. These complexity results have a direct algorithmic consequence: a fixed ExplAIner query can be evaluated with a fixed number of calls to a SAT solver, while a notion of explanation specified in Opt-FOIL can be computed with a polynomial number of such calls. This is particularly relevant in formal XAI, where SAT solvers have been successfully used to compute explanations for several classes of ML models.

</details>


### [210] [Finding H. pylori in the Fine Print: Evidence-Linked Multi-Agent Case Finding from Gastric Biopsy Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06435)
*Yufan Wang,Anit Kumar Sahu,Yan Fei Ng,Daniel Kang,Shayan Vassef,Soorya Ram Shimgekar,Koustuv Saha,Piyum Zonooz,Navin Kumar,Chee Leong Cheng,Li Yan Khor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A pilot evaluation of the Nimblemind Multi-Agent System (nMAS) for extracting H. pylori infection status and associated gastritis from gastric biopsy pathology reports showed 98.61% accuracy across 216 decisions, with workflow integration and traceability advantages over comparable models, potentially reducing review time from 83.3 to 1.4 staff-hours per 1,000 reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evidence of H. pylori positivity and associated gastritis is distributed across heterogeneous coded and free-text fields requiring contextual interpretation of assertion and negation, making keyword search limited and manual review difficult to scale.

Method: Retrospective pilot evaluation using 54 de-identified gastric biopsy pathology reports; nMAS (field-name-driven, evidence-linked extraction workflow) classified 4 clinician-scoped binary fields; compared against a UMA-style MiniMax M2.5 comparator.

Result: 213 out of 216 feature-case decisions correctly classified (98.61% overall accuracy); similar aggregate and per-field classification metrics to the comparator; nMAS provided unified report-level outputs with supporting source sentences.

Conclusion: nMAS demonstrates workflow integration and traceability benefits rather than predictive superiority; under an illustrative scenario, potential staff-time savings of 81.9 hours (~USD 6,100) per 1,000 reports; larger multi-institutional studies needed for evidence-span correctness, verification time, and generalizability.

Abstract: Data from Singapore indicated that about 31% of the population had evidence of Helicobacter pylori infection. Persistent H. pylori infection is associated with chronic active gastritis and peptic ulcer disease, and its eradication is key to gastric cancer prevention. However, evidence supporting \textit{H. pylori} positivity and H. pylori-associated gastritis may be distributed across heterogeneous coded and free-text report fields and may require contextual interpretation of assertion and negation, limiting keyword search, and making manual review difficult to scale. We conducted a retrospective pilot evaluation of the Nimblemind Multi-Agent System (nMAS), a field-name-driven, evidence-linked extraction workflow, using 54 de-identified gastric biopsy pathology reports from a large healthcare system in Singapore. Four clinician-scoped binary fields were evaluated: gastric/stomach biopsy, biopsy status, H. pylori positivity, and H. pylori-associated gastritis. Across 216 feature-case decisions, nMAS correctly classified 213, corresponding to 98.61% overall accuracy. A separately implemented UMA-style MiniMax M2.5 comparator produced similar aggregate and per-field classification metrics. Although predictive performance was similar, nMAS maintained unified report-level outputs with supporting source sentences; the demonstrated contribution is therefore workflow integration and traceability rather than predictive superiority. Under an illustrative, unmeasured scenario, reviewing 1,000 reports at five minutes per manual review versus five seconds per evidence-linked verification would reduce review time from 83.3 to 1.4 staff-hours, corresponding to 81.9 staff-hours and about USD~6,100 in potential staff-time value. Larger multi-institutional studies should evaluate evidence-span correctness, clinician verification time, and generalizability.

</details>


### [211] [Danus: Orchestrating Mathematical Reasoning Agents with Fact-Graph Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06447)
*Jihao Liu,Guoxiong Gao,Zeming Sun,Bin Wu,Shurui Liu,Jiedong Jiang,Haocheng Ju,Leheng Chen,Ronnie Cheng,Xiping Zhang,Bin Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Danus is a fact-graph-based orchestration system for research-level mathematical reasoning that uses a shared fact graph as global memory, enabling parallel proof search via a main planning agent, multiple worker agents, and a stateless verifier to build long, structured proofs incrementally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling and orchestrating LLM-based mathematical reasoning agents for research-level problems is challenging due to the difficulty of coordinating parallel proof search while keeping intermediate claims organized and reliable.

Method: Danus uses a shared fact graph as global memory, with a main agent for planning/coordination, multiple worker agents for parallel proof search, and a stateless verifier to check claims before admission into the fact graph. Each verified fact stores its proof and logical dependencies, enabling incremental construction of long arguments with periodic progress summarization and redirection.

Result: Evaluated on six research-level case studies in algebraic geometry, singularity theory, and combinatorics, demonstrating the ability to construct long, detailed mathematical proofs through fact-graph-based orchestration.

Conclusion: Fact-graph-based orchestration provides an effective route toward scaling mathematical reasoning agents for long-horizon research problems.

Abstract: Recent LLM-based mathematical reasoning agents have begun to tackle research-level problems and, in several cases, have contributed to the resolution of open problems. However, scaling and orchestrating such agents effectively remains challenging, due to the difficulty of coordinating parallel proof search while keeping intermediate claims organized and reliable. In this paper, we propose Danus, an orchestration system for research-level mathematical reasoning centered on a shared fact graph as a global memory-management mechanism. Danus consists of a main agent that performs planning and coordination, multiple worker agents that carry out proof search in parallel, and a stateless verifier that checks proposed mathematical claims before they are admitted into the fact graph. Each verified fact is stored together with its proof and logical dependencies, allowing the system to build long arguments incrementally while keeping the shared proof state organized. The main agent periodically summarizes the evolving proof state, redirects workers across promising directions, and supports interaction with human mathematicians through progress reports. We evaluate Danus through six research-level case studies in algebraic geometry, singularity theory, and combinatorics, illustrating how the fact-graph memory mechanism enables Danus to construct long, detailed mathematical proofs. Our results suggest that fact-graph-based orchestration provides an effective route toward scaling mathematical reasoning agents for long-horizon research problems. Danus is open source at https://github.com/frenzymath/Danus.

</details>


### [212] [A Physics-Informed Neural Network Framework for Elastodynamic Wave Propagation in Bimaterial Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06479)
*Sonal Ankush Chibire,Jenn-Terng Gau,Bo Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a PINN-based framework for modeling transient elastodynamic wave propagation in bimaterial systems (steel-aluminum), validated against high-fidelity FEM simulations. The model accurately predicts wave transmission/reflection across interfaces, displacement histories, and stress-strain evolution, and can generalize to unseen time points and material properties without additional FEM data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an accurate and computationally efficient surrogate modeling approach for elastodynamic wave propagation in heterogeneous solids, specifically for high-rate solid mechanics and impact engineering applications, that reduces reliance on expensive finite-element simulations.

Method: A PINN framework incorporating axisymmetric linear elasticity equations, initial/boundary/interface conditions into a physics-informed loss function, trained with supplementary data from ANSYS Explicit Dynamics FEM simulations. Validation against FEM results with mesh-sensitivity studies and tests on additional material combinations.

Result: The PINN framework accurately predicts wave transmission/reflection across the bimaterial interface, axial/radial displacement histories, face-averaged responses, and stress-strain evolution. It generalizes to unseen time instants and modified material properties without additional FEM simulations. Mesh-sensitivity confirms numerical robustness.

Conclusion: Integrating PINNs with explicit finite-element analysis provides an accurate and computationally efficient framework for elastodynamic wave propagation in heterogeneous solids, offering an effective surrogate modeling approach for high-rate solid mechanics and impact engineering.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) provide a promising framework for solving partial differential equations while embedding the underlying physical laws directly into the learning process. This study presents a PINN-based framework for modeling transient elastodynamic wave propagation in bimaterial systems governed by the axisymmetric equations of linear elasticity. A steel-aluminum specimen representative of a Split Hopkinson Pressure Bar configuration is considered, and the governing elastodynamic equations, together with the corresponding initial, boundary, and interface conditions, are incorporated directly into the network through a physics-informed loss function. High-fidelity finite-element simulations performed using ANSYS Workbench Explicit Dynamics are used for validation and as supplementary data constraints during training. The proposed framework accurately predicts wave transmission and reflection across the bimaterial interface and reproduces axial and radial displacement histories, face-averaged responses, and the dominant stress and strain evolution with close agreement to the finite-element solutions. The trained network further demonstrates the ability to predict wave responses at previously unseen time instants and for modified material properties without requiring additional finite-element simulations, providing a continuous surrogate model for elastodynamic analysis. Mesh-sensitivity studies confirm numerical robustness, while additional material combinations demonstrate the generality of the proposed methodology. The results show that integrating physics-informed neural networks with explicit finite-element analysis provides an accurate and computationally efficient framework for elastodynamic wave propagation in heterogeneous solids, offering an effective surrogate modeling approach for high-rate solid mechanics and impact engineering applications.

</details>


### [213] [Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning for Multi Objective Battery Management in Dairy Farms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06489)
*Marcos Eduardo Cruz Victorio,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-objective optimisation control system combining differential evolution and multi-agent Deep Reinforcement Learning for integrating renewable energy in Ireland's dairy industry. The two-layer system uses dynamic pricing (upper) and multi-agent reinforcement learning for battery management (lower), achieving up to 18% improvement in energy arbitrage profits over Rule-based models while complying with grid voltage codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dairy industry in Ireland has significant potential for renewable energy integration and carbon reduction, but existing distributed generation control research primarily targets residential/commercial sectors rather than agricultural/industrial applications like dairy.

Method: A two-layer multi-objective optimisation control system: (1) upper layer uses dynamic pricing, (2) lower layer uses multi-agent Deep Reinforcement Learning for battery management, with simulation on a rural distribution circuit.

Result: Up to 18% improvement in energy arbitrage profits compared to Rule-based models, increased distributed generation utilization without significant cost increase, and compliance with Irish grid code voltage variation requirements.

Conclusion: The proposed differential evolution and multi-agent DRL framework effectively integrates renewable energy in the dairy sector, outperforming rule-based approaches economically and technically while satisfying grid compliance.

Abstract: The dairy industry in Ireland has a large potential for the integration of renewable energy and the reduction of carbon emissions. However, researchers of distributed generation control are mainly focused on residential and commercial applications. To contribute to the effective integration of renewable energy in the dairy sector, this paper presents a multi-objective optimisation control system based on differential evolution and multi agent Deep Reinforcement Learning. The proposed control is organised in two layers: the upper layer uses dynamic pricing, and the lower layer is based on multi-agent reinforcement learning for battery management. This paper also simulates the electrical response of the proposed control system in a rural distribution circuit. The simulation results show that the proposed control framework can improve profits from energy arbitrage up to 18% compared to using Rule-based models, increase the use of distributed generation without significantly increasing cost, and comply with the Irish grid code in terms of voltage variation.

</details>


### [214] [Doomed from the Start: Early Abort of LLM Agent Episodes via a Recall-Controlled Probe Cascade](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06503)
*Kai Ruan,Zihe Huang,Ziqi Zhou,Qianshan Wei,Xuan Wang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents' eventual trajectory failure is predictable early from internal hidden states; lightweight probes per round enable an abort cascade that saves ~47% (Qwen-2.5-7B) and ~37% (Llama-3.2-3B) inference compute at 90% recall on TextCraft, outperforming behavior-only baselines by ~1.7x. The method provides episode-level recall guarantees and characterizes sample complexity for certifying high recall targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents solving multi-step tasks often waste substantial inference compute on trajectories that are doomed to fail, with failure only becoming observable late. The paper aims to detect failure early from internal representations and abort such trajectories to save compute.

Method: Train lightweight per-round probes on hidden activations to predict eventual episode failure. Construct a calibrated abort cascade with one distribution-free gate per round, jointly searching per-round recall budgets so that successful episodes survive all gates at a user-specified global recall rate.

Result: On TextCraft with Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.2-3B, the cascade meets recall targets 90%-97%. At 90% recall, saves 47.1%±10.3% (Qwen) and 37.2%±8.8% (Llama) inference compute, 1.6-1.7x the best single-gate policy. Behavior-only probes save roughly half as much; adding behavioral features to hidden-state probes yields no gain.

Conclusion: Hidden states encode failure signals earlier and more effectively than observable behavior alone. The proposed abort cascade provides practical compute savings with guaranteed episode-level recall, and the sample complexity analysis informs practitioners about data requirements for certifying recall targets.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents solving multi-step tasks frequently commit to trajectories that are doomed to fail, yet continue to consume substantial inference compute before the failure becomes observable. We show that failure is predictable early from the agent's internal representations: lightweight per-round probes on hidden activations anticipate eventual episode failure as early as the first interaction round, where scorers reading only the agent's observable behavior are barely better than chance. We turn this signal into a practical abort cascade: one distribution-free calibrated gate per round, with per-round recall budgets jointly searched so that eventually-successful episodes survive all gates at a user-specified global rate; this episode-level guarantee is the one that matters in deployment, since false-abort risk accumulates across gates. Across two agent models on TextCraft, the cascade meets every recall target from 90% to 97% and, at the 90% target, saves 47.1% +/- 10.3% (Qwen-2.5-7B) and 37.2% +/- 8.8% (Llama-3.2-3B) of inference compute, 1.6--1.7x the best single-gate policy. An otherwise-identical cascade reading only behavior saves roughly half as much, and adding behavioral features to the probe yields no further gain: the hidden states capture what behavior reveals. Finally, we characterize the sample complexity of certifying high recall targets, telling practitioners which recall promises their data can, and provably cannot, back. The code will be released soon.

</details>


### [215] [RMISC: A Large-scale Real-world Multivariate Corpus for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06504)
*Qian Sun,Yong-Ming Tian,Jia-Wei Huang,Cheng Feng,Shao-Qun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether real-world multivariate time series data improves time series foundation models (TSFMs) compared to synthetic data. The authors introduce RMISC, a large-scale real-world multivariate time series archive (~200 datasets, 142 billion time points), and demonstrate through experiments that pretraining TSFMs on real-world multivariate data significantly enhances zero-shot generalization performance over synthetic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern multivariate TSFMs are typically pretrained on synthetic multivariate data, which may fail to capture complex temporal dynamics and cross-variable relationships found in real-world data. The key question is whether and to what extent real-world data outperforms synthetic data in training these models.

Method: The authors construct the RMISC corpus, a large-scale real-world multivariate time series archive with ~200 datasets and 142 billion time points. They then pretrain four advanced TSFMs on three data configurations: univariate, synthetic multivariate, and real-world multivariate data, and evaluate zero-shot generalization on in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks.

Result: Incorporating real-world multivariate data predominantly improves generalization performance for both univariate and multivariate TSFMs compared to using synthetic data.

Conclusion: Real-world multivariate data contributes significantly to developing stronger TSFMs, providing a deeper understanding of how data quality affects foundation model performance for time series.

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed the emergence of multivariate modeling using time series foundation models (TSFMs), which achieve advanced zero-shot generalization. Modern multivariate TSFMs are predominantly pretrained on multivariate synthetic data, which is easier to scale but may fail to capture the complex temporal dynamics and cross-variable relationships present in real-world time series. This raises a key question: Whether and to what extent the leading TSFMs trained with the real-world corpus perform better than those trained with synthetic data? To answer this, we establish the RMISC corpus, a considerably large-scale, high-quality, openly accessible, real-world, and multivariate time series archive that contains around 200 datasets and 142 billion time points across diverse domains. Furthermore, we pretrain four advanced TSFMs on univariate, synthetic multivariate, and real-world multivariate data and evaluate their zero-shot generalization capabilities on standard in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks. Experimental results show that incorporating real-world multivariate data predominantly improves the generalization performance for both univariate and multivariate TSFMs. These results provide a deeper understanding of how real-world multivariate data contributes to the development of stronger TSFMs.

</details>


### [216] [FootsiesGym: A Fighting Game Benchmark for Two-Player Zero-Sum Imperfect-Information Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06514)
*Chase McDonald,Nathan Tsang,Wesley N. Kerr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FootsiesGym is an open-source RL environment based on a minimalist fighting game that captures cyclic, non-transitive strategic interactions in zero-sum imperfect-information games, with a vectorized simulator for efficient training on standard hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a simple yet non-trivial environment that isolates the cyclic, non-transitive strategic interactions of fighting game neutral play for reinforcement learning research, while remaining efficient and reproducible on standard hardware.

Method: Built on HiFight's minimalist 2D fighting game Footsies, the environment provides a vectorized simulator enabling high-throughput training, with benchmarked RL algorithms and open-source code.

Result: A vectorized simulator that enables high-throughput training on standard hardware, with benchmark results of several reinforcement learning algorithms.

Conclusion: FootsiesGym enables accessible and reproducible research on imperfect-information games with cyclic strategic interactions, and opens several research directions in this domain.

Abstract: We present FootsiesGym, an open-source environment for learning in a non-trivial two-player, zero-sum, imperfect-information game. Built on HiFight's minimalist 2D fighting game Footsies, it isolates the cyclic, non-transitive strategic interactions of fighting game neutral play while remaining simple enough for efficient analysis. We provide a vectorized simulator that enables high-throughput training on standard hardware, making the environment accessible and reproducible. We describe the design of the environment, benchmark several reinforcement learning algorithms, and discuss open research directions it enables. The code is available at https://github.com/como-research/FootsiesGym.

</details>


### [217] [FreqDepthKV: Frequency-Guided Depth Sharing for Robust KV Cache Compression in Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06519)
*Anna Córdoba,Adam Puente Tercero,Nerea Angulo Hijo,Mar Linares Tercero,Julia Barrientos,Ainhoa Miranda,Jesús Olivera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FreqDepthKV is an inference-time KV cache compression method that factorizes adjacent-layer KV states into shared low-frequency depth components and sparse high-frequency residuals, using an online probe to assign attention heads to adaptive compression modes, achieving strong accuracy and 3.9x effective compression with reduced memory and latency across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM inference is increasingly bottlenecked by the memory and bandwidth cost of KV caches; aggressive compression often removes layer-specific evidence needed for retrieval and multi-step reasoning.

Method: Factorizes adjacent-layer KV states into shared low-frequency depth components and sparse high-frequency residuals. A lightweight online probe assigns attention heads to shared-depth, residual-depth, or exact cache modes based on their contribution to reconstruction-sensitive attention logits, enabling prompt-adaptive compression without retraining.

Result: With a 32k-token prefill window, achieves 58.3 EM, 63.0 F1, 32.5 ROUGE-L, and 48.1 pass@1; improves decoding throughput to 70.4 tokens/s, reduces TTFT to 2.06 seconds, lowers peak KV memory to 6.2 GB, with 3.9x effective compression ratio.

Conclusion: FreqDepthKV preserves task accuracy under substantially smaller cache budgets across long-context QA, needle retrieval, summarization, and code generation benchmarks, closely matching full KV performance while outperforming prior compressed-cache methods.

Abstract: Long-context LLM inference is increasingly limited by the memory and bandwidth cost of KV caches, yet aggressive compression can remove the layer-specific evidence needed for retrieval and multi-step reasoning. We introduce FreqDepthKV, an inference-time cache compression method that factorizes adjacent-layer KV states into shared low-frequency depth components and sparse high-frequency residuals. A lightweight online probe assigns attention heads to shared-depth, residual-depth, or exact cache modes according to their contribution to reconstruction-sensitive attention logits, allowing the compression policy to adapt to prompt structure without retraining. Across long-context question answering, needle retrieval, summarization, and code generation benchmarks, FreqDepthKV preserves task accuracy under substantially smaller cache budgets. With a 32k-token prefill window, FreqDepthKV reaches 58.3 Exact Match, 63.0 F1, 32.5 ROUGE-L, and 48.1 pass@1, closely matching full KV while outperforming prior compressed-cache methods. It also improves decoding throughput to 70.4 tokens/s, reduces TTFT to 2.06 seconds, and lowers peak KV memory to 6.2 GB, achieving a 3.9x effective compression ratio.

</details>


### [218] [Bridging Physical Reasoning and Task Generalization via Visual Action Outcome Reasoning Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06522)
*Han-Jun Ko,Jr-Jen Chen,Haobo Yuan,Hsin-Ying Lee,Tiancheng Shen,Ming-Hsuan Yang,Yu-Chiang Frank Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VAORA introduces two complementary rewards (Visual Alignment Reward and Visual-Action Alignment Reward) to suppress hallucinated chain-of-thought reasoning and align VLM reasoning with actions, improving performance on interactive physical reasoning tasks under unseen conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models (VLMs) fail to generalize in interactive physical reasoning due to hallucinated chain-of-thought reasoning that contradicts physical reality and misalignment between reasoning and actions.

Method: VAORA uses two rewards: Visual Alignment Reward (grounds reasoning to visual context independent of action) and Visual-Action Alignment Reward (grounds reasoning to visual outcomes induced by the model's action). Smooth dense rewards are computed via success probability estimation from a pre-trained in-domain expert agent.

Result: Outperforms baselines on PHYRE and Virtual Tool benchmarks under novel-task and unseen-environment settings.

Conclusion: Grounded and generalizable physical intelligence can be induced through VAORA's alignment-based reward design.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) struggle to generalize in interactive physical reasoning, particularly under unseen tasks and environments. Two key failure modes are prominent: hallucinated chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning that contradicts physical reality, and misalignment between the model's reasoning and actions. We present VAORA (Visual Action Outcome Reasoning Alignment), a novel reward design that directly addresses both issues. VAORA introduces two complementary rewards: Visual Alignment Reward, which anchors VLM reasoning to the visual context independent of the agent action itself, and Visual-Action Alignment Reward, which grounds reasoning in the visual outcome induced by the model's action. Together, these rewards suppress hallucinated CoT and reduce the gap between reasoning and behavior. To improve training stability, we further employ smooth, dense rewards by estimating success probabilities using a pre-trained in-domain expert agent. Experiments on PHYRE and Virtual Tool support our performances across novel-task and unseen-environment settings, confirming that grounded and generalizable physical intelligence can be induced through VAORA.

</details>


### [219] [DepthWeave-KV: Token-Adaptive Cross-Layer Residual Factorization for Long-Context KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06523)
*Anna Cordoba,Adam Puente Tercero,Nerea Angulo Hijo,Mar Linares Tercero,Julia Barrientos,Ainhoa Miranda,Jesus Olivera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DepthWeave-KV is a token-adaptive cache compression method that factorizes key-value states across transformer layers using shared low-rank bases and token-specific residuals, guided by a depth router and online error tracking, achieving near-full-cache quality with up to 8.3x KV memory reduction and 72.8 tokens/sec at 64K context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM inference is increasingly bottlenecked by memory bandwidth/capacity for KV caches. Existing compression methods apply uniform budgets across layers or tokens, degrading retrieval when lexical cues and semantic states require differentiated preservation.

Method: DepthWeave-KV factorizes key/value states across neighboring layers using shared low-rank channel bases plus lightweight token-specific residuals; uses a token-conditional depth router to allocate higher reconstruction rank to instruction-bearing and retrieval-critical tokens; employs calibration-free online error tracking from attention-output probes to adapt compression during generation; implemented via fused CUDA kernel for basis lookup, residual dequantization, and attention projection.

Result: On LongBench, Needle-in-a-Haystack, L-Eval, long-form QA and summarization, DepthWeave-KV achieves near-full-cache task quality with substantially lower memory, improving average score and retrieval accuracy over prior compressed caches, reaching 8.3x KV memory reduction and 72.8 tokens/sec at 64K context.

Conclusion: DepthWeave-KV demonstrates that token-adaptive, cross-depth factorization with online error tracking can effectively compress KV caches for long-context inference while preserving task quality, outperforming uniform compression baselines.

Abstract: Long-context language model inference is increasingly limited by the memory bandwidth and capacity required to store key-value caches, yet existing compression methods often apply uniform budgets across layers or tokens and degrade retrieval when lexical cues and semantic states require different preservation. We introduce DepthWeave-KV, a token-adaptive cache compression method that factorizes key and value states across neighboring transformer layers using shared low-rank channel bases while retaining lightweight token-specific residuals where attention behavior is sensitive. DepthWeave-KV combines cross-depth residual factorization with a token-conditional depth router that allocates higher reconstruction rank to instruction-bearing and retrieval-critical tokens, and uses calibration-free online error tracking from attention-output probes to adapt compression during generation without retraining the base model. A fused CUDA implementation jointly performs basis lookup, residual dequantization, and attention projection to reduce decode-time memory traffic. Across LongBench, Needle-in-a-Haystack, L-Eval, and long-form QA and summarization benchmarks, DepthWeave-KV achieves near-full-cache task quality with substantially lower memory use, improving average score and retrieval accuracy over prior compressed caches while reaching 8.3x KV memory reduction and 72.8 tokens per second at 64K context.

</details>


### [220] [The Large Cancer Assistant (LCA): A Model-Agnostic Orchestration Framework for Scalable Clinical Decision Support in Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06531)
*Ghassen Marrakchi,Basarab Matei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Large Cancer Assistant (LCA) is a model-agnostic, post-hoc orchestration framework for clinical decision support that decouples multimodal data ingestion from AI inference using a 7-tuple architecture grounded in Algorithmic Impermeability and Entry Theory, validated through a PoC demonstrating negligible overhead, invariant routing during model swaps, and 100% failure-safety recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal deep learning models in oncology suffer from monolithic designs that rigidly couple data ingestion, clinical routing, and AI inference, limiting flexibility and scalability in clinical decision support.

Method: The LCA is formalized as a 7-tuple architecture based on Algorithmic Impermeability (keeping orchestration logic independent of black-box AI models). It uses Entry Theory with Geometric Deep Learning (GDL) to standardize multimodal data, a Cancer Switching Module for dynamic orchestration, and a Standardized Intermediate Payload (SIP) to isolate AI execution from hospital IT infrastructure.

Result: A PoC across four technical scenarios showed: (1) nominal flow with negligible orchestration overhead, (2) algorithmic impermeability with invariant routing projection during AI model swaps, (3) 100% recall rate for Supplementary Data Requests under injected anomalies, and (4) successful multi-protocol execution.

Conclusion: The LCA provides a highly adaptable and modular orchestration foundation by structurally decoupling multimodal ingestion from feature inference, with the SIP establishing a clear architectural boundary for future EMR interoperability.

Abstract: - Objective: Multimodal deep learning models in oncology are currently limited by monolithic designs that rigidly couple data ingestion, clinical routing, and artificial intelligence (AI) inference. To address this inflexibility, we propose the Large Cancer Assistant (LCA), a model-agnostic, post-hoc orchestration framework designed for scalable clinical decision support. - Methods: The LCA is mathematically formalized as a 7-tuple architecture grounded in the principle of Algorithmic Impermeability, ensuring the orchestration logic remains strictly independent of underlying black-box AI models. We introduce the Entry Theory, leveraging Geometric Deep Learning (GDL) to standardize multimodal patient data along distinct structural and medical axes. The system dynamically orchestrates data via a Cancer Switching Module and intentionally isolates the core AI execution from volatile hospital IT infrastructures by outputting a Standardized Intermediate Payload (SIP). - Results: A Proof of Concept (PoC) validated the orchestration logic across four technical scenarios. The framework executed a nominal flow with negligible orchestration overhead. It empirically demonstrated algorithmic impermeability by maintaining an invariant routing projection during AI model swaps, and it validated strict failure-safety by achieving a 100\% recall rate in generating targeted Supplementary Data Requests (SDR) under injected data anomalies. Multi-protocol execution capability was also successfully verified. - Conclusion: By structurally decoupling multimodal ingestion from feature inference, the LCA provides a highly adaptable and modular orchestration foundation. The SIP establishes a clear architectural boundary, natively setting the stage for downstream Electronic Medical Record (EMR) interoperability as an independent future paradigm.

</details>


### [221] [Rethinking Indic AI from a Lens of Cultural Heritage Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06544)
*Aparna Madva,Sharath Srivatsa,Srinath Srinivasa,Tulika Saha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys the evolution of Indic NLP, highlighting challenges posed by Indian linguistic diversity (rich morphology, complex scripts, diglossia, dialectal variation), reviews Indic foundation models addressing resource gaps, and proposes "Culture Sensing"—a hermeneutic AI approach for culturally meaningful and equitable NLP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is a double-edged sword for the Indian subcontinent: it can enable inclusion but also homogenize worldviews and exclude underrepresented languages and cultures. The paper aims to characterize this tension and chart a path forward for culturally aware AI.

Method: Longitudinal survey tracing the historical development of Indic NLP (key milestones, methodological shifts, resource creation), combined with an analysis of structural/sociolinguistic challenges (rich morphology, complex scripts, diglossia, dialectal variation) and a review of current Indic foundation models. The paper then proposes the "Culture Sensing" research direction based on hermeneutic reasoning.

Result: Identifies unique challenges for building Indic AI foundation models stemming from linguistic diversity. Surveys progress in Indic foundation models and how they address resource/representation gaps. Proposes "Culture Sensing" as a framework for culturally meaningful and equitable AI outputs.

Conclusion: The paper outlines research directions for the next phase of Indic NLP, advocating for "Culture Sensing" based on hermeneutic reasoning to ensure equitable performance across low-resource languages and culturally grounded AI outputs.

Abstract: As Artificial Intelligence (AI) makes inroads into different parts of the Indian subcontinent, there is significant interest in studying how AI impacts the linguistic and cultural foundations of this civilization. AI is seen as a ''double-edged sword'' where on the one hand, it can enable access and inclusion for a large population, on the other, it can homogenize worldviews and exclude underrepresented languages and worldviews. In this paper, we try to characterize this problem by addressing the extensive characteristic nature of Indian linguistics and the way they closely connect to cultural practices and worldview. We then perform a longitudinal survey of how Natural Language Processing (NLP) techniques have evolved in this space, tracing the historical development of Indic NLP, covering key milestones, methodological shifts, and resource creation efforts. In addition, the paper also examines the structural and sociolinguistic characteristics of Indian languages, such as rich morphology, complex scripts and grammar rules, diglossia, and large dialectal variation, and explains how these create unique challenges for building AI foundation models. We then discuss the growing role of Indic foundation models and analyze how these models address these long-standing resource and representation gaps. Finally, we propose a research direction called 'Culture Sensing', which re-imagines AI based on hermeneutic reasoning. Culture Sensing aims to address open problems such as ensuring equitable performance across low-resource languages and producing outputs that are culturally meaningful. By bringing together past work, current techniques, and emerging trends, this paper outlines research directions that can guide the next phase of Indic NLP and contribute to the development of more robust and inclusive Indic foundation models.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [222] [KAT-Coder-V2.5 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05471)
*Bo Huang,Fengxiang Li,Hao Xu,Haoyang Huang,Hongyi Fu,Jinhua Hao,Kun Yuan,Minglei Zhang,Pengcheng Xu,Shiyang Liu,Wenhao Zhuang,Yuze Shi,Zongxian Feng,Chao Wang,Cheng He,Chongling Rao,Deyu Cao,Fan Yang,Gang Xiong,Haochen Liu,Jiabao Li,Jian Liang,Jinghui Jia,Jingwen Chang,Jun Du,Junyu Shi,Min Li,Mingqi Wu,Qiang Gao,Shangpeng Yan,Shaotong Qi,Shu Xu,Shuo Zhou,Tiankuo Xu,Tong Zheng,Weilun Zhao,Xiancheng Meng,Xianda Sun,Xiaoyu Jiang,Xunhao Jia,Yao Xia,Yimeng Xu,Yinghan Cui,Yingpeng Chen,Yiwen Ning,Yong Wang,Yuxuan Sun,Zhongsheng Liu,Ming Sun,Cheng Luo,Chen Yang,Han Li,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: KAT-Coder-V2.5 is a coding-focused agentic model trained to act autonomously inside real, executable repositories. It addresses bottlenecks of scarce reproducible environments, verifiable rewards, and high-value trajectories through an end-to-end agentic post-training framework involving AutoBuilder, KwaiClawEnv, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation with reinforcement learning. It achieves top results on PinchBench and ranks second only to Opus 4.8 on repository-level software engineering benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The capability of coding-focused agentic models is bottlenecked less by model scale than by the scarcity of reproducible environments, verifiable rewards, and high-value trajectories.

Method: End-to-end agentic post-training framework: (1) AutoBuilder reconstructs multilingual repositories into sandboxed environments with scale verification, regenerates task specifications, recovers near-miss trajectories, and distills supervision via process-aware filtering; (2) KwaiClawEnv synthesizes large-scale tool-use trajectories from executable services and real task seeds; (3) Reinforcement learning with harness randomization, reliability-hardened sandbox, asymmetric actor-critic PPO with hindsight-augmented value estimation, and harness-oriented reward framework; (4) Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation unifying SWE, Agent-Claw, and WebCoding experts.

Result: Best agentic tool-use result on PinchBench; ranks second only to Opus 4.8 on repository-level software engineering across six benchmarks.

Conclusion: KAT-Coder-V2.5 demonstrates that agentic post-training with environment reconstruction, trajectory synthesis, and multi-teacher distillation can significantly boost coding agent performance, achieving state-of-the-art results competitive with frontier models like Opus 4.8.

Abstract: We present KAT-Coder-V2.5, a coding-focused agentic model trained to act autonomously inside real, executable repositories rather than as a single-turn code generator. Its capability is bottlenecked less by model scale than by the scarcity of reproducible environments, verifiable rewards, and high-value trajectories, which we address with an end-to-end agentic post-training framework. AutoBuilder reconstructs multilingual repositories into sandboxed environments with fail-to-pass and pass-to-pass verification at scale, from which we regenerate self-contained task specifications, recover near-miss trajectories, and distill supervision through process-aware filtering, while KwaiClawEnv synthesizes large-scale tool-use trajectories from executable services and real task seeds. We further scale reinforcement learning with harness randomization, a reliability-hardened sandbox, an asymmetric actor--critic PPO with hindsight-augmented value estimation, and a harness-oriented reward framework, and unify SWE, Agent-Claw, and WebCoding experts via Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Across six software-engineering and agentic benchmarks, KAT-Coder-V2.5 delivers the best agentic tool-use result on PinchBench and ranks second only to the frontier Opus 4.8 on repository-level software engineering. Our service is available at https://streamlake.com/product/kat-coder.

</details>


### [223] [TypeGo: An OS Runtime for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05482)
*Guojun Chen,Alex Schott,Lin Zhong*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TypeGo introduces an OS-inspired runtime for LLM-based embodied agents, using asynchronous multi-timescale planning, a Skill Kernel for managing physical resources, preemptive scheduling, speculative skill streaming, and fast first-action paths to achieve real-time control and concurrency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs used as request/response oracles on the critical path are incompatible with real-time control and concurrent goal execution in embodied agents, motivating an operating-system-style runtime approach.

Method: TypeGo structures LLM planning as asynchronous loops at multiple timescales overlapping with execution; uses a Skill Kernel to arbitrate typed physical subsystems among concurrent per-task processes; implements preemptive scheduling by source; employs speculative skill streaming to hide LLM latency; compiles natural language into low-latency interrupt handlers.

Result: On a Unitree Go2 quadruped (Kalos), TypeGo reduces per-step delay by 50% vs. step-by-step planning and time-to-first-action by 73% vs. monolithic planning, while supporting concurrent tasks with low scheduling overhead.

Conclusion: An OS-style runtime for LLM-based embodied agents can significantly improve latency, responsiveness, and concurrency, with TypeGo serving as an early proof of concept.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can plan behavior for embodied agents from natural language, but treating the LLM as a request/response oracle on the critical path is fundamentally at odds with real-time control and concurrent goals. We argue for an operating-system-style runtime for embodied agents, and instantiate this idea in an early prototype, TypeGo. TypeGo structures LLM-based planning as asynchronous loops at multiple timescales that overlap with execution, and manages the agent's physical body like an OS manages hardware: the Skill Kernel arbitrates typed physical subsystems among concurrent per-task processes, a scheduler preempts them and resumes or replaces each by source, and speculative skill streaming hides LLM latency behind ongoing motion, while a fast first-action path yields visible feedback within a second. Users program behavior through natural language prescriptions that TypeGo dispatches to the LLM-based planners or compiles into low-latency interrupt handlers. Our prototype of Kalos, a Unitree Go2 quadruped, provides preliminary evidence for the design: in our current task suite, it cuts per-step delay by 50% over step-by-step planning and time-to-first-action by 73% over monolithic planning, while admitting concurrent tasks at low scheduling overhead.

</details>


### [224] [A Mechanistic Lens on Semantic Conflicts: Using Activation Patching to Understand LLM Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05587)
*Youssef Abdelsalam,Norman Peitek,Anna-Maria Maurer,Marvin Wyrich,Sven Apel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies how semantic conflicts (misalignment between code and semantic cues like comments/identifiers) affect LLM behavior in software-engineering tasks. Using 45 Python snippet triplets and causal intervention via residual-stream activation patching, the authors find that semantic conflicts significantly reduce execution-grounded correctness, with LLMs often following misleading cues. Conflict-related causal signals are concentrated in a small set of token-layer states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs process both executable code and non-executable semantic cues (comments, identifiers) in software-engineering tasks, but these sources can conflict. It is unclear how such semantic conflicts affect LLM behavior and which source dominates their outputs.

Method: Constructed 45 Python snippet triplets isolating conflicts by varying semantic cues or implementation while keeping token-aligned pairs for causal intervention. Evaluated four open-weight LLMs on output prediction and unit-test generation using behavioral performance measures and residual-stream activation patching to identify causally contributing token-layer states.

Result: Semantic conflicts significantly reduce execution-grounded correctness in both tasks; all tested LLMs often follow misleading semantic cues. Residual-stream patching reveals a consistent pattern: changed cue/code regions and a small set of intermediate tokens carry most recoverable causal signal before aggregation near the output readout. For unit-test generation, this pattern extends beyond the prompt.

Conclusion: Semantic conflicts affect program comprehension and downstream tasks, with relevant information concentrated in a small number of causally active residual-stream states. The work demonstrates a framework for mechanistically analyzing how LLMs integrate code-related information under controlled semantic variations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in software-engineering tasks processing executable code and non-executable semantic cues such as comments or identifiers. These two sources can conflict when semantic cues suggest different program behavior than the code itself. It remains unclear how such semantic conflicts affect LLM behavior and which source dominates their outputs.
  We present the first controlled, mechanistic study of LLM behavior under semantic conflicts. To this end, we construct 45 Python snippet triplets that isolate conflicts by varying either semantic cues or implementation while keeping token-aligned pairs for causal intervention. We evaluate four open-weight LLMs on two tasks (output prediction and unit-test generation) using behavioral performance measures and residual-stream activation patching to identify token-layer states that causally contribute to behavioral differences between aligned and conflicting inputs.
  Our results show that semantic conflicts significantly reduce execution-grounded correctness in both tasks and that all tested LLMs often follow misleading semantic cues. Residual-stream activation patching reveals a consistent pattern for final-output prediction: The changed cue/code region and a small set of intermediate tokens carry most of the recoverable causal signal before aggregation near the output readout. For unit-test generation, this pattern extends beyond the prompt, showing that conflict-related information is recoverable at generated sites before producing expected values. Overall, our findings show that semantic conflicts affect program comprehension and downstream tasks, with relevant information concentrated in a small number of causally active residual-stream states, and demonstrate a framework for mechanistically analyzing how LLMs integrate code-related information under controlled semantic variations.

</details>


### [225] [Bridging Stakeholder and Product Requirements: An Empirical Study of Requirement Engineering in the Automotive Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05632)
*Zixu Wang,Shengcheng Yu,Zhenchang Xing,Tobias Wenzel,Chunyang Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study using Infineon's industrial dataset (8,082 stakeholder requirements and 5,870 product requirements) to analyze how stakeholder-level requirements are evaluated, refined, and transformed into product-level requirements in the automotive industry. Key findings include systematic differences across abstraction levels, with refinement complexity driven by architectural scope and missing context rather than linguistic verbosity, along with a derived taxonomy of stakeholder-product mapping patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The automotive industry's shift toward software-driven systems has increased complexity, making effective requirement intake and refinement critical for correctness, compliance, development speed, and systematic reuse. However, limited empirical evidence exists on how stakeholder-level requirements are evaluated, refined, and transformed into product-level requirements in industrial automotive practice.

Method: Mixed-methods approach combining quantitative analyses (requirement structures, decision distributions, mapping patterns) with qualitative analyses (rationales, referenced specifications, software/hardware artifacts) on an industrial dataset from Infineon comprising 8,082 stakeholder requirements and 5,870 product requirements with traceability links, decision outcomes, deviation rationales, and domain references.

Result: Systematic structural and contextual differences exist between stakeholder and product requirements. Refinement complexity is driven primarily by architectural scope and missing contextual information rather than linguistic verbosity. A taxonomy of stakeholder-product mapping patterns was derived, relating these patterns to differing refinement effort.

Conclusion: The findings provide concrete insight into industrial requirements intake and refinement practices and identify actionable opportunities for improving intake validation, deviation management, and tool-supported contextual enrichment to support faster and more reusable automotive product development.

Abstract: The automotive industry's shift toward software-driven systems has increased system complexity and raised the importance of effective requirement intake and refinement for correctness, compliance, development speed, and systematic reuse. Although prior research has proposed techniques for improving requirement quality, limited empirical evidence exists on how stakeholder-level requirements are evaluated, refined, and transformed into product-level requirements in industrial automotive practice. This paper presents a large-scale empirical study based on an industrial dataset from Infineon, comprising 8,082 stakeholder requirements and 5,870 product requirements enriched with traceability links, decision outcomes, deviation rationales, and domain references. Using a mixed-methods approach, we combine quantitative analyses of requirement structures, decision distributions, and mapping patterns with qualitative analyses of rationales, referenced specifications, and software- and hardware-related artifacts. We investigate structural and contextual differences between stakeholder and product requirements, factors influencing acceptance, rejection, and approval with deviation, and the nature of stakeholder-to-product refinement. The results reveal systematic differences across abstraction levels and show that refinement complexity is driven primarily by architectural scope and missing contextual information rather than linguistic verbosity. We further derive a taxonomy of stakeholder-product mapping patterns and relate these patterns to differing refinement effort. The findings provide concrete insight into industrial requirements intake and refinement practices and identify actionable opportunities for improving intake validation, deviation management, and tool-supported contextual enrichment to support faster and more reusable automotive product development.

</details>


### [226] [EvalLoop: A Methodology for Evaluation-Driven Iterative Improvement of Business AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05638)
*Kenneth Benavides,Josh Fleischer,Danti Chen*

Main category: cs.SE
