TL;DR: EvalLoop is a methodology for evaluation-driven iterative improvement of LLM-based systems, replacing static model benchmarking with a structured approach using dimensional metric grouping, failure mode classification, and controlled iteration. Validated on sales intelligence generation, it improved accuracy from 82.6% to 94.6% through targeted diagnosis, while reducing human review burden by 94%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation practices treat LLM evaluation as static model selection (run benchmarks, rank, deploy), which fails to diagnose why systems underperform or guide actionable fixes for production deployments.

Method: EvalLoop organizes evaluation around three mechanisms: (1) dimensional metric grouping decomposing quality into business-relevant dimensions for orthogonal failure diagnosis; (2) failure mode classification categorizing why outputs fail within weak dimensions; (3) structured iteration workflow varying one system variable per run and comparing dimensional profiles before and after.

Result: Dimensional diagnosis identified 69% of hallucination failures as prompt-induced interpretation errors. A targeted prompt fix improved the best model from 82.6% to 94.6% overall (Content Accuracy +16.8pp, Synthesis Power +26.4pp). Undirected configuration change produced zero impact. One-time blind human gate (4 models, 16 cases) reduced review burden by 94% while confirming dimensional rankings.

Conclusion: EvalLoop demonstrates that evaluation-driven iterative improvement with dimensional diagnosis outperforms static model selection. The methodology enables deployment-specific model selection, resolves multi-criteria trade-offs efficiently, and is packaged as reusable artifacts for adoption.

Abstract: Teams deploying large language models in business contexts need evaluation systems, yet most treat evaluation as static model selection: run benchmarks, rank models, deploy the winner. This framing misses evaluation's primary value for production systems--diagnosing why a system underperforms and guiding what to fix. We present EvalLoop, a methodology for evaluation-driven iterative improvement. EvalLoop organizes evaluation around three mechanisms: (1) dimensional metric grouping that decomposes quality into business-relevant dimensions enabling orthogonal failure diagnosis; (2) failure mode classification that categorizes why outputs fail within weak dimensions, bridging diagnosis to action; and (3) a structured iteration workflow where each evaluation run varies one system variable and compares dimensional profiles before and after. We validate EvalLoop through a case study on sales intelligence briefing generation (10 models, 3 providers, 18 metrics, 5 dimensions, 3 iterations). Dimensional diagnosis identified that 69% of hallucination failures were prompt-induced interpretation errors--invisible in aggregate scoring. A targeted prompt fix improved the best model from 82.6% to 94.6% overall, with improvement concentrated in diagnosed dimensions (Content Accuracy +16.8pp, Synthesis Power +26.4pp). An undirected configuration change in a prior iteration produced zero impact, illustrating the cost of iterating without diagnosis. We additionally demonstrate that dimensional profiling enables deployment-specific model selection, and that a one-time blind human gate on a finalist panel (4 models, 16 cases) confirms dimensional rankings while resolving multi-criteria deployment trade-offs--a 94% reduction in review burden compared to evaluating the full design. EvalLoop is packaged as reusable artifacts (playbook, agent specification, template repository) for adoption by other teams.

</details>


### [227] [Agents with Feelings? Personality and Emotion in Multi-Agent Software Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05659)
*Yunyan Ding,Thomas Zimmermann,Iftekhar Ahmed*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates how personality and emotion profiles (Big Five traits, basic emotions, work styles, task roles) impact multi-agent LLM teams in software engineering tasks, finding that profile choice significantly affects performance (up to 11.3 pp gap in pass@1), collaboration dynamics, and cost, with traits like fear and high-conscientiousness increasing revision activity without performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems for SE differentiate agents by roles and workflows, but the impact of agents' behavioral profiles (personality, emotion) on team performance is largely unknown, motivating a systematic investigation using a psychology-informed framework.

Method: The authors designed a psychology-informed framework combining Big Five personality traits, basic emotions, SE-relevant work styles, and task roles. They evaluated 78 team-profile configurations across code generation and code review tasks using four different LLMs and 659 task instances.

Result: Profile choice substantially affects performance and team behavior. The pass@1 gap between best and worst shared-profile configurations reached 7.1-11.3 percentage points across models for code generation. The best mixed-profile configuration outperformed the best shared-profile in six of eight model-task settings. Fear and high-conscientiousness profiles increased revision activity, over-revision, and token usage without consistent performance gains.

Conclusion: Agent profiles are an important design dimension in multi-agent SE systems, affecting not only task outcomes but also collaboration efficiency. This has implications for designing more effective multi-agent teams by carefully selecting personality and emotion profiles.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems for Software Engineering (SE) typically differentiate agents through roles and workflows, but little is known about how agents' behavioral profiles affect team performance. We investigate the impact of personality and emotion profiles on LLM agent teams using a psychology-informed framework that combines Big Five personality traits, basic emotions, SE-relevant work styles, and task roles. We evaluate 78 team-profile configurations across code generation and code review using four LLMs and 659 task instances. Results show that profile choice substantially affects both performance and team behavior. For code generation, the gap between the best and worst shared-profile configurations reaches 7.1-11.3 percentage points in pass@1 across models, while the best mixed-profile configuration outperforms the best shared-profile configuration in six of eight model-task settings. Profiles also influence collaboration dynamics and cost: fear and high-conscientiousness profiles increase revision activity, over-revision, and token usage without consistent performance gains. These findings identify agent profiles as an important design dimension in multi-agent SE systems, affecting not only task outcomes but also the efficiency of collaboration.

</details>


### [228] [What Do AI Agents Actually Change? An Empirical Taxonomy of Mutation Patterns in Performance-Improving Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05666)
*Illia Dovhoshliubnyi,Nima Soroush,Ashkan Sami,Alexander Brownlee*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 1,254 performance-relevant diff hunks from 216 AI agent PRs across five systems, classifying them against a syntactic mutation taxonomy. It finds that name modification (37.0%), object creation (26.4%), and type change (22.7%) dominate—markedly different from prior GI corpora—and that each agent system and performance strategy uses distinct mutation vocabularies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents are black boxes whose internal code generation processes are unobservable. For SBSE techniques like genetic improvement (GI), which depend on realistic mutation operators, understanding how agents actually transform code is critical. Performance-related agent PRs are rare (<1% of 33,596), making each a valuable window into opaque agent behavior.

Method: The authors collected 33,596 agent PRs from AIDev-pop, identified 216 performance-relevant PRs, extracted 1,254 diff hunks, and classified them against the 18-category syntactic mutation taxonomy of Even-Mendoza et al. (2025) using a dual-LLM intersection pipeline.

Result: Three categories dominate: name modification (37.0%), object creation (26.4%), and type change (22.7%). This profile differs sharply from prior GI corpora where "no change" accounted for 84%. Each agent system showed a distinctive mutation vocabulary, and each performance strategy used a largely disjoint subset of categories.

Conclusion: Agent identity and target strategy serve as informative priors that can narrow the effective SBSE operator space, enabling more targeted search-based optimization of AI-generated code.

Abstract: AI coding agents are black boxes: we cannot inspect how they generate code, but we can inspect what they change. This distinction matters for search-based software engineering (SBSE), where techniques such as genetic improvement (in the performance-optimisation application we study) depend on mutation operators that reflect how code is actually transformed. Fewer than 1% of the 33,596 agent PRs in AIDev-pop target performance, making each case a rare window into otherwise opaque agent behaviour. We classify 1,254 performance-relevant diff hunks from 216 of these PRs, spanning five agent systems, against the 18-category syntactic mutation taxonomy of Even-Mendoza et al. (2025) using a dual-LLM intersection pipeline. Three categories dominate: name modification (37.0%), object creation (26.4%), and type change (22.7%), a profile markedly different from prior GI corpora where no change accounted for 84%. Each agent's deployed system commits to a distinctive mutation vocabulary, and each performance strategy activates a largely disjoint category subset. Agent identity and target strategy are therefore informative priors that narrow the effective SBSE operator space. Replication package: https://github.com/5uper6rain/ssbse-challenge-2026

</details>


### [229] [The Cathedral and the Bazaar of Software Vulnerabilities: From the NVD to the CNAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05670)
*Siqi Zhang,Fabio Massacci,Mengyuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes divergence in CVSS metrics across the NVD ("Cathedral") and various CNA sources ("Bazaar"), finding widespread and non-uniform scoring disagreements—especially in Attack Complexity, User Interaction, and Impact—with some divergences being justified while others stem from errors or lack of guidance; models trained on one source suffer up to 40% accuracy drop when applied to another.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The NVD has long been the sole authoritative vulnerability source, but the rise of multiple CNAs creates divergent CVSS scores, threatening the reliability of downstream tasks (e.g., patch prioritization). The paper aims to systematically quantify, understand, and address this divergence.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic analysis of CVSS metric divergence between NVD and public CNAs, and self-divergence (same CNA scoring identical CVE descriptions differently). They perform a qualitative study by interviewing NVD and CNAs, and discuss findings with the CVSS Special Interest Group of FIRST.

Result: Divergence is widespread, non-uniform, and sometimes unexpected; Attack Complexity, User Interaction, and Impact are the most divergent metrics. Some divergence is justified by differing contexts, some stems from human error, and some requires additional FAQ clarification. Models trained on one source lose up to 40% accuracy on another. Since 2025, the situation is improving.

Conclusion: Divergence in CVSS scoring is a systemic issue with nuanced causes (intentional vs. erroneous). Industry-wide changes to CVE generation and clearer guidelines may be needed. Practitioners and researchers should be aware that cross-source model generalization is unreliable, though recent improvements are promising.

Abstract: For decades, the National Vulnerability Database (NVD), the "Cathedral", has been the reference source for vulnerability information for downstream research and industry tasks, e.g., software update prioritization. An emerging "Bazaar" of diverse CVE Numbering Authorities (CNAs) has created many alternative and sometimes diverging sources. We conduct a systematic analysis of divergence in Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) metrics covering the NVD and the public CNAs. We also check for self-divergence: two identical textual descriptions of CVEs with identical CWEs are rated differently by the same CNA. The odds of diverging are widespread, not uniform and sometimes unexpected. The assessment of Attack Complexity, User Interaction, and Impact are the major metrics where divergence happens. To understand the root causes, we perform a qualitative study by reaching out to the NVD and other CNAs (both open sources and proprietary products). We also discussed the findings at the CVSS Special Interest Group of FIRST, the community responsible for maintaining and evolving the CVSS standard. The key insights are that while something might be due to human errors, in some cases diverging is actually the right thing to do and might require changes in the way CVEs are generated industry-wide, in other cases explaining divergence requires access to additional FAQs. The good news is that the situation is improving since 2025, the bad news is that if one downloads the whole NVD (or another CNA dataset) from several years and uses it for predictions, the models trained on one source do not reliably generalize to a different source (accuracy can drop by 40%). We discuss the implications for practice and research.

</details>


### [230] [From Conversation to Contribution: Characterizing Coding Agent in Open-Source Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05677)
*Zihan Fang,Yueke Zhang,Ningzhi Tang,Collin McMillan,Toby Jia-Jun Li,Yu Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 13,360 AI conversation sessions from 1,356 OSS repositories to understand how chat-based interactions with AI coding assistants relate to open-source development. Key findings: heavier AI use in smaller, less mature repositories; AI adoption correlates with more active contributors and lower concentration but communication remains concentrated; Code Writing dominates chat purposes; no broad deterioration in code quality; developers perceive others' AI code as harder to maintain; 68% willing to share chat data but concerns remain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing recognition of AI coding agents and their impact on open-source development, a comprehensive understanding of how developers' chat-based interactions with AI relate to subsequent OSS development and collaboration remains limited, hindering effective design, evaluation, and governance of AI-assisted open-source software development.

Method: Collected 13,360 AI conversation sessions (79,172 user messages) from 1,356 OSS repositories, linked them to repository development histories, and complemented the analysis with a targeted developer survey.

Result: Heavier AI use in smaller, less mature, less collaborative repos; more active contributors and lower contributor concentration after AI adoption (p < .001); communication remained highly concentrated; Code Writing was dominant chat purpose; nearly all AI chats followed by commits; no broad code-quality deterioration; developers perceive others' AI code as harder to maintain (p = .029); 68% willing to share chat data; concerns about competence, reviewer burden, and idea exposure.

Conclusion: Provides a large-scale empirical characterization of AI-assisted OSS contribution and offers practical insights for designing and governing responsible vibe-coding practices in open-source development.

Abstract: AI coding assistants such as GitHub Copilot and Cursor have evolved from code-suggestion tools into conversational collaborators, enabling vibe-coding workflows in which developers guide AI-generated code through natural-language dialogue. Although researchers have increasingly recognized the importance of AI coding agents and begun examining their impact on open-source development, a comprehensive understanding of how developers' chat-based interactions with AI relate to subsequent open-source development and collaboration remains limited. This hinders efforts to effectively design, evaluate, and govern AI-assisted open-source software development. To address this gap, we collected 13,360 AI conversation sessions comprising 79,172 user messages from 1,356 OSS repositories, linked them to repository development histories, and complemented this analysis with a targeted developer survey. We find heavier AI use in smaller, less mature, and less collaborative repositories. After AI adoption, projects tended to show more active contributors and lower contributor concentration (p < .001), although communication remained highly concentrated. Code Writing was the dominant chat purpose, and nearly all AI chat sessions were followed by subsequent commits. We find no broad deterioration in code-quality signals or pull request merging rates. However, developers perceive others' AI-generated code as harder to maintain than their own (p = .029) and view AI as lowering barriers to OSS contribution. While most developers (68%) are willing to share their chat, concerns remain around appearing incompetent, increasing reviewer burden, and exposing ideas to competitors. These findings provide a large-scale empirical characterization of AI-assisted OSS contribution and offer practical insights for designing and governing responsible vibe-coding practices in open-source development.

</details>


### [231] [Articulating Assumptions in AI-Generated Scientific Analyses through Task Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05762)
*Ahmed Hammad,Mihoko Nojiri*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework for semantic differencing of LLM-generated scientific code is introduced, decomposing tasks across specialized agents to improve transparency, reliability, and enable smaller models to execute complete workflows, validated on collider physics analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-generated scientific analysis code often suffers from uncertainty in both the original natural language specification and the generated implementation, making results difficult to understand and reproduce even when code is executable.

Method: A multi-agent framework that assigns code generation, execution, tracing, and validation to separate agents, plus an ambiguity inspection module for rewriting user instructions before code generation; modular design allows domain-specific resource substitution.

Result: Validation on collider physics analyses shows the modular task decomposition improves transparency and reliability over single-prompt approaches, and enables substantially smaller models to execute the complete workflow.

Conclusion: Quantity grounded semantic differencing enhances reproducibility and understanding of LLM-generated scientific code through agent-based decomposition, with demonstrated effectiveness in high-energy physics.

Abstract: Scientific results produced by LLM generated analysis code must be understandable and reproducible. However, uncertainty can arise at different stages of the process, both in the original natural language specification and in the generated implementation. As a result, even executable code may not provide a clear understanding of which quantities are being computed or which assumptions determine the final results. To address this challenge, we introduce quantity grounded semantic differencing, a multi-agent framework for analyzing and comparing scientific programs generated by LLMs. The framework assigns code generation, execution, tracing, and validation to separate agents, allowing it to reconstruct how key output quantities are produced and to identify differences between the intended analysis and the implemented code. We also introduce a module that inspects ambiguities in the initial user instruction and suggests alternative rewrites before code generation. Its modular design enables application to different scientific domains by replacing domain specific resources while preserving the same workflow. We validate the framework on representative collider physics analyses. The results demonstrate that the modular task decomposition enhances both transparency and reliability relative to the previous single prompt approach, while enabling substantially smaller models to execute the complete workflow.

</details>


### [232] [Detecting Vulnerability-Inducing Commits via Multi-Stage Reasoning with LLM-Based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05772)
*Liyou Chen,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao,Yue Pan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VIC-RAGENT is an LLM-based multi-agent framework for detecting vulnerability-inducing commits (VICs) at submission time, using specialized agents and multi-stage reasoning to outperform existing methods by 1.2-1.7x F1-score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detecting VICs at submission time is critical for software security but remains highly challenging due to the need to reason about semantic impact of code changes from heterogeneous sources (code diffs, commit messages, contextual code); existing approaches fail to capture these complex interactions.

Method: Proposes VIC-RAGENT, an LLM-based multi-agent framework with specialized agents for structural analysis, intent understanding, and vulnerability inspection, plus a multi-stage reasoning process (preliminary inspection, reanalysis, final decision) to progressively refine candidate vulnerabilities.

Result: On a real-world dataset across multiple LLMs, VIC-RAGENT consistently outperforms baselines (Direct, CoT, CodeAgent), achieving 1.2-1.7x higher F1-scores compared to the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: VIC-RAGENT provides a robust, explainable, and practical solution for detecting VICs in modern software development workflows.

Abstract: Detecting vulnerability-inducing commits (VICs) at submission time is critical for improving the security and reliability of software systems. However, this task is highly challenging because it requires reasoning about the semantic impact of code changes from heterogeneous information sources, including code diffs, commit messages, and the surrounding contextual code. Existing approaches often struggle to fully capture these complex interactions, resulting in limited detection performance. In this paper, we propose VIC-RAGENT, an LLM-based multi-agent framework for effective and explainable vulnerability detection. VIC-RAGENT leverages multiple specialized agents to provide complementary perspectives, including structural analysis, intent understanding, and vulnerability inspection. To further improve detection reliability, the framework employs a multi-stage reasoning process that progressively refines candidate vulnerabilities through preliminary inspection, reanalysis, and a final decision stage. Experimental results on a real-world dataset across multiple LLMs demonstrate that VIC-RAGENT consistently outperforms baselines, including Direct, CoT, and CodeAgent. Compared to the strongest baseline, VIC-RAGENT achieves 1.2-1.7x higher F1-scores across different models. Overall, VIC-RAGENT offers a robust, explainable, and practical solution for detecting VICs in modern software development workflows.

</details>


### [233] [Can Large Language Models Generate Observability-Aware Code?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05785)
*Yongliang Tao,Hongyu Zhang,Pengfei Gao,Minghua Ma,Zhiyu Fan,Yu Kang,Jue Zhang,Si Qin,Liqun Li,Qingwei Lin,Saravan Rajmohan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies observability in agent-generated software systems, finding a significant gap between diagnostic semantics at the source level and effective fault signals at runtime. Agents partially recover observability artifacts but generate systems that expose fault signals for only up to 13.99% of failures, suggesting current evaluations focusing on functional correctness overlook observability as a critical quality dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While coding agents can generate complex software, existing evaluations focus only on functional correctness. Production systems require observability—the ability to expose failure evidence for monitoring and debugging. This gap motivates the need to study whether agent-generated systems can support observability.

Method: The authors examine whether agents can reconstruct source-level diagnostic semantics by restoring observability artifacts across 10 open-source and 8 industrial repositories. They also evaluate runtime fault signal effectiveness by deploying 200 generated microservice systems on Kubernetes with 13 injected faults.

Result: At the source level, agents partially recover observability artifacts but struggle to capture key diagnostic semantics. At runtime, generated systems expose fault signals for only a small fraction of failures (up to 13.99%), despite the presence of logging. An observability-oriented skill improves both dimensions but gains remain limited.

Conclusion: There is a persistent gap between source-level diagnostic semantics and runtime fault signals in agent-generated systems that is not easily addressed. Current evaluations focusing on functional correctness overlook observability as an important dimension of practical software quality.

Abstract: Recent advances in coding agents have enabled the generation of increasingly complex software systems. While existing evaluations primarily focus on functional correctness, production systems must expose failure evidence to support observability. In this paper, we present a systematic study of observability in agent-generated systems. We examine whether agents can reconstruct source-level diagnostic semantics by restoring observability artifacts in 10 open-source and 8 industrial repositories. We also evaluate whether these artifacts translate into effective fault signals at runtime through 200 generated microservice systems deployed on Kubernetes with 13 injected faults. Our results reveal a consistent gap between diagnostic semantics at the source level and fault signals (i.e., explicit, fault-specific evidence) at runtime. At the source level, agents partially recover observability artifacts but struggle to capture key diagnostic semantics. At runtime, generated systems expose fault signals for only a small fraction of failures (up to 13.99\%), despite the presence of logging, suggesting that the generated observability artifacts may lack the failure-specific semantics needed to effectively expose faults. We further introduce an observability-oriented skill, which can serve as a guidance to improve both diagnostic semantics and fault-signal exposure, but the gains remain limited, indicating that the gap is not easily addressed. More broadly, our findings suggest that current evaluations focusing primarily on functional correctness may overlook observability as an important dimension of practical software quality.

</details>


### [234] [SCOPE: Leveraging Subgoal Critiques for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05810)
*Yueke Zhang,Yifan Zhang,Zihan Fang,Kevin Leach,Wei Zhang,Yu Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SCOPE is a prover-initialized subgoal critic that improves LLM code generation by producing structured feedback (subgoals, gap analysis, robustness checklist) through supervised fine-tuning, process-aligned RL, and feedback-guided inference. It achieves 39.4% pass@1 on LiveCodeBench V6 and 42.6% on BigCodeBench (Hard), outperforming Reflexion and coder-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code generation with LLMs remains unreliable because generated programs can appear syntactically correct while violating key semantic requirements in the natural language specification. Existing feedback-based methods rely on unstructured critique or execution signals that fail to explicitly identify what the code is semantically missing.

Method: SCOPE adapts a Lean-oriented prover model to produce three parseable feedback fields: subgoals, gap analysis, and a robustness checklist. It combines supervised fine-tuning, process-aligned reinforcement learning (RL), and feedback-guided inference, using two complementary rewards during RL—a dense reward for structured critique quality and a sparse reward based on whether the critique improves the coder's execution score.

Result: On LiveCodeBench V6, SCOPE achieves 39.4% pass@1 vs. 36.6% for Reflexion and 20.6% for coder-only. On BigCodeBench (Hard), it reaches 42.6%, surpassing Reflexion (36.5%) and coder-only (34.5%). Gains are concentrated in tasks with concrete semantic constraints, and code corrections are more localized than Reflexion's.

Conclusion: SCOPE's structured, prover-initialized critique approach improves code generation reliability by explicitly identifying semantic gaps, outperforming existing feedback-based methods, particularly on tasks with concrete semantic constraints.

Abstract: Code generation with large language models (LLMs) remains unreliable because generated programs can appear correct while still violating key semantic requirements in the natural language specification. Existing feedback-based methods improve over coder-only generation, but they often rely on unstructured critique or execution signals that do not explicitly identify what the code is semantically missing. We present SCOPE, a prover-initialized subgoal critic for code generation. SCOPE adapts a Lean-oriented prover model to produce three parseable feedback fields for downstream code generation: subgoals, gap analysis, and a robustness checklist. Our approach combines supervised fine-tuning, process-aligned reinforcement learning (RL), and feedback-guided inference, with two complementary rewards during RL: a dense reward for structured critique quality and a sparse reward based on whether the critique improves the coder's execution score.
  Experiments show that SCOPE improves over the compared feedback baselines. On LiveCodeBench V6, SCOPE achieves 39.4% pass@1, compared with 36.6% for Reflexion and 20.6% for the coder-only baseline. On BigCodeBench (Hard), it reaches 42.6%, surpassing Reflexion at 36.5% and coder-only generation at 34.5%. Further analysis shows that SCOPE's gains are concentrated in tasks with concrete semantic constraints and that its code corrections are more localized than Reflexion's.

</details>


### [235] [Beyond Refusal: A Same-Lineage Study of Aligned and Abliterated LLMs for Vulnerability Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05842)
*Mingchen Li,Meikang Qiu,Zifan Peng,Heng Fan,Song Fu,Junhua Ding,Yunhe Feng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines how LLM safety state (Aligned vs. Abliterated) affects software security performance across tasks like vulnerability detection, localization, and patch validation. Tests on Gemma and Qwen model families show that refusal-ablated (Abliterated) models consistently outperform safety-aligned counterparts in actionable defensive utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations conflate safety behavior with confounding factors like architecture, scale, training data, and deployment by comparing unrelated model families. The authors aim to isolate the effect of safety state specifically on defensive software security workflows.

Method: Compare same-lineage Aligned (refusal-intact) vs. Abliterated (refusal-ablated) models from Gemma and Qwen families. Evaluate on vulnerability detection, CWE attribution, vulnerable-line localization, root-cause localization, and executable patch validation. Control prompt wording by varying framing from neutral code-review language to authorization context and cybersecurity terminology density.

Result: In Gemma-based Java/Vul4J repair-validation: Abliterated achieves 67.8% usable patches, 65.0% successful application, 32.8% successful compilation vs. 29.9%, 24.9%, 9.0% for Aligned. In Qwen: Abliterated improves line-level F1 from 2.08% to 3.91% and Top-1 accuracy from 4.10% to 6.95%.

Conclusion: Evaluations of LLM-based security assistants should jointly measure whether models respond, whether usable responses are correct, and whether outputs remain actionable across the engineering workflow.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-assisted software security operates at a difficult boundary: the vulnerability-analysis terminology needed for legitimate code review, triage, and repair can closely resemble terminology associated with misuse. Existing safety and cybersecurity evaluations are difficult to interpret in this setting because they often compare unrelated model families, thereby conflating safety behavior with differences in architecture, scale, training data, and deployment. To isolate this factor, we study safety state: whether refusal behavior remains intact (Aligned) or has been refusal-ablated (Abliterated) within same-lineage models. We ask how this safety state affects defensive utility across software-security workflows. We compare aligned instruction-tuned models with publicly released refusal-ablated descendants from two model families, Gemma and Qwen. We evaluate Aligned and Abliterated states on vulnerability detection, CWE attribution, vulnerable-line localization, root-cause localization, and executable patch validation. We further treat prompt wording as a controlled framing dimension: prompts begin with neutral code-review language, add authorization context, and vary the density of cybersecurity terminology. In a Gemma-based Java/Vul4J repair-validation study, Abliterated achieves higher early-stage validation rates, with 67.8%, 65.0%, and 32.8% of patches judged usable, successfully applied, and successfully compiled, respectively, compared with 29.9%, 24.9%, and 9.0% for Aligned. In the Qwen pair, Abliterated improves localization performance, increasing line-level F1 from 2.08% to 3.91% and Top-1 accuracy from 4.10% to 6.95%. These findings suggest that evaluations of LLM-based security assistants should jointly measure whether models respond, whether their usable responses are correct, and whether their outputs remain actionable across the engineering workflow.

</details>


### [236] [DebugTracker: Lightweight Process Evidence for Classroom Debugging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05871)
*Jiatong Liu,Xue Yao,Zehua Zhang,Yongqiang Tian*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DebugTracker is a VS Code extension that records lightweight debugging-process evidence (test commands, editor/debugger metadata, checkpoints, snapshots, etc.) by separating coached Training Mode from uncoached Evaluation Mode, storing append-only JSONL logs, and exporting timeline/Markdown reports. It is language-agnostic via standard VS Code mechanisms and validated across Python, TypeScript, Java, 16 automated checks, and an 11-case manual trial on three OSes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional debugging assessment relies on final code and test outcomes, hiding the process by which students reproduce failures, form hypotheses, inspect evidence, edit code, and verify fixes. DebugTracker addresses the need to capture this missing process-level evidence.

Method: DebugTracker is implemented as a VS Code extension that records debugging-process evidence by separating uncoached Evaluation Mode traces from coached Training Mode traces. It stores append-only JSONL events and exports timeline and Markdown reports. It captures test commands, editor/debugger metadata, student checkpoints, source snapshots, optional image evidence, human labels, and optional AI-assisted practice feedback. The approach is language-agnostic, using standard VS Code mechanisms rather than language-specific tooling.

Result: Validated with debugging tasks in Python, TypeScript, and Java, 16 automated checks, and an 11-case manual trial matrix spanning packaged VSIX installation and three operating systems.

Conclusion: DebugTracker enables capturing fine-grained debugging process evidence that is normally hidden in final code artifacts, supporting better assessment and feedback for debugging education.

Abstract: Debugging exercises are often assessed from final code and test outcomes, yet these artifacts hide how students reproduced failures, formed hypotheses, inspected evidence, edited code, and verified fixes. We present DebugTracker, a Visual Studio Code extension that records lightweight debugging-process evidence for classroom tasks. DebugTracker separates uncoached Evaluation Mode traces from coached Training Mode traces, stores append-only JSONL events, and exports timeline and Markdown reports for human review. The prototype records test commands, editor and debugger metadata, student checkpoints, source snapshots, optional image evidence, human labels, and optional AI-assisted practice feedback. DebugTracker is largely language-agnostic: it captures process evidence through standard VS Code mechanisms rather than language-specific tooling, although debugger evidence depends on the relevant VS Code language extension. We validate the prototype with debugging tasks in Python, TypeScript, and Java, 16 automated checks, and an 11-case manual trial matrix spanning packaged VSIX installation and three operating systems.

</details>


### [237] [Hidden Amplifiers: Cross-Level Risk in Software Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05894)
*Rakesh Podder,Rafael Fabian Gonzalez Arellano,Indrajit Ray*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A cross-level risk propagation framework is introduced to bridge code-level risk metrics with ecosystem-level dependency exposure, revealing hidden amplifiers—micro-dependencies with <50 methods but >50,000 dependents—that are invisible to current SCA tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analysis tools operate separately at ecosystem level (dependency graphs) or code level (static analysis), creating two failure modes: false-positive CVE alerts for unreachable code and blind spots for structurally critical micro-dependencies.

Method: A unified risk formula that bridges code-level risk metrics with ecosystem-level dependency exposure, enabling cross-level risk propagation analysis.

Result: Preliminary evaluation on 50 packages across npm and PyPI identified hidden amplifiers—micro-dependencies with fewer than 50 methods but over 50,000 dependents—that carry outsized supply-chain risk invisible to all current SCA tools, and can harbor exploitable code for years.

Conclusion: Cross-level analysis opens a new design space for supply-chain security, addressing limitations of current SCA tools that fail to simultaneously consider internal code structure and ecosystem position.

Abstract: Modern software supply chains comprise hundreds of transitive dependencies, yet existing analysis tools operate at either the ecosystem level (dependency graphs) or the code level (static analysis within packages). This separation creates two failure modes. First, false-positive CVE alerts for unreachable code. Second, blind spots for structurally critical micro-dependencies. We introduce cross-level risk propagation, a framework that bridges code-level risk metrics with ecosystem-level dependency exposure through a unified risk formula. Preliminary evaluation on 50 packages across npm and PyPI reveals a class of hidden amplifiers -- micro-dependencies with fewer than 50 methods but over 50,000 dependents -- that carry outsized supply-chain risk invisible to all current Software Composition Analysis (SCA) tools. Without cross-level analysis, such packages can harbor exploitable code for years because no current tool considers both internal code structure and ecosystem position simultaneously. These results suggest that cross-level analysis opens a new design space for supply-chain security.

</details>


### [238] [xDECAF: An Extensible Data Flow Diagram Analysis Framework for Information Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05913)
*Benjamin Arp,Felix Schwickerath,Alexander Vogt,Tom Hüller,Nils Niehues,Nicolas Boltz*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: xDECAF is an extensible tool for architecture-based data flow analysis focusing on information security, featuring a metamodel of labeled flows/nodes, a domain-specific constraint language, and a browser-based editor with analysis engine. The paper presents the tool library, a curated catalog of 20+ example models with documented constraints and expected violations, and publicly available resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an extensible, publicly available tool and reusable dataset for architecture-based data flow analysis with a focus on information security, addressing the need for community-shared resources in this domain.

Method: Combines an extended data flow diagram metamodel with labeled flows and nodes, a domain-specific constraint language supporting various flow operations, and a browser-based editor backed by an analysis engine.

Result: A curated catalog of over 20 example models with documented constraints and expected violations; adoption by multiple research lines demonstrating utility.

Conclusion: xDECAF proves useful for architecture-based data flow analysis in information security, with public availability of the tool, dataset, and online editor facilitating community adoption and reuse.

Abstract: xDECAF is an extensible tool for architecture-based data flow analysis with a focus on information security. It combines an extended data flow diagram metamodel of labeled flows and nodes, a domain-specific constraint language with different flow operations, and a browser-based editor backed by an analysis engine. In this paper, we present the xDECAF tool library and a curated catalog of over 20 example models with documented constraints and expected violations, intended as a reusable dataset for the community. The tool has already been adopted by several research lines, providing concrete evidence of its utility. The tool, dataset, and a hosted online editor are publicly available.

</details>


### [239] [Mitigating Errors in LLM-Generated Web API Invocations via Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Constrained Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05936)
*Daniel Maninger,Leon Chemnitz,Jannis Brugger,Tushar Lamba,Amir Molzam Sharifloo,Mira Mezini*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes and evaluates RAG and constrained decoding as complementary methods to improve LLM-generated web API invocation code. RAG reduces hallucinations when generating full invocations but hurts performance when endpoints are given; CD consistently prevents illegal syntax and improves correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Writing correct web API invocation code remains challenging due to complex, evolving specifications. LLMs have limited ability to generate correct web API integrations, and mitigation techniques for this setting are insufficiently understood.

Method: The authors propose two complementary approaches: (1) RAG with a retriever that processes OpenAPI specs and injects compact endpoint representations into prompts; (2) CD via automatic translation of OpenAPI specs into regex-based constraints enforced during generation. Evaluation is done on WAPIIBench's synthetic dataset and a new real-world GitHub-derived dataset.

Result: RAG reduces hallucinations and improves correctness for full API invocations but reduces correctness when the endpoint is already provided (due to unnecessary parameter generation). CD reliably prevents illegal URLs, HTTP methods, and arguments, substantially improving overall correctness for both starter codes.

Conclusion: RAG and CD are complementary approaches that improve LLM-generated web API code differently. RAG helps with full-invocation generation but can be detrimental when endpoint context is already given; CD offers robust syntactic constraint enforcement. The choice between them depends on the setting.

Abstract: Integration of web APIs is a cornerstone of modern software systems, yet writing correct web API invocation code remains challenging due to complex and evolving API specifications. Although LLMs are increasingly used for code generation, previous work has empirically shown that their ability to generate correct web API integrations is limited. At the same time, mitigation techniques and their effectiveness for this setting remain insufficiently understood.
  In this paper, we propose and systematically evaluate retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and constrained decoding (CD) as two complementary approaches to improving LLM-generated web API invocation code. For RAG, we design a retriever that processes OpenAPI specifications and retrieves compact endpoint representations to inject into model prompts. For CD, we introduce an automatic translation from OpenAPI specifications to regex-based constraints enforced during generation.
  We evaluate both approaches on WAPIIBench's existing synthetic dataset and on a new real-world dataset derived from GitHub repositories. Our results show that RAG reduces hallucinations and improves correctness when generating full API invocations but reduces it when the endpoint is already provided as it encourages the generation of unnecessary parameters. In contrast, CD reliably prevents illegal URLs, HTTP methods, and arguments and substantially improves overall correctness for both starter codes.

</details>


### [240] [Large Language Models Have Unreliable Understanding of Software Engineering Terminology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06004)
*Huzaifa Ejaz,Fabian C. Peña,Steffen Herbold*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLMs show high accuracy in detecting falsified SE terminology definitions but exhibit a systematic rejection bias against correct definitions, and explicit reasoning does not consistently improve—and can even harm—performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no systematic study determining whether LLMs actually understand standardized SE terminology (ISO/IEC/IEEE 24765:2017), which can lead to miscommunication in both LLM-consumed and LLM-generated text in software engineering contexts.

Method: LLMs were prompted with both correct and systematically falsified definitions (semantic substitutions and structural removals). Classification accuracy and reasoning token quality were measured.

Result: Most LLMs detect falsified definitions with high accuracy but also reject many correct definitions, indicating a systematic rejection bias. Explicit reasoning does not consistently improve results and may hinder performance through over-thinking.

Conclusion: Despite impressive performance in many SE tasks, LLMs have fundamental issues in understanding standardized SE terminology, which may impact their reliability in software engineering contexts.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in software engineering (SE), yet there is no systematic study that determines to which degree these LLMs actually understand standardized SE terminology. Lack of such understanding can lead to miscommunication and misunderstanding, both by LLMs consuming text but also by human-developers acting on LLM-generated text. Within this paper, we investigate to which degree state-of-the-art LLMs are able to identify whether definitions from the ISO/IEC/IEEE 24765:2017 Systems and Software Engineering - Vocabulary are correct. We prompt LLMs both with correct definitions, as well as systematically falsified definitions. The falsifications are both semantic (substitution of key terms) and structural (removing critical information). We measure both classification accuracy and whether reasoning tokens generated by the LLMs make sense with respect to understanding the definition. While most LLMs detect falsified definitions with high accuracy, they also reject many correct definitions, indicating a systematic rejection bias rather than genuine discriminative understanding. Explicit reasoning does not consistently improve results and may even hinder performance through over-thinking. Our work demonstrates that while the performance of LLMs (including their agentic use) in many SE tasks is impressive, there are still fundamental issues to understand how this will impact SE, including the consistent use of terminology.

</details>


### [241] [Automating Quality Assessment with NLP of LLM-Generated Defeaters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06039)
*Tihomir Rohlinger,Daniel Ratiu,Stefan Wagner*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes an automated NLP-based approach to assess LLM-generated defeaters (challenges) in assurance cases for high-integrity systems, combining structural features, semantic embeddings, and meta-classifiers. Evaluated on automotive and energy case studies, it achieves an average F1-score of 0.84 and reduces subjective variance compared to human reviewers (Cohen's kappa < 0.442).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-integrity systems require assurance cases to justify safety claims, which must be examined against defeaters under evolving conditions. LLMs can generate candidate defeaters at scale, but assessing their quality remains a manual and subjective process, motivating the need for automated assessment support.

Method: The approach combines structural features from assurance case graphs with semantic embeddings and meta-classifiers trained on expert-assessed defeater annotations, using natural language processing techniques.

Result: Human reviewer dissensus was substantial (Cohen's kappa < 0.442). The proposed classifiers achieved an average F1-score of 0.84 in validation and showed improved alignment with individual expert ratings.

Conclusion: Automated assessment can help reduce subjective variance and provide scalable decision support for assurance case review, while leaving final judgment to domain experts.

Abstract: High-integrity systems, such as autonomous vehicle fleets and large-scale energy infrastructures, rely on structured assurance cases to justify safety claims. To remain valid under evolving operational conditions, such cases must be examined against potential challenges, known as defeaters. While large language models (LLMs) can support the scalable generation of candidate defeaters, assessing their quality remains largely manual and subjective process. This paper presents an automated approach for supporting the assessment of LLM-generated defeaters using natural language processing techniques. The method combines structural features from assurance case graphs with semantic embeddings and meta-classifiers trained on expert-assessed defeater annotations. We evaluate the approach through two case studies in the automotive and energy domains. The results show substantial human reviewer dissensus, with Cohen's kappa values below 0.442, highlighting the difficulty of consistent manual assessment. Against this background, the proposed classifiers achieve an average F1-score of 0.84 in validation and show improved alignment with individual expert ratings. The findings suggest that automated assessment can help reduce subjective variance and provide scalable decision support for assurance case review, while leaving final judgment to domain experts.

</details>


### [242] [SWE-Review: Closing the Loop on Issue Resolution with Agentic Code Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06065)
*Ruoyu Wang,Jierun Chen,Shaowei Wang,Chaofan Tao,Sidi Yang,Yuxin Jiang,Kim-Hui Yap,Lifeng Shang,Xiaohui Li,Haoli Bai*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SWE-Review is a framework for closing the loop on AI-generated pull requests by introducing agentic code review, enabling systematic review, diagnosis, and revision to improve PR quality and move beyond one-shot generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI coding agents generate PRs in an open-loop, one-shot manner without systematic review, diagnosis, or revision, limiting their reliability and effectiveness for real-world software issues.

Method: The authors propose SWE-Review, featuring a reviewer agent that explores the repository, decides whether to accept the PR, and provides structured feedback. They introduce SWE-Review-Bench for evaluation and curate SWE-Review-Traj dataset to address data scarcity for training open reviewer models.

Result: Agentic review continuously improves PRs via a generate-review-revise loop, outperforms single-turn fixed-context review in decision accuracy and resolve rate, transfers to improve issue-resolution models, and enables effective test-time scaling.

Conclusion: Agentic code review is a practical mechanism for moving AI coding agents from one-shot PR generation toward closed-loop issue resolution.

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly generate pull requests (PRs) for real-world software issues, yet one-shot PR generation remains open-loop: the PR is proposed without systematic review, diagnosis, or revision. We introduce \textbf{SWE-Review}, a framework for closing this loop with agentic code review. Given an issue and an AI-generated PR, a reviewer agent explores the repository, decides whether the PR should be accepted, and provides structured feedback for revision. We evaluate this setting with our proposed \textbf{SWE-Review-Bench} to measure both review correctness and downstream revision usefulness. We further curate \textbf{SWE-Review-Traj} dataset to study broader applications of agentic review and fill the data-scarcity gap for open reviewer training. Experiments show that agentic review continuously improves PRs through a generate-review-revise loop, outperforms single-turn fixed-context review in both decision accuracy and resolve rate after revision, transfers beyond review to improve issue-resolution models, and enables effective and efficient test-time scaling. These results position agentic code review as a practical mechanism for moving AI coding agents from one-shot PR generation toward closed-loop issue resolution.

</details>


### [243] [Prompt Coach: An Empirical Evaluation of an Agentic Tutor for Learning Prompt Engineering in Software Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06074)
*Rohit Mehra,Kapil Singi,Vikrant Kaulgud,Vibhu Saujanya Sharma,Swapnajeet Gon Choudhury,Swati Sharma,Adam P. Burden,Majd Sakr*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Prompt Coach is an in-IDE agentic tutor that uses Socratic questioning to help developers learn prompt engineering for code generation. In a study with 15 developers, a single 60-minute session led to statistically significant improvements in prompt quality, high trust, and unanimous agreement that it improved their skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt engineering is a critical yet undertaught skill for software developers, and traditional learning approaches cannot support its evolving, interactive, and context-dependent nature.

Method: The authors built Prompt Coach, an agentic tutor that evaluates prompt quality across multiple dimensions and uses Socratic questioning to guide self-correction, grounded in the developer's codebase and the target LLM's behavior. They conducted an empirical study with 15 professional developers using quantitative prompt quality scoring and qualitative perception measures.

Result: Participants showed statistically significant improvements after a single 60-minute session, with largest gains in dimensions commonly overlooked by developers. They reported strong trust, high adoption readiness, and unanimous agreement that PC improved their prompt-writing skills.

Conclusion: In-IDE agentic tutoring with Socratic guidance is an effective approach for teaching prompt engineering skills to developers, particularly for dimensions that are typically neglected.

Abstract: Prompt engineering has emerged as a critical yet undertaught skill for software developers, one that traditional learning approaches are ill-equipped to support given its evolving, interactive, and context-dependent nature. In this paper, we introduce Prompt Coach (PC), an agentic tutor that helps developers learn how to craft high-quality code-generation prompts through Socratic guidance embedded in-flow within their IDE. PC evaluates prompt quality across multiple dimensions and surfaces targeted questions to guide self-correction, grounded in the developer's codebase and the behavior of the target LLM. We present an early empirical study with 15 professional developers combining quantitative prompt quality scoring with qualitative perception measures. Participants showed statistically significant improvements after a single 60-minute session, with the largest gains across dimensions commonly overlooked by developers. They also reported strong trust, high adoption readiness, and unanimous agreement that PC improved their prompt-writing skills.

</details>


### [244] [Collaborative Multi-Agent Testing for Emergent Failure Discovery in Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06078)
*Ruizhen Gu,Konstantinos Koufos,Donghwan Shin,Vahid Garousi,Mehrdad Dianati*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CREAD is a collaborative multi-agent testing framework for Autonomous Driving Systems that uses a shared blackboard and orchestrator to coordinate perturbation generation, behavioral validation, and search discovery, achieving ~2.1x more failures per 100 scenarios than single-agent baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ADS failures arise from both individual module faults and cross-module interactions (perception, planning, control), but existing testing approaches treat perturbation generation, behavioral assessment, and test case exploration as loosely coupled steps rather than coordinated roles.

Method: CREAD employs a multi-agent architecture with three agents: a Perception Fuzzer Agent (generates perturbations), a Metamorphic Validator Agent (assesses behavioral consistency via metamorphic relations), and an Orchestrator Agent (coordinates exploration). Agents communicate via a shared blackboard and orchestrator.

Result: In HighwayEnv simulations, CREAD achieves ~2.1x more failures per 100 scenarios on average across highway and roundabout environments compared to a single-agent baseline. Gains over a non-collaborative two-agent baseline vary by environment.

Conclusion: Collaborative multi-agent testing is a promising direction for discovering emergent ADS behaviors, and CREAD's extensible architecture allows future integration of planning/control module testing.

Abstract: Autonomous Driving Systems (ADS) can fail because of faults within individual modules as well as from interactions across perception, planning, and control. Yet existing ADS testing research often treats key testing functions, such as perturbation generation, behavioural assessment, and test case selection and exploration, as loosely coupled steps rather than coordinated roles for discovering such failures. We present CREAD, a collaborative multi-agent testing framework for testing ADS that organises perturbation generation, behavioural validation, and search coordination through a shared blackboard and an orchestrator. In the current work-in-progress instantiation, the framework focuses on perception-oriented perturbation generation, while remaining extensible to other ADS modules, including planning and control. It currently comprises a Perception Fuzzer Agent, a Metamorphic Validator Agent, and an Orchestrator Agent. Respectively, they generate perturbations, assess behavioural consistency across related scenario pairs, and coordinate further exploration. Experiments in HighwayEnv simulator show that the collaborative configuration improves failure discovery in the highway environment and remains competitive in the roundabout setting. Across the two environments, it yields about 2.1x as many failures per 100 scenarios as the single-agent baseline on average, while gains over a non-collaborative two-agent baseline vary across environments. These results suggest that collaborative multi-agent testing is a promising research direction for emergent ADS behaviour discovery.

</details>


### [245] [Agents That Teach: Towards Designing Incidental Learning Back into AI-Assisted Software Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06101)
*Rohit Mehra,Samdyuti Suri,Prithviraj K Tagadinamani,Kapil Singi,Vikrant Kaulgud,Adam P. Burden*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper argues that over-reliance on AI coding agents causes "Knowledge Debt" (a developer-level analogue of Technical Debt) as incidental learning is short-circuited, and proposes SHIELD, a multi-agent system based on six design principles that uses agents' reasoning to create contextual learning moments without disrupting developer flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI coding agents boost developer productivity, they eliminate the effortful problem-solving through which developers historically acquired informal knowledge and expertise. This silent atrophy of unpracticed skills creates a "Knowledge Debt" problem that must be addressed.

Method: The authors propose six design principles for learning-aware developer-agent interactions and present SHIELD, a multi-agent system grounded in "agents that teach" that leverages the AI coding agent's own reasoning to surface contextual, out-of-band learning moments without disrupting developer flow.

Result: Introduction of the Knowledge Debt concept and SHIELD system architecture that operationalizes the six design principles to embed incidental learning back into developer-agent interactions.

Conclusion: Productivity and learning should be complementary rather than competing forces in development environments, and incidental learning must be consciously designed into developer-agent interactions to prevent Knowledge Debt accumulation.

Abstract: AI coding agents are rapidly reshaping how software is built, with developers increasingly delegating substantial coding tasks to autonomous agents in pursuit of higher productivity. While these gains are real, they come at the cost of incidental learning. Developers historically acquired informal knowledge through effortful problem-solving, and this has long shaped how software engineering expertise develops. However, with over-reliance on agentic coding, unpracticed skills could atrophy silently over time. As this learning pathway is short-circuited, developers risk silently accruing Knowledge Debt, a developer-level analogue of Technical Debt, where changes the agent executes that the developer cannot fully understand accrue over time. In this paper, we argue that incidental learning will not re-emerge on its own and must be consciously designed back into developer-agent interactions, and propose six design principles to guide such systems. We then present "SHIELD", a multi-agent system grounded in the notion of "agents that teach", that operationalizes these principles by leveraging the AI coding agent's own reasoning to surface contextual, out-of-band learning moments without disrupting developer flow. Through this work, we envision a path toward learning-aware development environments where productivity and learning are complementary, not competing.

</details>


### [246] [Static Metrics Are Insufficient: Predicting Java Method Energy Usage with Execution Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06124)
*Muhammad Imran,Vincenzo Stoico,Ivano Malavolta*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study investigates method-level energy prediction in Java, finding that static source code metrics alone cannot accurately predict energy consumption (R² ≈ 0), but adding execution time as a lightweight dynamic feature improves prediction significantly (R² up to 0.46). Execution time, internal method calls, and cyclomatic complexity are the strongest predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable early-stage reasoning about software energy consumption without repeated profiling and direct measurement, helping developers make informed design and refactoring decisions before energy inefficiencies propagate.

Method: Profiled 2,786 Java methods to extract 33 static source code features and measure execution time and energy. Trained and compared 11 regression models to predict method-level energy consumption.

Result: Static source code metrics alone yield poor predictive performance (R² ≈ 0). Incorporating execution time improves accuracy significantly (R² up to 0.46). Execution time, internal method calls, and cyclomatic complexity are the strongest predictors.

Conclusion: Static metrics alone are insufficient for method-level energy prediction; lightweight dynamic inputs like execution time are necessary for meaningful estimation. Key predictors are execution time, internal method calls, and cyclomatic complexity.

Abstract: The increasing energy demand of software systems is raising concerns about their environmental impact and associated costs. Reasoning on energy usage early in the development flow has the potential to significantly reduce the overall energy usage of a software system, as it allows developers to make informed design and refactoring decisions before inefficiencies propagate. However, assessing energy usage without repeated profiling and direct measurement is difficult, which limits early reasoning in practice. This study investigates the limits of method-level energy prediction in Java, examining whether static source code metrics complemented with method-level execution time can estimate the energy consumption of Java methods. We profile 2,786 Java methods to extract 33 static features and measure execution time and energy, then train and compare eleven regression models. Our findings show that static source code metrics alone yield poor predictive performance, with average R2 values close to zero. Incorporating execution time as a lightweight dynamic input significantly improves accuracy, raising R2 to as high as 0.46. Execution time, internal method calls, and cyclomatic complexity consistently emerge as the strongest predictors of energy consumption.

</details>


### [247] [Evaluating Fine-Tuning and Metrics for Neural Decompilation of Dart AOT Binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06125)
*Raafat Abualazm,Ayman AboElhassan,Amr G. Wassal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Fine-tuning neural decompilation models shows no significant pass@k improvement, with the strongest base model (Qwen3-8B) regressing by -5.65 pp. Cross-lingual interference is significant at smaller scales but vanishes at 8B. CodeBLEU and compile@k diverge from pass@k, making pass@k the required primary metric. Assembly sequence length predicts task difficulty with a cliff at 200 instructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural decompilation is increasingly studied but its evaluation methodology for modern languages (specifically Dart AOT) remains underdeveloped; existing metrics may be unreliable.

Method: Systematic empirical study with six fine-tuned model variants across three base architectures (4B-8B parameters), evaluated via CodeBLEU, compile@k, and pass@k on a new HumanEval-Dart benchmark (154 tasks). Paired task-level statistical tests (McNemar) used for significance.

Result: (1) No configuration yields significant pass@k improvement; Qwen3-8B fine-tuning causes a significant -5.65 pp regression. (2) Cross-lingual interference from Swift is significant at 4B (-2.66 pp) but not at 8B. (3) CodeBLEU and compile@k can improve while pass@k degrades, showing metric divergence. Assembly sequence length is the strongest difficulty predictor (p=0.001), with a cliff at 200 instructions.

Conclusion: pass@k must be the primary evaluation metric for neural decompilation; CodeBLEU/compile@k alone are misleading. The HumanEval-Dart benchmark and Dart-adapted CodeBLEU are contributed. Fine-tuning benefits are limited and capacity-dependent.

Abstract: Neural decompilation is increasingly studied as a code-generation problem, yet its evaluation methodology remains underdeveloped for modern languages. We present a systematic empirical study of fine-tuning effectiveness and metric validity for Dart Ahead-of-Time (AOT) neural decompilation. We evaluate six fine-tuned model variants across three base architectures (4B-8B parameters) using three metrics: CodeBLEU, compile@k, and pass@k on a new 154-task HumanEval-Dart benchmark. Our study yields three principal findings grounded in paired task-level statistical tests. First, no fine-tuning configuration produces a statistically significant pass@k improvement. The sole positive case yields +0.71 pp (McNemar p=0.21), while fine-tuning the strongest base (Qwen3-8B) causes a highly significant regression of -5.65 pp (p<0.001). This capacity-dependent trend is consistent across architectures but needs broader scale sweeps. Second, cross-lingual interference from Swift training is highly significant at 4B (-2.66 pp, p<0.001) but statistically indistinguishable from zero at 8B, consistent with the scaling hypothesis. Third, we demonstrate metric divergence: CodeBLEU and compile@k can improve significantly while pass@k moves in the opposite direction. This has implications for any LLM code generation task where fine-tuning targets superficial similarity. Error analysis reveals assembly sequence length is the strongest predictor of task difficulty (p=0.001), with a capability cliff at 200 instructions. We contribute the HumanEval-Dart benchmark, a Dart-adapted CodeBLEU, and empirical evidence that pass@k must be the primary evaluation metric for neural decompilation.

</details>


### [248] [Property-Driven Synthetic Data Engineering for Data-Scarce Software Systems: Reflections from the Breast Cancer Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06133)
*Aurora Francesca Zanenga,Andrea Bombarda,Marsha Chechik,Saverio D'Amico,Rita De Sanctis,Alberto Zambelli,Claudio Menghi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper argues that synthetic data generation shifts rather than solves the engineering challenge of data scarcity, introducing a new problem called "property-driven synthetic data engineering"—deciding which properties synthetic data must preserve, how to elicit them from stakeholders, validate them under privacy constraints, and manage their evolution—based on experience with intraoperative radiotherapy (IORT) software.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern software systems require data for tasks like analysis and testing, but many critical domains (medicine, safety-critical systems, regulated industries) lack abundant, shareable, or representative real data. Synthetic data generation is often proposed as a remedy, but the authors' experience building software for intraoperative radiotherapy (IORT) in breast cancer treatment revealed that synthetic data does not eliminate the core engineering difficulty.

Method: The authors draw on a collaboration with oncologists and preliminary experiments using a sensitive IORT dataset. They identify and analyze challenges across four dimensions: requirements (which properties to preserve), validation (how to verify properties under privacy constraints), privacy (balancing utility and confidentiality), and pipeline evolution (how properties change over time).

Result: The paper identifies that synthetic data shifts the central engineering problem to "property-driven synthetic data engineering"—the challenge of determining which validity properties synthetic data must preserve, eliciting these from stakeholders, validating them under privacy constraints, and managing their evolution as systems and requirements change.

Conclusion: The authors argue that automated software engineering research should develop methods and tools for eliciting, formalizing, checking, and evolving validity properties for synthetic data in data-scarce software systems.

Abstract: Modern software systems increasingly depend on data for analysis, prediction, testing, and decision-making. Yet many important domains, including medicine, safety-critical systems, and regulated industries, lack abundant, shareable, or representative data. Synthetic data generation is often proposed as a remedy, but our experience engineering software for intraoperative radiotherapy (IORT) in breast cancer treatment suggests that synthetic data shifts rather than solves the central engineering problem. The key challenge becomes deciding which properties synthetic data must preserve, how these properties should be elicited from stakeholders, how they can be validated under privacy constraints, and how they evolve. We call this problem property-driven synthetic data engineering. Drawing on a collaboration with oncologists and preliminary experiments with a sensitive IORT dataset, we identify challenges in requirements, validation, privacy, and pipeline evolution. We argue that automated software engineering research should develop methods and tools for eliciting, formalizing, checking, and evolving validity properties for synthetic data in data-scarce software systems.

</details>


### [249] [A Global Author-Identity Map for the World of Code:62.7M Developer Identities from 106.8M Author Strings over 5.87B Commits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06183)
*Audris Mockus*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper releases a curated author-identity map for World of Code (WoC) version V2604, covering 5.8 billion commits, resolving 73.5% of commits into multi-id identities, achieving recall 0.70 / precision 0.88 against human-rated gold set, and identifying that the primary design challenge is clumping (over-merge) rather than recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mining software repositories at global scale is hindered by fragmented author identity—same developer uses multiple name/email strings, and same string is reused by many developers—necessitating a high-quality, curated identity resolution map.

Method: The authors release four co-versioned artifacts: (1) a global alias map (a2AFullSUG) folding 106M raw strings into canonical identities; (2) per-identity classification (A2clsFull) tagging ids as good, bad-by-attribute, local, bot, or partial; (3) within-project table (P2aAFull) recovering low-quality ids within unambiguous project context; (4) commit-to-identity table (c2AFull) tagging each commit with resolution provenance. The approach avoids naive transitive union over shared-attribute edges to prevent mega-clusters.

Result: The map resolves 73.5% of six billion commits into multi-id identities, raising human-id commit coverage to 98.17%. It is mega-cluster free (largest cluster 6,910 ids from GitHub noreply). Against the ALFAA gold set, recall=0.70, precision=0.88. Prior WoC map's apparent 0.95 precision collapses to 0.52 when its 3M-id mega-cluster is counted.

Conclusion: The core design problem is clumping (over-merge), not recall; global-scale union maps' high precision claims are artifacts of not measuring conflated regions. The map also serves as a cross-corpus join key for scholarly author graphs. All artifacts are publicly released with WoC V2604.

Abstract: Mining software repositories at global scale founders on author identity: the same developer commits under many name/email strings, and the same string is reused by many developers. We release a curated author-identity map for World of Code (WoC) version V2604, covering all 5,866,595,698 commits. It ships four co-versioned artifacts: a global alias map (a2AFullSUG) folding 106,826,059 raw author/committer strings into canonical identities; a per-identity classification (A2clsFull) tagging each id good, bad-by-attribute, local, bot, or partial; a within-project table (P2aAFull) recovering low-quality ids inside the one project where their reuse is unambiguous; and a commit-to-identity table (c2AFull) tagging every commit with its resolution provenance. The map is mega-cluster free, its largest cluster 6,910 ids (one GitHub noreply identity), and it resolves 73.5% of six billion commits into multi-id identities, raising human-id commit coverage to 98.17%. The design problem is clumping, not recall: a naive transitive union over shared-attribute edges welds three million unrelated people into one cluster, an over-merge that recall-only benchmarks price at zero. We report both error families, splitting and clumping, and show the high precision claimed by global-scale union maps can be an artifact of never measuring the conflated region. Against the ALFAA human-rated gold set the map scores recall 0.70 / precision 0.88, where the prior WoC map's apparent 0.95 precision collapses to 0.52 once its 3,006,318-id mega-cluster is counted. A canonical software-author identity is also a cross-corpus join key to scholarly author graphs, where clumping is again the binding constraint. All artifacts ship with the WoC V2604 release and a self-contained replication package.

</details>


### [250] [What Resolve Rate Hides: Trajectory Structure Diagnostics for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06184)
*Rui Shu,Chun Yong Chong,Xin Zhou,Yun Peng,Zihan Wu,Xu Han,Zeyang Zhuang,Guowen Yuan,Yuan Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TraceProbe is a trajectory-diagnostic framework that recovers process information hidden by binary resolve rates in coding agents. It normalizes runs into a nine-type action taxonomy and applies rule-based modules (Insight and Converge) to detect anti-patterns and compare behavior divergences. Applied to 2,500 trajectories, it reveals that function-level behavior, not file choice, distinguishes success from failure, anti-patterns serve as difficulty indicators, and resolved runs still differ in efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve rate (pass/fail on final tests) is the dominant metric for coding agents, but it hides all process-level information such as why a run failed, why it spent extra steps/tokens, or how two agents with the same outcome differed in behavior. The trajectory contains this evidence but raw traces are heterogeneous and hard to compare.

Method: TraceProbe normalizes each raw trajectory into a canonical nine-type action taxonomy with deterministic effect labels, then applies two modules: Insight (identifies single-trajectory anti-patterns like search loops, verification skips) and Converge (aligns paired runs to classify where behavior diverges under controlled references).

Result: Applied to 2,500 trajectories from five production settings on SWE-Bench Verified: (i) file choice is too coarse to separate success from failure, but function selection and completion behavior localize it; (ii) Insight anti-patterns act mainly as corpus-level difficulty clues, with search loops the most stable; (iii) even resolved runs differ in how quickly they reach relevant code and how much failed work they incur.

Conclusion: Trajectory structure adds auditable diagnostic context to binary outcomes by localizing inspection targets, suggesting failure hypotheses, and prioritizing runs for review.

Abstract: Coding agents are ranked almost entirely by resolve rate: whether their final patch passes the target tests. Yet two agents can reach the same outcome through very different processes, and a single pass/fail label says nothing about why a run failed or why an accepted run spent extra steps, time, or tokens.
